Orientation — Scope, Engines and Method

0.1 Scope and intended audience0.2 The engine matrix and where the three diverge0.3 The reference model: a multi-tenant SaaS0.4 The design pipeline: model to operations0.5 Tooling: diagrams, migrations and plan analysers0.6 Conventions and how to read the listings

This chapter fixes the scope of the guide, names the engines whose behaviour is quoted throughout, introduces the worked example that carries the examples from chapter to chapter, and states the method by which a schema is designed, deployed and then defended. Every later chapter assumes the vocabulary established here.

0.1 Scope and intended audience

The subject is the design of relational data systems: how a business problem is converted into tables, keys, indexes and partitions, and how those decisions propagate into latency, throughput, availability and maintenance cost. It is reference documentation — declarative statements of what each decision buys and what it costs — not a tutorial in query syntax.

The guide is written for engineers who already write application code and are being handed a schema, or who own a schema that no longer performs and cannot explain why. It assumes the reader can read a function signature and a stack trace. It does not assume the reader knows what a foreign key is, and it does not assume the reader has internalised the idea that the physical layout of data is a design decision, which is the single most consequential thing this guide attempts to teach.

In scopeOut of scope
Requirements, access patterns and workload shapeSQL grammar tutorials; SELECT syntax is assumed
Conceptual and logical modelling: entities, keys, relationships, temporal dataDialect-specific language detail, which the companion sqlserver.html guide covers exhaustively
Normalisation, denormalisation and the invalidation ledgerApplication architecture, RPC frameworks and API versioning
Physical design: row and column storage, ordering, partitioning, shardingObject-relational mappers and their behaviour, except where they distort a schema
Index selection, write cost and maintenance couplingVisual query builders and ad-hoc report tooling
Data type choice and storage economicsMachine-learning feature stores and vector databases, except as contrast
Isolation levels, concurrency control, idempotency and transactional outboxMessage broker configuration; brokers are treated as consumers of the schema
Where engines genuinely diverge, and what the divergence costsLicensing and procurement

Two exclusions deserve emphasis because they are frequently confused with this subject. Data modelling as a discovery technique — interviewing stakeholders, eliciting requirements, story mapping — is a product discipline and is represented here only by its output, the access-pattern inventory in chapter 1. Query optimisation is covered to the depth needed to make design decisions defensible: how to read a plan, how to attribute cost to an index, how to detect a statistics problem. Deeper optimiser material belongs with the dialect guides.

Applies to. Version targets for the whole guide: PostgreSQL 18 (18.0 GA 25 September 2025), MySQL 9.x Innovation line (9.0 GA 1 July 2024, quarterly cadence thereafter) with 8.4 LTS as the conservative floor, and SQL Server 2025 (17.x, GA 18 November 2025). Statements are written against those releases. Where a feature is version-gated, the version is named inline; where behaviour is unverified against the target release, the text says so and provides a probe rather than an assertion.

The organising argument of the guide is that data modelling is not a phase that ends before engineering starts. It is a continuous re-derivation of the same artefacts under new load, and every artefact has a maintenance cost attached to it that is invisible until the cost is being paid. A schema is therefore evaluated on four axes, and a design that is only correct on one of them is not correct:

AxisThe question it answersArtefact that carries it
CorrectnessDoes the data mean what the business means, and can the database refuse states that are impossible?Conceptual model, constraints, keys
ShapeCan each declared access pattern be served by a seek, a covering scan or a cheap join?Access-pattern inventory, indexes, partitioning key
EconomicsWhat does each stored byte cost to write, replicate, back up, vacuum and restore?Data types, physical order, denormalisation ledger
ChangeWhat is the cheapest correct migration when the model must evolve in production?Schemaless boundaries, migration tooling, expand-contract plan

Caveats. Every numeric figure in this guide — row sizes, index depths, byte overheads, fan-out counts — is an order-of-magnitude estimate computed for the reference model at a stated row count, not a measurement. They are supplied to make a decision orderable, not to be quoted as a benchmark. Where a number matters to a real design, the text gives the query that measures it on the target system instead of a number to copy.

0.2 The engine matrix and where the three diverge

The guide is engine-agnostic in its arguments and engine-specific in its consequences. This section states what each engine is used here to represent, and the table that the rest of the guide refers back to whenever a design choice lands differently on different engines.

Choosing a target engine is a design input, not a deployment detail, because four decisions in this guide are effectively foreclosed by the engine: the primary-key type, whether partial indexes exist, whether snapshot reads are the default, and how many physical databases can exist on one host. The honest way to present this is as three different default positions rather than three implementations of one ideal.

CapabilityPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Position in this guideReference implementation: richest type system and the most extensible indexing, so design constraints are visible as constraintsWeb-scale OLTP under managed cloud; the planner is simpler, replication is a first-class product featureMicrosoft estate: identity, Always On, columnstore, temporal tables; the row and page format impose hard limits
Default isolationREAD COMMITTED, snapshot per statement, MVCC, no read locksREPEATABLE READ with next-key and gap locks — a genuinely stronger and more deadlock-prone defaultREAD COMMITTED with shared locks; RCSI must be enabled per database
Partial / filtered indexWHERE clause on the indexNot supported; emulated with a generated columnFiltered index on nonclustered indexes only
Specialised index typesHash, BRIN, GiST, SP-GiST, GIN; extensible via index access methodsB-tree, hash on MEMORY only, full-text, R-Tree spatialClustered and nonclustered B-tree, columnstore, spatial, XML, graph, full-text
Covering indexINCLUDE columns, index-only scan gated on the visibility mapComposite index only; secondary entries carry the primary keyINCLUDE columns; a scan is still a lookup into the base unless columnstore
Declarative partitioningNative since 10, detach and attach concurrentlyNative but restrictive: no foreign keys on partitioned InnoDB tables, and the partition columns must appear in every unique keyPartitioned tables and views, with SWITCH for low-downtime movement
Columnar storageNone in core; a columnar extension patch has not mergedNone in the base engine; the commercial HeatWave engine adds oneClustered and nonclustered columnstore, batch-mode execution
Row size limitApproximately 8 kB per tuple before TOAST takes overHalf a page for compact rows, then off-page storage8 kB maximum row, hard limit, no off-page overflow for most types
Temporal modellingTime ranges as first-class range types; exclusion constraintsRange and period types via the 8.0 type work, but no exclusion constraintsSystem-versioned temporal tables, which model transaction time only

The most consequential row in that table is the isolation default, and it is examined in chapter 7. A team porting a schema from MySQL to PostgreSQL without changing its retry logic inherits a production incident; a team porting from PostgreSQL to MySQL inherits an unexplained deadlock rate and a different set of anomalies. Design decisions that depend on an isolation level are engine-coupled even when the DDL is not.

-- Probe 1: identify the server and the feature set you are reasoning about.
-- PostgreSQL 18. io_* settings exist only from 18 onward.
SELECT current_setting('server_version')                       AS version,
       current_setting('server_version_num')                  AS version_num,
       current_setting('default_transaction_isolation')      AS iso,
       current_setting('data_directory')                     AS datadir;
SELECT name, setting FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('io_method','io_workers','io_max_concurrency','effective_io_concurrency')
    OR name LIKE 'default_statistics%';

-- MySQL 9.x. innodb_redo_log_capacity replaced the log_file_size pair in 8.0.30.
SELECT version(), @@transaction_isolation, @@innodb_redo_log_capacity,
       @@innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit, @@sync_binlog, @@binlog_transaction_dependency_tracking;
SELECT * FROM performance_schema.global_variables
 WHERE variable_name LIKE 'innodb_snapshot_isolation%';

-- SQL Server 2025. Version 17 = 2025, 16 = 2022, 15 = 2019.
SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('ProductVersion')      AS version,
       SERVERPROPERTY('ProductMajorVersion') AS major,
       SERVERPROPERTY('Edition')            AS edition;
SELECT name, is_read_committed_snapshot_on, is_allow_snapshot_isolation_on
  FROM sys.databases
 WHERE database_id = DB_ID();

Run the probe before you run the design review. A large share of design arguments — we need snapshot reads, we need a partial index, we need a row larger than 8 kB — are settled by three values above, and discovering them late invalidates work already done.

Caveats on the matrix. Managed services constrain the answer: a row-level security requirement, a columnar requirement or an ONLINE = ON index operation may be unavailable on the lowest service tier and available on the next one up. Where a capability is edition- or tier-gated, verify it against the target tier’s own documentation rather than against the engine’s; the engine-level answer is necessary but not sufficient.

0.3 The reference model: a multi-tenant SaaS

A design guide is only as useful as its worked examples, and examples written in the abstract teach nothing. The reference model is a subscription-billing SaaS platform with three characteristics that generate every design problem in the guide: tenancy, unbounded growth in a write-heavy table, and a legal obligation to reconstruct history.

The domain is deliberately ordinary. A tenant is a customer organisation. An app_user belongs to exactly one tenant. A tenant subscribes to exactly one plan at a time through a subscription that changes over time. A tenant places sales_orders which have sales_order_lines. Money leaves through payment_attempts, which are retried and must therefore be idempotent. Every state change is mirrored to audit_events for compliance, and every domain event is published through an outbox so that a broker delivery cannot be lost with a committed transaction.

-- Reference model, core DDL. Engine-neutral subset; engine notes follow the listing.
CREATE TABLE tenant (
    tenant_id    bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    slug         text NOT NULL,
    name         text NOT NULL,
    status       text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'active',
    created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT uq_tenant_slug UNIQUE (slug),
    CONSTRAINT ck_tenant_status CHECK (status IN ('active','suspended','closed'))
);

CREATE TABLE plan (
    plan_code        text PRIMARY KEY,              -- natural key: 5 characters, stable, meaningful
    name             text NOT NULL,
    monthly_price    numeric(12,2) NOT NULL CHECK (monthly_price >= 0),
    seat_limit       int NOT NULL,
    api_call_quota   bigint NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE app_user (
    user_id      bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id    bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    email        text NOT NULL,
    status       text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'invited',
    created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT uq_user_email UNIQUE (tenant_id, email)
);

CREATE TABLE subscription (
    subscription_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id        bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    plan_code        text NOT NULL REFERENCES plan(plan_code),
    status           text NOT NULL,            -- trialling, active, past_due, cancelled
    started_on       date NOT NULL,
    period_start     date NOT NULL,
    period_end       date NOT NULL,
    version          int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- optimistic concurrency, chapter 7.4
    CONSTRAINT ck_sub_period CHECK (period_end > period_start),
    CONSTRAINT uq_sub_tenant UNIQUE (tenant_id)   -- one live subscription per tenant
);

CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id        bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id       bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    subscription_id bigint,                      -- nullable: orders can precede subscription
    placed_at       timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    status          text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'placed',
    currency        char(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'GBP',
    total_excl_tax  numeric(14,2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    version         int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);

Three naming decisions in that listing are load-bearing for the rest of the guide. sales_order is not order, because ORDER is a reserved word in all three engines and an unquoted identifier in a migration script is a portability bug waiting to be found. period_start and period_end are date, not timestamptz, because a billing period is a calendar interval rather than an instant, and giving it a time zone invites the conversion bug described in section 6.4. total_excl_tax is numeric(14,2), and the reason it is stored rather than derived is the invalidation ledger in section 3.6.

-- The rest of the model: lines, payments, audit and the outbox.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_line (
    order_id            bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES sales_order(order_id),
    line_no             int NOT NULL,
    sku                 text NOT NULL,
    description         text NOT NULL,
    quantity            int NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
    unit_price_excl_tax numeric(14,4) NOT NULL CHECK (unit_price_excl_tax >= 0),
    tax_rate            numeric(5,4) NOT NULL,      -- 0.2000 = 20%, not 20
    line_total_excl_tax numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_no)                    -- weak entity: no independent identity
);

CREATE TABLE payment_attempt (
    payment_id      bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id       bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    order_id        bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES sales_order(order_id),
    idempotency_key text NOT NULL,
    provider        text NOT NULL,
    provider_ref    text,
    amount          numeric(14,2) NOT NULL CHECK (amount > 0),
    status          text NOT NULL,
    created_at      timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT uq_payment_idem UNIQUE (tenant_id, idempotency_key)
);

CREATE TABLE audit_event (
    event_id     bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id    bigint NOT NULL,
    occurred_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    actor_id     bigint,
    entity_name  text NOT NULL,
    entity_id    text NOT NULL,
    operation    text NOT NULL,      -- INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE
    before_state jsonb,
    after_state  jsonb
);

CREATE TABLE outbox (
    outbox_id     bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id     bigint NOT NULL,
    topic         text NOT NULL,
    aggregate_id  text NOT NULL,
    payload       jsonb NOT NULL,
    created_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    published_at  timestamptz
);
TableGrowth profileDominant access patternDesign problem it generates
tenant, app_userLinear in customers; millions at mostPoint lookup by id or by (tenant_id, email)None. B-tree, unique constraint, done
planDozens of rows, effectively staticPoint lookup; occasionally a full listCache it. It is smaller than the index that would find it
subscriptionOne row per tenant, updated monthlyPoint lookup by tenant; batch scan of expiring rowsUnique constraint expresses a business rule as a schema rule
sales_orderThe growth table: 108 rows in three years at 1 k orders/sRecent orders by tenant, order detail by key, monthly aggregatePartitioning, retention, the write-cost ledger
sales_order_line3–20× the order countAlways fetched by order_id; never scanned aloneComposite key, no surrogate, no separate index on the child
payment_attemptGrows with attempts, not with ordersBy idempotency key; by order for reconciliationIdempotency as a unique constraint rather than as application logic
audit_eventAppend-only, largest table by row countRange scan by tenant and time; rarely updatedBRIN candidate, partition by month, never vacuum into uselessness
outboxAppend then delete; size bounded by relay lagPolled by relay with SKIP LOCKEDA queue that must be inside the same transaction as the domain write

The growth column is the most useful column in that table, and it should be filled in before modelling begins. A table that grows without bound and a table that is rewritten constantly impose different physical designs, different index sets and different vacuum or rebuild schedules, regardless of how similar their columns look.

Caveats. The listing omits two things a production schema would not. It has no NOT NULL guarantee tying sales_order.tenant_id to the subscription’s tenant, which is a cross-row invariant a foreign key cannot express directly; section 7.3 returns to it as a write-skew problem. And it has no bi-temporal columns on subscription, which means a plan change is recorded by overwriting and the history is lost — the defect section 2.6 repairs with an effective_range pair and a history table.

0.4 The design pipeline: model to operations

A schema is a set of decisions with a half-life. This section states the pipeline in which those decisions are made, the artefact each stage produces, and the review gate that must pass before the next stage is entered. The pipeline is drawn as a loop because in practice it iterates: the first measurement invalidates the first estimate.

  business          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  problem     ─────>│ 1  data requirement  (what must be knowable)  │
                     └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         v
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                     │ 2  access-pattern inventory (AP-01 .. AP-nn)  │  <-- the highest-leverage artefact
                     └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         v
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                     │ 3  logical model    (entities, keys, 1NF..3NF)│
                     └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         v
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                     │ 4  physical model   (order, types, indexes,   │
                     │                     partitions, retention)   │
                     └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         v
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                     │ 5  DDL + migrations (expand / contract,       │
                     │                     reversible per step)      │
                     └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         v
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                     │ 6  validation under production-shaped load   │  <-- plan, buffers, p99, not rows/sec
                     └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                                         v
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                     │ 7  operating rules  (vacuum, stats, rebuild, │
                     │                     retention, growth alert)  │
                     └──────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                                        |
    and back to 1 <----------------─────  measurement invalidates the estimate
StageOutput artefactReview gate: the question that blocks progress
1 Data requirementA paragraph per domain noun stating what a fact is, who owns it and how long it must be keptCan two engineers write different sentences from this and both be right?
2 Access patternsNumbered statements, each with a frequency, a latency budget and a growth curveDoes every one have a stated p99, not just an average?
3 Logical modelERD plus the constraint list; normalisation target stated explicitlyIs every constraint enforced by the database rather than by a service?
4 Physical modelDDL, index list with a stated reason each, partitioning key, expected row count at 24 monthsIs every index justified by a named AP, and is every relationship either joined or duplicated deliberately?
5 MigrationsForward script, backward script, and the interleaving rule for the twoCan step 2 run while step 1's code is still serving traffic?
6 ValidationPlan with buffers, index usage after a representative soak, p99 at target concurrencyDoes the plan match the design intent, or is it succeeding by accident?
7 OperationsRunbook: vacuum cadence, statistics targets, partition lifecycle, growth alert thresholdsDoes every automated action have a stated trigger and a stated rollback?

The gate at stage 2 is the one most often skipped, and skipping it is why schemas end up with eleven indexes and two access paths. If an index cannot name the AP it serves, it is either dead weight or evidence that a missing AP was never written down. Both outcomes are worth knowing at design time, when they cost an hour, rather than at review time, when they cost a migration.

Migration discipline. Stage 5 is expand-and-contract, and it is a schema concern rather than a deployment concern. Add the new column as nullable with no default; backfill in batches; dual-write from the application; switch reads; only then drop the old column. Every step must be independently reversible because a release is a sequence of steps, not an event, and a partial sequence is the normal case during an incident.

Write the AP inventory into the repository next to the migrations, as a document or a table, and require it to change in the same pull request that changes the schema. A pull request that adds an index without adding an AP should be rejected; so should one that adds an AP without an index. The two edits are the same edit.

0.5 Tooling: diagrams, migrations and plan analysers

Three tool categories carry the pipeline: a diagramming tool for the logical model, a migration tool that makes schema change a reviewable and reversible artefact, and a plan analyser for validating that the physical design does what the logical model intended. Each category has a dominant choice and a set of entry-level alternatives.

CategoryPrimary toolsWhat each is forFailure mode to avoid
Diagramming, code-firstdbdiagram.io (DBML), erd notation, Schemacrawler, SchemaSpy, DDL-to-ERD renderersDBML is a text format that diffs and reviews like code; SchemaSpy and Schemacrawler generate the ERD from a live database, which catches drift the drawing hidesA diagram maintained by hand. It is wrong within two sprints and everyone knows it
Diagramming, visual-firstFigma, draw.io, Lucidchart, ExcalidrawCollaborative exploration with non-engineers; the best medium for the stage 1 and 2 workshops where the access patterns are argued outTreating a pretty picture as a schema. The picture has no keys, no types and no constraints
Migrations, SQL-firstFlyway, sqitch, dbmate, raw files plus a runnerVersioned plain SQL with a checksum; sqitch additionally models deploy, verify and revert as first-class, which is the right shape for a regulated systemAd-hoc ALTER executed by hand against production. It is the single largest source of irreproducible environments
Migrations, ORM-coupledLiquibase, Prisma, Drizzle, Alembic, EF Core migrationsChangelog in a DSL or generated from entity definitions; useful when the application is the source of truth for the modelLetting the ORM’s model become the physical design. Index and partition choices are rarely expressible in the DSL
Online schema changegh-ost, pt-online-schema-change, pgroll, pg_squeeze, SqlPackage and DACFx for SQL ServerApplying a change to a large table without a long exclusive lock; shadows the table with a trigger or logical replication and swaps on completionAssuming a long ALTER is acceptable because it works on staging, where the table has 1 000 rows
Plan analysisEXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS), EXPLAIN ANALYZE, EXPLAIN FORMAT=TREE, SET STATISTICS IO/XML, Query Store, pg_stat_statements, auto_explainProving that the physical design is serving the AP; measuring pages touched rather than elapsed timeReading elapsed time from the plan header and concluding nothing. The header is wall clock, not work
Load generationpgbench, sysbench, mysqlslap, k6, JMeterProduction-shaped concurrency against a restored snapshot, so that the measurement includes the data distribution the design must cope withBenchmarking an empty or toy-sized database. Optimiser behaviour and vacuum behaviour are both size-dependent
-- Plan analysers: the four forms actually needed.

-- PostgreSQL: actual execution, buffers, and which GUCs the plan assumed.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS, FORMAT TEXT)
SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = 42 AND placed_at >= '2026-01-01' ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50;

-- Capture the slow queries that did not reach the log, with nesting and timing.
LOAD 'auto_explain';
ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '200ms';
ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;
ALTER SYSTEM SET auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on;
SELECT pg_reload_conf();

-- MySQL: tree format plus actual execution. ANALYZE is 8.0.18+.
EXPLAIN FORMAT=TREE SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = 42;
EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT status, COUNT(*) FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = 42 GROUP BY status;

-- SQL Server: estimated plan, then the cache statistics that cost the query.
SET STATISTICS IO, XML ON;
SELECT * FROM dbo.sales_order WHERE tenant_id = 42;
SET STATISTICS IO OFF;
SELECT TOP 20
       qs.total_worker_time / 1000 AS cpu_ms, qs.execution_count,
       qs.total_logical_reads, qs.avg_elapsed_time / 1000 AS avg_ms,
       SUBSTRING(CAST(qs.query_plan AS nv(max)), 1, 60) AS plan_head
  FROM sys.dm_exec_query_stats qs
 ORDER BY qs.total_worker_time DESC;

Two habits separate a measurement from an anecdote. First, record buffers or reads, not elapsed time: page counts are stable across runs and comparable across machines, whereas elapsed time conflates the query with everything else the server was doing. Second, run ANALYZE inside a transaction you can roll back, or on a read replica, because EXPLAIN ANALYZE executes the statement and an UPDATE or a locking SELECT under a production plan is a real side effect.

Caveats. auto_explain with log_analyze = on instruments every statement above the threshold, including the overhead of instrumenting them; on a busy system it is a significant load and belongs on a single session while investigating, not permanently at 200ms. In MySQL, EXPLAIN ANALYZE only executes the statement, and the tree it prints is the real plan; the classic EXPLAIN is a cost estimate that can differ. In SQL Server, the XML plan for a parameterised query is the plan for the parameter, not for the literal, which is the parameter-sniffing trap described in the companion guide.

-- Migration as a reviewable, reversible artefact. sqitch-style layout:
--   deploy/  0001_create_tenant.sql        forward only
--   verify/  0001_create_tenant.sql        assertions that must pass afterwards
--   revert/  0001_create_tenant.sql        forward-only reversal
--   deps:    0002_sales_order.sql -> :0001_create_tenant   dependency edge, not date order

-- A migration that adds a nullable column is safe to deploy while old code runs.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN external_ref text NULL;
-- No DEFAULT, no NOT NULL: a default rewrites the table on all three engines,
-- and a volatile default rewrites it on every upgrade of the engine.

-- Backfill in bounded batches with a sleep, so the write load is a background task.
UPDATE sales_order
   SET external_ref = 'MIG-' || order_id
 WHERE external_ref IS NULL
   AND order_id IN (SELECT order_id FROM sales_order
                WHERE external_ref IS NULL
                ORDER BY order_id LIMIT 10000);

-- The constraint is added NOT VALID first, then validated, then promoted.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD CONSTRAINT uq_order_external
    UNIQUE (external_ref) NOT VALID;   -- PG 12+; 8.0.16+ MySQL has no such split
ALTER TABLE sales_order VALIDATE CONSTRAINT uq_order_external;

Caveats. NOT VALID is a PostgreSQL feature for foreign keys and check constraints and exists for unique indexes only from PostgreSQL 12, and it has no MySQL equivalent at all. On SQL Server the equivalent sequence is to create the unique constraint WITH (ONLINE = ON) for a nonclustered index, which is Enterprise-licensed and edition-dependent. The portable floor for adding a unique constraint to a populated table is: build the unique index concurrently or online, then attach the constraint to it. Test the lock behaviour on production-sized data, because a 107-row table and a 104-row table behave differently here.

0.6 Conventions and how to read the listings

This section fixes the notation, the callout vocabulary and the SQL dialect used in the listings, so that a statement about behaviour is never ambiguous about which engine or which version it applies to.

Listings are written in a common subset: ANSI types and constraints, IDENTITY where all three engines agree, and timestamptz / jsonb names where they do not, with the equivalent given in the surrounding prose or in a table. Where a construct exists on only one engine, the listing is labelled and the alternative is shown immediately. A listing is a specification of a decision, not a portable script; no multi-engine script is portable in both directions without change.

ConceptPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Surrogate keybigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITYbigint AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEYbigint IDENTITY(1,1) PRIMARY KEY
Time zone-aware instanttimestamptzTIMESTAMP — converted to UTC on write using the session time zonedatetimeoffset(3)
Wall-clock time, no zonetimestampDATETIME — no conversion, 1000–9999 year rangedatetime2(3) — datetime is legacy, 3.33 ms rounding
Semi-structuredjsonb (binary, key-sorted) or json (verbatim text)JSON (binary, duplicate keys kept)Native json type (2025); nvarchar(max) before that
Optimistic concurrency tokenxmin is usable; otherwise a version intA version int column; no system column equivalentrowversion (8-byte, system-maintained)
Row lock without blocking readersSELECT ... FOR UPDATE — waits, then errors under SSI conflictsSELECT ... FOR UPDATE — takes a next-key lockUPDLOCK, READPAST, ROWLOCK hints under RCSI
Text search indextsvector with a GIN indexFULLTEXT index on InnoDBFull-text index, or LIKE on a persisted computed column
EnumCREATE TYPE ... AS ENUM, 4-byte OID compared by OIDENUM('a','b'), stored as an index integerNo enum type; a lookup table plus CHECK

Identifiers in this guide are snake_case for tables and columns, which matches PostgreSQL convention and is portable; the companion sqlserver.html guide uses PascalCase because that matches T-SQL house style. A schema that is multi-engine should pick one and enforce it with a linter, because identifier case is folded differently by each engine: PostgreSQL folds unquoted identifiers to lower case, MySQL follows lower_case_table_names, and SQL Server collation determines case sensitivity of comparison even when the name is stored as written.

MarkerMeaning
Applies to.Engines, versions and settings under which the statement is valid
Requirement.A privilege, setting, extension or edition without which the statement fails
Caveats.Known failure modes, silent wrongness, and the conditions under which the approach is unsafe
Decision.A choice that the chapter makes explicitly, with the rejected alternative named
Cross-referenceThe concept has its own section and is not restated
-- Row-size accounting. The single most useful storage measurement, and the one
-- most often replaced by a guess. Measures the live tuple, not the row format.
SELECT c.relname                                   AS table_name,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_table_size(c.oid))               AS table_total,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(c.oid))           AS heap_only,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_indexes_size(c.oid))            AS all_indexes,
       ROUND(pg_relation_size(c.oid) / GREATEST(c.reltuples,1)) AS bytes_per_row,
       c.reltuples::bigint                                AS est_rows
  FROM pg_class c
  JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace
 WHERE n.nspname = 'public' AND c.relkind = 'r'
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(c.oid) DESC LIMIT 10;

-- Per-column storage in the live tuple, so a wide column is attributable.
SELECT a.attname,
       pg_size_pretty(sum(pg_column_size(c.*)) FILTER (WHERE a.attnum = 0)) AS ignored,
       ROUND(AVG(pg_column_size(
                 (SELECT NULL::record) )), 1)              AS ignored2
  FROM pg_attribute a, pg_class c
 WHERE c.oid = 'sales_order'::regclass AND a.attrelid = c.oid
 GROUP BY a.attname ORDER BY 1;

Applies to. The first query is PostgreSQL-specific. The MySQL equivalent is information_schema.TABLES joined to information_schema.INNODB_TABLES, and the SQL Server equivalent is sys.dm_db_partition_stats summed by index identifier with sp_spaceused as the coarse fallback. The second listing above is illustrative rather than operational: per-column size needs either the pg_column_size trick with a generated row expression or an extension such as pg_column_size in a lateral pass. Prefer the first query plus the schema itself; per-column measurement is a diagnostic, not a routine.

Do the whole guide against a live instance, and keep one. The chapters are written so that each section ends with something executable, and most of the design claims in chapters 4 to 7 are falsifiable in under a minute on a table you create yourself with ten thousand rows. The failure modes described are reproducible; reproduce them.

Exercises 0

  1. Run the three version probes in section 0.2 against every engine you have access to and record the results in a table. Note which of the capability claims in the matrix above you can confirm empirically and which you are taking on trust.
  2. Create the reference model from sections 0.3 on one engine of your choice. Then write, without running it, the DDL that would create the same model on each of the other two, and list every point at which the translation is not mechanical.
  3. Load 106 rows into sales_order with a realistic distribution: 3 % of tenants hold 40 % of the rows, and 90 % of orders are in the last 90 days. Record the measured bytes per row, then explain the difference between it and the sum of the declared column widths.
  4. Produce an access-pattern inventory for your own system or a system you know: at least twelve numbered statements, each with a frequency, a p99 budget and a growth curve. Mark every one you cannot currently serve with an index and explain why.
  5. Draw the stage 1 to stage 7 pipeline for a schema change you have actually shipped, marking the stage at which it was hardest to reverse. Identify which artefact was missing that made it hard.

1 · Requirements, Workload and Access Patterns

1.1 From business problem to data requirement1.2 Access patterns as executable statements1.3 Cardinality, fan-out and the multiplication factor1.4 The three questions that decide the model1.5 SLAs, the durability budget and the data tier1.6 OLTP, OLAP, and the cost model of a relationship

Modelling decisions are downstream of workload facts. This chapter establishes how a business problem becomes a set of data requirements, how those requirements become a numbered inventory of access patterns, and how the inventory, the write volume and the retention policy together determine the model rather than the other way round.

1.1 From business problem to data requirement

A data requirement is a statement about what must be knowable later, not about what must be displayed now. The distinction is what separates a model that survives three years of feature work from one that is rewritten annually, because display requirements are cheap to satisfy by joining while knowledge requirements are not.

The conversion from problem to requirement has four steps, and each step has a characteristic failure. Naming the fact means writing down what a row means in a sentence that contains no adjectives of intent: "a line on a sales order", not "a purchase item". Fixing the grain means stating what one row represents, and that sentence is the schema’s most important comment. Fixing the key means deciding what uniquely identifies that grain over time. Fixing the history rule means deciding whether a change overwrites the row, appends a new row, or writes a new row in a history table while the current table is a projection.

Problem statementNaive requirementData requirementWhy the naive form fails
Users must see their invoice history with the price they paidJoin order_line to product to get the current priceorder_line.unit_price_excl_tax is a fact about the line, immutable once written; product.price is a fact about the product, mutableA price change silently rewrites history. The invoice total stops reconciling against the lines, and no constraint catches it
Finance needs to know which plan a tenant was on when an order was placedJoin order to subscription for the planCopy plan_code onto the order at write time, or maintain a bi-temporal subscription historyThe join answers a different question: what plan is the tenant on now. Three-way join per row, and wrong for every historical order
Support must list a customer’s recent loginsQuery the app_user table for a last_login columnA login_event table, append-only, one row per attemptA single column cannot answer "all logins in the last 30 days" or "was this user’s login anomalous"; it also forces an UPDATE per login on a hot row
Compliance needs to reconstruct who changed a priceAdd updated_by to the product tableAn audit_event table carrying before_state and after_stateOne column records the last change only, and no before-image, so the previous value is unrecoverable

The right-hand column of that table is the same rule in every row: a value that is stored once and read through a relationship is a fact about the relationship’s history, and history must be materialised at the point where the fact was created. This is the single largest source of retroactive data-model corrections, and it is always discovered by a finance reconciliation rather than by a design review.

-- The write side of the requirement: who touches the row, and in which state.
-- This is the API contract expressed as data operations. It is the artefact
-- the model is checked against, and it is written before the tables exist.

-- AP-W1 (write, ~40/s): confirm a payment attempt. Idempotent by key.
INSERT INTO payment_attempt (tenant_id, order_id, idempotency_key, provider, amount, status)
VALUES (:tenant, :order, :idem_key, 'stripe', :amount, 'pending')
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, idempotency_key) DO NOTHING;

-- AP-W2 (write, ~1/s): place an order and its lines atomically.
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO sales_order (tenant_id, subscription_id, status, currency, total_excl_tax)
VALUES (:tenant, :sub, 'placed', :ccy, :total) RETURNING order_id;
INSERT INTO sales_order_line (order_id, line_no, sku, description, quantity,
                                          unit_price_excl_tax, tax_rate, line_total_excl_tax)
VALUES (:order, 1, :sku, :desc, :qty, :unit_price, :tax_rate, :line_total), ...;
INSERT INTO outbox (tenant_id, topic, aggregate_id, payload)
VALUES (:tenant, 'order.placed', :order, :payload);
COMMIT;

Requirement. A write pattern that spans two tables plus an outbox table is a single transaction and must be written as one. Any design in which a business operation is satisfied by two separate commits — order first, then outbox — has a window in which the order exists and the event does not, and that window is the reason section 7.5 exists. The requirement is stated at the modelling stage precisely so that the transaction boundary is a deliberate decision rather than a consequence of how three service methods happen to be ordered.

Caveats. The four-step conversion has a failure mode specific to regulated domains: a requirement that is only as good as its retention rule. If no answer is recorded for "how long must this fact be kept, and what happens when it is deleted", the retention question reappears at the point where the largest table is the hardest to delete from — which is the point of section 4.3 and the partitioning decision. Record the retention rule in the same sentence as the grain.

1.2 Access patterns as executable statements

An access pattern is a statement, not a use case. The inventory is a numbered list of statements in SQL or a close dialect, each annotated with frequency, latency budget, row-count expectation and growth. Written this way, it is directly testable against a schema, and it is the only artefact that can prove an index is justified or a partition key is sufficient.

The discipline that makes the inventory worth writing is that each statement is the whole query, not a fragment. "Look up orders by tenant" is a fragment. "The 50 most recent orders for a tenant, returned in reverse chronological order, under 40 ms at p99" is a pattern, and it constrains the schema in three ways at once: it needs a leading tenant_id in an index, it needs placed_at as the second column in the same index so the sort is free, and it needs a limitable access path or it becomes a full scan plus a top-N sort.

IDStatementFreqp99 budgetRows touched
AP-01Latest 50 orders for one tenant~2 000/s across all tenants40 ms50 of ~106 per tenant
AP-02One order with all its lines~1 500/s20 ms1 + 8
AP-03Orders for one tenant in a date range, paginated~120/s200 ms20 000
AP-04Order count and revenue per tenant per month~30/min2 s106 per tenant scanned
AP-05Subscriptions whose period ends in the next 7 days~5/min100 ms~1 000
AP-06Payment attempts for one order, most recent first~200/s30 ms1 + 3
AP-07Audit events for one entity, newest first~50/s150 ms200
AP-08Outbox rows not yet published, oldest firstcontinuous relay—batch of 100
-- AP-01: latest 50 orders for a tenant. Requires an index whose leading column
-- is the tenant and whose second column is the time, in descending order.
SELECT order_id, placed_at, status, total_excl_tax, currency
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = :tenant_id
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC
 LIMIT 50;

-- AP-04 is the pattern that decides the physical design. Read it twice.
-- It touches every row of one tenant, every month, forever.
SELECT date_trunc('month', placed_at) AS month,
       COUNT(*)            AS orders,
       SUM(total_excl_tax) AS revenue
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = :tenant_id
   AND placed_at >= :from AND placed_at < :to
 GROUP BY 1
 ORDER BY 1;

The one-line difference between AP-01 and AP-04 is the difference between a seek that reads 50 index entries and a scan that reads 106 rows. They share a table, a filter column and a time range, and no index design serves both optimally: an index that makes AP-01 a limitable seek serves AP-04 as well as any index can, and the only way to make AP-04 fast is to not perform it against the base table. That conclusion — roll the aggregate up — is a design output, and it is reached here at the access-pattern stage, which is exactly where it belongs.

Pattern classShapeAccess path it demandsExample
Point lookupEquality on the full unique keyUnique B-tree seek, one to three pagesAP-02, AP-06
Limited listEquality plus ordering plus small limitComposite index in filter-then-sort order, so the sort is elidedAP-01, AP-07
RangeBounded interval, no ordering requirementB-tree range scan within a narrow key prefixAP-03, AP-05
AggregateGroup by over a large fraction of a partitionNothing in the base table — a pre-aggregated rollup, or a columnar copyAP-04
QueueUnpublished rows, claim one batch, oldest firstPartial index over the unpublished subset plus a locking read that skips claimed rowsAP-08
AuditAppend-only history, always by timeTime-ordered physical layout; a BRIN on the time column at large row countsAP-07, audit retention

Write the AP inventory so that each statement is one line of SQL and one line of metadata. When a statement runs to five lines, it is almost always two patterns that have been merged, and merging them hides the fact that they want different indexes. A useful test: if no single index could serve two patterns, they are two patterns, and writing them as one entry will produce a compromise index that serves neither.

-- The queue pattern. Three requirements, and they interact:
--   1. only unpublished rows are in the index at all   -> partial index
--   2. oldest first, and the index is ordered by time  -> created_at leading
--   3. concurrent relays must not claim the same row  -> SKIP LOCKED
CREATE INDEX ix_outbox_unpublished ON outbox (created_at, outbox_id)
    WHERE published_at IS NULL;   -- MySQL has no equivalent; SQL Server uses a filtered index

-- PostgreSQL and MySQL 8.0+ / SQL Server 2022+ support SKIP LOCKED directly.
WITH claimed AS (
    SELECT outbox_id FROM outbox
     WHERE published_at IS NULL
     ORDER BY created_at
     LIMIT 100
       FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED)
UPDATE outbox o
   SET published_at = now()
  FROM claimed c
 WHERE o.outbox_id = c.outbox_id
 RETURNING o.outbox_id, o.topic, o.payload;

-- SQL Server equivalent: the hints are load-bearing, and there is no LIMIT.
-- UPDLOCK + READPAST + a TOP with an ordered subquery is the documented pattern.
-- WITH (UPDLOCK, READPAST) SKIP LOCKED
-- UPDATE o SET published_at = SYSUTCDATETIME()
--  OUTPUT inserted.* FROM outbox AS o WITH (UPDLOCK, READPAST)
-- WHERE published_at IS NULL AND outbox_id IN
--   (SELECT TOP (100) outbox_id FROM outbox WITH (UPDLOCK, READPAST)
--    WHERE published_at IS NULL ORDER BY created_at);

Applies to. FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED is PostgreSQL 9.5+ and MySQL 8.0+. SQL Server has no SKIP LOCKED keyword; the READPAST hint on a table with UPDLOCK is the documented equivalent, and it silently degrades to blocking if UPDLOCK is omitted, which is the most common way a relay ends up serialised behind itself.

1.3 Cardinality, fan-out and the multiplication factor

Cardinality is the number of rows on each side of a relationship, and it is the only input to query cost that can be computed before the data exists. Estimating it correctly at design time prevents the class of defect where a three-table join that is instant at 103 rows becomes a 40-second scan at 108 rows, with no change in the SQL.

The arithmetic is worth doing explicitly because it is almost never done. The reference model has four relationships in the order path, and each is multiplicative. A realistic tenant is a mid-sized customer: 40 users, one subscription, 5 000 orders, 8 lines per order. A query that joins tenant to subscription to order to line therefore produces 40 000 rows for a single order, of which the useful ones are 8.

JoinRows produced for one tenantWhat the caller wantedMultiplication
tenant11 tenant—
+ app_user4040 users×40
+ sales_order200 0005 000 orders×5 000
+ sales_order_line1 600 00040 000 lines×8
+ payment_attempt2 100 00012 000 payments×1.3
-- Measure the multiplication instead of asserting it. COUNT(*) versus
-- COUNT(DISTINCT ...) on the far key exposes every accidental fan-out.
SELECT COUNT(*)                              AS rows_produced,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.order_id)            AS distinct_orders,
       COUNT(DISTINCT l.order_id || '/' || l.line_no) AS distinct_lines,
       ROUND(COUNT(*)::numeric
             / NULLIF(COUNT(DISTINCT o.order_id), 0))  AS fanout
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = :tenant_id
   AND o.placed_at >= :from;

-- Lines per order, as a distribution rather than a mean. A mean of 8 hides
-- the bulk orders at 400 lines that will dominate any join cost estimate.
SELECT width_bucket(n, 1, 401, 8) AS bucket,
       COUNT(*) AS orders
  FROM (SELECT order_id, COUNT(*) AS n
          FROM sales_order_line GROUP BY order_id) s
 GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;

The concatenation in the first query is PostgreSQL syntax; the MySQL equivalent is CONCAT(l.order_id, '/', l.line_no) and the SQL Server equivalent is CAST(l.order_id AS varchar(20)) + '/' + CAST(l.line_no AS varchar(10)). The reason for counting distinct composite keys rather than distinct order_id is that a query joining a child at two levels looks correct when only the near key is checked, and the second child silently multiplies the first.

Observed fan-outReadingAction
1.0One-to-one, and the unique constraint proves itConfirm the unique constraint exists; a 1.0 fan-out without one is a coincidence of the sample
1.0 to 1.2Optional child, present on a minority of parentsConfirm the index on the child’s foreign key; the join is cheap but the foreign-row lookup must be a seek
1.2 to 5Ordinary one-to-manyNo structural change. Budget for the multiplication in any aggregate over the parent
5 to 20One-to-many with a long tailConsider aggregating the child rather than joining it, and check whether the parent query needs the child at all
> 20Usually a modelling error: a nested collection accessed as a joinRe-examine whether this is a relationship or a duplicated attribute. Chapter 3.5 is the remedy
Growing over timeAn unbounded child collection — audit, events, messagesThe child table will eventually be larger than the parent. Partition it, and give it a different physical design

Caveats. Mean fan-out is a misleading statistic, and a schema that is tuned to the mean is tuned for the wrong orders. Worse, the fan-out is not stationary: the busiest order day of the year has a different distribution from a normal Tuesday, and a table that is comfortable at the median is the table that fails in December. Record the distribution, and record the worst observed value rather than the average.

-- Cardinality, computed from the data rather than asserted in a wiki page.
-- This is the query to run monthly against the reference model.
SELECT 'tenant'                                        AS parent,
       ROUND(AVG(n)::numeric, 2)                            AS avg_children,
       PERCENTILE_DISC(0.99) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY n)  AS p99_children,
       MAX(n)                                              AS max_children
  FROM (SELECT tenant_id, COUNT(*) AS n FROM sales_order GROUP BY tenant_id) s
UNION ALL
SELECT 'sales_order', ROUND(AVG(n)::numeric, 2),
       PERCENTILE_DISC(0.99) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY n), MAX(n)
  FROM (SELECT order_id, COUNT(*) AS n FROM sales_order_line GROUP BY order_id) s;

-- Tenant skew: the number that decides whether "partition by tenant" works.
SELECT tenant_id,
       COUNT(*)                       AS orders,
       ROUND(COUNT(*) * 100.0 / SUM(COUNT(*)) OVER (), 2) AS pct_of_table
  FROM sales_order
 GROUP BY tenant_id
 ORDER BY orders DESC LIMIT 10;

1.4 The three questions that decide the model

Given an access-pattern inventory, the model follows from three questions asked in order. They are asked before any table is named, because the answers determine whether the domain is relational, hierarchical, event-shaped or immutable — and getting this wrong produces a schema that is internally consistent and unfixable.

#QuestionWhat each answer implies
1What is the shape of the read? Is a single row returned by a key, a limited list, a range, or an aggregate over a large fraction of the data?Key lookup → B-tree, nothing exotic. Limited list → composite index in filter-then-sort order, limitable seek. Aggregate → the answer is not an index, it is a rollup or a second storage format. Range over time → a time-ordered physical layout
2What is the write volume and its shape? Rows per second, bytes per second, and how they are distributed over time and over keys.Low and bursty → a wide row with expensive constraints is affordable. High and uniform → every column, every index and every constraint is a per-row cost, and the row must be narrow. Highly skewed keys → the hottest key is the one that sets the design
3What is the retention policy? Is data kept forever, deleted on a schedule, archived, or legally purged?Forever → the table is the archival tier; index every field a future query might use, because there is no second chance. Scheduled deletion → the deletion axis becomes the partitioning key, or DELETE becomes a partition drop. Purge → the purge must be provable, which is an audit requirement before it is a storage requirement
-- Question 1, decided: AP-04 aggregates 10^6 rows per tenant per month.
-- The remedy is not an index. It is a second table with a different grain.
CREATE TABLE monthly_order_rollup (
    tenant_id     bigint NOT NULL,
    month         date NOT NULL,           -- first day of month, UTC
    order_count   int NOT NULL,
    revenue_excl  numeric(16,2) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, month)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (month);

-- Question 2, decided: 1 000 inserts/s means every index is a tax.
-- The ledger in section 5.5 prices the indexes in the reference model.
--
-- Question 3, decided: 7 years of orders kept, 2 years online, then archive.
-- The deletion axis is month, so month is the outer partitioning key.
-- The lookup axis is tenant, so tenant is a secondary consideration
-- that a hash subpartition or a composite index must serve.

Caveats. The three questions are asked in order because question 3 can invalidate the answer to question 1. A table that is retained forever and queried by any dimension of its history needs every plausible access path indexed, which is a different table from one whose history is a fixed window. Conversely, a table with a 30-day retention and a heavy aggregate can drop the aggregate materialisation entirely and recompute from nothing. Retention is not a storage policy; it is an input to the model.

-- The rollup, maintained correctly. Three properties matter and each is a design choice:
--   idempotent      -> re-running the same month produces the same rows
--   rebuildable     -> a single month can be recomputed from the base table
--   inside a txn    -> the rollup and the base change never disagree

INSERT INTO monthly_order_rollup (tenant_id, month, order_count, revenue_excl)
SELECT tenant_id,
       date_trunc('month', placed_at)::date,
       COUNT(*),
       SUM(total_excl_tax)
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE placed_at >= :month_start
   AND placed_at <  :month_start + interval '1 month'
 GROUP BY 1, 2
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, month) DO UPDATE
   SET order_count  = EXCLUDED.order_count,
       revenue_excl = EXCLUDED.revenue_excl;

-- Correction path for a late-arriving or amended order. The rollup is a
-- cache of the base table, so it must always be repairable from it.
INSERT INTO monthly_order_rollup (tenant_id, month, order_count, revenue_excl)
SELECT tenant_id, date_trunc('month', placed_at)::date, 0, SUM(total_excl_tax)
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE order_id = :amended_order
 ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, month) DO UPDATE
   SET revenue_excl = monthly_order_rollup.revenue_excl
                             + EXCLUDED.revenue_excl;

Decision. The rollup table is a materialised aggregate, not a denormalised column, and the difference is the correction path. A denormalised column is authoritative and must be updated by every writer; a rollup is derived, may be rebuilt at any time, and the only invariant that must hold is that any divergence is detectable and repairable. Choosing the second is strictly easier to operate, at the cost of one extra read of the base table when the rollup is rebuilt.

Record the answers to the three questions as three lines above the ERD in the repository. When a fourth question arrives — a new dashboard, a new regulator, a new customer segment — the change is three lines and a diff rather than a redesign, and the review starts from an explicit disagreement rather than from a blank page.

1.5 SLAs, the durability budget and the data tier

A data model is chosen under a durability and latency budget, and both budgets are made of milliseconds that are spent in specific places. This section attributes the latency budget, states the RPO and RTO that the tiering decision implies, and gives the configuration that turns a stated requirement into an enforced one.

The order of magnitude that matters: a durable commit on spinning or network-attached storage costs one to five milliseconds of synchronous flush, a network round trip between hosts costs 0.2 to 2 milliseconds, and an asynchronous replica commits the primary immediately and lags by whatever the network and the redo volume allow. The entire engineering content of replication is deciding which of those three costs to pay, per write, and the answer is different for a payment and for a view count.

TierConsistencyLatency addedCost per 1 000 writes/sUse for
Primary onlyDurable, no replication0BaselineDevelopment; single-node production with a nightly dump
Local synchronous replicaDurable on two nodes; survives host loss1 flush + 0.5–2 ms network per commit~2× host cost, halved by commit batchingOrder of magnitude where a lost second is a lost customer
Async replica in regionPrimary durable; replica may lag seconds0+1 instance, network egressReporting reads, backup, regional failover accepting RPO > 0
Async replica across regionsPrimary durable; RPO is the replication lag0 on the write pathCross-region transfer per unit of redoDisaster recovery, with a written-down RPO
Read/write splitReads may serve stale data; writes never doReads are subject to lagRead capacity scales separatelyOnly with an explicit read-your-writes policy — section 7.6
-- PostgreSQL 18: the durability settings, and what each one costs.
-- synchronous_commit = on        : WAL flushed to disk before COMMIT returns. ~1-5 ms.
-- synchronous_commit = off       : COMMIT returns immediately. A crash loses recent commits.
-- synchronous_commit = remote_apply: waits for the standby to apply. RPO 0, highest latency.
-- synchronous_standby_names    : which standbys must acknowledge, and in what quorum.
SHOW synchronous_commit;
SHOW synchronous_standby_names;
SELECT client_addr, application_name, sync_state, write_lag, flush_lag, replay_lag
  FROM pg_stat_replication;

-- MySQL 9.x: the equivalent pair. Both must be 1 for a durable commit.
-- 2 is durable to the OS but not to the disk: it survives a process crash only.
SELECT @@innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit, @@sync_binlog;
SELECT channel_name, service_state, last_sqldelivered, last_sqlapplied
  FROM performance_schema.replication_connection_status;

-- SQL Server 2025: durability is the recovery model, and the synchronised
-- commit is the Always On synchronised-commit flag, not a session setting.
SELECT name, recovery_model_desc, is_read_only, log_reuse_wait_desc
  FROM sys.databases WHERE name = DB_NAME();
SELECT is_synchronized, is_healthy, synchronization_health_desc
  FROM sys.dm_hadr_database_replica_states;

Caveats. The most damaging misconfiguration in this section is a replica that is not actually synchronous when the runbook says it is. A synchronous_standby_names that names a host that is down makes the primary block every commit until the host returns, which is a availability-for-durability trade that must be made deliberately. In MySQL, setting innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit = 2 is often chosen for benchmark throughput and quietly left in production; it is one line of configuration and it is the difference between recovering a database and recovering to an earlier point. Audit it, do not assume it.

-- RPO and RTO are measurable, and the measurement is a table.
-- An RPO that is not measured is a hope.
CREATE TABLE recovery_point_measurement (
    measured_at        timestamptz NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT now(),
    backup_id          text NOT NULL,
    wal_lsn_at_backup  pg_lsn NOT NULL,
    bytes_since_backup bigint NOT NULL,
    wall_clock_gap     interval NOT NULL,   -- measured RPO in the worst case
    restore_tested      boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    notes              text
);

-- Measure the gap the way a restore would experience it: from the last
-- full backup to the last byte of WAL that exists anywhere.
INSERT INTO recovery_point_measurement
     (backup_id, wal_lsn_at_backup, bytes_since_backup, wall_clock_gap)
SELECT :backup_id,
       COALESCE((SELECT pg_walfile_name_offset(GREATEST(pg_wal_lsn_diff(0, checkpoint_lsn), 0)) FROM pg_control_checkpoint()), '0/0'),
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), checkpoint_lsn),
       now() - stats_reset
  FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();

Requirement. An RTO figure is a claim about a restore, not about a backup. A backup that has never been restored is an untested hypothesis: the restore_tested column above exists because the most common disaster-recovery failure is not data loss but a restore that does not complete, or does not complete in time, or completes into a schema that no longer matches the application. Restores are rehearsed on a schedule and the rehearsal is recorded in the same table.

Write the durability decision as a sentence with a number in it: "order and payment writes are synchronously replicated to the standby in the same availability zone, giving RPO 0 at a measured p99 commit latency of 3.1 ms; audit events are asynchronously replicated with a measured maximum lag of 900 ms, giving an RPO of one second." That sentence is reviewable, and it is the form in which a customer’s question can be answered without a meeting.

1.6 OLTP, OLAP, and the cost model of a relationship

A row store is optimised for finding a few rows and for writing one row at a time. An aggregate over a large fraction of a table inverts both assumptions. This section states why one database often serves both badly, gives the threshold at which it does not, and then returns to the conceptual cost that underlies every modelling decision: a relationship is a decision to join, or a decision to duplicate.

The technical reason the two workloads conflict is physical. In a row store, reading 106 rows means reading 106 tuples, each carrying a header, a null bitmap, and every column whether or not the query needs it. Reading one column of 106 rows therefore reads the same bytes as reading all of them, and the row store cannot avoid that. A column store reads 106 values of one column, compresses them to a few bits each, and vectorises the arithmetic. The gap is one to two orders of magnitude, not a factor of two, and no amount of indexing closes it.

Symptom of a mixed workloadMechanismRemedy
Reporting queries make transactional latency worseLong scans pin the buffer pool and evict hot pages; long transactions prevent version pruning, so dead tuples accumulateSeparate instance, or separate table with a narrow covering index, or a rollup
One query’s plan fits the transaction plan cache and vice versaDifferent row-count distributions, different selectivity, one shared set of parameterised plansSeparate cache, or RECOMPILE / plan guides for the outlier
Statistics go stale on the hot tableAnalytic predicates are outside the data distribution, so histograms describe a slice no query usesSeparate statistics maintenance, or a separate copy of the data
Index bloat after a reporting windowLong-running scans update the visibility map, so subsequent index-only scans degrade, and the transaction log grows without boundStop scanning the OLTP table, or accept the vacuum cost explicitly
Backup size is set by the largest table, restore time with itThe growth table dominates every backup and every restoreRetention and partitioning, not engine tuning
-- The decision, stated as thresholds so it can be revisited rather than argued.
--
-- ONE database is correct when all four hold:
--   (a) total fact rows < ~10^8 in the tables being aggregated
--   (b) the aggregate covers < ~1% of those rows on each run
--   (c) the aggregate runs < once per minute per table
--   (d) the aggregate is served by a covering index or a rollup, not a scan
--
-- SEPARATE store is correct as soon as ONE fails, and the cheapest
-- first step is a rollup table rather than a second engine.

-- The decisive measurement: bytes read per aggregate, and how it grows.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT date_trunc('day', placed_at) d, SUM(total_excl_tax)
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE placed_at >= '2026-08-01' AND placed_at < '2026-09-01'
 GROUP BY 1;
-- Read the Buffers line: if it is in the hundreds of MB, the cost is
-- I/O and cache pressure, and it grows linearly with the retention window.

Decision. In the reference model, AP-04 does not justify a second engine. A monthly rollup table of 2 000 tenants × 36 months is   000 rows — smaller than a single index on the orders table — and it turns a 106-row scan into a seek. Introducing a separate analytical store for a problem of that size adds operational surface, an ingestion path and a consistency mechanism, and buys nothing. The threshold at which a second store becomes correct is a per-aggregate cost above roughly 109 bytes read, or a query set that must scan a column the transaction schema does not hold.

Caveats. A columnstore copy of the same table is not automatically cheaper, and a common failed design is a nightly copy of the orders table into a columnar secondary that then serves a dashboard nobody uses. The maintenance of the copy — catching up, deduplicating, handling amendments — is a permanent cost, and amendments are the part most such designs get wrong. A rollup whose grain matches the question is nearly always the better answer than a full copy at a different row format.

-- The cost model, stated once. Every relationship is one of two designs:
--
--   JOIN:      the fact is stored once. Every read that needs both sides
--              pays a join: a foreign-row lookup per parent row, an index
--              entry per child, a wider index entry, and a fan-out over
--              the child's cardinality.
--
--   DUPLICATE: the fact is stored on both sides. Reads are free and
--              independent, and the fact now has an invalidation ledger:
--              every writer that can change either copy must change both.
--
-- Worked: "the order must remember the plan price it was billed at."

-- JOIN design. One copy. The order reads a price that is true NOW,
-- not the price it was billed, so this is not the same fact at all.
SELECT o.order_id, p.monthly_price
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN subscription s ON s.tenant_id = o.tenant_id
  JOIN plan p        ON p.plan_code = s.plan_code
 WHERE o.order_id = :order_id;
-- Cost: two extra seeks per order row. Benefit: one copy, no invalidation.
-- Failure: after a plan price change, last month's invoices re-price.

-- DUPLICATE design. Two copies. The invoice is now correct forever.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN billed_plan_code text,
                       ADD COLUMN billed_monthly_price numeric(12,2);
-- Cost: 24 bytes per row forever, plus the write path that must populate them.
-- Benefit: the invoice is a fact about the order, not about the plan.

-- The ledger, in full. Any writer not on this list is a defect.
--   1. the order-placement application path
--   2. the plan-price change path (updates plan, must NOT touch past orders)
--   3. the backfill migration
--   4. the finance amendment script
--   5. the data import
--   6. any future bulk correction

Write the invalidation ledger as a table with a column per writer and a column per duplicated fact, and make the table part of the schema review. The number of columns is the cost of the denormalisation, stated in advance, in units that a reviewer can count. A denormalisation with one writer and one fact is cheap; one with six writers and two facts is a distributed system wearing a database schema, and it should be argued as such.

Exercises 1

  1. Write the four-step data requirement for five nouns in a domain you know. For each, state the grain, the key and the history rule in a single sentence each. Identify any noun where the naive form and the data requirement differ, and explain the failure that difference prevents.
  2. Produce an access-pattern inventory of at least twelve statements for a real system. For each, record frequency, p99 budget, expected row count and the current access path. Mark every pattern that is currently a full scan, and rank them by business impact.
  3. Compute the fan-out for every join in the reference model, and then run the COUNT versus COUNT(DISTINCT ...) query against real data. Reconcile the measured figures with the estimates and explain the difference.
  4. Answer the three questions of section 1.4 for one aggregate query in your own system. State the grain of the rollup you would build, and prove that a single month can be rebuilt from the base table without reading any other month.
  5. Measure the current durability configuration of a system you have access to. Record synchronous_commit or its equivalents, the measured replication lag, and the RPO those settings actually provide. Compare with whatever the runbook claims.
  6. Take one denormalisation in a system you know and draw its invalidation ledger. Count the writers, count the duplicated facts, and identify any writer that is not on the list. Explain the time, in units of money or of engineer-hours, of the next divergence.

2 · Entity-Relationship Modelling

2.1 Entity, attribute, relationship: the three primitives2.2 Identifying entities: a thing, a value or an event2.3 Primary key strategy: surrogate, natural and the UUID debate2.4 Weak entities and identifying relationships2.5 Inheritance: TPH, TPT and the single-table variant2.6 Associative entities and bi-temporal modelling

Modelling is the act of deciding what a row means. This chapter sets out the primitives, the test for what counts as an entity, the key strategies available and their costs, the three ways to model inheritance, and the two independent time axes that a system with an audit obligation must model explicitly.

2.1 Entity, attribute, relationship: the three primitives

A relational model is built from three primitives. Entities are the things rows are about, attributes are the facts about them, and relationships are the named facts connecting two of them. Most modelling errors are a confusion between one primitive and another, and each confusion has a characteristic symptom in the resulting schema.

ConfusionSymptom in the schemaCorrect treatment
An attribute used as an entitycustomer_name, customer_email and customer_city all on the order, so a rename is a mass updateExtract an app_user entity and reference it. The order’s copy of a name is then a snapshot fact and is named as one
An entity used as an attributelines varchar(4000) holding a delimited list, or tags text holding a setMake it a child table. The list form cannot be constrained, indexed, joined or deleted selectively
A relationship used as an entityTwo foreign keys on one table with no name, or a manager_id that means "manages" rather than "is managed by"Name the relationship and its direction. If it carries attributes of its own it is an entity
Cardinality asserted, not enforcedA document says "a tenant has exactly one subscription" and the schema has a plain foreign keyAdd the unique constraint. A cardinality is only real if the database refuses the violation
An attribute that is really an enumstatus varchar(20) with values validated only in the applicationLookup table plus foreign key, or a native enum, or a check constraint. All three are defensible; the free-text column is not
  REFERENCE MODEL -- logical ERD
  notation: 1 --- N  one to many      ||  exactly one      o{  zero or more

  plan       ||---o{ subscription          subscription ||---o{ sales_order
  tenant     ||---o{ subscription          tenant       ||---o{ app_user
  tenant     ||---o{ sales_order           tenant       ||---o{ audit_event
  tenant     ||---o{ payment_attempt       tenant       ||---o{ outbox
  sales_order ||---o{ sales_order_line     sales_order ||---o{ payment_attempt
  app_user   ||---o{ audit_event           (actor_id: nullable, the actor may be a service)

  Cardinalities the DDL must actually enforce, not merely document:
    tenant     1 --- 0..1  subscription    UNIQUE (subscription.tenant_id)
    plan       1 --- 0..N  subscription    FK  (subscription.plan_code)
    tenant     1 --- 0..N  app_user        FK  (app_user.tenant_id)
    sales_order 1 --- 1..N sales_order_line PK  (order_id, line_no)  -- see section 2.4
    tenant     1 --- 0..N  audit_event     FK  (audit_event.tenant_id)
    outbox     (no foreign keys at all: the row must outlive the aggregate it describes)

Two relationships in that diagram are deliberately different in kind. sales_order → app_user is absent: an order belongs to a tenant, not to a person, and an order placed by one user on behalf of another would be unrepresentable if the foreign key pointed at the user. audit_event.tenant_id is a foreign key that is not paired with cascading delete, because an audit row that disappears when its subject is deleted is not an audit row.

-- Making the cardinality real. Three constraints, three different meanings,
-- and each of them is a different product decision.

ALTER TABLE subscription ADD CONSTRAINT uq_sub_tenant
    UNIQUE (tenant_id);            -- cardinality 1 --- 0..1
-- A tenant can have no subscription (before signup) or exactly one.
-- Two concurrent signups collide here, which is the desired outcome:
-- the loser gets a unique violation, not a duplicate subscription.

-- A 1..N cardinality is expressed by the child key, and its minimum is
-- NOT a constraint at all: "at least one line" cannot be enforced by a row
-- in the child table, only by a deferred trigger or by application logic.
-- Write that limitation into the model rather than implying it is enforced.

-- A 0..1 on the parent side of a 1:1 is enforced by putting the unique
-- constraint on the child, never by the foreign key alone.
ALTER TABLE payment_attempt ADD CONSTRAINT uq_payment_settled_ref
    UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT (provider, provider_ref);
-- PG 15+. MySQL: UNIQUE treats NULLs as distinct, so a second NULL is
-- allowed, which is the behaviour you want for a nullable provider_ref.
-- SQL Server: UNIQUE with a filtered index WHERE provider_ref IS NOT NULL.

Caveats. Most of a modelling dispute is settled by naming the cardinality precisely, and most cardinality claims are approximately true. The three that matter are the minimum, the maximum, and whether the relationship is optional on the side that will be deleted. Zero or one and exactly one produce different foreign key nullability; zero or more and one or more produce different transaction requirements, because a child row that must exist cannot be created before its parent in a system that assigns identities from the client.

Put the grain in a COMMENT ON statement, or in the table’s own description field, and make it a single sentence starting with "one row per". A table whose description is missing is a table whose grain will be guessed at differently by two engineers within a month, and both guesses will be wrong in a way the constraints do not catch.

2.2 Identifying entities: a thing, a value or an event

Not every row describes a thing. A row may describe a persistent thing that has identity, a value that is one of a set, or an event that happened once. The three are modelled differently, and confusing an event with a thing is the most consequential modelling error in a system that keeps history.

ThingValueEvent
Exampletenant, app_user, planplan.monthly_price, status = 'active', a currency codeaudit_event, login_event, payment_attempt
IdentityStable for the life of the thingNone — two rows with the same value are the same valueUnique, and generated at the moment it happens
MutationUpdated in place; old values usually not keptReplaced; history lives in a temporal table if it mattersNever updated, and never deleted before the retention policy allows
Reference integrityFull: children may point at it and forbid its deletionFull, via a lookup table if the value is namedNone: an event outlives its subjects, so its foreign keys are deliberately absent or non-cascading
Physical orderArbitrary; the clustered key is a free choicen/aMonotonically increasing: append at the right-hand edge
Index consequenceNeeds indexes for the lookup pathsn/aNeeds a time-ordered index and a partition key aligned to time
-- The same business fact, modelled as a thing (wrong) and as an event (right).

-- WRONG: a last-seen column on the user. One fact, one row, overwritten.
ALTER TABLE app_user ADD COLUMN last_login_at timestamptz;
-- Cannot answer: how many logins, from where, in the last 30 days;
--             was this login followed by a failed one;
--             what was the session token in use at 09:14 on a given day.
-- Also: every login is an UPDATE of the hottest row in the table, which is a
-- lock-contention hotspot on exactly the row the whole system reads.

-- RIGHT: an event table, append-only, one row per login attempt.
CREATE TABLE login_event (
    login_event_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id       bigint NOT NULL,
    user_id         bigint,                  -- null on an unknown user
    occurred_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    source_ip       inet,                     -- 16 bytes, not varchar(45)
    user_agent      text,
    outcome         text NOT NULL,           -- success, bad_password, unknown_user
    session_id      uuid,
    CONSTRAINT ck_login_outcome CHECK (outcome IN ('success','bad_password','unknown_user','mfa_challenge'))
) PARTITION BY RANGE (occurred_at);          -- events partition by time

The instructive part of that comparison is not the modelling, which is easy, but the growth profile. The last_login_at design writes one 16-byte value to a row that is 200 bytes wide and already hot. The login_event design appends a narrow row at the right-hand edge of an ordered structure, which is the cheapest write a B-tree can perform, and it partitions cleanly. The event table is larger, and it is the correct design: it is larger because it answers five questions instead of one.

-- A value that outgrows its column: the enum-as-lookup decision, done properly.
-- Both forms are correct; they differ in what a future change costs.

-- Form A: a lookup table. Adding a value is an INSERT, and the value
-- carries a display name, a sort order and effective dates.
CREATE TABLE order_status (
    status_code  text PRIMARY KEY,
    display_name text NOT NULL,
    is_terminal  boolean NOT NULL,
    sort_order   int NOT NULL
);
