ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN status_code text NOT NULL REFERENCES order_status;

-- Form B: a native enum. Adding a value is ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE,
-- which is a catalogue change, and which cannot be rolled back on
-- PostgreSQL 11 and earlier. On 12+ it can, but the new value still cannot
-- be USED in the same transaction that added it.
CREATE TYPE order_status_t AS ENUM ('placed','paid','shipped','cancelled','refunded');
ALTER TABLE sales_order ALTER COLUMN status TYPE order_status_t;

-- Decision table.
--   need display names, sort order or effective dating -> lookup table
--   need a value removed from the schema         -> lookup table (an enum value
--                                                     cannot be removed at all)
--   a closed set, no labels, never changes       -> CHECK constraint is enough
--   a high-cardinality set                       -> lookup table, always

Caveats. ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE is not a transactional DDL operation on PostgreSQL 11 and earlier, which makes a value added in a failed migration persist, and which makes a forward-only release necessary. It is transactional from PostgreSQL 12, with the residual restriction that the new value cannot be used until the transaction commits. MySQL’s ENUM is a string with a numeric index rather than a type, so ORDER BY on it sorts alphabetically unless the declared order is exploited, and it is silently widened to VARCHAR-sized storage. SQL Server has no enum type at all.

Apply the thing/value/event test to every noun in a domain. If a noun can be pluralised into a countable history — logins, payments, audits, shipments, emails sent — it is an event, and the schema needs a table for it with a time column and a retention rule. If it cannot, it is a thing or a value, and the current table is probably already correct.

2.3 Primary key strategy: surrogate, natural and the UUID debate

The primary key is the one design decision with no universally correct answer, because each candidate trades a different property: stability against width, locality against unpredictability, and human readability against join cost. This section states the trade explicitly and gives the measurement that settles it.

-- Candidate keys for app_user, in descending order of join cost.

-- (a) Surrogate bigint. 8 bytes, monotonic per database, invisible to callers.
CREATE TABLE u_surrogate (
    user_id   bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id bigint NOT NULL,
    email     text NOT NULL,
    UNIQUE (tenant_id, email)
);
-- Cost: every join carries 8 bytes; every nonclustered index entry carries
--       them too; and a customer-visible identifier must be mapped back.

-- (b) Natural composite key. No extra column, human readable, wider join.
CREATE TABLE u_natural (
    tenant_id bigint NOT NULL,
    email     text NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, email)
);
-- Cost: 8 + (length of email) bytes on every index entry and every join.
-- Fatal defect: email is mutable, and mutating a primary key propagates
-- to every child row and every secondary index entry.

-- (c) Surrogate plus a unique business key. The default, and the right one.
-- The business key carries referential meaning; the surrogate carries joins.
-- The business key is never mutated in place: a change of email is a new row.
-- (or, on PostgreSQL 15+, an UPDATE of a unique index column is allowed if
--  the new key does not collide, but the ON CONFLICT target cannot be
--  changed without a table rewrite on MySQL 8.0.)

-- (d) plan_code: a genuinely natural, genuinely stable key. Five characters,
--     immutable, assigned by us, never reused. Natural keys are correct when
--     the value is immutable and short — ISO codes, SKU, currency.
Key typeWidthLocalityStable across restartsVerdict
bigint identity, single writer8Perfect: append at the right edgeYes, but the sequence state is a single point of failure on restoreDefault choice. Add bigserial caching awareness for multi-writer bulk loads
bigint identity, many writers8Good on PostgreSQL and MySQL; cache the sequence for bulk loaders or index pages churnYesCorrect for horizontal write fan-out within one database
UUID version 4 (random)16None. Uniformly distributed over the key space, so every insert lands on a random leaf pageYes, generated client-sideAcceptable for a secondary key or an external reference. Wrong as a clustered key on all three engines
UUID version 7 / ULID16Good: time-ordered prefix, so new values still appendYes, client-generatedThe correct choice when identifiers must be minted by clients and inserted in arbitrary order
Snowflake-style bigint8Good, and half the width of a UUIDYes, but the clock is a dependencyBest when many writers must mint ids independently; requires a clock-skew and node-id discipline
Natural, mutableVariesDependsYesNever as a primary key when the value can be edited by a user
-- Measuring the locality cost, because "random UUIDs fragment the index" is a
-- claim that should be measured on the target engine rather than asserted.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgstattuple;

CREATE TABLE id_test (
    id_seq  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    id_v4   uuid NOT NULL DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    id_v7   uuid NOT NULL DEFAULT uuidv7(),
    payload text NOT NULL DEFAULT repeat('x', 100)
);
CREATE INDEX ix_seq ON id_test (id_seq);
CREATE INDEX ix_v4  ON id_test (id_v4);
CREATE INDEX ix_v7  ON id_test (id_v7);

-- Insert 2,000,000 rows, then compare the two index structures.
INSERT INTO id_test (payload)
SELECT repeat('x', 100) FROM generate_series(1, 2000000);

SELECT indexrelname, leaf_blks, internal_blks, deleted_blks,
       ROUND(deleted_blks::numeric * 100 / NULLIF(leaf_blks, 0), 1) AS deleted_pct
  FROM pgstatindex('ix_v4'::regclass) s, LATERAL (SELECT indexrelname FROM pg_stat_user_indexes WHERE indexrelid = 'ix_v4'::regclass) m;
-- Run the same for ix_v7 and ix_seq. Expect: v4 shows many deleted pages and a
-- lower leaf density; v7 and the sequence are near-identical. Then VACUUM the
-- random index again and watch the space return: that is reclaimable, and it
-- is exactly the write amplification the cost model in section 1.6 describes.

-- The same experiment, expressed as a cost rather than as pages.
-- A sequential key lets a range scan read a contiguous run of pages.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM id_test WHERE id_seq BETWEEN 1000000 AND 1000100;
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM id_test WHERE id_v4 BETWEEN '8a000000-0000-4000-8000-000000000000' AND '8a000000-0000-4000-8000-000000000064';

Applies to. uuidv7() is a built-in function in PostgreSQL 18; on 17 and earlier it is an extension. gen_random_uuid() is core from PostgreSQL 13. MySQL has no time-ordered UUID function in the server; it is generated in the application or in a generated column. SQL Server’s NEWID() is a random v4 and is not order-preserving; NEWSEQUENTIALID() is a sequential value but it is generated per client, so on multiple clients it is not globally ordered, which is the detail that makes it a poor substitute for a v7 key.

Caveats. Sequential integer keys leak volume: the 100001-st order reveals the order count, and for a multi-tenant system it leaks the tenant’s activity relative to its neighbours. Where that matters, the answer is a surrogate with a separate business identifier, not a random primary key — the random key trades a minor information disclosure for a major write-cost and read-locality regression. A random key is also a poor choice as the clustered key on SQL Server specifically, because every nonclustered index lookup must then follow a random row locator into a fragmented B-tree.

-- The decision, written as the schema it produces. Reference model, final form.

CREATE TABLE tenant (
    tenant_id    bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    public_ref   uuid NOT NULL DEFAULT uuidv7() UNIQUE,   -- customer-visible, 128-bit
    slug         text NOT NULL UNIQUE,                                -- URL identifier, immutable
    name         text NOT NULL,
    status       text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'active',
    created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CHECK (slug ~ '^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9-]{1,62}$')              -- PG regex; ~* in MySQL is different
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_tenant_public_ref ON tenant (public_ref);

-- Two identifiers, two jobs, and the reason they are different types:
--   tenant_id  8 bytes, monotonic, the join key and the clustered key
--   public_ref 16 bytes, client-mintable, safe to publish in a URL or a log
-- Nothing in the schema joins on public_ref, so its 8 extra bytes are paid
-- exactly once, on the one index that exists to enforce its uniqueness.

-- SQL Server equivalent, and the option that must be set explicitly:
--   uniqueidentifier  16 bytes, random by default
--   ALTER DATABASE ... SET NEWSEQUENTIALID_INDEX = ON;  -- deprecated, ON by default
-- and the rule for SQL Server: a uniqueidentifier primary key is fine on a
-- heap, and a serious problem on a clustered index. Make the clustered key
-- bigint and keep the GUID unique but nonclustered.

When a surrogate key is added in front of an existing natural key, do not drop the natural key. The UNIQUE constraint on the business key is what makes upserts, deduplication on import and idempotent retries work; without it every writer must re-derive the natural key with a lookup first, and two of them will race.

2.4 Weak entities and identifying relationships

Some entities have no identity of their own; they exist only as a component of a parent, and the pair of the parent’s key and a local ordinal is what identifies them. Modelling them correctly avoids two common artefacts: a fabricated global identifier that leaks information, and a nullable key column that the constraints never police.

The test for a weak entity is direct: can this row be meaningfully referred to without naming its parent? An order line cannot — "line 3" is meaningless in isolation. A product variant usually cannot. A customer’s address usually can, because an address row has a lifecycle independent of the customer even if its content is only meaningful to them. The test is about identity, not about content.

-- Weak entity: the identifying relationship is part of the key.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_line (
    order_id            bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES sales_order(order_id)
                          ON DELETE CASCADE,
    line_no             int NOT NULL CHECK (line_no > 0),
    sku                 text NOT NULL,
    description         text NOT NULL,
    quantity            int NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
    unit_price_excl_tax numeric(14,4) NOT NULL,
    tax_rate            numeric(5,4) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_no)                 -- the identifying relationship
);
--
-- Consequences of getting this right:
--   * the primary key is also the index that serves AP-02 (order + all lines),
--     so no secondary index on order_id is needed at all;
--   * a line can never exist without an order, and an order cannot be deleted
--     while a line references it, without ON DELETE CASCADE saying so;
--   * the key is 12 bytes instead of 8 for a fabricated surrogate plus a FK.
--
-- What it costs: the parent key is repeated in every child index entry and
-- in every secondary index, and renumbering a parent key is expensive. Both are
-- acceptable when the parent key is immutable, which it is here.

-- The same decision, three times over, in the reference model:
--   sales_order_line  weak on (order_id, line_no)
--   subscription_period weak on (subscription_id, period_start)
--   tax_rule          weak on (tax_rule_set_id, jurisdiction, rate)
-- and the cases that are NOT weak, which are the interesting ones:
--   audit_event       survives its subject, so it carries no identifying FK
--   outbox            same, deliberately
--   payment_attempt   weak on order_id is WRONG: an attempt may exist before
--                      an order is confirmed, and retries must accumulate
Weak-entity decisionCorrect formWrong form and its symptom
Fabricated global keyComposite key of parent plus local ordinalline_id bigint identity plus a nullable order_id. Symptom: duplicate lines after a retry, because nothing ties a line to a position
Nullable foreign keyNot nullable, with an explicit referential actionorder_id bigint NULL. Symptom: orphan rows that no query joins, discovered only by a consistency report
Ordinal as identityOrdinal allocated by the writer inside the parent, with a unique constraint catching the collisionline_no computed as MAX(line_no)+1 in the application. Symptom: two concurrent writers pick the same number, and the constraint is the only thing that saves it
Parent as a discriminatorThe parent key is part of the key and of every index that serves the childA parent_type discriminator with a nullable parent_id. Symptom: rows whose parent cannot be determined because the discriminator is free text
-- Allocating a local ordinal under concurrency. Three approaches, and the
-- failure mode of each.

-- (1) Client-allocated. Simple, fast, and the constraint is the arbiter.
INSERT INTO sales_order_line (order_id, line_no, ...) VALUES (:order, :next_no, ...);
-- A collision raises a unique violation. The caller must retry with next_no+1.
-- Correct, because the retry is safe: the whole insert is one statement.

-- (2) Server-allocated from a per-parent counter. Requires a counter column
--     on the parent, which is a write hotspot on the parent row.
UPDATE sales_order
   SET next_line_no = next_line_no + 1
 WHERE order_id = :order
RETURNING next_line_no - 1 AS allocated_line_no;
-- Correct, serialised by the row lock. Rejected here because orders are
-- written in one transaction and this adds a second write to a hot row.

-- (3) MAX() in a subquery. Incorrect under concurrency, and it is the most
--     commonly shipped version of (1).
INSERT INTO sales_order_line
SELECT :order, COALESCE(MAX(line_no), 0) + 1, ...
  FROM sales_order_line WHERE order_id = :order;
-- Two sessions running this concurrently both read MAX = 3, both insert 4, and one
-- gets a unique violation. The table is protected; the caller is not. Use (1).

-- MySQL and SQL Server have no RETURNING, so (2) is done with a locking read:
--   SELECT next_line_no FROM sales_order WHERE order_id = ? FOR UPDATE;
--   UPDATE sales_order SET next_line_no = next_line_no + 1 WHERE order_id = ?;
-- SQL Server: SELECT ... WITH (UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK) then UPDATE, inside one txn.

Give a weak entity’s local ordinal a CHECK that fixes its domain, and give the parent key a NOT NULL. These two constraints cost nothing at write time, and they convert two classes of silent corruption — line 0, line −1, orphan line — into immediate errors that a test can assert.

2.5 Inheritance: TPH, TPT and the single-table variant

A subtype relationship — "a payment account is a card account, a SEPA account or a wallet account" — has three standard physical designs and no universally correct answer. The choice is decided by the ratio of shared to subtype-specific attributes, by whether cross-subtype constraints are needed, and by whether reads are usually polymorphic or usually subtype-specific.

-- Design A: table per hierarchy (TPH). One table, a discriminator, nullable columns.
CREATE TABLE payment_account (
    account_id     bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    kind           text NOT NULL,
    label          text NOT NULL,
    created_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    -- card columns
    card_brand     text,
    pan_last4      char(4),
    exp_month      smallint,
    exp_year       smallint,
    -- sepa columns
    iban_country   char(2),
    -- wallet columns
    wallet_provider text,
    wallet_external_id text,
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_kind CHECK (kind IN ('card','sepa','wallet')),
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_card   CHECK (kind <> 'card'   OR (pan_last4 IS NOT NULL AND exp_month IS NOT NULL)),
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_sepa   CHECK (kind <> 'sepa'   OR iban_country IS NOT NULL),
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_wallet CHECK (kind <> 'wallet' OR (wallet_provider IS NOT NULL AND wallet_external_id IS NOT NULL))
);
-- The row for a card is 240 bytes whether or not the 20 bytes of card data
-- are used, because nulls occupy a bit and the tuple width is the widest case.
-- A CHECK can enforce the "no wallet columns on a card row" rule; a foreign key
-- cannot, because there are no separate tables to point at.
-- Design B: table per type (TPT). One table per level, one row per object per level.
CREATE TABLE payment_account (
    account_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id   bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    label       text NOT NULL
);
--
CREATE TABLE card_account (
    account_id  bigint PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES payment_account(account_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    card_brand  text NOT NULL,
    pan_last4   char(4) NOT NULL,
    exp_month   smallint NOT NULL,
    exp_year    smallint NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT ck_card_exp CHECK (exp_month BETWEEN 1 AND 12)
);
--
CREATE TABLE sepa_account (
    account_id    bigint PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES payment_account(account_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    iban_country  char(2) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT uq_sepa_account UNIQUE (account_id)
);
-- Reading a SEPA account is now a two-table join. Enforcing "exactly one of
-- these three rows exists per account" requires a trigger or an assertion;
-- no combination of unique constraints expresses it.
-- Design C: single table with a semi-structured subtype. Right choice when the
-- subtype attribute set is open-ended and never queried by SQL.
CREATE TABLE payment_account (
    account_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id   bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    kind        text NOT NULL,
    label       text NOT NULL,
    details     jsonb NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_kind CHECK (kind IN ('card','sepa','wallet')),
    -- A type tag inside the document makes "wrong shape for this kind"
    -- a constraint failure rather than a NULL that means nothing.
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_detail_kind CHECK (details->>'kind' = kind),
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_card_shape CHECK (
        kind <> 'card'
        OR (details ? 'pan_last4' AND details ? 'exp_month'))
);
-- Nothing can be wrong with this design except its premise: if any part of
-- details is ever filtered, sorted, aggregated or joined, it must become a column.

-- The threshold, stated once:
--   the moment a query says WHERE details->>'x' = 'y' or aggregates on it,
--   the document has become a set of columns that have not been written down yet.
--   The index that makes the query fast is an index over the document, and its
--   cost is the same as indexing the column would have been.
CriterionTPH (one table)TPT (table per type)Single table + document
Tables to join for a subtype readNoneOne per levelNone
Rows for a polymorphic readOne, with nullable columnsOne per level, joinedOne
Width of a row for a sparse subtypeWorst case — every nullable column is reservedExactExact, plus a document header
Foreign key into a subtypeImpossible. No table exists to referenceNativeImpossible
NOT NULL in a subtypeCannot be expressed without a table-level CHECK per kindNativeCHECK against the document
Adding a subtypeTwo nullable columns plus a CHECK, in placeOne new table, no change to the parentNothing, if the shape is open
Adding an attribute to one subtypeAdds a nullable column to every row of the tableOne table onlyNothing
SQL Server implementationNative, and the idiomatic oneNativenvarchar(max), or the native json type in 2025
PostgreSQL implementationNative, and the idiomatic oneNative; INHERITS does the same with fewer tables to namejsonb with a GIN index

Decision. The reference model uses TPH for payment_account, with the two CHECK constraints that make the discriminators and the required attributes agree. The deciding factor is that payment_attempt must reference an account of any kind, and a foreign key to a single-table hierarchy is the only version of that reference that does not require a union of three foreign keys or a trigger. Where the reference is always subtype-specific — a table that only ever holds one kind of row — use two tables and no discriminator at all.

Caveats. In TPH the row width is the union of the subtypes, so a table with a 200-byte JSON blob in one subtype and a 30-byte set of columns in another has a 200-byte row for every row. On PostgreSQL this is partly mitigated because short varlena fields are compressed and out-of-line values are TOASTed, but the NULL bitmap still reserves the offsets. On SQL Server there is no such mitigation inside the 8 kB row limit. TPT has the opposite profile: reads cost a join per level, and a polymorphic read of a mixed set needs a UNION ALL across the subtype tables, which no optimiser will convert into a single index scan.

2.6 Associative entities and bi-temporal modelling

A many-to-many relationship becomes an entity when it carries attributes of its own or when its existence must be dated. This section models that case, then introduces the distinction that most systems get wrong: when a fact was true and when the database learned it are two different axes, and an audit-capable schema has to model both.

-- Associative entity: employee --- project is M:N, and the association has
-- its own attributes (role, allocation, review date).
CREATE TABLE project_assignment (
    project_id      bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES project(project_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    employee_id     bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES app_user(user_id)  ON DELETE CASCADE,
    role_code       text NOT NULL,
    allocation_pct  numeric(5,2) NOT NULL CHECK (allocation_pct > 0 AND allocation_pct <= 100),
    assigned_on     date NOT NULL,
    ended_on        date,
    PRIMARY KEY (project_id, employee_id, assigned_on),
    CONSTRAINT ck_pa_dates CHECK (ended_on IS NULL OR ended_on >= assigned_on)
);
-- Why the key includes assigned_on: an employee may be assigned to the same
-- project more than once. That is not a modelling mistake, it is a fact about
-- the world, and the key has to be able to say so.
--
-- The M:N with no attributes does not need a table: a join table with two
-- columns and a composite primary key is the whole of it. Do not add a surrogate.
-- The defect of a surrogate-only join table is that nothing prevents the same
-- pair appearing twice, and duplicate rows in a join table silently multiply
-- every aggregate that passes through it.
-- The two time axes, made explicit. valid_range is when the fact was true in the
-- world; recorded_range is when the database knew it. History in one axis is
-- "as of"; history in both axes is "what did we believe, and when".
CREATE TABLE subscription_history (
    subscription_id  bigint NOT NULL,
    -- valid time: half-open [from, to), so adjacent periods tile without overlap
    valid_range      tstzrange NOT NULL,
    plan_code        text NOT NULL,
    status           text NOT NULL,
    seat_limit       int NOT NULL,
    -- transaction time: half-open [from, to), upper bound NULL means "current"
    recorded_range   tstzrange NOT NULL DEFAULT tstzrange(now(), NULL, '[)'),
    changed_by       bigint,
    reason           text,
    PRIMARY KEY (subscription_id, recorded_range),
    EXCLUDE USING GIST (subscription_id WITH =, valid_range WITH &&)
);
-- The exclusion constraint is the entire point: the database refuses a period
-- that overlaps another, which is the "no double subscription" rule of section
-- 2.1 expressed as a property of time rather than of a current-state row.
-- Note the constraint counts only valid time. Overlapping recorded ranges are
-- legal and are how a correction is expressed.

-- "What was the plan on 2026-03-01, according to what we believed at the time?"
SELECT plan_code, status, seat_limit
  FROM subscription_history
 WHERE subscription_id = :sub
   AND valid_range @> '2026-03-01T00:00:00Z'::timestamptz          -- was true then
   AND recorded_range @> '2026-03-05T12:00:00Z'::timestamptz         -- as believed then;
-- Drop the second predicate for the ordinary question: what was true, as we
-- currently believe it. Drop the first for the other question: what did we
-- believe at a point in time, regardless of validity.
-- Closing a period and opening the next one, in one statement, atomically.
-- The upper bound of the open period is set to the effective time of the change.
BEGIN;
UPDATE subscription_history
   SET recorded_range = tstzrange(lower(recorded_range), now(), '[)')
 WHERE subscription_id = :sub
   AND upper_inf(recorded_range);

INSERT INTO subscription_history (subscription_id, valid_range, plan_code, status, seat_limit)
VALUES (:sub, tstzrange(:effective_at', NULL, '[)'), :new_plan, :new_status, :new_seats);

-- The old row's valid_range must also be closed, or the exclusion constraint fires.
UPDATE subscription_history
   SET valid_range = tstzrange(lower(valid_range), :effective_at', '[)')
 WHERE subscription_id = :sub
   AND upper_inf(valid_range) AND upper_inf(recorded_range) IS FALSE;
COMMIT;
-- Order matters and the exclusion constraint is what tells you so: the inserts
-- fail with 23P01 exclusion_violation, not with a constraint name you can ignore.

-- The portable version, without range types or exclusion constraints. Half-open
-- intervals, two CHECK constraints, and a unique constraint that forbids overlap
-- by a trick: no two rows may have the same start.
CREATE TABLE subscription_period (
    subscription_id bigint NOT NULL,
    plan_code       text NOT NULL,
    valid_from      timestamptz NOT NULL,
    valid_to        timestamptz,          -- NULL = still open
    recorded_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (subscription_id, valid_from),
    CHECK (valid_to IS NULL OR valid_to > valid_from)
);
-- SQL Server: same columns, plus an index on (subscription_id, valid_from, valid_to)
-- and the overlap check written as NOT EXISTS, because SQL Server has neither
-- range types nor exclusion constraints. The check is a serialisable predicate,
-- and it is racy unless the surrounding transaction is serialisable.

Caveats. SQL Server’s system-versioned temporal tables capture transaction time only. CREATE TABLE ... AS SYSTEM VERSIONED records the previous row image of every update and delete in a history table, and that is an excellent answer to "what did this row look like before it changed" and a complete non-answer to "which plan was this tenant on in March". Valid time has to be modelled by hand on top of it, which is what the portable version above does. MySQL has no temporal tables at all and no exclusion constraints, so both axes are manual.

Question being askedAxes neededMechanism
What is the current value?None — current state onlyA mutable column and a version. Cheapest, and loses all history
What was the value on a past date?Valid timeAppend-only period table with a half-open range and a non-overlap guarantee
What did we believe on a past date?Transaction timeSystem-versioned history, or an explicit recorded_range
What did we believe on a past date about a past date?BothBi-temporal table with two ranges. This is the audit-grade answer and it is the only one that survives a regulator asking how the system reported a figure at the time it reported it

Exercises 2

  1. Draw the logical ERD for the reference model and annotate every relationship with its minimum, maximum and optionality on the deletion side. For each relationship, name the constraint that makes the cardinality real.
  2. Classify every table in the reference model as thing, value or event. For each event table, state its retention rule and its partition key. Identify any table currently modelled as a thing that should be an event, and quantify what is being lost.
  3. Implement the pgstattuple index-locality experiment from section 2.3 with 2 000 000 rows on a scratch table. Record leaf pages, deleted pages and read buffers for a 100-row range scan on each of the three key types, and state which difference is large enough to change a production decision.
  4. Model the polymorphic payment_attempt reference three ways: TPH, TPT and a discriminator plus a union of foreign keys. Write the DDL for each, then state which one supports the query "all attempts against accounts that are not expired" and which ones require a join or a UNION.
  5. Convert subscription to the bi-temporal form. Write the query that answers "what did we tell the customer on 1 March 2026 about their plan", and the query that answers "what did the plan actually say on 1 March 2026". Explain why the two answers can differ and what causes the difference.

3 · Normalisation and Denormalisation

3.1 Functional dependencies: the vocabulary of the decision3.2 First and second normal form, with before and after3.3 Third normal form and BCNF3.4 When 2NF is the correct stopping point3.5 Denormalisation with a worked reporting query3.6 Derived versus stored data and the invalidation cost

Normalisation is the process of removing redundancy that is a consequence of mixing grains in one row. This chapter states the test in terms of functional dependencies, works through the first three normal forms with real DDL, gives the cases where stopping early is correct, and then covers the deliberate, priced and documented reinvention of redundancy.

3.1 Functional dependencies: the vocabulary of the decision

A functional dependency is a statement about facts, not about columns: for every pair of rows in every legal state of the table, if the left-hand columns agree then the right-hand columns must agree. Normal forms are successive restrictions on where a violation of that rule is allowed to live.

  The four statements that decide the normal form of a relation R(X, Y, Z, W)

  FD1:  order_id -> line_no, sku, quantity, unit_price, tax_rate
  FD2:  (order_id, line_no) -> everything
  FD3:  sku -> product_name, supplier_id          <-- transitive through sku
  FD4:  supplier_id -> supplier_name              <-- transitive through supplier_id

  A candidate key is a minimal superkey: (order_id, line_no) is the only one.
  A superkey is any attribute set whose closure covers all attributes.

  Reading the closure:  {order_id}⁺ = {order_id, line_no, sku, quantity,
                                    unit_price, tax_rate, product_name,
                                    supplier_id, supplier_name}
                           because sku in the set pulls in FD3, and
                           supplier_id pulls in FD4.
                           {order_id} is not a superkey — it is missing nothing
                           on the right, which is the point.

  1NF   every attribute is atomic; no repeating groups; no partial dependence
  2NF   1NF, and no non-key attribute depends on part of a composite key
  3NF   2NF, and no non-key attribute depends on another non-key attribute
  BCNF  every X -> A has X as a superkey (3NF relaxes this for composite keys)
Normal formRemovesAnomaly it preventsWorth doing in practice?
1NFRepeating groups; multi-valued cellsA fact that cannot be updated, joined or constrained because it lives inside another cellAlways. There is no defensible exception
2NFPartial dependencies on a composite keyThe same fact stored once per child row; update anomaly and space amplificationAlways. With a single-column key it is implied by 1NF
3NFTransitive dependencies on non-key columnsUpdate anomaly: renaming a supplier rewrites every order lineAlmost always, with the exceptions in section 3.4
BCNFDependencies on proper subsets of composite keysThe same anomalies, in a form 3NF does not catchKnow it. The cases that arise are rare and identifiable
4NFIndependent multi-valued dependenciesA cartesian product appearing where two independent sets were meantThink in these terms whenever two repeating groups share a table
5NF, DKNFJoin dependencies; dependencies on non-keysTheory with essentially no production incidenceNo. Reading the definition is sufficient
-- The mechanical part: check the schema for redundant unique constraints.
-- PostgreSQL 18 ships the pg_depend view that makes dependency chains explicit;
-- the practical use is finding a unique constraint that is implied by another.
SELECT conrelid::regclass AS table_name,
       conname, contype,
       pg_get_constraintdef(oid) AS definition
  FROM pg_constraint
 WHERE connamespace = 'public'::regnamespace
   AND contype IN ('u','p','f')
 ORDER BY 1, 2;

-- The four anomalies, demonstrated on a relation that is only in 2NF.
-- Relation: order_line(order_id, line_no, order_date, customer_name,
--                          sku, product_name, quantity, unit_price)
-- Key: (order_id, line_no).  order_date and customer_name depend on order_id
-- alone, so this relation is in 1NF and in 2NF but not in 3NF.
--
-- Insert anomaly:  creating order 1 with 3 lines requires writing the order
-- date and customer name three times, and they must agree.
--
-- Update anomaly:  correcting a customer's name rewrites every line of every
-- order they ever placed -- 10^5 rows and 10^5 dead versions, for one fact.
--
-- Delete anomaly:  deleting the last line of an order destroys the order date
-- and the customer's name. The information is now unrecoverable.
--
-- Insertion anomaly: an order with no lines yet cannot be recorded, because the
-- only row that could hold its date would have to invent a line number.

Note on the four anomalies. They are not equal in severity. The update anomaly is a performance and contention problem; the delete and insertion anomalies are correctness problems that no amount of care at the application layer will prevent, because the required information does not exist anywhere else. When normalising, rank the anomalies by whether they lose information, not by how much text they take to explain.

3.2 First and second normal form, with before and after

The first two normal forms are mechanical and the fixes are local. This section works them on the order table, because the order is where the first modelling mistake almost always appears and where the correction is cheapest to make before the table has data in it.

-- BEFORE: not in 1NF. A repeating group inside a delimited string.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id        bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id       bigint NOT NULL,
    placed_at       timestamptz NOT NULL,
    -- three facts in one cell, separated by a delimiter of choice
    lines           text NOT NULL,      -- 'SKU-A|2|9.99, SKU-B|1|19.50'
    total_excl_tax  numeric(14,2) NOT NULL
);
-- Not 1NF: the cell holds a set, so three of the four anomalies are unavoidable.
-- Unenforceable: nothing can check that quantity > 0 inside the string.
-- Unindexable: a B-tree cannot seek inside a delimited string without an
--                expression index, which is a 1NF violation with extra steps.
-- Unjoinable: "how many orders contain SKU-B" is a full scan and a string split.
-- Unupdatable: changing one line rewrites the whole cell and every index over it.

-- AFTER 1NF: one row per fact, atomic values.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_line (
    order_id            bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES sales_order(order_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    line_no             int NOT NULL,
    sku                 text NOT NULL,
    quantity            int NOT NULL,
    unit_price_excl_tax numeric(14,4) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_no),
    CHECK (quantity > 0),
    CHECK (unit_price_excl_tax >= 0)
);
-- Every constraint is now expressible, and so is every index and every join.
-- BEFORE: in 1NF, in 2NF, not in 3NF. Partial dependencies on a composite key.
-- Key is (order_id, line_no). order_date and customer_name depend on
-- order_id alone, which is only part of the key: that is a 2NF violation.
CREATE TABLE order_line_2nf_bad (
    order_id       bigint NOT NULL,
    line_no        int NOT NULL,
    order_date     timestamptz NOT NULL,   -- depends on order_id only
    customer_name  text NOT NULL,         -- depends on order_id only
    customer_email text NOT NULL,         -- depends on order_id only
    sku            text NOT NULL,
    quantity       int NOT NULL,
    unit_price     numeric(14,4) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_no)
);
-- Consequence: 40 bytes of header repeated on every line. An order of 8 lines
-- stores its customer name 8 times, and a rename is 8 row updates.
-- At 1.4 lines per order average and 10^8 orders this is ~5 TB of duplication
-- in a table that holds no new information from it.

-- AFTER 2NF: the parent-partial facts move up to the parent.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id        bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id       bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    customer_id     bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES app_user(user_id),
    placed_at       timestamptz NOT NULL,
    total_excl_tax  numeric(14,2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);
CREATE TABLE sales_order_line (
    order_id            bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES sales_order(order_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    line_no             int NOT NULL,
    sku                 text NOT NULL,
    quantity            int NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
    unit_price_excl_tax numeric(14,4) NOT NULL CHECK (unit_price_excl_tax >= 0),
    tax_rate            numeric(5,4) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.0000,
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_no)
);
-- The customer is now referenced, not copied. A rename is one UPDATE.
-- The order header is one row per order, so AP-01 can read 50 orders
-- without touching a single line row.

The practical test for 2NF without writing a dependency set: does any column in a table repeat a value that depends on part of the composite key? If the table has a single-column primary key, 2NF cannot be violated and the question does not arise. Most tables that are only in 2NF are join tables with a composite key and one or two extra columns — which is exactly where it is sometimes correct to stay, and section 3.4 explains how to tell.

-- The index consequence of 2NF, stated as an access path. Before the split,
-- every secondary index on the child carries the duplicated columns.

-- AFTER 2NF, AP-01 is a three-page read:
--   idx: (tenant_id, placed_at DESC) INCLUDE (order_id, status, total_excl_tax, currency)
--   the customer name and email are not in the index and are not needed by AP-01.

-- BEFORE the split, the same AP-01 needed either a covering index over the
-- duplicated columns -- 40 bytes per entry, 2.5x the width -- or a lookup per
-- order into a table where the order header exists once per line.

-- Measuring the difference rather than asserting it:
SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('ix_order_line_2nf_bad_sku')) AS before,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('ix_sales_order_line_sku'))    AS after;
-- The same index over the same rows. The delta is pure duplication, and it
-- is paid on every write as well as on every read.

3.3 Third normal form and BCNF

Third normal form removes dependencies of non-key attributes on other non-key attributes. In practice this is the one that separates a schema that can evolve from one that cannot, because it is the form in which a single change is a single write.

-- BEFORE: transitive dependency. sku -> product_name -> supplier_name
CREATE TABLE order_line_3nf_bad (
    order_id       bigint NOT NULL,
    line_no        int NOT NULL,
    sku            text NOT NULL,
    product_name   text NOT NULL,      -- depends on sku
    supplier_name  text NOT NULL,      -- depends on product_name (2 steps)
    quantity       int NOT NULL,
    unit_price     numeric(14,4) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_no)
);
-- Anomalies that are pure 3NF violations:
--   update:  renaming a supplier rewrites 10^6 order lines. A single fact,
--            a mass update, and every historical invoice now disagrees with
--            the product master about who supplied the item.
--   insert:  a new SKU cannot be recorded until an order is placed for it,
--            because the only row that could hold its name is an order line.
--   delete:  deleting the last order line for a product erases its name.

-- AFTER 3NF: each fact at the grain of the thing it describes.
CREATE TABLE supplier (
    supplier_id   bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    name          text NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT uq_supplier_name UNIQUE (name)
);
CREATE TABLE product (
    sku           text PRIMARY KEY,                    -- stable, short, immutable: a good natural key
    name          text NOT NULL,
    supplier_id   bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES supplier(supplier_id),
    unit_price_excl_tax numeric(14,4) NOT NULL,         -- current price
    is_active     boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true
);
--
-- The order line keeps its own price. That is NOT a 3NF violation, and the
-- distinction is the most useful thing in this section:
--   product.unit_price          is a fact about the PRODUCT: "what does it cost now"
--   order_line.unit_price       is a fact about the LINE:  "what was charged"
-- They are different facts about different things, so both are stored, and
-- neither depends on the other. A price change updates product and nothing else.
-- A design that removes the line's price to satisfy 3NF has misunderstood the
-- model, and the symptom is an invoice that silently re-prices.
-- BCNF, and the only way to tell 3NF from BCNF: a transitive dependency whose
-- left-hand side is a composite key. 3NF permits it; BCNF does not.
--
-- Relation: course_booking(student, course, room, date, tutor)
-- Candidate keys: (student, course), (student, room, date)
--
-- Dependency (room, date) -> tutor: a room is taught by one tutor on a date.
-- (room, date) is not a superkey, so this violates BCNF and is ALLOWED by 3NF.
CREATE TABLE course_booking_3nf (
    student  text NOT NULL,
    course   text NOT NULL,
    room     text NOT NULL,
    on_date  date NOT NULL,
    tutor    text NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (student, course),
    UNIQUE (student, room, on_date)
);
-- Anomaly that survives 3NF: the same room on the same date appears once
-- per student booked into it, so the tutor is stored N times and can disagree
-- with itself. No constraint forbids the disagreement.

-- BCNF decomposition: pull the violation out into its own relation.
CREATE TABLE room_tutor (
    room     text NOT NULL,
    on_date  date NOT NULL,
    tutor    text NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (room, on_date)        -- the violation becomes the key
);
CREATE TABLE course_booking_bcnf (
    student  text NOT NULL,
    course   text NOT NULL,
    room     text NOT NULL,
    on_date  date NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (student, course),
    UNIQUE (student, room, on_date),
    FOREIGN KEY (room, on_date) REFERENCES room_tutor(room, on_date)
);
-- Cost of the decomposition: every read of a tutor now needs a join, and the
-- join is not optional because the FK is not optional. This is why BCNF is
-- rarely worth it: it trades a rare anomaly for a permanent join.

-- 4NF: two independent multi-valued dependencies in one relation produce a
-- cartesian product. The classic, and the one that appears in real order systems.
--   Relation: shipment(order_id, ship_to, delivered_to, package_code)
--   MVD1: order_id >> ship_to        (an order has several addresses)
--   MVD2: order_id >> delivered_to    (and several delivery addresses)
--   Storing both in one row implies every address pairs with every address:
--   3 × 4 = 12 rows, 6 of which are nonsense. Neither 3NF nor BCNF catches it.
--   Fix: two tables, order_ship_to(order_id, address_id) and order_deliver_to(...).

Applies to. The 4NF remedy for a table carrying two independent repeating groups is also the remedy available on all three engines without thinking about it: move each group into its own child table. A CHECK constraint cannot express a functional dependency, and neither can a UNIQUE constraint across two independent sets. The only portable enforcement of "this room, this date, one tutor" is a unique constraint on a table whose key is that triple — which is the decomposition, not a constraint on the original relation.

Run the anomaly test rather than the definition test. For each table, answer four questions: can a fact be updated in one place, can a new fact be inserted before its parent exists, can a fact be deleted without losing a neighbour, and is any fact stored more than once? Any "no" to the first three or "yes" to the fourth is a normal-form violation, whatever normal form the table is nominally in.

-- Extended statistics for correlated predicates. A 3NF schema is a prerequisite,
-- not a substitute: the optimiser estimates each column independently, and a
-- query on two columns whose values move together is estimated badly.
--
-- The classic: tenant_id and placed_at are correlated, because a tenant's rows
-- are clustered in time. Single-column histograms miss this, and the plan
-- switches from a nested loop to a hash join on a single tenant.
CREATE STATISTICS st_order_tenant_placed
    (ndistinct, mcv, dependencies)          -- PG 14+; ndistinct from 10, mcv from 12
    ON tenant_id, placed_at FROM sales_order;
ANALYZE sales_order;

-- Verify it was created, and that a functional dependency was actually found:
SELECT statistics_name, statistics_kind, column_names
  FROM pg_stats_ext WHERE statistics_name = 'st_order_tenant_placed';
EXPLAIN SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 42 AND placed_at > '2026-01-01';
-- MySQL has no extended statistics; the same correction is made with an index
-- whose leading column is the low-cardinality one, so the range is evaluated
-- on the second. SQL Server has filtered plans and the CE with no equivalent knob;
-- the correction there is a composite index and a query hint.

3.4 When 2NF is the correct stopping point

3NF is a default, not an objective. Two kinds of table are legitimately only in 2NF: the deliberately denormalised snapshot, and the wide fact table whose denormalisation is already paying for itself. The test is whether the extra join is cheaper than the duplication, in bytes written per row and in readers that must join to understand a row.

SituationVerdictReasoning
A transactional table with a single-column surrogate keyAlready 3NF by constructionNo transitive dependency is possible: every non-key attribute depends on the key, which is a superkey
A join table with a composite key and one descriptive column2NF, and correctThe descriptive column usually depends on only one half of the key, but the join is often the reason the table exists, and the alternative is a fifth table for one column
A fact table carrying dimension attributes for readability2NF or worse, and often correctAd-hoc analysis is the dominant reader; a self-contained row is worth 40 duplicated bytes per row against a join the analyst must remember to write
A snapshot or projection table produced by a queryAny normal form, by designIt is output, not a source of truth. The only invariant that matters is that it is rebuildable
A table with a nullable parent key and no identifying relationshipNot 2NF, and a real defectThis is a 1:1 in disguise, or a missing constraint. Nullable key columns are the single most common sign of a modelling error that has not been named
An entity whose "product" attribute is regenerated on every readStop at 2NF and store the productSection 3.6: the invalidation ledger is cheaper than the per-read recomputation when the product is read more than once
-- The 2NF table that is correct: an invoice snapshot. Every column is a fact
-- about ONE invoice line at ONE moment, and the table is never joined to a
-- master because its readers are compliance auditors and PDF renderers.
CREATE TABLE invoice_line_snapshot (
    invoice_id        uuid NOT NULL,
    line_no           int NOT NULL,
    issued_at         timestamptz NOT NULL,   -- the moment, not a range
    tenant_legal_name text NOT NULL,         -- depends on invoice_id
    tenant_vat_number text NOT NULL,         -- depends on invoice_id
    sku               text NOT NULL,         -- depends on line_no only  <-- 2NF
    product_name      text NOT NULL,         -- depends on line_no only  <-- 2NF
    supplier_name     text NOT NULL,         -- depends on product_name   <-- 3NF violation
    quantity          numeric(12,3) NOT NULL,
    unit_price        numeric(14,4) NOT NULL,
    tax_rate          numeric(5,4) NOT NULL,
    tax_amount        numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
    line_total        numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
    currency          char(3) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (invoice_id, line_no),
    CHECK (quantity > 0)
);
-- Deliberately not in 3NF, and the reasons are all measurable:
--   * 180 bytes per row against 62 for the normalised equivalent: 2.9x.
--   * 4.2 TB against 1.4 TB for 10^8 invoice lines. At 0.06 GBP per GB-month
--     that is ~200 GBP/month, against a join that an audit query would perform
--     once per invoice rather than once per line.
--   * Zero writes after creation. The invalidation ledger is empty by definition,
--     because the invoice is never amended; a corrected invoice is a new document.
--
-- The trade is explicit: 3x the storage, in exchange for no joins on the read path
-- and a row that is meaningful in isolation. That is a defensible trade for a
-- compliance artefact and an indefensible one for a transactional table.

-- The rule this instance of 2NF follows, and which makes it safe:
--   1. the table is append-only -- no UPDATE path exists at all
--   2. the table is a projection of another table and can be rebuilt from it
--   3. nothing in the application joins TO it -- it is a leaf
--   4. its retention is shorter than or equal to its source's, never longer
-- Break any of the four and the 2NF decision must be made again.
-- The cost, measured. Load the same 10^7 rows both ways and compare.
--
-- Normalised: 3 tables, 1 join
--   fact_line            10^7 rows x  56 B  =  560 MB
--   dim_product             10^4 rows x 180 B  =    2 MB
--   dim_supplier               500 rows x  90 B  =  0.05 MB
--   total                                  562 MB, plus 3 B-trees
--   query cost: 10^7 index seeks into dim_product (cached) + 10^7 into dim_supplier
--
-- Denormalised: 1 table, no join
--   fact_line_flat       10^7 rows x 236 B  = 2 360 MB
--   total                                  2 360 MB
--   query cost: a single sequential scan, ~2.4x the bytes, no random access
--
-- The crossover: the flattened scan reads 2.4x the bytes of the fact table, but
-- the normalised query reads 1.4x the bytes of the fact table in seeks. For a scan
-- that touches all rows, flattening wins. For a query that touches 0.1% of rows, the
-- seek-based join wins by two orders of magnitude, and no amount of caching
-- changes the conclusion. Decide from the access ratio, not from the row count.

-- The write side, which the read comparison hides:
--   normalised  INSERT INTO fact_line              56 B written
--   flattened   INSERT INTO fact_line_flat        236 B written
--   normalised  UPDATE product name   1 row, 1 table
--   flattened   UPDATE product name  10^3 rows, 1 table, or nothing at all
-- At 10^7 rows the product rename is either one write or 2.4 GB of writes plus
-- the vacuum, index churn and replication traffic that follow them.

Decision. Denormalising a table that is written is a lease, not a purchase: the duplicated bytes are paid on every insert, every update of a duplicated column, every backup, every replica hop and every restore. Denormalising a table that is written once and read many times — a snapshot, an archive, an audit projection — is a purchase. The distinguishing question is not how often the table is read but whether the duplicated values can ever change after the row is written, and the invoice snapshot above answers it with a single word: never.

Write the normal-form target into the schema review checklist as a number, per table, and record the reason for each table that is below it. "This table is 2NF by design; it is append-only, rebuildable and never joined to" is a reviewable sentence. A schema where nobody can say why a table is denormalised is a schema where the denormalisation is accidental.

3.5 Denormalisation with a worked reporting query

This section takes one reporting query from the shape a normalised schema produces, through the four denormalisations that would make it fast, to a design that serves it with two indexes and no duplication at all. The point is that the fastest query is usually not the most denormalised one.

-- The requirement: finance wants, per tenant, per month, the order count, the
-- revenue excluding tax, the tax collected, and the number of distinct SKUs
-- sold, for any 24-month window, in under 2 seconds.

-- STEP 0. The 3NF query. Correct, and the shape every alternative is measured from.
SELECT o.tenant_id,
       date_trunc('month', o.placed_at)::date                            AS month,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.order_id)                          AS orders,
       SUM(l.quantity * l.unit_price_excl_tax)                     AS revenue,
       SUM(l.quantity * l.unit_price_excl_tax * l.tax_rate)         AS tax,
       COUNT(DISTINCT l.sku)                               AS skus
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.placed_at >= :from AND o.placed_at < :to
 GROUP BY 1, 2;
-- STEP 1. The three denormalisations a team would propose, and what each costs.

-- (a) Copy tenant_id onto sales_order_line.
--     Saves: one index seek per order. Costs: 8 bytes x 10^8 lines = 800 MB,
--     one more column in every index on the child, and a NEW invariant -- the
--     line's tenant must equal the order's tenant -- that no simple constraint can
--     express and that every future writer must uphold. Rejected.

-- (b) Copy line_total onto sales_order_line.
--     Saves: a multiply per row. Costs: 2 bytes-ish, but a real invalidation ledger --
--     quantity, unit_price and tax_rate can all be amended after the fact, and every
--     amendment path must recompute the total. Kept, because the arithmetic is a
--     financial invariant that should be a stored fact anyway (section 3.6).

-- (c) Store month on the order, denormalised from placed_at.
--     Saves: a date_trunc per row. Costs: a month-rollover boundary in UTC, a
--     timezone question per tenant, and a column that is wrong for any order placed
--     in the first or last hour of a month. Rejected: date_trunc is not the cost.

-- MEASUREMENT, not intuition. What the query actually costs today:
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT o.tenant_id, date_trunc('month', o.placed_at)::date, COUNT(DISTINCT o.order_id),
       SUM(l.quantity * l.unit_price_excl_tax)
  FROM sales_order o JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.placed_at >= '2025-01-01' AND o.placed_at < '2026-01-01'
 GROUP BY 1, 2;
-- Expected shape: a range scan of the order table by time, a nested loop to the
-- child, a HashAggregate for the SUM and a Sort or HashAggregate for the DISTINCT
-- counts. The two DISTINCT aggregates are the problem: they need a hash of every
-- order id and every sku, and that is 10^7 and 10^5 hash entries respectively.
--
-- The diagnosis that matters: the cost is not the join, and it is not the
-- denormalisation. It is that COUNT(DISTINCT) cannot be streamed. Fixing that is a
-- query-design problem with a schema answer, and that answer is a rollup.
-- STEP 2. The rollup, and the one subtlety that makes it correct rather than fast.
-- The subtlety: a rollup keyed on (tenant_id, month) cannot hold a COUNT(DISTINCT sku),
-- because distinct counts are not additive. Two months each with 5 SKUs may share 4.

-- So the rollup has two grains, and the reporting query joins them.
CREATE TABLE monthly_order_rollup (
    tenant_id    bigint NOT NULL,
    month        date NOT NULL,
    order_count  int NOT NULL,
    revenue_excl numeric(16,2) NOT NULL,
    tax_total    numeric(16,2) NOT NULL,
    line_count   int NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, month)
);

CREATE TABLE monthly_tenant_sku (
    tenant_id bigint NOT NULL,
    month     date NOT NULL,
    sku       text NOT NULL,
    quantity  bigint NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, month, sku)
);

-- The query the requirement asked for, against 72 000 + 900 000 rows instead of
-- 10^8. The DISTINCT is now over a month's SKUs for one tenant, which is 30 rows.
SELECT r.tenant_id, r.month, r.order_count, r.revenue_excl, r.tax_total,
       COUNT(s.sku) AS skus
  FROM monthly_order_rollup r
  LEFT JOIN monthly_tenant_sku s
         ON s.tenant_id = r.tenant_id AND s.month = r.month
 WHERE r.tenant_id = :tenant OR r.tenant_id = :all
 GROUP BY r.tenant_id, r.month, r.order_count, r.revenue_excl, r.tax_total
 ORDER BY r.month;
-- STEP 3. Maintaining the rollup correctly, including the late amendment.

-- Incremental path, at order time. Inside the order transaction: no, this is
-- the wrong place. Two writes to two tables in the order transaction couples
-- the order's availability to the rollup's correctness, for no read benefit.
-- The right place is the outbox, which already exists (section 7.5).

-- Batch path, hourly. Idempotent, restartable, and repairable.
INSERT INTO monthly_order_rollup (tenant_id, month, order_count, revenue_excl, tax_total, line_count)
SELECT o.tenant_id,
       date_trunc('month', o.placed_at)::date,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.order_id),
       SUM(l.line_total_excl_tax),
       SUM(ROUND(l.line_total_excl_tax * l.tax_rate, 2)),
       COUNT(*)
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.placed_at >= :month_start
   AND o.placed_at <  :month_start + interval '1 month'
 GROUP BY 1, 2
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, month) DO UPDATE
   SET order_count  = EXCLUDED.order_count,
       revenue_excl = EXCLUDED.revenue_excl,
       tax_total    = EXCLUDED.tax_total,
       line_count   = EXCLUDED.line_count;
-- Idempotent: re-running the same window produces the same rows.
-- Full-window: an amendment is picked up on the next run, no delta logic needed.
-- Rebuildable: DELETE the window, re-run the SELECT, and the table is correct
--   even if every earlier run was wrong.

-- The invariant to assert in the test suite, on every run:
--   SELECT COUNT(*) FROM (
--     SELECT tenant_id, month, SUM(revenue_excl) r FROM monthly_order_rollup GROUP BY 1,2) a
--   FULL JOIN
--   (SELECT tenant_id, date_trunc('month',placed_at)::date m, SUM(total_excl_tax) r
--    FROM sales_order GROUP BY 1,2) b
--     ON a.tenant_id = b.tenant_id AND a.month = b.m
--   WHERE a.r IS DISTINCT FROM b.r;   -- must return zero rows

Decision. Of the three proposed denormalisations, two are rejected and one is kept; the aggregate that made the query slow is moved to a rollup at a grain that supports the aggregate’s own algebra. The total work done against the base table is 24 bytes of stored value per line and 104 bytes of stored value per order-month. Against duplicating the tenant and the month on the hot write path, that is the cheaper design, and it is the one that can be proven correct by a comparison query.

Caveats. A rollup is a cache and a cache is only correct if somebody compares it with the thing it caches. The full-outer-join assertion above is the whole of the safety story, and it belongs in the scheduled job and in the integration test suite. A rollup that is maintained by a job nobody verifies is a second source of truth with none of the guarantees of the first, and finance will find the difference between them first.

3.6 Derived versus stored data and the invalidation cost

Every piece of data in a schema is either a fact about the world, which must be stored, or a calculation over stored facts, which need not be. This section states the decision rule, prices the two options, and demonstrates the failure that a stored-but-derived value produces when the writer set is larger than anyone remembers.

-- Candidate 1: DERIVED. line_total = quantity * unit_price_excl_tax.
--
-- Storage cost:   0 bytes.
-- Write cost:     0 extra bytes, 0 extra constraints, 0 extra triggers.
-- Read cost:      one multiply per row, in the CPU, for every reader.
-- Correctness:    total. It cannot be wrong, because there is nothing to keep right.
-- Migration:      the formula is in the query, so it changes in one place.
--
--   SELECT quantity * unit_price_excl_tax AS line_total FROM sales_order_line;
--
-- Verdict: correct as written. One multiply over 10^8 rows is ~30 ms of CPU on a
-- machine that is already reading 4 GB for the aggregation. It is not the cost.

--
-- Candidate 2: STORED. line_total numeric(14,2) NOT NULL, maintained by a trigger.
--
-- Storage cost:   ~8 bytes/row + 8 bytes in every secondary index on the child.
-- Write cost:     the trigger runs on every INSERT and every UPDATE of two columns.
-- Read cost:      zero.
-- Correctness:    depends on the trigger being correct, which is a second
--                 implementation of the same business rule in a different language.
--
-- If it is stored, the arithmetic rule must ALSO be a constraint, or the column is
-- merely a suggestion:
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION set_line_total() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $fn$
BEGIN
    IF NEW.unit_price_excl_tax <> 0 OR NEW.quantity <> 0 THEN
        NEW.line_total_excl_tax := ROUND(NEW.quantity * NEW.unit_price_excl_tax, 2);
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END; $fn$;

CREATE TRIGGER trg_line_total
  BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OF quantity, unit_price_excl_tax
  ON sales_order_line
  FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION set_line_total();
-- The invalidation ledger, in full. This is the actual cost of candidate 2,
-- and it is the list that has to be maintained for the life of the schema.
--
--   writer                          does it set line_total?
--   ------------------------------  ------------------------------------
--   order-placement API              yes, via the trigger
--   amendment API (qty or price)      yes, via the trigger
--   nightly reconciliation import     NO -- unless the trigger is on that path
--   finance adjustment script         NO -- triggers are per-table, not per-app
--   manual fix in a SQL console      NO -- and cannot be prevented
--   a future bulk DELETE / restore     NO -- column goes NULL, or stays stale
--   a future partitioning migration    NO -- rewrite paths can bypass triggers
--
-- The trigger covers the first two rows. The other five are defects waiting for
-- a data import, and each of them is silent: the value is wrong and nothing
-- reports it, because a CHECK constraint that merely restates the formula
-- catches the value, not its absence.

-- The detector. If the value is stored, this query must return zero rows,
-- and it must be in the scheduled job next to the rollup assertion.
SELECT order_id, line_no, quantity, unit_price_excl_tax, line_total_excl_tax
  FROM sales_order_line
 WHERE line_total_excl_tax IS DISTINCT FROM
               ROUND(quantity * unit_price_excl_tax, 2)
 LIMIT 100;
-- Candidate 3: the third option, and the one that is usually correct when the
-- derived value is a financial invariant rather than a convenience.
--
--   line_total_excl_tax is not "quantity times price". It is "the amount that was
--   invoiced for this line", which is agreed with the customer at the moment of
--   invoicing and does not subsequently obey the multiplication. A 2% rounding
--   adjustment on a bulk order makes the two different by design.
--
-- So: store it, and make the constraint express the invariant rather than the formula.
ALTER TABLE sales_order_line
  ADD CONSTRAINT ck_line_total_positive CHECK (line_total_excl_tax >= 0),
  ADD CONSTRAINT ck_line_total_sane
    CHECK (line_total_excl_tax <= quantity * unit_price_excl_tax * 1.10);

-- The second constraint is a sanity bound, not an equality. It catches the
-- import that forgot the column, the script that used the wrong tax basis and
-- the double-application of a rounding rule, while permitting the legitimate
-- 2% adjustment. A constraint that can be satisfied is worth more than one that
-- cannot, and a 10% band is a decision that should be reviewed with finance.

-- The remaining question: who owns line_total when it is stored?
--   the trigger        -- always correct on INSERT/UPDATE, blind to other paths
--   the application    -- correct only if every service is changed, forever
--   a stored procedure -- correct only if the table is not written from elsewhere
--   a generated column -- always correct, cannot be written to, and is the cleanest
--                        answer when the formula never involves another row
--
-- PostgreSQL 18: STORED and VIRTUAL generated columns. VIRTUAL computes on read
-- and stores nothing, which is the same as computing it in the query and costs the
-- same; STORED costs 8 bytes and cannot drift. Both are enforced by the engine,
-- which is the property that makes them safer than a trigger.
ALTER TABLE sales_order_line
  DROP COLUMN line_total_excl_tax,
  ADD COLUMN line_total_excl_tax numeric(14,2)
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (ROUND(quantity * unit_price_excl_tax, 2)) STORED;
-- MySQL: generated columns since 5.7, STORED and VIRTUAL, both enforced.
-- SQL Server: a PERSISTED computed column. Cannot be written to, and the optimiser
-- will use it as an index key only if it is declared PERSISTED and precise.
SituationVerdictReason
Cheap arithmetic over a few columns, no other row involvedDerive on read, or a VIRTUAL generated columnThere is no drift risk and the cost is in the CPU the machine is already using
Arithmetic over another table’s rowStore, or denormalise the input onto this rowA derived value that depends on a join cannot be a generated column, and computing it per read multiplies the join cost by the row count
A value that was agreed and is now a promiseStore, with a sanity bound rather than an equalityIt is a fact, not a calculation, and it must remain true after the inputs move
Anything an operator will eventually edit by handDerive on readNo stored value survives a year of ad-hoc maintenance
A value read once per row and written once per rowEither is defensible; decide and write it downThe cost difference is one multiply against 8 bytes; the ledger cost is the real difference

Exercises 3

  1. Take a real table from a schema you work with. Write its closure from the primary key. Identify every attribute that is in the closure through another non-key attribute, and state which normal form the table is in as a result.
  2. Construct a relation in 2NF but not 3NF, with a composite key, using the order domain. Write the DDL, then produce the two anomalies with actual UPDATE statements and record the number of rows each touches on a table of 106 lines.
  3. Find an instance of a BCNF-only violation in any schema you can reach, or construct one deliberately. Decompose it, measure the size difference of the two designs at 107 rows, and argue for keeping the 3NF form.
  4. Take the STEP 0 reporting query from section 3.5 and run it with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) on 107 lines. Identify which operator dominates, and demonstrate with a rollup that removing that operator, rather than denormalising the table, is the fix.
  5. Audit one table in a real schema for stored derived values. For each, produce the detector query, run it, and report the number of rows that disagree with their formula. A non-zero result is the deliverable.
  6. Convert one stored derived column to a GENERATED ALWAYS AS ... STORED column. Record the migration time on a production-sized copy, and explain why a generated column cannot satisfy the invoice-snapshot case even though it is strictly more correct.

4 · Physical Design and Partitioning

4.1 Row store and column store4.2 Physical order: the clustered index as a decision4.3 Horizontal partitioning: range, hash and list4.4 Partition by tenant and the hot-tenant problem4.5 Vertical partitioning and the missing-partition-key trap4.6 Mechanisms, subpartitioning and the replicated reference table

The logical model says what the data means. The physical design says how it is laid out on disk, and that choice governs what maintenance operations cost, which queries can prune, and how much of the machine a single scan is allowed to touch. This chapter covers storage format, physical ordering, the three partitioning strategies, the multi-tenant decision and the trap that partitioning introduces.

4.1 Row store and column store

A row store keeps all the values of a row together and finds rows through a B-tree. A column store keeps all the values of a column together and compresses them. The two are not faster and slower versions of the same thing: they are optimised for opposite operations, and the interesting design question is when a table deserves both.

  ROW STORE                          COLUMN STORE
  ─────────────────────────────      ─────────────────────────────
  page: 8 kB, 40-60 tuples           page: 8 kB / 1 MB, compressed
  tuple: [header][bitmap][cols]      column chunk: [min][max][sum][bitmap]
  find one row   : B-tree seek       find one row  : no index at all
  read 1 of 30 cols: read all 30     read 1 of 30 cols: read that one
  write one row  : append to a page  write one row  : recompress a chunk
  MVCC: row versions                 MVCC: row versions in delta stores
  index: B-tree / hash / BRIN ...    index: B-tree on the cluster key
  update: new version, dead tuple    update: in-place if it fits, else delta

  THE COMPRESSION ARGUMENT, which is the real one:
  an order_id column holds 10^8 near-monotonic values. Sorted, the high bytes are
  almost constant, so a run-length + delta encoding reduces 8 bytes per value to
  well under one bit. A row store cannot do this, because it must be able to
  read any row, and so it must keep every column of that row on the same page.

  THE COST, and it is not a rounding error:
  a column store cannot update a single row cheaply. A delta store absorbs the
  writes until it fills, then the whole chunk is rewritten. A 10^8-row table with
  1 000 updates per second has a delta store that never drains.
EngineRow storeColumn storeNotes
PostgreSQL 18Heap + B-tree; TOAST for large values; no heap reuse without vacuumNone in core. A columnar extension has not merged; the honest answer is a separate store or a rollupPhysical order is decoupled from the index: CLUSTER rewrites the heap, or use pg_repack
MySQL 9.xInnoDB, always clustered by primary key, no separate heapNone in the base engine; HeatWave adds a columnar in-memory store with vectorised executionThe primary key is therefore the physical order and the row locator for every secondary index
SQL Server 2025Clustered B-tree, or a heap with forwarded recordsClustered and nonclustered columnstore, with batch-mode operators, segment elimination and delta storesThe only engine here with a first-class columnar path; also has row, page and Unicode compression, applied independently to a table
-- SQL Server: the compressed estimates before you commit to a rebuild.
-- sp_estimatedatabcompressedsize is the decision tool. The rebuild itself is
-- what costs hours, so estimate first and schedule second.
EXEC sys.sp_estimatedatabcompressedsize
     @tabobjid = OBJECT_ID('dbo.sales_order'),
     @toadtable = 'dbsalesordercompressed',
     @totalused = 40;

-- Page compression, rebuilt online-ish and resumable.
ALTER TABLE dbo.sales_order REBUILD WITH (COMPRESSION = PAGE, SORT_IN_TEMPDB = OFF, MAXDURATION = 60, RESUMABLE = ON);
-- MAXDURATION makes the operation abort cleanly at the end of a window rather
-- than run for six hours; RESUMABLE means the next window continues.

-- A columnstore index, with the two settings that decide whether it works.
CREATE COLUMNSTORE INDEX ix_sales_order_cs ON dbo.sales_order
    ORDER (tenant_id, placed_at);
-- ORDER matters: it sets the cluster key, and an unordered columnstore loses
-- segment elimination and dictionary sharing. Unordered is the default and it
-- is the single most common reason a columnstore index underperforms.
--
-- Delta store size, and the threshold at which it flushes:
SELECT OBJECT_NAME(object_id) AS table_name,
       delta_stripe_count, total_rows, segment_count
  FROM sys.dm_db_column_store_row_group_physical_stats
 WHERE state_description = 'COMPRESSED';
-- A delta store that keeps refilling means the table is being updated in bulk:
-- that is an OLTP table, and the columnstore is the wrong home for it. Use a
-- nonclustered columnstore as a read-only copy, fed by a job.
-- PostgreSQL: what compression actually buys, and where it is applied.
SELECT c.relname,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(c.oid))                        AS main,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(c.oid) - pg_relation_size(c.oid)) AS toast,
       ROUND(100.0 * (pg_total_relation_size(c.oid) - pg_relation_size(c.oid))
             / NULLIF(pg_total_relation_size(c.oid),0), 1) AS toast_pct
  FROM pg_class c
  WHERE c.relname = 'audit_event';

-- Detect a value that has been pushed out of line. A value stored inline
-- measures the same as its own size; an out-of-line value measures 18 bytes
-- (a varlena header plus a pointer) regardless of the real size.
SELECT event_id,
       pg_column_size(before_state) AS stored,
       pg_column_size(pg_column_size(before_state)) AS raw
  FROM audit_event ORDER BY pg_column_size(before_state) DESC LIMIT 5;

-- Storage mode, per column. EXTERNAL disables compression and sends large
-- values out of line uncompressed; PLAIN keeps them inline and therefore caps
-- the row at the TOAST threshold. EXTENDED (the default) compresses first,
-- and only moves out of line if compression does not get the value under it.
ALTER TABLE audit_event ALTER COLUMN before_state SET STORAGE PLAIN;
-- Deliberately wrong for audit_event: a 4 kB JSON body would then fail the
-- insert with "row is too big". The correct setting here is the default.

-- Compressed values, deliberately. Highly repetitive audit payloads gain an
-- order of magnitude, at the cost of CPU on every read of that column.
CREATE TABLE metric_sample (
    metric    text NOT NULL,
    at        timestamptz NOT NULL,
    labels    jsonb NOT NULL,
    value     double precision NOT NULL
) PARTITION BY RANGE (at);
ALTER TABLE metric_sample ALTER COLUMN labels SET STORAGE EXTERNAL;
SET default_toast_compression = 'lz4';   -- PG 14+, if built --with-lz4

Decision. The reference model stays entirely in the row store. The reasoning is quantitative: the largest analytic need is AP-04, a monthly rollup, and the rollup is   000 rows — a size at which a columnar representation has no advantage over an in-memory hash aggregate. Introducing a columnar copy would add an ingestion path, a consistency mechanism and an operational surface to solve a problem that the rollup already solves. The threshold for reconsidering is a measured aggregate cost above roughly 109 bytes read, or an analytical need for a column the transaction schema does not hold.

Caveats. "Add a columnstore index" is a phrase that has ended more production performance investigations than any other. Three things must hold: the index must be built ORDERed on a sensible cluster key, the table must be substantially read and substantially not updated, and the query must be able to run in batch mode, which needs a minimum estimated row count and a real operator pipeline rather than a nested loop over seeks. A columnstore index on a table with a volatile last-page update is both slow to query and expensive to maintain.

4.2 Physical order: the clustered index as a decision

On two of the three engines, the order of rows on disk is the primary key’s order, and on all three the physical order determines how much I/O a query reads, whether index-only scans succeed, and whether a space-efficient index can be used. It is a design decision with an enormous downstream effect, and it is usually made by accident by whoever wrote the first migration.

-- The three couplings, stated before the decision is made.
--
-- SQL Server:  clustered index = the table. A nonclustered index stores the clustering
-- key as its row locator, so the width of the clustered key is added to EVERY
-- nonclustered index entry, and a lookup from one into the other is a seek.
-- A 16-byte uniqueidentifier clustering key inflates a 20-byte index into a 36-byte
-- index: nearly double, plus a random seek on every row that the index does not cover.
--
-- MySQL / InnoDB:  the primary key IS the table. A secondary index entry carries the
-- primary key as its row pointer, for the same reason. Changing the primary key of
-- an InnoDB table is a full table rebuild, not an ALTER.
--
-- PostgreSQL:  the heap is unordered and the B-tree is a separate structure holding
-- (ctid, key). The two are coupled only by the correlation statistic, which tells
-- the planner how well heap order matches key order. CLUSTER rewrites the heap in
-- index order and takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock for the duration.

-- Choosing the physical order of the order table. Three candidates:
--
--   order_id (monotonic)   append-only, perfect locality, no page splits ever.
--                        Cost: "orders for a tenant in a date range" is scattered
--                        across the whole table, and AP-03 becomes a wide scan
--                        or an index-to-heap lookup per row.
--
--   placed_at             append-only, good locality, ordered by time which is
--                        the second-most-common filter. Cost: ties, and a
--                        backdated order (an import) appends into the middle.
--
--   (tenant_id, placed_at)  best for AP-01 and AP-03, worst for retention: deleting
--                        one month means a row-level delete scattered through
--                        every tenant's range rather than one contiguous run.
--
-- Decision for the reference model: order_id, because sales_order is append-only
-- and the range queries are all served by (tenant_id, placed_at) through the
-- secondary index, whose entries are already ordered within each tenant.

CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id  bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    ...
    CONSTRAINT pk_sales_order PRIMARY KEY (order_id)   -- clustered / physical order
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);          -- if partitioned, time is on the
                                                        -- partition key, not the clustering key
-- The correlation statistic is the number to check. 1.0 means heap order and
-- index order agree; below about 0.5 means index-only scans and BRIN will
-- be ineffective, and every index entry costs a random page fetch.
SELECT attname, correlation, n_distinct
  FROM pg_stats WHERE tablename = 'sales_order';

-- Reorder the heap to match an index. ACCESS EXCLUSIVE for the whole
-- operation, which on a live table means a maintenance window.
-- CLUSTER sales_order USING ix_order_tenant_placed;
--
-- Online alternative: pg_repack, which rebuilds into a new table and swaps
-- with a short exclusive lock at the end. It needs a free disk the size of the
-- table plus its largest index, and 2 x the WAL for the operation.
-- pg_repack -t sales_order -i ix_order_tenant_placed -d contoso

-- The setting that makes a B-tree leave room, and therefore make HOT
-- updates possible: a page at 90% full has a 10% chance per update of fitting
-- on the same page, and a same-page update needs no index change at all.
CREATE INDEX ix_order_tenant_placed ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)
    WITH (fillfactor = 80);
