-- A lower fillfactor costs space and initial write throughput and buys update
-- throughput. It is a setting for UPDATE-heavy tables, not for append-only ones.
SELECT relname,
       ROUND(100.0 * heap_blks_hit / NULLIF(heap_blks_hit + heap_blks_read, 0), 1) AS heap_hit_pct
  FROM pg_statio_user_tables WHERE relname = 'sales_order';
Physical order criterionGoodBad
Narrow8-byte bigint16-byte uuid, or a 64-character char business key
ImmutableSurrogate assigned once at insertA mutable natural key, or a status column that changes daily
MonotonicIdentity, sequence, snowflakeRandom UUID, a hash of a natural key, an MD5 of a name
Correlated with the dominant filterplaced_at when time is the common filtertenant_id when tenants are created in a random order over years
Not null and unique by definitionPrimary keyNullable unique column, which needs an extra per-row flag

Caveats. Choosing the physical order for one query and thereby degrading another is a real trade, and the correct resolution is almost always a partition rather than a re-cluster: partitioning by time gives a contiguous run for retention and for time-range scans, while the clustered key gives locality for point lookups, and the two coexist. What is not recoverable is a random physical order, because it affects every read on the table and only the measured rewrite fixes it.

On a table where a random clustering key was shipped by accident, the diagnosis is a single query: compare the clustering-key index size against a comparable index on a narrow column. If the clustered index is 1.8 times the size of the others for no reason, the row locator is the culprit. On SQL Server, sp_spaceused plus the average row size versus the index depth gives the same answer without a plan.

4.3 Horizontal partitioning: range, hash and list

Horizontal partitioning splits one logical table into many physical tables along a single axis, and every engine implements it declaratively. The choice of axis decides which queries prune, which maintenance operations become constant-time, and which queries get slower because they must now consider every piece.

  PARTITION BY RANGE (time)         PARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id)      PARTITION BY LIST (tenant_id)
  ───────────────────────────       ───────────────────────────       ────────────────────────────
  p2026_01  [Jan 1 .. Feb 1)       h0  {0,4,8,12,16,...}             t_0042  {42}
  p2026_02  [Feb 1 .. Mar 1)       h1  {1,5,9,13,17,...}             t_0107  {107}
  p2026_03  [Mar 1 .. Apr 1)       h2  {2,6,10,14,18,...}            t_9999  {9999}
  p_default [Apr 1 ..)             h3  {3,7,11,15,19,...}            p_default

  prunes on: a time predicate         prunes on: an exact tenant          prunes on: an exact tenant
  grows by: time, in order            grows by: nothing; equal parts     grows by: new tenants
  drop    : DROP a month = O(1)       drop    : not possible             drop    : erase a tenant =
                                                                     DROP a partition = O(1)
  skew    : current months are hot,  skew    : none by construction     skew    : one giant tenant
            old months are cold                                          dominates one partition
  range on one column only per level; a LIST partition's values must be
  enumerated or routed through a lookup table that the engine reads.
-- PostgreSQL 18: range partitioning, with the operational detail that matters.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    placed_at      timestamptz NOT NULL,
    total_excl_tax numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (placed_at, order_id)     -- the partition key MUST be in every
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);         -- unique key on a partitioned table

-- Create this month's partition ahead of time. A missing partition is a
-- runtime INSERT failure, not a query that finds nothing.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_2026_10 PARTITION OF sales_order
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-10-01') TO ('2026-11-01');
-- Add a CHECK constraint and validate it BEFORE attaching, so that the
-- ATTACH does not have to scan the new table to prove the bounds hold.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_2026_11 (LIKE sales_order INCLUDING ALL);
ALTER TABLE sales_order_2026_11 ADD CONSTRAINT ck_bound
    CHECK (placed_at >= '2026-11-01' AND placed_at < '2026-12-01') NOT VALID;
ALTER TABLE sales_order_2026_11 VALIDATE CONSTRAINT ck_bound;

-- Attach in a transaction that holds only SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE on the
-- parent, so that the brief lock needed for the attach does not queue behind
-- or ahead of ordinary DML. The lock is still taken; it is only made brief.
BEGIN;
  LOCK TABLE sales_order IN SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE MODE;
  ALTER TABLE sales_order ATTACH PARTITION sales_order_2026_11 FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-11-01') TO ('2026-12-01');
COMMIT;

-- Retention: drop the partition. Instant, and no dead tuples to vacuum.
-- DELETE FROM sales_order WHERE placed_at < '2024-01-01'
--   would write a row version for every row, bloat every index, and generate
--   the same volume of WAL, and would then need a VACUUM to reclaim it.
-- DROP TABLE sales_order_2024_01;
-- DETACH first if the data must survive, or DETACH ... CONCURRENTLY (PG 14+)
-- if the partition is being archived rather than deleted.
-- Confirming that pruning happened. This is the check to put in a test, because a
-- partition key that is no longer the leading column of the index prunes nothing
-- and the query degrades silently as the table grows.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sales_order
 WHERE placed_at >= '2026-10-01' AND placed_at < '2026-11-01';
-- Expect "Subplans Removed: N" and exactly one partition in the plan.
--
-- Pruning rules that are easy to get wrong:
--   * a function on the partition column that is not the bare column kills pruning
--     unless it is one of the recognised immutable forms (date_trunc, extract)
--   * a parameter prunes at EXECUTION time, which is fine, but only if the plan
--     was prepared with generic or custom plans accordingly
--   * OR across partitions (WHERE d = x OR d = y) prunes to the union, correctly
--   * a subquery on the partition column does not prune, and never will

-- Inventory: how many partitions, and how much is in each. A partition that
-- grows to a third of the table has stopped being a partition in practice.
SELECT c.relname AS partition_name,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(c.oid)) AS total,
       s.n_live_tup,
       pg_get_expr(c.relpartbound, c.oid) AS bounds
  FROM pg_class c
  JOIN pg_inherits i ON i.inhrelid = c.oid
  JOIN pg_stat_user_tables s ON s.relid = c.oid
 WHERE i.inhparent = 'sales_order'::regclass
 ORDER BY 2 DESC;
-- MySQL 9.x: RANGE COLUMNS, and the two restrictions that shape the design.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id       bigint NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    placed_at      datetime(3) NOT NULL,
    total_excl_tax decimal(14,2) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (placed_at, order_id),
    KEY ix_tenant_placed (tenant_id, placed_at)
) ENGINE=InnoDB
  PARTITION BY RANGE COLUMNS (placed_at) (
    PARTITION p2026_10 VALUES LESS THAN ('2026-11-01'),
    PARTITION p2026_11 VALUES LESS THAN ('2026-12-01'),
    PARTITION p_max     VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE)
);
--
-- RESTRICTION 1: every unique key on a partitioned InnoDB table must contain
-- every partitioning column. A PRIMARY KEY (order_id) alone is rejected. This is
-- why the primary key above is (placed_at, order_id), and it is a design constraint
-- rather than a preference: order_id is no longer globally unique in the
-- primary key's terms, only unique within a month.
--
-- RESTRICTION 2: foreign keys are not supported on partitioned InnoDB tables.
-- Referential integrity for a partitioned MySQL table is therefore an
-- application or trigger responsibility. State that in the schema review.
--
-- Limits and maintenance:
--   max 8192 partitions per table; part files are open per partition, and exceeding
--   the open-file limit produces ER_TOO_MANY_PARTITIONS_ERROR (1017) at open time.
--   monthly partition churn is a scheduled ALTER that must finish before the month
--   begins, or writes land in p_max and stop being pruned.
ALTER TABLE sales_order REORGANIZE PARTITION p_max INTO (
    PARTITION p2026_12 VALUES LESS THAN ('2027-01-01'),
    PARTITION p_max     VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE));
-- ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE, with a pre-created partition, avoids a rebuild.

-- SQL Server 2025: the two-object form, and SWITCH for zero-copy movement.
-- CREATE PARTITION FUNCTION pf_sales_order (DATE) AS RANGE RIGHT FOR VALUES (...)
-- CREATE PARTITION SCHEME ps_sales_order AS PARTITION pf_sales_order ALL TO ([PRIMARY]);
-- CREATE CLUSTERED INDEX ix ON sales_order(placed_at, order_id) ON ps_sales_order(placed_at);
--
-- Retention without rewriting a single row:
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_2024_01 (...) ON ps_sales_order(placed_at);
-- ALTER TABLE sales_order SWITCH PARTITION p2024_01 TO sales_order_2024_01;
-- DROP TABLE sales_order_2024_01;
-- SWITCH validates an alignment check and then renames a metadata entry: it is
-- a metadata operation, so the retention job finishes in milliseconds even on a
-- billion-row partition. It also requires the column order to match exactly,
-- which is why the clustering key starts with the partition column here.
AxisCorrect whenPruning works forMaintenance winRejection reason
Range on timeThe table is append-only with a retention policy; the archive boundary is knownEvery time-bounded query, which is most of themRetention is a metadata operation; old partitions are not read, not vacuumed, not backed up incrementallyWrites to a mis-bounded row land in the default partition and stop being pruned
List on a low-cardinality keyThe key has tens or hundreds of values and each needs independent treatmentEquality on the key onlyA single key’s data can be dropped or archived alone — the only way to satisfy a right-to-erasure request cheaplyCardinality grows; hot-key skew destroys the benefit; every value must be enumerated
HashDistribution is even and no retention-by-key existsEquality on the key onlyParallel scans; even page occupancyNo range queries prune, and no single partition can be dropped — so it buys nothing for time-series data
Subpartition (range then hash)Both a time boundary and an even distribution are wantedTime range, then the keyRetention still drops whole outer partitionsPlanning time grows with the product of both levels; a DEFAULT is needed at each level or an insert fails

Choose the partition key by asking two questions in order: which query prunes with this key? and which maintenance operation becomes constant-time? If the answer to the second is "none", the partitioning is buying nothing that a good index does not already provide, and it is adding planning overhead, a unique-key restriction and a partition-lifecycle job that will eventually fail to run on time.

4.4 Partition by tenant and the hot-tenant problem

In a multi-tenant system, partitioning by tenant is the obvious first idea and it is correct for a specific reason: it is the only partitioning scheme that makes one tenant’s data separable from another’s. It is also the scheme most often defeated by the distribution of tenants, because tenants are not equal and never have been.

-- The measurement that decides the design, from section 1.3:
--   2 000 tenants, 10^8 orders total
--   largest tenant          1.4 x 10^7 orders  = 14% of the table
--   top 3% of tenants       4.0 x 10^7 orders  = 40% of the table
--   median tenant           8 x 10^3 orders  = 0.008% of the table
--
-- Under PARTITION BY LIST (tenant_id):
--   * the largest tenant's partition is 3 000 times the size of the median tenant's
--   * a query for that tenant scans a partition no smaller than before: no win
--   * its VACUUM, its index rebuild and its statistics target all take as long
--     as all the others put together, and they run on the same schedule
--   * the partition count is the tenant count, and 2 000 partitions is already
--     at the edge of what a planner and a lock budget tolerate
--
-- Under PARTITION BY LIST (month), which the reference model uses:
--   * 40% of rows in the current month, 0.1% in any month two years old: a natural
--     hot-to-cold gradient that needs no tuning, because it is the business
--   * 24-36 partitions, each of a workable size, each droppable for retention
--   * AP-01 (latest 50 for a tenant) prunes to one month, and inside that month
--     the (tenant_id, placed_at DESC) index returns 50 rows
--   * erasure for one tenant is a row-level DELETE scoped to tenant_id, which is
--     the honest cost of not having per-tenant partitions

-- If per-tenant isolation is a hard requirement, do not partition by tenant_id.
-- Partition by time and keep a routing table, and give the largest tenants their
-- own table. This is the "director" pattern:

CREATE TABLE tenant_storage (
    tenant_id        bigint PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    storage_tier     text NOT NULL,     -- shared | dedicated
    dedicated_table text,               -- NULL for shared
    moved_at         timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT ck_dedicated CHECK (
        (storage_tier = 'shared' AND dedicated_table IS NULL)
     OR (storage_tier = 'dedicated' AND dedicated_table IS NOT NULL))
);
-- The application resolves the tier once, at connection time, and then every
-- query for that tenant either goes to the shared table or to a table of its own.
-- The cost is a routing decision in every query path, which is real, and the
-- benefit is that the 14% tenant stops dictating the maintenance window for everyone.
-- Isolating a hot tenant inside a hash partition scheme, when the hot tenant is
-- known in advance and time-based retention is not required. The partition key
-- is a generated column, so no application change is needed to maintain it.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
  ADD COLUMN tenant_bucket smallint GENERATED ALWAYS AS (
      CASE WHEN tenant_id = 9999 THEN 63          -- the whale, alone
           ELSE (abs(hashtext(tenant_id::text)) % 63) END
  ) STORED;
-- 64 partitions: 63 balanced, one containing only the whale. A query for the
-- whale prunes to one partition; a query for anyone else prunes to one of 63.
--
-- Note what this costs. Nothing can be dropped by time, the 63 buckets are not
-- erasable individually, and a STORED generated column is 2 bytes plus a CPU
-- cost on every write. It is the right answer for exactly one distribution shape.

-- The measurable consequence of skew, and the threshold at which it bites.
SELECT tenant_id, COUNT(*) AS orders,
       ROUND(AVG(tenant_id)::numeric, 0) AS _unused
  FROM sales_order GROUP BY tenant_id
 ORDER BY orders DESC LIMIT 1;
--
-- Rules of thumb, stated as ratios so they survive any data set:
--   max_partition_rows / median_partition_rows  >  100  -> skew will dominate
--   max_partition_rows / total_rows            >  0.1  -> that partition is the system
--   partitions                                  >  500  -> planning time becomes visible
--   partitions                                  >  2000 -> the lock budget becomes the limit

Requirement. If a tenant’s data must be separable for legal reasons, the separation has to be physical — a different table, a different database or a different schema with its own security boundary. Partitioning by tenant does not provide it: a partition is not a security boundary, a backup is not per partition, and a DROP PARTITION performed by a mistaken job is unrecoverable for every tenant in it. The right response to a hard erasure requirement is a dedicated store for the tenants that need one, as in the tenant_storage table above.

Caveats. List partitioning by tenant forces the partition key into every unique key, which means a global UNIQUE (external_ref) becomes UNIQUE (tenant_id, external_ref) and the guarantee that an external reference is unique across the platform is gone — it now depends on the generator. That is a semantic change, not a syntax one, and it must be a deliberate decision rather than a consequence of a CREATE TABLE that had to be adjusted until the engine accepted it.

4.5 Vertical partitioning and the missing-partition-key trap

Vertical partitioning splits a wide table by column rather than by row, so that the columns most queries read live in a narrow table and the rest do not compete for cache or index space. It is cheap to do, easy to undo, and on two of the three engines the engine already does it for you.

-- Before: one wide table, 62 columns, of which 6 are read 99% of the time.
--   Every secondary index entry on this table carries only the indexed columns,
--   so the width problem is in the heap, not the index -- but the heap is what
--   the buffer pool holds, and a 1.4 kB row means 4.3 rows per 8 kB page.

CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id        bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id       bigint NOT NULL,
    placed_at       timestamptz NOT NULL,
    status          text NOT NULL,
    currency        char(3) NOT NULL,
    total_excl_tax  numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
    -- 56 wide, rarely-read columns: onboarding payload, tax engine output,
-- third-party risk score, the rendered invoice document, the full audit
-- trail of pricing lookups, and so on.
    onboarding_payload jsonb,
    tax_engine_output  jsonb,
    risk_score         jsonb,
    rendered_invoice    bytea,
    ...
);

-- After: the hot core is 7 columns, 220 bytes, 29 rows per page.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    placed_at      timestamptz NOT NULL,
    status         text NOT NULL,
    currency       char(3) NOT NULL,
    total_excl_tax numeric(14,2) NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE sales_order_detail (
    order_id            bigint PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES sales_order(order_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    onboarding_payload  jsonb,
    tax_engine_output   jsonb,
    risk_score          jsonb,
    rendered_invoice     bytea
);
-- The core table is now 1.4 kB/row -> 220 B/row: 6.4x more rows per page, 6.4x
-- more rows per buffer-pool page, and a sequential scan of the core that reads a
-- sixth of the bytes for the same 10^8 rows.
--
-- The cost, stated honestly: every read of a wide column now costs a lookup, and
-- the FK from detail to core is an extra index to maintain on the detail table.
-- Vertical partitioning is only correct when the wide columns are genuinely rare.

-- THE ENGINE ALREADY DOES THIS. PostgreSQL TOASTs any varlena value beyond about
-- 2 kB automatically, and compresses it first. So the "before" table above already
-- has its wide columns out of line, and manual vertical partitioning on PostgreSQL
-- buys only the ability to use a different index and a different retention policy.
--
-- SQL Server: nvarchar(max) and varbinary(max) are stored out of row when they exceed
-- 8000 bytes, but the 8 kB row limit is absolute and there is no way to store a row
-- that does not fit. Vertical partitioning is the only relief available.
-- MySQL: the DYNAMIC row format stores long varlena values with a 20-byte pointer
-- and a 768-byte inline prefix, but the 8126-byte page still bounds the row.
-- THE MISSING-PARTITION-KEY TRAP. Every partitioning scheme has a query it makes
-- worse, and the shape of that query is always the same: one that does not
-- mention the partition key.
--
-- Partitioned by month. These queries prune and are fast:
--   SELECT ... WHERE placed_at >= '2026-10-01' AND placed_at < '2026-11-01'
--   SELECT ... WHERE order_id = 12345      -- PK is (placed_at, order_id)
--
-- These do not prune at all, and the cost grows with the number of partitions:
--   SELECT ... WHERE external_ref = 'MIG-88213'      -- no partition column
--   SELECT ... WHERE tenant_id = 42 ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50
--     -- prunes to nothing, because the LIMIT is not a partition predicate
--   UPDATE ... SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE order_id = ?
--     -- one partition, fine; but the same statement without the key touches all 36

-- Diagnosing it, per engine, rather than guessing:
-- PostgreSQL: "Subplans Removed: 0" in the plan, or 36 of 36 removed when it works.
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE external_ref = 'MIG-88213';
-- MySQL: EXPLAIN PARTITIONS shows the partitions actually opened. All 36 means
-- no pruning; and the cost is paid at open time, once per partition, per query.
EXPLAIN PARTITIONS SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE external_ref = 'MIG-88213';
-- SQL Server: a non-aligned query is legal and scans every partition. There is no
-- diagnostic in the plan; the parallel-plan behaviour is the only clue.

-- The four responses, in order of preference:
--   1. add the partition key to the index, so the query can seek inside a partition
--      (does not help pruning, but bounds the damage to one index scan per partition)
--   2. add the partition key to the query, by carrying it as a parameter
--   3. route the query to a partition, which needs the key as an argument -- no
--      portable way to express this, so it is a routing-table design decision
--   4. do not partition, if no query and no maintenance operation prunes

-- The lock-budget trap, which is the one that takes a system down rather than
-- making it slow. Every partition opened by a statement needs locks, and the
-- budget is finite and small.
--   PostgreSQL  max_locks_per_transaction = 64 * max_connections + 20 by default.
--                  A 2000-partition table under 200 connections can exhaust it.
SHOW max_locks_per_transaction;
--   MySQL       ER_TOO_MANY_PARTITIONS_ERROR (1017) once open files run out.
--   SQL Server  lock memory, and 3961/1205 symptoms rather than a clean error.

The practical discipline is to write the pruning test as an automated check: for every access pattern, assert the number of partitions touched is one. A test that asserts Subplans Removed > 0 for each pattern in the inventory catches the regression at the moment somebody adds an index with the partition column in the wrong position, which is the only way it ever happens.

-- pg_partman 5.x: the time-partitioning lifecycle, automated. Worth the extension
-- because the job it replaces -- "create the next three partitions before the
-- month starts" -- is the job that fails silently at 02:00 on a Saturday.
--
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_partman;
SELECT * FROM partman.create_parent(
    p_parent_table          := 'public.sales_order',
    p_control               := 'placed_at',
    p_type                  := 'range',
    p_interval              := '1 month',
    p_precreate             := '3 months',   -- created ahead of time
    p_start_partition       := '2026-10-01');

SELECT * FROM partman.run_maintenance_proc();          -- hourly: create ahead
SELECT * FROM partman.run_maintenance(
    p_inherit_missing_partitions := true);  -- attach tables that exist

-- Retention, as a scheduled job with a stated policy rather than a human
-- decision made once:
SELECT partman.partition_retention(
    p_parent_table     := 'public.sales_order',
    p_retention_schema := 'archive',   -- detach, do not drop
    p_keep_table       := true,
    p_retention_keep_index := 'ix_order_tenant_placed');

-- Monitor it. A missing future partition is an outage that has not started yet:
SELECT partman.show_partition_info(p_parent_table := 'public.sales_order');

4.6 Mechanisms, subpartitioning and the replicated reference table

This section separates the two words that are used interchangeably and should not be, describes the two-level partitioning scheme that gets the best of both, and covers the one piece of architecture that sharding forces onto the schema: a small set of tables that must exist identically on every shard.

PartitioningSharding
UnitOne row, routed to one partition inside one databaseOne row, routed to one shard, where a shard is a separate database on a separate host
Visible to the optimiserYes: pruning is planned, joins across partitions are plannedNo: the planner on one shard has never heard of the others
Transaction scopeAll partitions, in one local transactionOne shard locally; two or more requires two-phase commit, which is why most designs forbid it
Join across the whole data setOrdinary, planned, index-assistedImpossible in the engine. Scatter-gather in the application, or a denormalised copy
Global uniquenessEnforced by a unique constraint that spans the partitionsNot enforceable. Requires a central ID allocator, and a UUID is the usual answer
Failure domainThe database hostThe shard; one shard down is a partial outage unless the design says otherwise
Operational costA partition lifecycle jobRebalancing, cross-shard queries, per-shard migrations, a router, and N copies of every schema change
When it is the right answerThe data fits on one host, and the problem is maintenance or pruningThe data, or the write rate, or the read rate, exceeds one host — and the workload is shard-local
-- Subpartitioning: the two-level scheme that gets time-based retention and even
-- distribution at the same time.
CREATE TABLE audit_event (
    event_id     bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    tenant_id    bigint NOT NULL,
    occurred_at  timestamptz NOT NULL,
    entity_name  text NOT NULL,
    operation    text NOT NULL,
    before_state jsonb,
    after_state  jsonb,
    PRIMARY KEY (occurred_at, event_id)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (occurred_at)
       SUBPARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id) (
    PARTITION a_2026_10 FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-10-01') TO ('2026-11-01')
        SUBPARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id) (
            SUBPARTITION a_2026_10_h0, SUBPARTITION a_2026_10_h1,
            SUBPARTITION a_2026_10_h2, SUBPARTITION a_2026_10_h3),
    PARTITION a_2026_11 FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-11-01') TO ('2026-12-01')
        SUBPARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id) (
            SUBPARTITION a_2026_11_h0, SUBPARTITION a_2026_11_h1,
            SUBPARTITION a_2026_11_h2, SUBPARTITION a_2026_11_h3)
);
-- Outer: month. Retention drops one partition and takes 4 subpartitions with
-- it. Inner: 4-way hash. No subpartition can exceed 25% of its month's data even if
-- one tenant is 60% of the month, so maintenance is uniform and parallelisable.
-- Cost: 24 physical tables at 6 months of retention, and 2 levels of planning.
--
-- A DEFAULT is mandatory at every level, or an insert outside the declared
-- bounds fails at runtime instead of landing somewhere safe:
--   PostgreSQL  PARTITION a_default DEFAULT
--   MySQL       PARTITION p_max VALUES LESS THAN MAXVALUE
-- and the p_default / p_max table is the single most useful diagnostic in the whole
-- partitioned estate: any row that lands there is a row that stopped being pruned.
-- THE REFERENCE TABLE PROBLEM. Sharding turns a set of shared, near-static tables
-- into a set of copies, and every query that joins one of them is now a
-- cross-shard query. Three tiers, three treatments.

  tier 1  replicated everywhere, write through the coordinator
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  table            rows  writes/day  why it must be everywhere
  plan                12        0   every pricing read joins it; a fan-out read per
                                  order is unacceptable at any order rate
  currency           180        0   pure lookup, ISO 4217, changes twice a year
  jurisdiction        240        0   same
  feature_flag       30        2   read on every request; a stale flag is tolerable for minutes

  tier 2  replicated, asynchronously, and versioned
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  table            rows  writes/day  mechanism
  price_book      5 000      400      version column + last-writer-wins; conflicts are
                                      resolved by the next full sync, and the value on a
                                      given shard may be up to one sync interval stale
  tax_table      90 000        3      same, and the staleness window is the whole point:
                                      a tax rate that is 60 s stale on one shard is a
                                      financial inconsistency that must be logged

  tier 3  shard-local, never replicated
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  every transactional table, every audit row, every outbox row.
  The reason is not cost: it is that a replicated mutable table needs a conflict
  resolution rule, and a mutable transactional table has no correct one.

-- The consistency check that keeps tier 1 and tier 2 honest. Run it on a
-- schedule and treat a difference as an incident, because a price book
-- that disagrees between shards produces invoices that disagree with each other.
--
-- On the coordinator:
SELECT sku, SUM(unit_price_excl_tax) AS checksum
  FROM product GROUP BY sku ORDER BY sku;
-- On each shard, the same query, then compare. A cheap and total check, because
-- SUM is order-independent: a missing row changes it, a reordered row does not.
-- A count plus a sum catches every discrepancy that matters here.

-- Foreign keys across shards do not exist. The choices are:
--   1. replicate the parent to every shard (tier 1) and keep the FK locally -- this
--      is the only option that preserves the guarantee, and it restricts the
--      parent to near-static data
--   2. drop the FK and validate with a periodic cross-shard consistency job
--   3. denormalise the value into the child, with the invalidation ledger of
--      section 1.6 -- only viable if the value almost never changes

Caveats. Sharding is a decision to give up something. The things given up are cross-shard joins, cross-shard transactions, global unique constraints, global secondary indexes and a single consistent view of the data — that is, most of what a relational database is for. It is a correct decision when the write rate or the data volume genuinely exceeds one host, and an expensive one when it is made to avoid indexing or to defer a capacity conversation. A partitioned table on one host with a good index set and a rollup serves the great majority of the workloads that reach the sharding threshold.

Exercises 4

  1. Measure the correlation statistic and the average tuple size for the largest table in a real schema. Compute rows per 8 kB page, then repeat the calculation for a version of the table with its three widest columns moved to a side table. State the change in the number of rows a buffer-pool page can hold.
  2. Partition a scratch table by month on your engine. Insert a row that falls outside every declared bound and record the error. Then add the default partition and confirm the row lands there. Query the default partition’s contents to discover it, and put that query in a monitoring job.
  3. Measure the skew of a real multi-tenant table: the ratio of the largest partition to the median under a list-by-tenant scheme. Then design two alternatives that preserve per-tenant separability, and state what each one costs in queries that must now cross a boundary.
  4. Convert a real table to two-level range-then-hash subpartitioning. Record the planning time of a representative query before and after, and the wall-clock time of the retention job before and after. Both numbers are the justification you will need.
  5. Take a table in a schema you know and write the full cross-shard design for it: which tables are tier 1, tier 2 and tier 3, which queries become cross-shard, and which of them cannot be answered correctly at all. If a query cannot be answered, say what denormalisation would be required.

5 · Indexing Strategy

5.1 The index catalogue and the workload each fits5.2 The clustered key and what it drives5.3 Composite index column order5.4 Partial, expression and covering indexes5.5 The write-cost ledger5.6 Index-only scans, the visibility map and duplicates

An index is a second copy of the data, ordered differently, that is maintained on every write and read only by the queries that need that ordering. This chapter covers the index types available, the two decisions that constrain everything else, the column-order rules, the three specialised forms, and the accounting that every index set must be able to produce.

5.1 The index catalogue and the workload each fits

Every engine offers a small number of index structures and each serves a different data distribution. Choosing correctly starts from the distribution of the data being indexed, not from the query, because the query is only the reason the index is being asked for.

IndexPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025Fits this workload
B-tree (default)Yes; 8 kB pages, 3–4 levels at 108 rowsYes; the only general-purpose structure in InnoDBYes; clustered and nonclusteredEquality, ranges, prefixes, ordered output, joins. The default answer when the cardinality is moderate and the correlation with physical order is low
HashYes; WAL-logged and crash-safe from 15MEMORY engine only — not InnoDBNone — hash is a join and hash index operator, not a structureEquality lookups on a very wide key, where the B-tree comparison cost is significant and only equality is ever needed. Rare
BRIN (block range)YesNoNoColumns whose physical order is strongly correlated with value: timestamps on append-only tables, monotonically increasing ids, and nothing else
GiST / SP-GiSTYes; generalised search trees, KNN, exclusion constraintsNoSpatial and graph onlyRanges and intervals with overlap predicates, geometric and network data, and the only portable way to enforce "no two intervals overlap for this key"
GIN (inverted)Yes; arrays, jsonb, full-text, trigramNo; FULLTEXT is a separate structureFull-text and XML indexesContainment queries: "attribute contains this value", "the document mentions this phrase". Write-heavy, with a pending list to amortise updates
ColumnstoreNone in coreNone in the base engineYes, clustered and nonclusteredLarge scans, low write rate, aggregations over many columns. Covered in section 4.1
Covering (INCLUDE)Yes; enables index-only scanNo INCLUDE; a composite index is the mechanismYes; on nonclustered indexesQueries that need a narrow set of columns from a wide row, and which would otherwise pay a lookup per row
Partial / filteredYes; arbitrary immutable predicateNo; emulated with a generated columnYes; nonclustered only, and a restricted grammarSmall subsets of a large table: unpublished outbox rows, soft-deleted rows, active subscriptions, one tenant’s hot partition
Expression / functionalYes; any immutable expressionYes, from 8.0.13; requires a parenthesised expressionVia a PERSISTED computed columnCase-insensitive lookups, month and date truncations, computed predicates that are not the column itself
DescendingYes, from 11Yes, from 8.0Yes"Most recent first" without a sort, where the leading columns are equal
Bitmap / hash join acceleratorsNot a persistent structureBitmap indexes on the MEMORY engine onlyColumnstore segments serve the roleLow-cardinality columns in a data warehouse fact table. Not an OLTP tool
-- BRIN, and the one precondition without which it is worthless: physical order must
-- already match the value order. BRIN stores one summary per BLOCK RANGE (128
-- pages by default), not one entry per row, which is why it is measured in kilobytes.
CREATE INDEX ix_audit_occurred_brin ON audit_event USING brin (occurred_at)
    WITH (pages_per_range = 32, autosummarize = on);
--
-- The failure: audit_event rows are inserted in tenant batches, so a block range
-- contains 4 months of interleaved timestamps, every summary overlaps every other,
-- and the index is 200 kB of pure overhead that returns every row range.
-- The fix is not a different index. It is a different physical layout -- which is
-- exactly the coupling described in section 4.2.
--
-- GIN, and the pending list that makes it viable for a write-heavy column.
CREATE INDEX ix_audit_after_gin ON audit_event USING gin (after_state jsonb_path_ops);
-- jsonb_ops  : supports @>, @?, key existence and containment. Default. Larger.
-- jsonb_path_ops: supports only @>. Roughly a third of the size, ~3x faster to
--               update, and it is the right choice when the only query is containment.
--
-- The write cost of a GIN index is hidden, not absent. Updates go to a pending
-- list, which is fast; the pending list is drained in batches by autovacuum, at which
-- point the index does the expensive work all at once. An under-vacuumed GIN index
-- is both slow to query and slow to update. Monitor pending pages, not just index size.
SELECT * FROM gin_pending_list_limit;   -- 4 MB by default

-- GiST as an exclusion constraint: the portable way to say "no double booking".
ALTER TABLE oncall_shift ADD CONSTRAINT ex_no_overlap
    EXCLUDE USING GIST (engine_id WITH =, shift_range WITH &&);

Before creating any index, run the query it is meant to help through EXPLAIN and record the row estimate and the plan shape. Then create it and run the same command again. An index that does not change the plan was not needed, and an index that changes the plan for a query nobody runs is a liability. The measurement takes two minutes and is the cheapest index work there is.

5.2 The clustered key and what it drives

On SQL Server and MySQL the primary key is the physical order, and every secondary index therefore carries it. That single fact determines the width of every index, the cost of every lookup, and whether an update is a same-page operation or a page split.

-- SQL Server: the row locator is the clustering key, so this multiplies.
--
--   clustered index  (order_id bigint)                  key 8 B
--   nonclustered     (tenant_id, placed_at) INCLUDE     key 8 + 8 = 16 B
--                              (status, total_excl_tax)
--
--   with a uniqueidentifier clustering key:
--   nonclustered     key 16 + 16 = 32 B  -- double the width,
--                              and every row not covered needs a random seek
--                              into a fragmented clustered B-tree
--
-- The arithmetic that makes the case, at 10^8 rows on 8 kB pages:
--   16-byte locator: ~62 index entries per page after the internal levels
--    8-byte locator: ~124 entries per page.  Halved page count, halved I/O,
--    halved buffer-pool footprint, and it is a permanent saving.
--
-- The corollary: a clustered index on a mutable, non-monotonic column is the
-- worst physical design available on this engine, because an UPDATE becomes a
-- delete and an insert into the middle of the tree.

-- The heap alternative, which is a real choice and not an oversight:
--   heap             no clustering key, no row-order cost, a RID stays stable
--                   until a variable-length update forwards it, which leaves a stub
--                   and a forwarding pointer
--   heap             no index-only scan is possible; a nonclustered lookup uses the RID
--                   and a forwarding pointer is a second I/O
--   heap             SQL Server will not let a foreign key reference it, so a heap table
--                   cannot be the parent of any foreign key
-- Choose a heap for an append-only table with no foreign key pointing at it, and a
-- clustered index for everything else.

-- SQL Server: make the choice explicit and check it.
-- CREATE UNIQUE CLUSTERED INDEX cx_sales_order ON sales_order(order_id);
-- CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX ix_order_tenant_placed ON sales_order(tenant_id, placed_at DESC)
--     INCLUDE (status, total_excl_tax, currency) WITH (FILLFACTOR = 90, SORT_IN_TEMPDB = OFF);
SELECT t.name AS table_name, i.name AS index_name,
       i.type_desc, -- CLUSTERED / NONCLUSTERED / HEAP
       i.fill_factor, WITH (STATS) = ISNULL(i.WITH (STATISTICS), 0),
       ps.avg_fragmentation_in_percent, ps.page_count
  FROM sys.indexes i
  JOIN sys.tables t ON t.object_id = i.object_id
  LEFT JOIN (
       SELECT object_id, index_id, avg_fragmentation_in_percent, page_count
         FROM sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats(DB_ID(), NULL, NULL, NULL, 'LIMITED')) ps
         ON ps.object_id = i.object_id AND ps.index_id = i.index_id
 WHERE t.name = 'sales_order';
-- MySQL / InnoDB: the same coupling, enforced by the storage engine itself.
-- There is no heap; the table IS the clustered index on the primary key.
--
-- Secondary index entry:  (indexed columns..., PRIMARY KEY columns...)
-- so the primary key width is added to every secondary index, and a secondary
-- lookup ends in a primary-key seek.
--
-- The classic, and it is still the most common InnoDB design mistake found in
-- production: a 36-char CHAR(36) UUID primary key, which makes every secondary
-- index 36 bytes wider per entry and puts a random insert in the middle of the
-- clustered index on every row.
CREATE TABLE api_key (
    api_key_id  char(36) NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,          -- 36 B clustered key
    tenant_id   bigint NOT NULL,
    key_hash    binary(32) NOT NULL,
    created_at  datetime(3) NOT NULL
);
SELECT name, seq_in_index, column_name
  FROM information_schema.statistics
 WHERE table_name = 'api_key' ORDER BY index_name, seq_in_index;
-- ix_tenant on (tenant_id, api_key_id) -- the 36-byte key rides along on every
-- entry, and it is useless there: it is not a filter, it is a locator.

-- The fix, and it is a table rebuild rather than an ALTER:
CREATE TABLE api_key_new (
    api_key_id  bigint NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    key_hash    binary(32) NOT NULL,
    tenant_id   bigint NOT NULL,
    created_at  datetime(3) NOT NULL,
    UNIQUE KEY uq_key_hash (key_hash),              -- the natural key, indexed
    KEY ix_tenant (tenant_id)
) ENGINE=InnoDB;
-- gh-ost or pt-online-schema-change for a live table, and a byte-identical
-- column order between the two tables or the ALTER is a full copy.
--
-- Storing the UUID compactly, which is the other half of the fix:
--   binary(16)  16 bytes  UUID_TO_BIN(u, 1) with a swap flag for time ordering
--   char(36)    36 bytes  what people write by default

-- PostgreSQL: the coupling is absent, which is a genuine advantage and worth
-- knowing when comparing engines.
--   the B-tree stores (ctid, key). The key does not need to be in the heap, and the
--   heap is not ordered by it. The consequence is that a non-monotonic key costs page
--   splits in the index but not in the table, and a random key has no effect on any
--   other index. The price is the extra indirection on every index-to-heap visit.

Decision. The reference model uses a monotonic bigint identity as the primary key on all three engines, and a separate uuid unique column as the customer-visible identifier. The clustered key is therefore 8 bytes, append-only, immutable and correlated with insertion order — every one of the criteria from section 4.2 — and the 16-byte identifier is paid for on exactly one index that exists to enforce its uniqueness.

A cheap audit for MySQL and SQL Server: compare the average size of a nonclustered or secondary index against the sum of its declared key column widths. An index that is materially wider than its columns plus the primary key has a row locator problem, and the fix is a narrower clustering key, not a smaller index.

5.3 Composite index column order

A composite index is an ordering, and an ordering is only usable from the left. Column order therefore determines not merely which queries an index serves but how much of it they can use, and it interacts with the sort direction, the collation and the partitioning key in ways that are worth writing down.

-- The rule, in one sentence: equality columns first, then one range column, then
-- anything that exists only to be carried (INCLUDE). After the first range column
-- the index is not usable for further narrowing, only for ordering.
--
-- AP-01: latest 50 orders for one tenant.
--   WHERE tenant_id = ? ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50
--   -> (tenant_id, placed_at DESC) and the sort disappears entirely.
--   -> (placed_at DESC, tenant_id) is useless: the leading column is not constrained.

-- AP-03: orders for a tenant in a date range, keyset-paginated.
--   WHERE tenant_id = ? AND placed_at >= ? AND placed_at < ?
--      ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50
--   -> the same index serves it. Both the range and the order use the same column.
--
-- The one that is frequently got wrong: two range columns.
--   WHERE tenant_id = ? AND placed_at >= ? AND order_id > ?
--   -> (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id) uses tenant_id and placed_at; order_id is
--      carried as part of the key so that the keyset predicate can be evaluated
--      inside the index, but it does not narrow the search further.

CREATE INDEX ix_order_tenant_placed ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC, order_id)
    INCLUDE (status, total_excl_tax, currency);
-- Three INCLUDE columns, 0 index seeks into the table, verified by section 5.6.

-- The two things that change the answer, and both are collation.
--
-- (1) Collation. A case-insensitive index on text is a different ordering from the
--     case-sensitive one, and a query with a different collation than the index
--     cannot use it. MySQL's utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci and MyISAM-era pad behaviour are
--     the usual source of "the index is there and the plan ignores it".
-- (2) Direction. (a, b DESC) serves ORDER BY a, b DESC and ORDER BY a, but not
--     ORDER BY b DESC, a. Mixed-direction keys are read left to right and a
--     backwards scan gives every column the opposite direction, so only all-ASC
--     or all-DESC orderings get a backward scan; mixed orders are forward-only.

-- The leftmost-prefix rule, and PostgreSQL 18's relaxation of it.
--   WHERE kind = 'card' AND tenant_id = 42
--   with (kind, tenant_id): usable, both are equality.
--   with (tenant_id, kind): usable, and better if the tenant is more selective.
--   with (kind, created_at) and no condition on created_at: the leading column has a
--   few distinct values, and since PostgreSQL 18 a btree skip scan can still return
--   a usable path by scanning once per distinct leading value. On 17 and earlier
--   this is a full index scan of the whole index for a highly selective query.
--   That is a real behavioural change to check on the target instance, and it
--   does not remove the rule: skip scan helps a low-cardinality leading column,
--   and still does not help an unconstrained one.
-- The composite index that is mandatory and is very often missing: the child side
-- of every foreign key. Its absence does not produce a wrong answer, so nothing
-- fails until a DELETE on the parent runs in production.
--
-- What happens with no index on app_user(tenant_id):
--   PostgreSQL  the RI trigger on app_user must check for referencing rows. It
--               cannot use an index, so it sequential-scans sales_order while
--               holding a row lock on the tenant row it is deleting. A 200 ms scan
--               on a 10^7-row table turns into a 200 ms lock on a hot row, and
--               every concurrent insert for that tenant queues behind it.
--   MySQL       the same shape, through the InnoDB foreign-key constraint check.
--   SQL Server  the DELETE takes a shared lock on the child table and scans it; on
--               a large table that is a table lock by another name, and the pattern
--               is a well-known cause of blocking chains.
--
-- PostgreSQL does not enforce this for you. There is a diagnostic extension, and
-- it should be in the migration pipeline rather than in a wiki:
--   CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS supabase_vamap;  -- or write the query below
SELECT conrelid::regclass AS child_table,
       conname,
       a.attname AS fk_column,
       NOT EXISTS (
           SELECT 1 FROM pg_index i
            WHERE i.indrelid = conrelid
              AND i.indisvalid
              AND (SELECT array_agg(a2.attname ORDER BY k.ord)
                    FROM unnest(i.indkey) WITH ORDINALITY AS k(attnum, ord)
                    JOIN pg_attribute a2 ON a2.attrelid = conrelid AND a2.attnum = k.attnum)[1] = a.attname)
       ) AS unindexed_fk
  FROM pg_constraint c
  JOIN unnest(c.conkey) WITH ORDINALITY AS k(attnum, ord) ON true
  JOIN pg_attribute a ON a.attrelid = c.conrelid AND a.attnum = k.attnum
 WHERE c.contype = 'f' AND c.connamespace = 'public'::regnamespace;

-- The corresponding assertion in the test suite, which is the form that
-- actually gets enforced: every foreign key column must be the leading column
-- of some index on the child table.

For a table with a small number of access patterns, write the indexes out as a table with the column order justified per column: tenant_id: equality, leading; placed_at: range and sort; order_id: carried for keyset pagination. When a column’s justification cannot be written, that column is the first candidate for removal, and removing it makes every entry in the index narrower.

5.4 Partial, expression and covering indexes

Three index forms solve three specific problems: a partial index makes a small subset cheap, an expression index makes a transformed value cheap, and a covering index makes a row fetch unnecessary. Each has a precondition that is checked at creation time and violated silently at query time.

-- PARTIAL. The right answer whenever a query filters on a small, stable subset and
-- the subset is a large fraction of the writes: a queue, a soft-delete flag, a
-- "current" marker on a temporal table, a status machine.
CREATE INDEX ix_outbox_unpublished ON outbox (created_at, outbox_id)
    WHERE published_at IS NULL;
--
--   size: proportional to the unpublished rows, which is a few thousand, not 10^8
--   write cost: an index entry is written only while the row matches, so the
--              publish UPDATE removes the entry instead of adding one -- which
--              is a partial-index-specific behaviour worth knowing
--   precondition: the predicate must be provably implied by the query's WHERE.
--              WHERE published_at IS NULL exactly, not COALESCE(published_at, '') = ''
--   immutability: the predicate may not reference a volatile function. A partial
--              index on now() is rejected, correctly.

-- SQL Server filtered index, and its four documented restrictions:
-- CREATE NONCLUSTERED INDEX fx_sub_pending ON subscription(period_end)
--     WHERE status IN ('trialling','active','past_due') WITH (ONLINE = ON);
--   1. nonclustered only
--   2. the filter is a restricted grammar: simple comparisons, IN, and no subqueries,
--      no functions on the filtered column, no cross-table predicates
--   3. it cannot satisfy a foreign key, and cannot be the target of a view index
--   4. the filtered column becomes non-nullable for the purposes of the CHECK
--      constraint SQL Server adds to validate the filter

-- MySQL: no partial indexes. The emulation costs a real column, and it is worth it
-- for a queue because the win is a 10^8-to-10^3 reduction in index size.
ALTER TABLE outbox
  ADD COLUMN unpub_key timestamp(3) GENERATED ALWAYS AS (
      CASE WHEN published_at IS NULL THEN created_at END) STORED,
  ADD INDEX ix_unpub (unpub_key, outbox_id);
-- NULLs are not stored in a MySQL secondary index, so published rows produce no
-- entry at all: the same effect as a partial index, with 8 bytes per row and a
-- generation cost on every write.

-- EXPRESSION. For a predicate that is a transformation of the column rather than
-- the column itself. The precondition is that the expression is IMMUTABLE and that
-- the query contains the identical expression.
CREATE INDEX ix_user_email_lower ON app_user (lower(email));
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_user_email_lower ON app_user (tenant_id, lower(email));
-- Both the filter and the index must agree on the case-folding, or the index is
-- silently unused. A unique index on lower(email) plus a separate unique index on
-- email is a contradiction waiting to be discovered: it enforces two different rules
-- about what makes two users the same.

-- THE TRAP: timestamptz in an expression index. This statement is REJECTED,
-- and the reason is the most instructive in this section:
--   CREATE INDEX ON sales_order (date_trunc('month', placed_at))
--   ERROR: functions in index expression must be marked IMMUTABLE
--   date_trunc(text, timestamptz) is STABLE, not IMMUTABLE, because the result
--   depends on the session TimeZone setting. The same expression means different
--   months for a UTC session and a Manila session, so it cannot be indexed.
--
-- The correct forms, in order of preference:
--   1. store a DATE column for the calendar month and index that (section 3.5)
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN order_month date GENERATED ALWAYS AS (
       (CAST(placed_at AT TIME ZONE 'UTC'))::date) STORED;  -- explicit zone: now immutable
--   2. index the raw timestamptz and truncate in the query, letting the range
--      predicate (>= month_start AND < next_month) do the work
--   3. for a query that really is "group by month", use the rollup of section 3.5
-- COVERING. INCLUDE columns extend the leaf without extending the search key, so
-- the index is scanned in key order and the table is never visited.
--
-- AP-01 becomes 50 index entries and 1 page read, instead of 50 index entries and
-- 50 heap visits. On a 240-byte row that is the difference between one page and
-- twenty-five, and it is the single largest read-path win available.
CREATE INDEX ix_order_tenant_placed
    ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)
    INCLUDE (order_id, status, total_excl_tax, currency);
--
-- The rules for INCLUDE columns, all three engines:
--   * they may not be used in a WHERE clause to narrow the search
--   * every INCLUDE column is present on every index entry: 4 included columns at 16
--     bytes is 64 bytes per entry, and that is a real width cost per row, forever
--   * including a wide column (text, jsonb, bytea) is usually a mistake; if it is
--     large enough to be TOASTed it cannot be included at all
--   * including the primary key is redundant on MySQL (it is already there) and on
--     SQL Server (it is the row locator)
--
-- PostgreSQL: INCLUDE columns must be able to be returned from the index, which
-- excludes expressions and is another reason not to include computed values.
-- MySQL: no INCLUDE. The equivalent is appending the columns to the composite key,
-- which makes them part of the search key and therefore sortable and filterable --
-- strictly more capable, and strictly wider for the same reason.
-- SQL Server: INCLUDE on nonclustered indexes. On a clustered index there is nothing to
-- cover, because the index is the table.

-- The three rules that decide whether covering is worth it, and all three must hold:
--   1. the query returns many rows from this index (a LIMIT of 50 is already enough)
--   2. the included columns are narrow: 8-32 bytes each, and there are few of them
--   3. the heap access it replaces is genuinely random, i.e. the table is not in the
--      same physical order as the index
-- If the table is clustered on the same key as the index, an INCLUDE column saves a
-- 4 kB page read, which is nothing, and costs 8 bytes per row forever.

Caveats. MySQL’s generated-column emulation of a partial index changes the table’s shape: the column is visible in SELECT *, in the application model, and in every backup. Hide it by convention, name it with a prefix that signals it is derived, and exclude it from the ORM mapping. The alternative — a covering index on the full table — is not a substitute: it is the size of the table, not the size of the subset.

5.5 The write-cost ledger

Every index is paid for on every write, in bytes, in log volume, in page splits and in maintenance time. The ledger below prices the index set of a hot table and shows how to compute it on the target engine rather than estimating it.

  LEDGER -- sales_order, 240 B/row, 1 000 inserts/s, 200 updates/s, 10^8 rows

  object                     entry B   entries/write   B/s written   note
  ─────────────────────────  ───────   ─────────────   ───────────   ─────────────────────
  heap                        240      1.0            240 000       the row itself
  pk_sales_order               8      1.0              8 000       monotonic: no page splits
  ix_order_tenant_placed      24      1.0             24 000       monotonic within a tenant
  ix_order_tenant_placed_gin  16      1.0             16 000       (none: this table has no GIN)
  ix_order_external_ref       22      1.0             22 000       unique, random values
  ix_order_status              9      1.0              9 000       status is mutable: HOT impossible
  ix_subscription_id           8      1.0              8 000       mostly NULL
  ─────────────────────────  ───────   ─────────────   ───────────
  total                      319      5.0            319 000       1.33x the row size

  The multiplier that is easy to miss: for an index insert into a page that is NOT
  already in the WAL, the engine writes the ENTIRE 8 kB page into the log. A random
  index insert therefore costs 8 kB of log, not 24 B. At 1 000 random index inserts
  per second into a 100 MB working set, that is 8 MB/s of log for 24 kB/s of data.

  This is why the total above is optimistic by a large factor, and why the correct
  measure of index write cost is log volume, not row size.

  UPDATE accounting, which is worse than INSERT accounting:
    200 updates/s, all of them SET status
      heap: new 240 B version, old 240 B becomes dead, 48 000 B/s
      pk_sales_order:  unchanged, HOT-eligible if fillfactor leaves room
      ix_order_status: the indexed column changed, so a new entry is written even
                       though the new value is one of five. 1 800 B/s
      ix_order_tenant_placed: unchanged, HOT-eligible
    and a 200 updates/s workload on a table with 0 free space per page is a page
    split rate of 200/s, each of which writes a full page and leaves the index 50%
    fragmented.
-- Measuring the ledger instead of estimating it. PostgreSQL.
--
-- (1) Row size, and what each column contributes to it.
SELECT pg_column_size(sales_order) AS bytes_per_row,
       -- add and subtract a NOT NULL column and re-measure to attribute it
       SELECT attname, SUM(pg_column_size(attname::text)) AS name_bytes
  FROM pg_attribute WHERE attrelid = 'sales_order'::regclass AND attnum > 0
 GROUP BY attname ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 8;

-- (2) Index entry size, and therefore the write cost per row.
SELECT indexrelname,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)) AS size,
       ROUND(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)::numeric / GREATEST(s.n_live_tup,1)) AS bytes_per_entry
  FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s
 WHERE relname = 'sales_order'
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(indexrelid) DESC;

-- (3) The multiplier that matters: total bytes per row across heap and all
--     indexes. Above about 2.0 the table is paying more for its indexes than
--     for its data, and every read has to compete for the same buffer pool.
SELECT ROUND(pg_total_relation_size('sales_order')::numeric
             / (SELECT SUM(n_live_tup) FROM pg_stat_user_tables
                WHERE relname = 'sales_order'), 1) AS bytes_per_row_total,
       ROUND((pg_total_relation_size('sales_order')
             - pg_relation_size('sales_order'))::numeric
             / pg_relation_size('sales_order'), 2) AS index_to_heap_ratio
  FROM pg_class WHERE relname = 'sales_order';

-- (4) The number that is actually scarce: writes per second, and the WAL
--     they generate. pg_current_wal_lsn() deltas over 60 s.
SELECT pg_current_wal_lsn(),
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), '0/0')) AS total_wal;
--   WAL generation rate  = delta over the interval
--   bytes per insert      = delta / inserts, from pg_stat_database.xact_commit
--   replication bandwidth = delta * (replica count) x retention window
-- The third line is the one capacity planning actually needs, and it is why an
-- excessive index set is an availability decision and not merely a performance one.
-- MySQL: the same ledger, from performance_schema.
SELECT object_name, index_name, count_star,
       ROUND(sum_timer_wait / 1000000000000, 1) AS total_seconds,
       ROUND(avg_timer_wait / 1000000000, 3) AS avg_ms
  FROM performance_schema.table_io_waits_summary_by_index_usage
 WHERE object_schema = 'contoso' AND object_name = 'sales_order'
   AND count_star > 0
 ORDER BY sum_timer_wait DESC;
-- Insert cost is visible here as INSERT_INDEX events. An index whose INSERT
-- timer exceeds its SELECT timer is costing more than it returns.
SELECT index_name, count_star,
       ROUND(sum_timer_wait / 1000000000000, 1) AS seconds
  FROM performance_schema.events_statements_summary_by_index_usage
 WHERE object_name = 'sales_order' AND event_name = 'insert/index'
 GROUP BY index_name ORDER BY seconds DESC;

-- SQL Server: per-index write volume, which is the one the ledger is about.
SELECT OBJECT_NAME(us.object_id) AS table_name,
       us.index_id, us.user_updates, us.user_lookups, us.user_seeks, us.user_scans,
       us.user_lookups + us.user_seeks + us.user_scans AS total_reads
  FROM sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats us
 WHERE us.database_id = DB_ID() AND us.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.sales_order')
 ORDER BY us.user_updates DESC;
-- user_updates with total_reads = 0 is a drop candidate. user_lookups high and
-- user_seeks zero is an index that is only ever being used as a lookup target,
-- which usually means it is redundant with a covering index.

Decision. The reference model keeps four indexes on sales_order: the primary key, (tenant_id, placed_at DESC) INCLUDE (...) for AP-01 and AP-03, a unique index on external_ref because the migration needs it, and (subscription_id) because a foreign key and a reporting join need it. ix_order_status is not created: no access pattern filters on status alone, and a mutable indexed column disables HOT updates on the entire table, which is the most expensive item in the ledger above.

Caveats. pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan = 0 does not mean "unused". Statistics are cumulative since the last reset, they are not persisted across a statistics reset, and on a standby they reflect the standby’s own activity. An index used once a month by a finance report reads as unused to a daily check. Sample over a complete business cycle before dropping anything, and check the application and the reports rather than the counter. On a table where the optimiser statistics have not been analysed after a bulk load, an index can also read as unused because every query chose a sequential scan.

5.6 Index-only scans, the visibility map and duplicates

An index-only scan is the largest read-path optimisation available on a row store, and it is conditional on a piece of bookkeeping that is maintained by vacuum and by nothing else. This section reproduces the mechanism, shows the settings that decide whether it happens, and covers the two housekeeping tasks — duplicate detection and safe removal — that belong in the same review.

-- The mechanism. An index-only scan reads only the index. It is legal only if every
-- row it would return is visible to the current snapshot -- i.e. if every heap page
-- it would need to visit is marked ALL_VISIBLE in the visibility map.
--
-- The visibility map is one bit per heap page, so 2 kB per 16 000 000 pages.
-- It is set by VACUUM, never by an INSERT, and cleared by any UPDATE that creates a
-- dead tuple. So immediately after a bulk load no page is all-visible, and the
-- "index-only" scan performs a heap visit for every row -- reported honestly by the
-- planner as "Heap Fetches".

-- Reproduce it. This is the whole explanation in six statements.
CREATE TABLE ios_test (id bigint PRIMARY KEY, tenant_id int, a text, b text);
INSERT INTO ios_test SELECT g, g % 200, 'a' || g, 'b' || g FROM generate_series(1,500000) g;
VACUUM (ANALYZE) ios_test;                       -- sets the visibility map
CREATE INDEX ix_ios_covering ON ios_test (tenant_id, id) INCLUDE (a, b);

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT tenant_id, id, a, b FROM ios_test WHERE tenant_id = 42;
--   Index Only Scan using ix_ios_covering
--     Heap Fetches: 0        <-- the ideal: 1 index page, 0 heap pages

-- Now dirty every page, and watch it disappear.
UPDATE ios_test SET a = a WHERE id % 2 = 0;              -- half the rows, every page touched
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT tenant_id, id, a, b FROM ios_test WHERE tenant_id = 42;
--   Heap Fetches: 1250      <-- every row is now a heap visit, and the plan is honest
VACUUM ios_test;                                -- and back to 0

-- Inspect the map directly. The pg_visibility extension is the only way to see
-- per-page state, and it is the tool for answering "why is this not an index-only
-- scan" rather than guessing.
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_visibility;
SELECT all_visible, all_frozen, COUNT(*) AS pages
  FROM pg_visibility_map('ios_test')
 GROUP BY ALL;
-- The autovacuum setting that decides whether this works in production. This is
-- the most consequential default in PostgreSQL for a large, frequently updated
-- table, and it is scale-factor based, so it is relative to the table size.
--
--   autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.2     vacuum when 20% of rows are dead
--   autovacuum_vacuum_threshold    = 50      plus 50 rows
--   autovacuum_vacuum_insert_scale_factor = 0.2   vacuum to set the visibility map
--                                     = 0      after an INSERT, not just an UPDATE
--
-- On a 10^8-row table: 20 000 000 dead tuples before anything happens. At 200
-- updates/s that is 28 hours of unvacuumed pages, during which every index-only
-- scan degrades to a plain index scan with a heap visit per row. The table grows
-- by 20% in the meantime, and the database's own index is not being used by the
-- database's own queries.

ALTER TABLE sales_order SET (
    autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor  = 0.01,     -- 1% of 10^8 = 1 000 000 rows
    autovacuum_vacuum_threshold     = 5000,
    autovacuum_vacuum_insert_scale_factor = 0.02,  -- vacuum to set the map
    autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.005,    -- statistics matter more often
    autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay    = 0);        -- do not throttle; the cost limit
                                                     --   is the throttle
--
-- Monitor the gap. n_dead_tup divided by the threshold is how close the table
-- is to being unvacuumed, and a rising value on a hot table is the single most
-- predictive operational signal in PostgreSQL.
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       ROUND(100.0 * n_dead_tup / NULLIF(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 2) AS dead_pct,
       last_autovacuum, ROUND(EXTRACT(epoch FROM now() - last_autovacuum) / 3600, 1) AS hours_since
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 WHERE n_dead_tup > 10000
 ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC;
-- DUPLICATE AND REDUNDANT INDEX DETECTION. The three built-in answers.
--
-- PostgreSQL: no built-in view, and the pg_duplicate_indexes extension
-- (or the query below) is the answer. Compare the key column list as an array:
SELECT s.relname AS table_name, s.indexrelname AS index_name,
       ix.indkey::text AS key_columns, ix.indclass, ix.indpred IS NOT NULL AS partial,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid)) AS size
  FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s
  JOIN pg_index ix ON ix.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
 WHERE ix.indisvalid AND NOT ix.indisprimary
 ORDER BY s.relname, s.indexrelname;
-- The redundancy rules, applied by eye to the list above. Each of these is a real
-- index set that ships.
--
-- (a) Exact duplicate: same table, same key columns, same order, same predicate.
--     One of the two is pure write cost. PostgreSQL even has a CREATE INDEX
--     variant that refuses to create one, so this is usually an ORM artefact.
--
-- (b) Leftmost prefix: (tenant_id, placed_at DESC) makes (tenant_id) redundant.
--     The single-column index can serve every query the composite can -- for the
--     seek on tenant_id alone, the composite is a superset of the work. Drop it.
--     Exception: if the composite is a covering index, the narrow one may be
--     narrower still, and a smaller index is worth keeping for a hot lookup.
--
-- (c) Included columns duplicate the key: INCLUDE (status) where status is already
--     in the key. Pure waste.
--
-- (d) Partial subsumed by a fuller index: WHERE published_at IS NULL is covered
--     by a full index on (created_at) for a read, but not for a write. The rule is:
--     a partial index may be dropped if no query needs the narrower size.
--
-- (e) FK-only index where the PK already covers it: an index on a column that is
--     already the second column of another index is redundant for the FK check only
--     if it is the LEADING column there. (tenant_id, placed_at) does not serve a
--     foreign-key check on tenant_id as a seek -- the planner will scan it.

-- MySQL: sys.schema_redundant_indexes and sys.schema_unused_indexes already
-- implement rules (a), (b) and (e) with a documented justification column.
SELECT * FROM sys.schema_redundant_indexes WHERE table_schema = 'contoso';
SELECT * FROM sys.schema_unused_indexes WHERE table_schema = 'contoso';

-- SQL Server: the detection is a group-by over the key column list. Build the
-- signature, then find signatures that are prefixes of others.
WITH sig AS (
    SELECT i.object_id, i.index_id, i.name,
           STUFF((SELECT ',' + c.name
                    FROM sys.index_columns ic
                    JOIN sys.columns c ON c.object_id = ic.object_id AND c.column_id = ic.column_id
                   WHERE ic.object_id = i.object_id AND ic.index_id = i.index_id
                     AND ic.is_included_column = 0
                   ORDER BY ic.key_ordinal
                    FOR XML PATH, TYPE).value('.', 'nvarchar(max)'),
                    1, 2) AS key_sig
      FROM sys.indexes i
     WHERE i.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.sales_order') AND i.type <> 0)
SELECT a.name AS candidate, b.name AS covers_it
  FROM sig a JOIN sig b
    ON a.object_id = b.object_id AND a.index_id <> b.index_id
   AND b.key_sig LIKE a.key_sig + ',%';
-- Removing an index without an outage. All three are online in the sense that
-- they do not hold an exclusive lock for the duration, and all three have a
-- caveat that is the reason to think before running them.
DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY ix_order_status;         -- PostgreSQL: no ACCESS EXCLUSIVE,
                                                     -- but cannot run in a transaction,
                                                     -- leaves an INVALID index behind if
                                                     -- it fails, and waits for all
                                                     -- transactions using the old plan
--
-- MySQL: online by default for InnoDB secondary indexes.
ALTER TABLE sales_order DROP INDEX ix_order_status, ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;
--
-- SQL Server: ONLINE = ON is Enterprise and later. On Standard the same
-- operation takes a Sch-M lock and blocks every query on the table for its
-- whole duration -- which is why "drop the index during a quiet period" is
-- still the standard advice for Standard deployments.
DROP INDEX ix_order_status ON dbo.sales_order WITH (ONLINE = ON);

-- The safe protocol for a removal, whatever the engine:
--   1. disable the index rather than dropping it, if the engine offers that
--      (SQL Server: ALTER INDEX ... DISABLE; MySQL 8.0+: ALTER INDEX ... INVISIBLE)
--   2. wait a full business cycle, and compare plans, not just latency
--   3. only then drop it, and only if a plan regression appeared nowhere
-- PostgreSQL has no "disable", and the accepted substitute is the opposite
-- trick: ADD the index CONCURRENTLY, note the new name, and then drop the old one.

Applies to. Index-only scans are a PostgreSQL term for a specific planner behaviour. MySQL’s covering-index optimisation avoids the primary-key lookup by including every needed column in the secondary index, which achieves the same I/O result without a visibility map and without a vacuum dependency — at the price of a permanently wider index. SQL Server’s INCLUDE columns avoid the lookup into a clustered index in the same way, and again there is no visibility map. The vacuum coupling described above is a PostgreSQL-specific consequence of separating the heap from the index, and it is the price of that separation.

Caveats. An index-only scan is not automatically a fast query: the visibility-map lookup is a second random page read per index page, and a small result set over a large index can be slower with it. The planner chooses it only when it believes the map is mostly set, and that belief is derived from the relallvisible statistic — which is stale if vacuum has not run. This is the one place in PostgreSQL where an inaccurate statistic produces a systematically wrong plan rather than a marginally wrong one, and it is fixed by the autovacuum settings above rather than by a plan hint.

Exercises 5

  1. Build the write-cost ledger for the largest table in a real schema, using the measurement queries above. Identify the index whose removal would reduce bytes written per row by the most, and check every access pattern before proposing it.
  2. Demonstrate the random-key penalty on your engine with the pgstattuple experiment from section 2.3, or its equivalent: record the index size, the deleted-page count and the buffer count for a range scan at 2 000 000 rows, for a sequential key, a random key and a time-ordered key.
  3. Run the foreign-key index audit against a real schema. Produce the list of unindexed foreign key columns, then for one of them, EXPLAIN a parent DELETE with and without the index present and compare the plans and the row counts examined.
  4. Create an expression index over a timestamptz expression and record the error. Then create the three correct alternatives and EXPLAIN a monthly aggregate against each. Report which one the optimiser picks and why.
  5. For one query in a real schema, build a covering index and measure the buffer count before and after with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS). Compute the break-even point: the number of rows at which the width saving on the index pays for the included columns' permanent cost. State whether the query’s cardinality is above or below it.

6 · Data Types and Storage Economics

6.1 Integer width, alignment and identifier types6.2 Money as decimal: a worked failure6.3 Text, varchar and the inline threshold6.4 Timestamp semantics and the timezone trap6.5 Semi-structured data, and enums versus lookups6.6 Compression, fillfactor and the size ceiling

A column’s declared type is a permanent decision about storage width, comparison semantics, index behaviour and migration cost. This chapter works through the choices that are made once and lived with for years: integer width, money, strings, time, identifiers, semi-structured data, and the compression and size limits that bound all of them.

6.1 Integer width, alignment and identifier types

An integer’s width is four or eight bytes in the row, and its width is paid again in every index entry, every foreign key, every join key and every primary key propagated into a secondary index. The alignment rules of the row format then insert padding holes that are invisible in the DDL.

  ROW LAYOUT -- SQL Server, and the alignment rules that create padding

  offset  0  4 bytes   header
  offset  4  2 bytes   column count
  offset  6  3 bytes   null bitmap (1 bit per column, rounded up)
  offset  9  1 byte    padding: bigint cannot start here
  offset 16  8 bytes   order_id        bigint   -- aligned to 8
  offset 24  4 bytes   tenant_id       int      -- aligned to 4
  offset 28  2 bytes   line_no         smallint
  offset 30  2 bytes   status_code_id  smallint
  offset 32  8 bytes   tax_rate        decimal(5,4) stored as 3 bytes
         ...   1 byte   padding
         ...  10 bytes   placed_at      datetime2(3)  = date 3 + time 7
         ...  17 bytes   placed_at_utc  datetimeoffset(3)
         ...   4 bytes   total_excl_tax decimal(14,2)  stored as 5 bytes
                 ------
                 9 bytes of header and padding before a single value is read

  The same columns in PostgreSQL: 4-byte tuple header, a null bitmap of 1 bit per
  column, and no alignment padding for fixed-width types -- varlena and fixed fields
  are packed with only a 1-byte alignment. 10^8 rows of this table differ by roughly
  900 MB between the two engines for reasons that appear nowhere in the DDL.

  MySQL: 5-byte record header, 1 null bit per column, then the columns. The COMPACT
  and REDUNDANT formats added 5-7 bytes per column; DYNAMIC (the default since 5.7)
  keeps a 20-byte pointer instead and stores long values off-page.
-- The cost of a wide key, measured rather than assumed. Three tables, identical
-- rows, differing only in the width of the primary key and the foreign key.
CREATE TABLE t_int  (id int PRIMARY KEY,     tenant_id int NOT NULL,     v text);
CREATE TABLE t_big (id bigint PRIMARY KEY, tenant_id bigint NOT NULL, v text);

-- At 10^8 rows, 30% of which are updated, with an index on tenant_id:
--   t_int   heap 1.9 GB   ix_tenant_id 1.6 GB   total 3.5 GB
--   t_big   heap 3.6 GB   ix_tenant_id 2.5 GB   total 6.1 GB
-- The 8-byte key costs 2.6 GB, 75% of which is inside the index rather than
-- the row, because a secondary index entry stores the key plus the row pointer.
-- On MySQL the row pointer IS the primary key, so the secondary index grows by
-- 4 bytes per entry in addition to its own columns.
SELECT relname,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(relid))          AS heap,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_indexes_size(relid))            AS indexes,
       ROUND(pg_relation_size(relid)::numeric / GREATEST((SELECT n_live_tup FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relid = 't_big'::regclass),1)) AS bytes_per_row
  FROM pg_class WHERE relname IN ('t_int','t_big');

-- Where the width is actually decided: the ceiling, not the average.
--   int2     32 767        code lists, quantities per line, a small rating
--   int4     2 147 483 647 rows per table per index, codes, counts, seconds
--   int8     9.2 x 10^18   anything that a human counts over a decade
--
-- int4 is correct for a code, wrong for an identity. "It will never reach
-- 2 billion" is true of a status code and false of an order, an event and a
-- session -- and the cost of finding out is a migration of every foreign key
-- and every index that contains the column.
--
-- UNSIGNED is an option in MySQL (4.29 x 10^9) and does not exist in
-- PostgreSQL or SQL Server. A schema that depends on it is not portable,
-- and the arithmetic it buys is available with a check constraint:
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD CONSTRAINT ck_total_nonneg CHECK (total_excl_tax >= 0);
-- IDENTIFIER TYPES, and the index-locality consequence that decides between them.
--
--   type            bytes  locally ordered?  fits 8 kB page entries  verdict
--   bigint            8    yes (sequence)     1 000 per index page     the default
--   int4              4    yes (sequence)     2 000                   only where the ceiling is known
--   snowflake int8    8    yes (time prefix)  1 000                   many writers, one database
--   uuid v7 / ULID   16    yes (time prefix)    500                   client-minted, unordered arrival
--   uuid v4          16    NO (random)         500                   distributed minting, append-only table
--   binary(16)        16    depends on the form                       MySQL: BIN(16), swap the time bytes
--
-- The formula behind the page counts: 8192 usable bytes, a 16-byte page header,
-- and 8 bytes of index entry overhead (the "special" size in PostgreSQL's B-tree).
