--   entries per leaf page ~= (8192 - 16 - 8) / (key bytes + 8)
--     key  4  -> 1 363 entries    key  8  ->  910    key 16  ->  510
-- A 16-byte key therefore halves the fan-out, which raises the B-tree height a
-- third at 10^8 rows and makes every seek one page read more likely to miss cache.
--
-- A snowflake id, and the two hazards it carries:
--   bits 63..22  milliseconds since a fixed epoch (41 bits, ~69 years)
--   bits 21..12  node id (10 bits, 1 024 writers)
--   bits 11..0   sequence within the millisecond (4 096 ids/ms/node)
--
--   hazard 1: clock skew. An id generated from a clock 5 ms behind sorts before
--             ids generated before it, so a time-ordered index accepts a row at
--             the left edge and a later transaction is not last by id. If anything
--             in the system derives order from the id, that is a correctness bug.
--   hazard 2: node id exhaustion and re-use. A node id must never be reused by a
--             different machine, or the ids collide silently.
-- Neither hazard exists in a database-generated bigint, and both are acceptable in
-- exchange for client-side minting. State which trade is being made.

-- ULID, which is a 128-bit value with the timestamp in the first 48 bits, and
-- therefore sorts by creation time as text as well as as bytes:
--   01ARZ3NDEKTSV4RRFFQ69G5FAV   Crockford base32, case-insensitive, no I, L, O, U
--
-- The trap: MySQL's default collation is case-insensitive, so a ULID CHAR(26) index
-- treats two ids differing only in case as equal. Either use a binary collation or
-- store BINARY(16) and convert at the boundary.
ALTER TABLE tenant
  MODIFY public_ref CHAR(26) COLLATE utf8mb4_bin NOT NULL;
-- MySQL 8.0.17+: UUID_TO_BIN / BIN_TO_UUID with a swap flag for time ordering.
ALTER TABLE tenant ADD COLUMN public_ref_bin BINARY(16)
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (UUID_TO_BIN('00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000', 1)) STORED;

Decision. The reference model uses bigint identities for every surrogate key, a uuid unique column as the customer-visible identifier, and a five-character text natural key where the value is immutable and assigned by the system. It deliberately does not use a snowflake: nothing in the model needs ids to be minted by a client, and the clock-skew hazard buys nothing that a RETURNING clause does not.

When a table is expected to pass 231 rows, decide at design time rather than at 231 rows. The migration is not one ALTER: it is every foreign key that references the column, every index entry that contains it, every stored procedure signature, every client model, and a period during which the old and new types must coexist. Budget it as a project.

6.2 Money as decimal: a worked failure

Floating point cannot represent most decimal fractions exactly, and money is a decimal concept. This section demonstrates the failure in three lines of arithmetic, shows what happens when it reaches an invoice, and states the rules that make the arithmetic agree across all three engines.

-- The failure, in three lines. 0.1 is not representable in binary floating point,
-- so it is stored as the nearest double, which is 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827
-- The same applies to 0.2, 0.3, 0.29, 0.07 and every price in this guide.
SELECT 0.1::float8 + 0.2::float8 = 0.3::float8;   -- false
SELECT 0.1::float8 * 3 <= 0.3::float8;             -- false: 0.30000000000000004 > 0.3
SELECT SUM(x) FROM (VALUES (0.1::float8),(0.1),(0.1)) v(x);   -- 0.30000000000000004

-- The same thing in exact decimal, which is the whole point of choosing it:
SELECT CAST(0.1 AS numeric) + CAST(0.2 AS numeric) = CAST(0.3 AS numeric);  -- true
SELECT 19.99::numeric * 3;  -- 59.97, exactly, and 59.97 = 19.97 + 19.99 + 20.01

-- The exact statement of why a CHECK written in float cannot be trusted:
--   CHECK (total_excl_tax <= 100.0)
-- with float8, a value of 99.99999999999999 passes and 100.00000000000001 fails.
-- The boundary is not where it appears to be, and the failures are not reproducible.
-- THE WORKED FAILURE, arriving at an invoice reconciliation. 3 lines, 20% tax.
--
-- order 1: 3 lines of 19.99, tax_rate 0.2000, 2 decimal places.
WITH lines(order_id, line_no, quantity, unit_price, tax_rate) AS (
  VALUES (1,1,1,19.99::numeric, 0.2000::numeric),
         (1,2,1,19.99, 0.2000),
         (1,3,1,19.99, 0.2000))
SELECT order_id,
       SUM(ROUND(quantity * unit_price, 2))                        AS sum_of_line_totals,
       ROUND(SUM(quantity * unit_price), 2)                             AS total_then_round,
       SUM(ROUND(quantity * unit_price * tax_rate, 2))                AS tax_line_by_line,
       ROUND(SUM(quantity * unit_price * tax_rate), 2)                     AS tax_then_round
  FROM lines GROUP BY order_id;
--   sum_of_line_totals = 59.97   total_then_round = 59.97   -- agree
--   tax_line_by_line   = 12.00   tax_then_round    = 11.99   -- DISAGREE by one penny
--
-- Three lines of 19.99 at 20%: the exact total is 59.97, and 20% of 59.97 is
-- 11.994, which rounds to 11.99. Rounding each line first gives 3.998, which
-- rounds to 4.00, three times.
--
-- This is not a bug in decimal arithmetic. It is a business decision about which
-- rule applies, and it must be made once, written down, and enforced in the schema:
--
--   line-level rounding:  tax is charged per line, as on most consumer invoices
--   invoice-level:       tax is charged on the invoice total, as in some VAT regimes
--
-- Whichever is chosen, the storage must support it. With float8 the amounts are
-- not merely rounded differently, they are not the same numbers on different rows,
-- and the reconciliation is unreproducible because the sum depends on the order in
-- which the rows are aggregated.
-- The type choice, per engine, and the trap in each.
--
-- PostgreSQL: numeric(p,s), arbitrary precision, variable-width binary. Slower than
-- bigint by roughly an order of magnitude on arithmetic -- which is the price, and
-- it is paid only on the columns that are genuinely monetary.
CREATE TABLE payment_account_balance (
    account_id bigint NOT NULL,
    currency   char(3) NOT NULL,
    -- 18 significant digits, 4 decimal places: up to 9 999 999 999 999.99 in any
-- currency, which covers every circulating currency except the dinar, dong
-- and rupiah conversions, and which is what finance systems actually use.
    balance    numeric(18,4) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    PRIMARY KEY (account_id, currency)
);
-- CHECK (balance >= 0)  -- per-currency balance cannot go negative

-- MySQL: DECIMAL is exact and is implemented in base-10000, so it is slower than
-- BIGINT. Two traps:
--   DOUBLE is a float, and the default for a non-specified float type.
--   FLOAT(p) is DOUBLE for p > 24, and IGNORING the p entirely in older modes.
CREATE TABLE payment_account_balance (
    account_id bigint NOT NULL,
    currency   char(3) NOT NULL,
    balance    decimal(18,4) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    PRIMARY KEY (account_id, currency)
) ENGINE=InnoDB;
--   Also: sql_mode must contain STRICT_TRANS_TABLES (the 8.0 default) or an
--   out-of-range DECIMAL is silently truncated to the column scale with a warning.
SELECT @@sql_mode;

-- SQL Server: decimal/numeric, exact, up to 38 digits. The trap is MONEY, which
-- Microsoft documents as not recommended for monetary data: it is a 8-byte
-- value with 4 implied decimal places, fixed at that scale, and it rounds
-- differently from decimal(9,2).
-- CREATE TABLE balance (amount money);          -- do not
-- CREATE TABLE balance (amount decimal(18,4));  -- this
--
-- datetime2(3) is the correct companion type, not money and not float.

-- The alternative representation, and when it is better: minor units as an
-- integer, with the currency beside it.
--   amount_minor bigint      1999 means 19.99 in a 2-scale currency
--   currency    char(3)     the scale is a property of the currency, not the value
-- Pros: exact, fast, 8 bytes, arithmetic is integer arithmetic, and no rounding
-- rule to choose. Cons: every currency has a different scale, a currency with 0
-- or 3 decimals breaks the assumption, and displaying requires the scale. Choose
-- this when the system holds one currency, and numeric when it holds several.

-- Rounding, and the cross-language trap. All three engines round half away from
-- zero on numeric multiplication. Python's built-in round() uses banker's rounding:
--   ROUND(2.5)   in SQL    = 3      round(2.5)   in Python = 2
--   ROUND(-2.5)  in SQL    = -3     round(-2.5)  in Python = -2
-- An application that computes totals in Python and stores them in a decimal column
-- will disagree with a report that computes them in SQL, on exactly the half-way
-- cases. Pick the engine as the single source of rounding and never round twice.
-- Making the reconciliation a database invariant rather than a report. A CHECK
-- cannot span rows, so the constraint that the order total equals the sum of
-- its lines is enforced three ways, in increasing order of strength.
--
-- (1) Application assertion, verified by a scheduled job. Cheapest, and the job is
--     the only thing that catches the paths the application does not own.
SELECT o.order_id, o.total_excl_tax, s.line_sum
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN (SELECT order_id, SUM(line_total_excl_tax) AS line_sum
          FROM sales_order_line GROUP BY order_id) s
    ON s.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.total_excl_tax IS DISTINCT FROM s.line_sum
 LIMIT 100;
--     Must return zero rows. This belongs in the nightly job, in the deployment
--     pipeline against production-shaped data, and in the integration suite.

-- (2) A deferred constraint trigger, which makes the database itself refuse to
--     commit an order whose total disagrees. PostgreSQL 9.2+; the trigger is fired
--     at COMMIT rather than per row.
CREATE OR REPLACE CONSTRAINT TRIGGER trg_order_total
  AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE ON sales_order
  DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED
  FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION assert_order_total();

-- (3) Store the total in a generated column of a VIEW, and grant UPDATE on the view
--     rather than the table. Then the total cannot be wrong because nothing can
--     write it. The cost is that the ORDER BY and INSERT paths go through the view,
--     and an updatable view with a generated column is not portable.
CREATE VIEW sales_order_v AS
SELECT o.order_id, o.tenant_id, o.placed_at, o.status, o.currency,
       (SELECT SUM(line_total_excl_tax) FROM sales_order_line l
         WHERE l.order_id = o.order_id) AS total_excl_tax
  FROM sales_order o;
--
-- For the reference model the answer is (1) plus a stored total: the total is a
-- financial promise, so it is a stored fact (section 3.6), and its correctness is
-- asserted by the reconciliation query above, which is the only one of the three
-- that requires no mechanism to be trusted beyond itself.

Caveats. Three failures that all present as "the total is a penny out" and have three different causes: rounding applied twice (once in the application, once in the column definition); rounding at a different scale (unit price at 4 places, line total at 2, invoice at 2); and a conversion applied on the way in (amount / 100 before an INSERT into a column that already holds a decimal amount). The first two are visible in the schema; the third is not, and it is the one that survives longest.

6.3 Text, varchar and the inline threshold

A string column’s declared type determines where it is stored, how it is compared, whether it can be indexed without truncation, and what happens when a row grows past the page. The engines agree on the categories and disagree on the names, and the disagreements are the source of most portability surprises.

CategoryPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Bounded, in rowvarchar(n), char(n) — same storage as textVARCHAR(n), CHAR(n) — n counted in charactersnvarchar(n) — n in characters; nchar is padded
Unbounded, in rowtext — preferred, and shorter to writeNo TEXT in row; the TEXT types are off-pagenvarchar(max) is off-row beyond 8 000 bytes
Padding semanticschar(n) is blank-padded and ignores trailing spaces on comparisonCHAR strips trailing spaces on retrieval; VARCHAR preserves themTrailing spaces are significant in comparison, which is why 'a' = 'a ' is false
Max row~2 032 bytes inline, then TOAST (compress, then out of line)Half a page (~8 126 bytes) in the DYNAMIC format8 060 bytes, hard limit, no overflow for most types
IndexingWhole value, or a varchar(n_ops) operator class that indexes a prefix by length rather than by charactersPrefix index (col(64)), or the whole value up to the key length limitMax index key 1 700 bytes for nonclustered, 900 for clustered; nvarchar is 2 bytes per character so a nvarchar(1000) column cannot be indexed at all
-- The rule, on the reference model: text for anything unbounded, varchar(n) only
-- where n is a business rule the database is responsible for enforcing.
CREATE TABLE app_user (
    user_id    bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id  bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    -- 254 is a hard business rule (RFC 5321 path limit), so it belongs here.
    -- A limit of 2000 would not: it is a guess about the application, and when it
-- is wrong every row fails an UPDATE that should have succeeded.
    email      varchar(254) NOT NULL,
    -- No length. A customer's name has no maximum, and inventing one creates a
    -- migration for a class of real names the schema never anticipated.
    display_name text NOT NULL,
    -- Fixed 3: ISO 4217. A char(3) rather than a check constraint, because the
    -- length itself is part of the format.
    locale     char(5) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'en-GB',
    CONSTRAINT uq_user_email UNIQUE (tenant_id, email)
);
--
-- What a length limit actually costs, and why the default is text:
--   a wrong limit rejects an UPDATE that should have succeeded, in a migration
--   that is applied while the old code is still serving traffic;
--   the rejection appears in the application as a 500, because the constraint is
--   not part of any API contract;
--   and a widened limit is a metadata-only change in all three engines, so a limit is
--   cheap to add later and expensive to add later than the data that needs it.
-- Where a limit IS a rule, enforce it. Where it is a guess, do not.

-- Encoding, and the truncation error that only appears under emoji.
-- MySQL's utf8 is a 3-byte encoding. An emoji is 4 bytes and is REJECTED, not
-- truncated, with error 1366 "Incorrect string value". utf8mb4 is 4-byte.
SELECT @@character_set_server, @@collation_server;   -- utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci on 8.0+
-- The column inherits the table, the table inherits the database, the database
-- inherits the server -- and ALTER DATABASE ... CHARACTER SET does not change
-- existing columns, so a fix applied at the wrong level looks applied and is not.
--
-- SQL Server: nvarchar is 2 bytes per character (UTF-16) and a supplementary-plane
-- character is 2 of them, i.e. 4 bytes. An index key of 900 bytes for a clustered
-- index means a nvarchar(450) column is the longest indexable string.
SELECT c.name, t.name AS type_name, c.max_length, c.is_nullable
  FROM sys.columns c JOIN sys.types t ON t.user_type_id = c.user_type_id
 WHERE c.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.app_user');
--
-- The 4 KB index key limit, the one that surprises everyone. A 900-character name
-- column cannot be part of a clustered index, and the error only appears when a
-- migration adds the index -- not when the column is created.
-- Fix: index a hash of the value, or a prefix, or move the column out of the key.
-- The inline threshold, demonstrated. PostgreSQL: 2 032 bytes, then TOAST.
SELECT oid, relname, reltoastrelid <> 0 AS has_toast
  FROM pg_class WHERE relname IN ('app_user', 'audit_event', 'sales_order');

-- Where the TOAST threshold is, and what a value costs inline versus out of line.
SELECT name, setting FROM pg_settings
 WHERE name IN ('toast_tuple_threshold', 'block_size');
SELECT 200::text   AS small, pg_column_size(200::text) AS small_bytes,
            repeat('x',2000) AS large, pg_column_size(repeat('x',2000)) AS large_bytes;
--   2000 random-ish bytes compress poorly, exceed the threshold, and are stored
--   out of line: the tuple carries an 18-byte pointer and the value lives in the
--   TOAST table. The 18 bytes, not the 2 000, is what the row pays.
--
-- The consequence that decides whether you care: an out-of-line value is a second
-- random read, and it is not in the same page as the row that points at it. On a
-- table of 10^8 rows this is the difference between one page read and two for every
-- access pattern that touches a wide column.

-- SQL Server, the same threshold and the same consequence.
SELECT c.name, t.name AS type_name, c.max_length
  FROM sys.columns c JOIN sys.types t ON t.user_type_id = c.user_type_id
 WHERE c.object_id = OBJECT_ID('dbo.audit_event');
--   nvarchar(max) and varbinary(max) are stored in row while they fit in 8 000 bytes
--   and in a LOB beyond it. There is no compression for nvarchar(max) other than
--   page compression, so a 4 MB JSON audit payload is 4 MB.
--
-- MySQL: the DYNAMIC row format keeps a 20-byte pointer and up to 768 bytes inline.
--   Above that the value goes to overflow pages, and a row with several large values
--   is read from several places. TEXT, LONGTEXT and JSON all behave this way.
SELECT TABLE_NAME, ROW_FORMAT, ENGINE_ROW_FORMAT
  FROM information_schema.TABLES WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = 'contoso';

Measure the string columns that are actually large rather than the ones that might be: SELECT max(length(col)), avg(length(col)) FROM t for each candidate. A column with a maximum of 40 and an average of 12 belongs in varchar(64); a column with a maximum of 40 000 belongs in text and will never TOAST; and the interesting case — a column that is small for 99% of rows and enormous for 1% — is exactly the case where out-of-line storage is what you want and where a fixed-width column would push the whole table over the page limit.

6.4 Timestamp semantics and the timezone trap

A timestamp can mean three different things: an instant on the global timeline, a wall-clock reading at an unnamed location, or a wall-clock reading at a known location. Only the first is unambiguous without another column, and the three engines disagree on which type name means which.

MeaningPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
An instanttimestamptz — stored as UTC, rendered in the session’s TimeZoneTIMESTAMP — stored as UTC, converted by the session time_zone; range 1970–2038 onlydatetimeoffset(3) — stores the offset with the value; datetime2(3) is not an instant type
A wall clock, no locationtimestampDATETIME — no conversion, range 1000–9999datetime2(3)
A calendar datedateDATEdate
Elapsed timeintervalNo interval type; use INTERVAL compound or SECOND/MINUTEtime / datetime2 difference, or AT TIMEZONE
-- THE TRAP, on PostgreSQL. date_trunc on timestamptz depends on TimeZone, which is
-- per session. The same row is assigned to a different day by two sessions.
SET TimeZone = 'UTC';
SELECT date_trunc('day', '2026-03-01 00:30:00+08'::timestamptz);   -- 2026-02-28 16:30:00+00  = 28 Feb UTC
SET TimeZone = 'Asia/Manila';
SELECT date_trunc('day', '2026-03-01 00:30:00+08'::timestamptz);   -- 2026-03-01 00:00:00+08  = 1 Mar Manila
--
-- A Manila customer places an order at 00:30 local on 1 March. The UTC session
-- calls it 28 February. A daily revenue report therefore differs by one day
-- depending on which connection ran it, and neither answer is wrong.
--
-- The schema must state which one it means. Two designs, both correct:
--
-- (a) The reporting boundary is UTC, for every tenant. Simple, consistent, and one
--     tenant's day is 8 hours out of step with their invoice.
--   WHERE placed_at >= date_trunc('day', now())
--   and the report states "all times UTC" in its title.
--
-- (b) The reporting boundary is the tenant's local calendar day, and the tenant has a
--     time zone as a column. Correct for invoices, and it needs the stored month
--     column of section 3.5, because date_trunc(timestamptz) cannot be indexed.
ALTER TABLE tenant ADD COLUMN report_tz text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'UTC';
--   timestamptz AT TIME ZONE 'UTC' yields a timestamp; the expression is IMMUTABLE
--   once the zone is a literal, and can be wrapped in a generated column:
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN order_month date
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (((placed_at AT TIME ZONE 'UTC'))::date) STORED;
-- MySQL: TIMESTAMP versus DATETIME, and why mixing them is a bug factory.
--
--   TIMESTAMP   4 bytes. Stored as UTC. Converted on read and write using the session
--               time_zone. 1970-01-01 to 2038-01-19. NOT NULL is the default and a
--               zero date is possible unless NO_ZERO_DATE is in sql_mode.
--   DATETIME    5 bytes. Stored exactly as given. 1000-01-01 to 9999-12-31. No
--               conversion, no 2038 limit, and a zero value is representable.
--
-- The consequences, all of which are real incidents rather than hypotheticals:
--   1. Changing the server or session time_zone changes what every TIMESTAMP column
--      contains when it is read. The stored bytes never change; the meaning does.
--   2. A TIMESTAMP column silently truncates a date beyond 2038 on insert, or errors.
--   3. Two TIMESTAMP columns written by two services with different session
--      time_zone settings contain genuinely different instants for the same wall clock.
--   4. A DATETIME column is what a developer writes when they mean an instant, and it
--      is wrong the moment a second service in another zone reads it.
SELECT @@global.time_zone, @@session.time_zone, NOW(), UTC_TIMESTAMP();
-- The safe combination for an event log, and it is the one to standardise on:
--   event_at    DATETIME(3)   the literal instant, in UTC, by convention
--   recorded_on TIMESTAMP     MySQL's own write time, for forensics
-- with an explicit CHECK that nothing but UTC is written:
--   CHECK (CONVERT_TZ(event_at, '+00:00', @@session.time_zone) = event_at)
--   -- true only when the session zone is UTC. Cheap, and it turns a convention into
--   -- an enforced rule for any connection that gets it wrong.

-- SQL Server: the three functions, and the trap in each name.
SELECT GETDATE()        -- local server time, datetime, 3.33 ms precision
SELECT GETUTCDATE()     -- UTC, but still typed datetime: 3.33 ms rounding
SELECT SYSDATETIME()     -- local, datetime2, 100 ns precision. Use this.
SELECT SYSUTCDATETIME() -- UTC, datetime2. Use this.
SELECT SYSUTCDATETIMEOFFSET(); -- with the offset attached
-- GETUTCDATE() is still the most widely used of these, and its 3.33 ms rounding is
-- the legacy of a type that counted clock ticks. Ordering by it is nearly stable;
-- ordering by SYSUTCDATETIME() is exactly stable.
--
-- datetimeoffset costs 10 bytes against datetime2's 8, and it is NOT stored in UTC:
-- the offset is stored with the value and no conversion happens, so a column can
-- hold the same instant in two representations. Normalise on write:
ALTER TABLE sales_order
  ADD CONSTRAINT ck_placed_utc
    CHECK (DATEPART(TZOFFSET, placed_at) = 0);   -- stored with a zero offset
-- Ordering, clock skew and monotonicity. Three things a timestamp must be for
-- to be usable as an ordering key, and only one of them is guaranteed.
--
-- 1. Generate it in one place. An application's clock and the database's clock
--    differ, and both differ from a colleague's laptop. A row created "now" by
--    a host whose clock is 400 ms fast is genuinely later than a row committed
--    after it.
--    Fix: DEFAULT now() / SYSDATETIMEOFFSET() / CURRENT_TIMESTAMP, and let the
--    database be the only clock in the system. If the application must have its own
--    value for latency measurement, store it in a separate column and never sort by it.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
  ALTER COLUMN placed_at SET DEFAULT now();

-- 2. Store an explicit sequence for tie-breaking. Two orders in the same
--    millisecond are ordered arbitrarily by placed_at, and a pagination query that
--    orders by placed_at alone will repeat or skip rows across a page boundary.
--    Fix: ORDER BY placed_at, order_id. The keyset predicate in section 5.3 depends
--    on this, and it is the most common cause of "pagination skips a record".

-- 3. Elapsed time is an interval, not a difference. Subtracting two timestamps
--    measured across a DST boundary or a leap second is a wall-clock difference, not
--    a duration. Neither PostgreSQL nor .NET counts leap seconds, so the two
--    disagree with POSIX and with a GPS receiver by a growing number of seconds.
SELECT (timestamptz '2026-03-29 02:00:00+01' - timestamptz '2026-03-29 01:30:00+00')
       AS wall_clock,   -- 00:30:00  (the clock jumped forward an hour)
       (extract(epoch FROM (
            timestamptz '2026-03-29 02:00:00+01' - timestamptz '2026-03-29 01:30:00+00'))
        / 3600 AS real_hours;                 -- 0.5     (30 minutes really passed)
-- For a billing period, use date and half-open ranges (section 2.6). For a duration
-- measured by a process, use a monotonic counter and never a wall clock.

-- What to store, as a decision table. The rightmost column is the trap that
-- appears if the row is chosen wrongly.
--
--   quantity                              store as        trap if wrong
--   ------------------------------------  --------------  ---------------------------
--   an order was placed                   timestamptz     off-by-hours reports
--   a billing period                      date            boundary off by one day
--   "09:00 every weekday, Europe/London"  time + IANA tz  DST: 09:00 is 08:00 in winter
--   a 30-day retention window             interval        recomputed every day, drifts
--   a 2 p.m. shift in a fixed city        timestamp + tz  the tz must never change
--   how long a job took                    interval or ms  subtraction across a DST edge
--   when the row was written (forensics)  timestamptz     -- correct by default

Caveats. The largest single source of time-zone incidents is a session setting. SET TIME ZONE, a connection string’s timezone parameter, a pooler that reuses sessions, and a managed service that sets it per connection all change the meaning of every timestamptz and TIMESTAMP value the next query reads. Pin the session time zone to UTC at connection time on every engine, assert it in a periodic check, and treat a difference as an incident. A database that renders a stored instant in the operator’s local zone is not storing instants; it is storing strings that look like instants.

6.5 Semi-structured data, and enums versus lookups

A JSON column and an enum both trade a schema guarantee for flexibility. Both are legitimate, both are abused, and the decision rule for each is a short list of questions whose answers are checkable rather than a matter of taste.

-- What jsonb actually is, and the differences that are not cosmetic.
--
--   json    the input text, byte for byte: key order preserved, duplicate keys
--           preserved, whitespace preserved. Equality is text equality, so two
--           semantically identical documents with different key orders are not equal.
--   jsonb   a binary form: a version byte, a container with a key count and the
--           length of each key and value, then a JEntry array and the data.
--           Keys are de-duplicated and sorted, so comparison and lookup are by
--           content. 2-5x smaller, and the only form that can be indexed usefully.
--   json    201 bytes   {"b":1,"a":2}  stored twice, twice
--   jsonb    44 bytes   one copy, keys ordered, lookup by key is a binary search

-- The costs, which are the argument against defaulting to jsonb.
SELECT pg_column_size('{"b":1,"a":2}'::json) AS json_bytes,
            pg_column_size('{"b":1,"a":2}'::jsonb) AS jsonb_bytes;
--
-- 1. No partial update. jsonb_set rewrites the whole document. A counter incremented
--    1 000 times a second in a 2 kB document writes 2 MB a second of WAL for a
--    4-byte change. This is the single most important cost, and it is invisible until
--    the document is big enough to matter.
-- 2. No statistics by default. The planner has no histogram of a key inside a
--    document, so a predicate on ->'x' is estimated from a fixed rate unless a
--    statistics target is raised or an expression index exists.
-- 3. No constraint other than what you write. A CHECK can assert the type of one
--    key; it cannot assert the absence of twenty others.
-- 4. A GIN index over the whole document costs an entry per key per row, and its size
--    is unpredictable from the schema.

-- THE DECISION RULE. Six questions, all answerable before the column is created.
--
--   1. Is the value ever filtered, sorted, joined on, or aggregated in SQL?
--        yes -> column. This question alone settles most cases.
--   2. Is it joined against another table?  yes -> column, or a lookup table.
--   3. Is it a financial quantity that must sum exactly?  yes -> numeric column.
--   4. Is its set of keys known and stable?  yes -> columns. A document whose keys
--        never change is a table that has not been written yet.
--   5. Is it a pass-through of an external payload you neither own nor index?
--        yes -> jsonb, and do not index it at all.
--   6. Is it written once and read whole?  yes -> jsonb is ideal and cheap.
--
-- In the reference model: audit_event.before_state and after_state are jsonb
-- because their keys are the union of every table's columns, which is unbounded by
-- construction, and they are written once and read whole. outbox.payload is jsonb
-- because it is a message envelope produced by a schema registry elsewhere. Nothing
-- else in the model uses jsonb.

-- Indexing a document, and the two ways. The btree expression index is smaller,
-- faster and supports ordering; the GIN index supports containment and key existence.
CREATE INDEX ix_audit_after_ref ON audit_event (((after_state ->> 'order_id')))
    WHERE entity_name = 'sales_order';      -- 60 bytes/entry
CREATE INDEX ix_audit_after_gin ON audit_event USING gin (after_state jsonb_path_ops);
                                                    -- 400+ bytes/entry, supports @>
-- The two mistakes: querying with a different key case than the index built
-- (jsonb keys are case-sensitive, unlike MySQL JSON where they are folded), and
-- assuming a GIN index makes a -&gt>'x' = 'y' query fast. It does not: use the
-- expression index for that, and GIN only for containment.
-- MySQL has no GIN: the equivalent is a generated column plus an index on it,
-- and the generated column is the same decision applied explicitly.
ALTER TABLE audit_event
  ADD COLUMN after_ref varchar(64)
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (JSON_UNQUOTE(JSON_EXTRACT(after_state, '$.order_id'))) STORED,
  ADD INDEX ix_audit_after_ref (entity_name, after_ref);
-- ENUMS versus LOOKUP TABLES, at the storage and operations level.
--
--                        PostgreSQL enum       MySQL ENUM            Lookup table
--   storage per row        4 bytes (an OID)     1-2 bytes (an index)   4-8 bytes + a join
--   comparison             by OID, O(1)         by index, O(1)          by string, or a cached array
--   reading a label        a CAST per value     a join or a function  the label, directly
--   adding a value         ALTER TYPE, cannot    ALTER TABLE, instant   INSERT, instant
--                          be rolled back
--   removing a value       impossible           impossible            DELETE, and the rows
--   reordering the set     impossible           possible, changes the  possible
--                                index meaning
--   effective dating       impossible           impossible             a range and a history table
--   per-value attributes   impossible           impossible             any column
--   enforced not-null      CHECK per value      NOT NULL on the column FK: cannot be NULL
--   DDL risk               type-level, affects  column rewrite in      none
--                          every dependent table some versions
--
-- The rule: a lookup table whenever the value set is open, dated, labelled or
-- governed. A native enum only for a closed set of names that the system itself
-- defines and that will never be reordered, dated or removed -- and the honest
-- count of such sets in a business application is very small.

-- The third option, which is right more often than either, and is free:
-- a CHECK constraint on a text column. It gives the not-null guarantee and the
-- domain restriction, and nothing else -- and for a code that the application owns
-- and that is genuinely closed, that is exactly enough.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
  ADD COLUMN status text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'placed',
  ADD CONSTRAINT ck_order_status CHECK (
      status IN ('placed','paid','fulfilled','cancelled','refunded'));
--
-- What a CHECK cannot do, and what people try to make it do:
--   * it cannot reference another table, so "cancelled orders must have a reason"
--     is not expressible. That constraint is a trigger or an application rule, and
--     it is better as a trigger than as an application rule, because the trigger is
--     not bypassable by a second application.
--   * it cannot be extended with a label. A CHECK says what is allowed; a lookup table
--     says what it means, and a system that only says what is allowed cannot render
--     a status page, cannot localise, and cannot tell a customer-facing state from an
--     internal one.
--   * a CHECK is evaluated on every write of the row, and a long IN list is parsed every
--     time. A lookup-table foreign key is a single 4-byte comparison against an index.

Decision. The reference model uses text columns with CHECK constraints for closed status sets that the system defines, and reserved lookup tables for anything a business user can change — plan and order_status being the examples. tax_rate is a numeric(5,4) and not a percentage integer, because 0.2000 and 20 are different columns and confusing them is a class of incident. source_ip is inet (16 bytes, with containment operators) rather than text, because the interesting queries are "how many logins from this network", and a string index answers only equality.

Caveats. On MySQL, an ENUM is a string type with a numeric index, not a type. Consequences: ORDER BY sorts alphabetically rather than in declaration order unless the optimiser is clever, the numeric index is one byte for up to 255 values and two bytes beyond, and the label cannot be retrieved without the ELT() function — so every report needs a join to a information_schema lookup that slow path writes get wrong. The declared-order semantics that the ENUM type appears to promise are not part of the SQL standard and are not portable to either other engine.

6.6 Compression, fillfactor and the size ceiling

Every engine can store more bytes per page than the data requires, and every engine can be told how much room to leave on a page. Both are throughput-versus-space decisions, and both have thresholds above which the default is wrong. This section also states when a single table has become too large to design well.

-- The four compression mechanisms, and what each one actually compresses.
--
--   name              granularity   algorithm   best for
--   ----------------  ------------  ----------  ------------------------------
--   TOAST pglz        per value     LZ77        the default; simple, always built in
--   TOAST lz4         per value     LZ4         2-3x faster than pglz, similar ratio
--   TOAST zstd        per value     Zstandard   best ratio; needs --with-zstd at build
--   index dedup       per page      prefix      repeated key prefixes in a B-tree leaf
--   SQL Server ROW     per row       dictionary   fixed-width columns, repeated strings
--   SQL Server PAGE    per page      dictionary   all row types; the usual choice
--   MySQL DYNAMIC     none         --          long values go off-page, uncompressed
--   MySQL COMPRESSED  per row/page  zlib        historical; superseded by page compression
--   MySQL page zstd   per page      zstd        per-tablespace, the modern answer
--
-- PostgreSQL: per-column storage mode, which is the control that exists.
--
--   SET STORAGE PLAIN     never compress, never out of line. Row is capped at 2 kB.
--   SET STORAGE EXTENDED  compress first, out of line only if needed. THE DEFAULT.
--   SET STORAGE EXTERNAL  no compression, out of line freely. For already-compressed
--                          values, where compressing again wastes CPU for nothing.
ALTER TABLE metric_sample ALTER COLUMN labels SET STORAGE EXTERNAL;
-- and the session-level default, which is usually the wrong thing to change
SET default_toast_compression = 'lz4';    -- PG 14+, requires --with-lz4
--
-- What compression cannot do: it operates on a value, not on a row, and it cannot
-- exploit the correlation BETWEEN columns. An order_id and a placed_at that are
-- both near-monotonic in the same row compress individually, and their relationship
-- is not exploited. That is exactly the case a columnar engine exists to serve, and
-- it is the honest reason to reach for one.

-- SQL Server: estimate, then decide, then rebuild in bounded windows.
-- EXEC sys.sp_estimatedatabcompressedsize @tabobjid = OBJECT_ID('dbo.sales_order'), ...
ALTER TABLE dbo.audit_event REBUILD WITH (COMPRESSION = PAGE);
--
-- The catch with PAGE on an update-heavy table: the space is reclaimed by the
-- engine for new rows, which means the rebuild makes the file appear to shrink, and
-- every UPDATE afterwards is slower because its new version has to be compressed.
-- Compression is for read-mostly tables. On a hot table, measure the write
-- regression before committing to it.

-- MySQL: page compression per tablespace, which is the 8.0 answer.
CREATE TABLESPACE audit_ts ADD DATAFILE 'audit.ibd' ENGINE=InnoDB
    COMPRESSION='zstd' KEY_BLOCK_SIZE=8;
-- Requires the file_per_table setting (the default) and a version of the library
-- compiled in. Transparent page compression uses a hole-punching punch-through,
-- which is why it is fast and why the free space is not reusable in the usual way.
-- FILLFACTOR, and the only question that matters: does this table get updated?
--
--   PostgreSQL   B-tree default 90. 90 leaves 10% free, so a same-page update is
--                likely, and a same-page update is HOT: no index is touched at all.
--                80 for a queue-like table where rows are updated many times.
--   SQL Server   clustered default 100, nonclustered default 90. A clustered index at
--                90 on an append-only table wastes 10% forever; a nonclustered at
--                100 on an update-heavy table splits constantly.
--   MySQL        No fillfactor. Pages split instead, and innodb_fill_factor applies only
--                to the COMPACT and REDUNDANT row formats, which nobody should use.
CREATE INDEX ix_subscription_period_end ON subscription (period_end, tenant_id)
    WITH (fillfactor = 75);      -- updated every billing cycle

-- The HOT connection, which is PostgreSQL-specific and worth a design decision of
-- its own: an UPDATE that changes no indexed column, and fits on the same page,
-- produces a new tuple version and ZERO index entries.
UPDATE sales_order SET note = 'x' WHERE order_id = 1;   -- HOT, no index write
UPDATE sales_order SET status = 'x' WHERE order_id = 1; -- not HOT: status is indexed
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relname = 'sales_order';
--   n_tup_hot_upd near n_tup_upd   -> the table is update-friendly
--   n_tup_hot_upd near zero        -> every update is writing every index
--   Two remedies, in order: lower the fillfactor, and stop indexing mutable columns.
-- HOW MUCH DATA IS TOO MUCH FOR ONE TABLE. The thresholds, and why each one.
--
-- 1. Index depth. A B-tree over 10^8 entries is 3 levels; over 10^9 it is 4, and
--    every seek becomes one more page read. Past 4 levels on a hot path, the index
--    is not the answer and neither is a bigger disk: the fix is fewer entries, which
--    means a partial index, a composite key with fewer columns, or a rollup.
SELECT indexrelname, ROUND(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)::numeric / 8192) AS pages
  FROM pg_class WHERE relkind = 'i' AND relname LIKE 'ix_order%';
--    Depth ~= log_base(900) (entries). 900^3 = 7 x 10^8 entries, 900^4 = 6 x 10^11.

-- 2. Maintenance. Autovacuum's scale-factor default of 0.2 means the largest
--    table in a 10 TB database gets a 2 TB vacuum. Whatever the threshold in rows,
--    the time to vacuum is proportional to the dead tuples AND to the number of
--    indexes, and it is a single-threaded foreground operation per table.
--    Above roughly 5 x 10^7 rows on a hot table, per-table autovacuum settings and a
--    partitioned layout both become necessary rather than optional.

-- 3. Backup and restore. The largest table sets the size of every full backup and
--    the duration of every restore. An RTO that requires a 4-hour restore of a table
--    that is 90% of the bytes is an RTO nobody has tested.

-- 4. Statistics. default_statistics_target is 100 samples per column by default. On a
--    10^8-row table with a skewed distribution, 100 samples is not enough to see the
--    hot tenant, and the plan is wrong in a way no amount of tuning will fix.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ALTER COLUMN tenant_id SET STATISTICS 10000;
ANALYZE sales_order;

-- 5. The operational one, and the real one: is there a query whose cost is a
--    constant fraction of the table? If any access pattern touches more than about
--    10% of a table, the access pattern inventory is wrong: that pattern is an
--    analytic one and it belongs on a rollup or a separate store. Ten per cent is not a
--    storage limit; it is a signal that two workloads have been put in one table.

-- The measured statement to put in the design document, for the largest table:
SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size('sales_order')) AS total,
       ( (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sales_order) ) AS rows,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size('sales_order')
             / GREATEST((SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sales_order),1)) AS bytes_per_row,
       (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM pg_index i WHERE i.indrelid = 'sales_order'::regclass) AS indexes;
-- Then: the projection at 24 months, using the measured growth rate of the last
-- 90 days rather than the average over the table's life, because the rate is almost
-- never constant and the early history understates it.

Every large table needs a stated ceiling, and the ceiling is a design decision rather than an observation. "sales_order will hold 4 x 108 rows and 340 GB at 24 months; the partitioning scheme is monthly, retention is 7 years with 2 years online, and the online portion is therefore 96 partitions of at most 3.5 GB." That sentence tells a future operator what maintenance to expect, tells a future engineer when to start redesigning, and is falsifiable against a measurement.

Exercises 6

  1. Build three versions of one table — int4 key, int8 key, uuid key — load 107 rows into each with an identical secondary index, and record the heap size, the index size and the pages read for a 1 000-row range scan. Present the ratio of index bytes to row bytes as the argument for the choice you would make.
  2. Reproduce the penny reconciliation from section 6.2 with three engines and with three lines of 19.99 at 20 % tax. Record which rounding rule each engine applies for ROUND, and state what your application’s language does, since that is where the disagreement enters.
  3. Inventory the string columns in a real schema: declared type, maximum observed length, average length, and the character set. Identify every column that is TOASTed, out-of-row, or off-page, and measure the number of pages read with and without that column for its dominant access pattern.
  4. Take one timestamptz column and one timestamp column in a real schema, set the session time zone to two different zones, and record every report that changes its answer. Then propose the change that makes the answer explicit, including any generated column needed.
  5. Move one jsonb column to typed columns, or argue from the six-question rule in section 6.5 why it should stay. Whichever way, state the number of queries that reference the field directly and the number of constraints that would be enforced by the move.
  6. For your largest table, record the total size, the bytes per row, the index-to-heap ratio, the B-tree depth estimate, the dead-tuple percentage and the projection at 24 months. Then state which of the five thresholds in section 6.6 it is closest to, and what the design change would be if it crossed it.

7 · Consistency, Transactions and Concurrency

7.1 The isolation matrix, side by side7.2 How the engines implement it7.3 Write skew: a concrete failure under snapshot isolation7.4 Retry loops, pessimistic locks and optimistic versioning7.5 Idempotent writes, upsert semantics and the outbox7.6 Read-your-writes and the replica lag trap

This is the chapter where the three engines genuinely diverge, and a schema that is safe on one of them can be silently unsafe on another with no change to a single statement. It covers the isolation levels compared side by side, how each engine implements them, the anomalies that survive snapshot isolation, the retry discipline that turns an abort into a successful write, and the two distributed-systems problems that belong in the schema rather than in the application.

7.1 The isolation matrix, side by side

The SQL standard defines four phenomena and five isolation levels, and every engine implements a different subset with different mechanisms. The consequence for a designer is that the isolation level in a transaction header is a statement about the engine, not a portable statement about the guarantee.

LevelPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.x (InnoDB)SQL Server 2025
READ UNCOMMITTEDNot implemented; treated as READ COMMITTEDAllowed. Dirty reads are possible and are used by some replication appliersAllowed. Dirty reads, via READUNCOMMITTED or the NOLOCK hint, and the hint is per-table so it is easy to apply by accident
READ COMMITTEDDefault. A fresh snapshot per statement; no read locks, so writers are never blocked by readersExists but is not the default. Gap locks are mostly disabled, except for foreign-key and duplicate-key checksDefault. Shared locks held for the duration of a read, released as the read passes the row. Non-repeatable reads and phantoms are possible
READ COMMITTED SNAPSHOTn/a — this is how READ COMMITTED already behavesn/a — see innodb_snapshot_isolationA database option, not a level. Turns shared locks into row versions. Must be enabled with ALTER DATABASE before any connection uses the database
REPEATABLE READA snapshot for the whole transaction. Read-only transactions are effectively serialisable; a transaction that writes may raise could not serialize access due to concurrent updateDefault. A consistent snapshot for the transaction and next-key gap locks, so writers block each other even when the rows do not overlapNot MVCC at all: shared and exclusive locks held to commit, plus key-range locks that prevent phantoms. Blocks readers and writers heavily
SNAPSHOTSame mechanism as REPEATABLE READ; the distinction is historicalSame mechanism as REPEATABLE READ with innodb_snapshot_isolation=ON, which removes the gap locksRow versions for the whole transaction. Requires ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION. A concurrent update of a row already read raises error 3961 and the transaction must be retried
SERIALIZABLESerializable snapshot isolation. Aborts one transaction with error 40001 rather than blocking. Provably correct, with a false-positive rate set by the predicate read setAll plain SELECTs are upgraded to locking reads, so the whole database is effectively locked. Rarely usedFully serialisable with range locks; blocks and deadlocks rather than aborting
What a conflict costs youA retryable exception, code 40001, with no blockingA deadlock, code 1213, or a lock wait timeout, code 1205 — one transaction is chosen as the victim and rolled backA deadlock, 1205, or a snapshot conflict, 3961. SET DEADLOCK_PRIORITY chooses the victim
AnomalyPrevented byReadsWrites
Dirty readAny level at or above READ COMMITTEDUncommitted data from another transactionA transaction that later rolls back leaves the reader holding a value that never existed
Non-repeatable readREPEATABLE READ, SNAPSHOT, SERIALIZABLEThe same row twice in one transaction returns different valuesA second transaction commits an update between the two reads
PhantomSERIALIZABLE, and REPEATABLE READ on MySQL and SQL ServerThe same query twice returns a different number of rowsA second transaction inserts a matching row between the two reads
Write skewSERIALIZABLE only. Not prevented by SNAPSHOT or REPEATABLE READ on PostgreSQL or SQL ServerEach transaction sees a consistent snapshotEach transaction writes a different row, and together they violate a constraint over the set. Section 7.3.
Lost updateExplicit optimistic concurrency or a lock; no isolation level prevents it by itselfEach reads the same valueEach writes back its own version and the second overwrites the first. The classic read-modify-write race
-- Setting the level, and the three different spellings.
--
-- PostgreSQL: per transaction, per session, or per user or database.
BEGIN TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
SET SESSION CHARACTERISTICS AS TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
ALTER DATABASE contoso SET default_transaction_isolation = 'serializable';
ALTER ROLE reporting SET default_transaction_isolation = 'repeatable read';
-- Set the default per ROLE for a workload rather than per session for a person:
-- a connection pool that a developer changed is a production incident.

-- MySQL: the default is REPEATABLE READ, which must be stated deliberately if changed.
SET SESSION TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED;
--   @@global.transaction_isolation  is the fleet-wide setting; a pool that sets it
--   per connection produces two different systems behind one URL.
SELECT @@transaction_isolation, @@innodb_snapshot_isolation;

-- SQL Server: RCSI is a DATABASE option, so it cannot be set from inside a session
-- and only takes effect for connections opened after it is changed. A database with
-- RCSI off is not "slower"; it is a different consistency model, and the same code runs
-- with different guarantees on it.
-- ALTER DATABASE contoso SET READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;
-- ALTER DATABASE contoso SET ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION ON;
SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT;

-- The three-line version of the whole section, to state in a design document:
--
--   PostgreSQL   READ COMMITTED by default; use SERIALIZABLE for the correctness-
--               critical paths, which means building retry handling in, not locking.
--   MySQL       REPEATABLE READ by default; it prevents write skew with gap locks and
--               pays for it in deadlocks. The default is defensible; changing it to
--               READ COMMITTED removes the protection and requires explicit locks.
--   SQL Server  READ COMMITTED with shared locks by default, which blocks readers
--               against writers; RCSI removes that and makes it behave like PostgreSQL,
--               with the version store in tempdb as the cost.
-- Which default to choose, per workload, and what it costs. The choice is a
-- database-level decision and it belongs in the design document.
--
--   workload                      PG 18              MySQL 9.x         SQL Server 2025
--   ----------------------------  ----------------  ----------------  ------------------
--   order processing              READ COMMITTED    REPEATABLE READ   RCSI + READ COMMITTED
--   balance transfers             SERIALIZABLE      REPEATABLE READ   SNAPSHOT + retry
--   seat allocation               SERIALIZABLE      REPEATABLE READ   SERIALIZABLE or a counter
--   catalogue browsing            READ COMMITTED    READ COMMITTED    READ COMMITTED (or RCSI)
--   reporting / long reads        READ COMMITTED    READ COMMITTED    READ COMMITTED snapshot
--   batch imports                 READ COMMITTED    READ COMMITTED    READ COMMITTED
--
-- Why SERIALIZABLE on PostgreSQL is cheaper than it looks: it does not lock, it
-- aborts. An aborted transaction is a retry, and a retry loop with backoff is a few
-- lines of code. A blocking lock is an unbounded wait inside a request thread, and
-- an unbounded wait is what a deadlock detector, a lock timeout and an on-call rota
-- all exist to manage.
--
-- Why MySQL's default is defensible: REPEATABLE READ with gap locks actually prevents
-- write skew without the application knowing, which PostgreSQL and SQL Server SNAPSHOT
-- do not. The cost is that two transactions touching adjacent key ranges deadlock, and
-- that the deadlock rate becomes a function of the workload rather than of the code.
--
-- Why RCSI is the first change to make on SQL Server: without it, a reporting query
-- holding shared locks for 30 seconds blocks a point write, and an application with a
-- long-running reader is a self-inflicted denial of service on the write path. The
-- cost is version store growth in tempdb, which is a disk that must be sized and
-- monitored (section 7.2).

Requirement. A transaction must be short, and the reason is per engine. On PostgreSQL an open transaction holds back the xmin horizon, which prevents vacuum from removing dead tuples, so a transaction that is idle for five minutes bloats the table it read. On MySQL an open transaction holds back the purge of undo, so History list length grows and undo tablespace does too. On SQL Server an open transaction holds back version store cleanup in tempdb. In all three cases the symptom is disk growth in a table that is not being written, and in all three cases the cause is a transaction that was left open by a connection pool, a debugging session or an un-awaited promise.

Alert on transaction age, not on transaction count. A query that finds open transactions older than 30 seconds, on any engine, catches idle-in-transaction, a forgotten BEGIN, a pool that lost its rollback, and a slow external call inside a transaction. It is one of the highest-value monitors a database can have, and on all three engines it is a single query against a system view.

7.2 How the engines implement it

The three mechanisms are multiversion concurrency control with a transaction snapshot, multiversion concurrency control with a read view plus gap locks, and row versioning with a version store. The differences are visible in the system views, and reading them is the only reliable way to diagnose a concurrency problem.

-- PostgreSQL: MVCC in the tuple header, and vacuum is the garbage collector.
--
--   every tuple carries xmin (the inserting transaction) and xmax (the deleting or
--   updating transaction), 8 bytes each, and a visibility test against the current
--   snapshot decides whether a tuple is visible. A reader never blocks a writer.
--
--   A snapshot is a set of running transaction ids:
SELECT pg_current_snapshot(), txid_current_snapshot();  -- the first is current
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state = 'idle in transaction';
--
--   Two consequences that are not obvious and both bite in production:
--
--   1. xmin wraparound. Transaction ids are 32 bits. A tuple whose xmin is older than
--      2^32 transactions ago is ambiguous, and the tuple must be frozen to be sure.
--      If autovacuum cannot keep up, the database refuses writes to protect itself:
--      "ERROR: database is not accepting commands to avoid wraparound data loss".
--      The remedy is a manual VACUUM FREEZE, and the lesson is that the wraparound
--      autovacuum is the one that must never be disabled.
--
--   2. A replication slot that is not being consumed holds xmin open forever, and the
--      table grows without any writes at all. A slot with a lagging consumer is the most
--      common cause of "the database got bigger and nothing changed".
SELECT slot_name, active, wal_status, pg_size_pretty(
           pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn)) AS retained_wal
  FROM pg_replication_slots;
-- MySQL InnoDB: undo logs provide the old versions, a read view decides visibility,
-- and the purge thread reclaims. The diagnostic is the history list length.
SELECT trx_id, trx_state, trx_started,
       TIMESTAMPDIFF(SECOND, trx_started, NOW()) AS age_seconds,
       trx_rows_locked, trx_rows_modified, trx_query
  FROM information_schema.INNODB_TRX
 ORDER BY trx_started LIMIT 20;

-- The history list length: the number of undo records that purge has not yet
-- removed. It is the number that tells you a long transaction is running, and it
-- is a performance_schema metric rather than a status variable.
SELECT name, count FROM information_schema.INNODB_METRICS
 WHERE name = 'trx_rseg_history_len';
-- Above ~100 000 the undo tablespace is growing faster than purge can reclaim, and
-- history list scans start appearing in query plans. Above 1 000 000 the instance is
-- in trouble. The cause is always the oldest open transaction.
--
-- The statement that finds it, including from other sessions:
SELECT trx_mysql_thread_id, trx_started,
       TIMESTAMPDIFF(SECOND, trx_started, NOW()) AS secs
  FROM information_schema.INNODB_TRX ORDER BY trx_started LIMIT 3;
KILL 12345;   -- and the transaction rolls back

-- Deadlocks: the last one is always in the status output, and it is a complete
-- post-mortem with both transactions, the statements and the lock graph.
SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS\G
--
-- Live lock waits, with the blocking and blocked rows:
SELECT r.trx_id        AS waiting_trx,
            b.trx_id        AS blocking_trx,
            rl.lock_table   AS table_name,
            rl.lock_index   AS index_name,
            rl.lock_type    AS lock_mode,
            rl.lock_data    AS locked_record
  FROM performance_schema.data_locks      rl
  JOIN performance_schema.data_lock_waits w ON w.REQUESTING_ENGINE_LOCK_ID = rl.ENGINE_LOCK_ID
  JOIN performance_schema.data_locks      bl ON w.BLOCKING_ENGINE_LOCK_ID  = bl.ENGINE_LOCK_ID
  JOIN information_schema.INNODB_TRX        r  ON r.trx_id     = rl.ENGINE_TRANSACTION_ID
  JOIN information_schema.INNODB_TRX        b  ON b.trx_id     = bl.ENGINE_TRANSACTION_ID;
-- A gap lock shows lock_type = GAP or NEXT_KEY with a NULL lock_data, which is
-- how you identify the REPEATABLE READ behaviour that produces most MySQL deadlocks.
-- SQL Server: the version store in tempdb, and the three settings that size it.
SELECT * FROM sys.dm_tran_version_store_space_usage;
--   version_store_data_pages: the row versions themselves
--   version_store_index_pages: versions in index entries
--   version_store_reserved_page_count: space reserved in tempdb
--
-- The two free-space thresholds. The second is the one people do not set:
EXEC sys.sp_configure 'tempdb_config';   -- current values
-- ALTER SERVER CONFIGURATION SET TEMPDB_VERSION_STORE_MAX_SIZE_GB = 16;
--
-- Cleanup is blocked by the oldest open transaction in the database, exactly as on
-- the other two engines, and the symptom is version store growth with no writes:
SELECT s.session_id, s.transaction_id, s.database_id,
       DATEDIFF(second, at_begin_time, GETDATE()) AS age_seconds,
       OBJECT_NAME(s.database_id) AS db_name
  FROM sys.dm_tran_active_transactions s
  JOIN sys.dm_tran_session_transactions st ON s.transaction_id = st.transaction_id
 ORDER BY at_begin_time;

--   3961 "Cannot perform the requested operation because the transaction
--           associated with this command is not in an active snapshot state" -- the
--           SNAPSHOT isolation conflict, and it is retryable, not a bug.
--   3960 "Snapshot isolation transaction aborted due to update conflict" -- same.
--   1205 deadlock, 1222 lock request timeout.
--
-- RCSI can be turned on while the database is in use only with a rollback:
--   ALTER DATABASE ... SET READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;
--   -- WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE disconnects every other session, and is why this is a
--   -- maintenance-window operation with a written-down list of consequences.
--
-- Deadlock graph, the equivalent of SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS:
SELECT CAST(event_data(XML) AS nv(max)) AS deadlock_graph
  FROM sys.dm_xe_session_targets st
  JOIN sys.dm_xe_sessions s ON s.address = st.event_session_address
 WHERE s.name = 'system_health' AND st.target_name = 'ring_buffer';
-- system_health rolls over at 20 MB, so a deadlock that happened yesterday may
-- already be gone. Raise the buffer deliberately, or ship the graphs to a table.
PostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Where old versions liveIn the heap, until vacuumIn the undo tablespace, until purgeIn tempdb, until version store cleanup
What blocks reclamationThe oldest snapshot; a lagging replication slotThe oldest read view; a lagging replicaThe oldest open transaction; ALTER DATABASE recovery model changes
Size knobNone — it is a consequence of the dead tuplesinnodb_undo_log_capacity per file; the history list is the metricNone; it is bounded by the smallest of the file, the version store limit and free space in tempdb
Readers block writersNever, except a FOR UPDATEYes, for gap ranges under REPEATABLE READYes by default; not at all with RCSI
Writers block readersNeverYes, for the row or the gapYes, for the row; not at all with RCSI
Conflict resolutionAbort with 40001, deterministic and repeatableAbort the victim; which one is a heuristicAbort with 1205 or 3960; the victim is chosen by cost and by DEADLOCK_PRIORITY

7.3 Write skew: a concrete failure under snapshot isolation

Write skew is the anomaly that survives every isolation level except true serialisability, and it is the one that a team most often believes it is protected against. This section reproduces it with two sessions, then gives the four fixes in order of preference.

-- The setup. A business rule that spans a SET of rows:
--   "at every instant, at least two distinct engineers are on call, and an
--    engineer may not be on two overlapping shifts."
CREATE TABLE oncall_shift (
    shift_id    bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    engineer_id bigint NOT NULL,
    shift_range tstzrange NOT NULL,
    EXCLUDE USING GIST (engineer_id WITH =, shift_range WITH &&)
);
INSERT INTO oncall_shift (engineer_id, shift_range) VALUES
  (1, tstzrange('2026-03-01 09:00Z', '2026-03-01 17:00Z', '[)')),
  (2, tstzrange('2026-03-01 09:00Z', '2026-03-01 17:00Z', '[)'));
-- 09:00-17:00 on 1 March is covered by two engineers. The rule holds.

-- SESSION 1                                    SESSION 2
-- ------------------------------------------  ------------------------------------------
-- BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;  or     BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
-- BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;      or BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT;
-- BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT;
--
-- SELECT count(*) FROM oncall_shift          SELECT count(*) FROM oncall_shift
--  WHERE shift_range @> '2026-03-01 12:00Z';   WHERE shift_range @> '2026-03-01 12:00Z';
--   -> 2                                 -> 2        (both see the same committed state)
--
-- -- "I am engineer 1, not on call"          -- "I am engineer 2, not on call"
-- INSERT INTO oncall_shift VALUES          INSERT INTO oncall_shift VALUES
--   (1, ['12:00Z','13:00Z'));                   (2, ['12:00Z','13:00Z'));
--   -- does 2 >= 2? yes                    --   -- does 2 >= 2? yes
--
-- COMMIT;                                    COMMIT;
--
-- RESULT: engineer 1 is now on call twice in overlapping ranges. The EXCLUDE
-- constraint did not fire, because neither row overlaps the OTHER row -- only
-- its own history. And the coverage rule is satisfied by the same two engineers,
-- so the roster shows a full shift that is one person deep.
--
-- This is write skew: two transactions, two different rows, one violated
-- constraint that is a property of the SET, not of either row.
-- THE SAME FAILURE IN THE REFERENCE MODEL. A limit over a set, in the order path:
-- "an order may not exceed 100 lines".
--
--   session 1                          session 2
--   SELECT count(*) = 99               SELECT count(*) = 99
--   INSERT line 100  <-- fine         (blocked or not, depending on the engine)
--                                   INSERT line 100  <-- fine
--   COMMIT                             COMMIT
--   result: 101 lines
--
-- Same shape, same cause: the predicate is a COUNT over a set, and two writers
-- evaluating it against the same snapshot both conclude it is satisfied.

-- THE SAME FAILURE, and this one is subtle enough to survive a code review:
-- "a subscription must always have at least one usable payment method".
--   session 1: mark payment method A revoked  -- sees B active, so the rule holds
--   session 2: mark payment method B expired  -- sees A active, so the rule holds
--   both commit: the subscription has no usable payment method.
-- No constraint in the schema can prevent it, because the rule is about the absence
-- of a row, and a CHECK constraint cannot see other rows. This is the shape to
-- look for in every "at least one" rule in a system.
-- FIX 1: SERIALIZABLE, and accept the abort. The correct answer when the rule must hold.
BEGIN TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM oncall_shift WHERE shift_range @> now();
INSERT INTO oncall_shift (engineer_id, shift_range) VALUES (:eng, :range');
COMMIT;
-- One of the two sessions gets: ERROR: could not serialize access due to read/write
-- dependencies among transactions. That is the safety property working. The
-- transaction must be retried in full, and the retry sees the other's commit and fails
-- the rule check, so it does not insert.
-- On MySQL, SERIALIZABLE turns the SELECT into a locking read, so the second session
-- waits rather than aborting, and the first one to acquire the range wins.
-- On SQL Server, SERIALIZABLE takes a range lock on the same predicate, and the two
-- sessions serialise on it.
--
-- Cost: the false-positive rate. PostgreSQL's SSI aborts transactions whose read and
-- write sets merely overlap, not only those that truly conflict. On a hot path this
-- shows up as a retry rate in the single digits, which is the price of the guarantee.
SELECT datname, xact_rollback, xact_commit,
       ROUND(100.0 * xact_rollback / NULLIF(xact_commit + xact_rollback, 0), 2) AS rollback_pct
  FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();
-- FIX 2: make the rule a property of ONE row. The best fix, and the one that scales.
-- Denormalise the count into the parent, so the read and the write touch the same row
-- and a write-write conflict does the serialising. No retry, no SERIALIZABLE, no gap
-- locks, and it works identically on all three engines.
CREATE TABLE tenant_usage (
    tenant_id        bigint PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    active_users     int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    open_orders      int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    order_lines      int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    CONSTRAINT ck_order_lines_max CHECK (order_lines <= 100)
);
--
-- Now the check and the write are the same statement, on the same row, so two
-- concurrent transactions serialise on that row. Under READ COMMITTED on
-- PostgreSQL, under REPEATABLE READ on MySQL, under RCSI on SQL Server.
UPDATE tenant_usage
   SET order_lines = order_lines + 1
 WHERE tenant_id = :tenant
   AND order_lines < 100;          -- 0 rows affected = limit reached
--
-- The counter costs: it must be incremented and decremented by every path, and it
-- must be reconciled. It is a stored derived value with an invalidation ledger
-- (section 3.6), and the ledger is short: exactly the paths that insert and delete
-- lines. The reconciliation is one query and belongs in the nightly job:
--   UPDATE tenant_usage u SET order_lines = s.n FROM
--     (SELECT tenant_id, count(*) n FROM sales_order_line GROUP BY tenant_id) s
--   WHERE u.tenant_id = s.tenant_id AND u.order_lines <> s.n;
--
-- FIX 3: lock something both transactions must take, in the same order.
-- An advisory lock keyed on the aggregate is the portable-ish version, and the
-- order is what prevents a deadlock between two of them.
SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock('oncall'::text, :slot_date'::date::int);
-- MySQL: SELECT GET_LOCK('oncall:2026-03-01', 5);  and RELEASE_LOCK in a finally block
--         (session-scoped, so a leaked connection leaks the lock -- a real hazard)
-- SQL Server: EXEC sp_getapplock @Resource = N'oncall:2026-03-01',
--                    @LockMode = 'X', @LockOwner = 'Transaction', @LockTimeout = 5000
--
-- FIX 4: change the schema so the rule is expressible. The strongest version.
--   coverage_slot      (slot_start, slot_end, engineer_id)  with an EXCLUDE on
--                      (slot_start WITH =, engineer_id WITH =) -- no engineer twice
--   and a coverage table with a per-slot counter, so "at least two" is a CHECK on
--   a single row per slot. The write skew disappears because the two invariants now
--   live in two different rows, each individually enforceable.

Caveats. The same schema is safe on PostgreSQL SERIALIZABLE and on MySQL REPEATABLE READ, and unsafe on SQL Server SNAPSHOT, with no change to a single statement. PostgreSQL and MySQL prevent write skew by aborting or by gap-locking; SQL Server SNAPSHOT takes a row version of each row read and does not detect the set-level dependency at all. A schema migrated from SQL Server to PostgreSQL gains a guarantee it never had; a schema migrated the other way loses one silently, and the loss is only visible in the first incident that depends on it.

Caveats. Advisory locks have three different failure modes across the three engines, and all three are serious. They are invisible to the transaction manager, so a tool that kills a blocked session may not release one. They are not counted in deadlock detection, so two of them in different orders deadlock forever until a timeout. And on MySQL and SQL Server they are session-scoped, so a pooled connection that returns to the pool holding a lock blocks every other user of that connection. pg_advisory_xact_lock is the only one of the three that is released automatically at commit or rollback, and that is the reason to prefer it or to prefer the counter.

Audit for write skew by grepping the schema for phrases in comments and constraints: "at least", "at most", "no more than", "must have", "only one of", "not both". Every one of those is a rule over a set, and every one of them is invisible to a CHECK constraint, a foreign key, and any isolation level below serialisable. Then check whether the rule is enforced by a counter, an exclusion constraint, or a serialisable transaction — and if none of the three, it is not enforced.

7.4 Retry loops, pessimistic locks and optimistic versioning

An aborted transaction is not a failure; it is a scheduling decision made by the database. This section classifies the errors that must be retried, implements the loop with bounded backoff on each engine, and covers the two locking disciplines that avoid needing SERIALIZABLE at all.

-- THE CLASSIFICATION. Retrying the wrong error turns a permanent failure into a
-- timeout, and that is the most common way a retry loop causes an incident.
--
--   RETRY, in full, including re-reading every value the transaction depends on:
--     PostgreSQL   40001 serialization_failure
--                 40P01 deadlock_detected
--     MySQL        1213 deadlock
--                 1205 lock wait timeout exceeded
--     SQL Server    1205 deadlock victim
--                 3960 / 3961 snapshot isolation conflict
--                 41302 / 41304 / 41305 update conflict, on a MERGE or a version check
--                 4060 cannot open database during an availability-group transition
--                 49918-49920 "not enough resources", during failover
--
--   DO NOT RETRY: the request is wrong and will be wrong again.
--     PostgreSQL   23505 unique_violation      23503 foreign_key_violation
--                 23514 check_violation        22P02 invalid_text_representation
--     MySQL        1062 duplicate entry        1452 cannot add FK
--                 3819 check constraint        1366 incorrect string value
--     SQL Server    2627/2601 PK or unique       547 FK violation
--                 547 check constraint          2202 truncation
--
--   The trap in the middle: a unique violation on an INSERT that is part of an
--   idempotent upsert is the SUCCESS path of an ON CONFLICT-free retry. Distinguish
--   "this key already exists, read it" from "this key exists with a different payload".

-- The loop, in SQL. Bounded attempts, exponential backoff, full jitter, and the
-- whole transaction re-run. PostgreSQL 9.x+; a PL/pgSQL DO block.
--
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION place_order_serially(p_tenant bigint, p_payload jsonb)
RETURNS bigint
LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $fn$
DECLARE
    v_order_id bigint;
    v_attempt  int := 0;
    v_delay    double precision;
    v_err      text;
BEGIN
    LOOP
        v_attempt := v_attempt + 1;
        BEGIN
            SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
            INSERT INTO sales_order (tenant_id, status, total_excl_tax)
            VALUES (p_tenant, 'placed', 0) RETURNING order_id INTO v_order_id;
            -- the reads that the invariant depends on go here, inside the transaction
            PERFORM 1 FROM tenant_usage WHERE tenant_id = p_tenant FOR UPDATE;
            RETURN v_order_id;
        EXCEPTION WHEN WHEN query_canceled OR WHEN lock_not_available THEN
            -- 40001 and 40P01 are SQLSTATE classes, and the two above are the
            -- specific ones worth retrying here.
            RAISE WARNING 'retrying order for tenant % (attempt %)', p_tenant, v_attempt;
        END;
        EXIT WHEN v_attempt >= 5;
        -- full jitter: random between 0 and the backoff, not the backoff itself
        v_delay := random() * (0.05 * power(2, v_attempt - 1));
        PERFORM pg_sleep(v_delay);
    END LOOP;
    RAISE EXCEPTION 'could not place order after % attempts', v_attempt;
END; $fn$;
-- The same loop where the transaction is driven by the application, which is where it
-- usually lives. The three properties that make it correct.
--
--   1. the retry re-reads everything. A retry that re-uses values read in the aborted
--      attempt is not a retry, it is the same bug with extra latency.
--   2. the backoff is jittered. Without jitter, N workers that all abort together
--      retry together, and the contention that caused the abort is reproduced exactly.
--   3. the attempt count is bounded and the total time is bounded. A request that
--      retries for 30 seconds is a request that holds a connection for 30 seconds, and
--      a connection pool is a finite resource that the retry loop is competing for.

-- SQL Server, where the loop is a WHILE and the errors are caught individually,
-- because there is no exception class for a set of SQLSTATE values.
--
DECLARE @attempt int = 0, @order_id bigint, @done bit = 0;
--
-- SET XACT_ABORT ON;   -- so that an error rolls back the whole transaction, and so
--                  -- that a THROW from a nested procedure is not swallowed
WHILE @attempt < 5 AND @done = 0
BEGIN
    BEGIN TRY
        SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT;
        BEGIN TRANSACTION;
            INSERT INTO dbo.sales_order (tenant_id, status, total_excl_tax)
            VALUES (@tenant, 'placed', 0);
            SET @order_id = SCOPE_IDENTITY();
            UPDATE dbo.tenant_usage SET open_orders = open_orders + 1
             WHERE tenant_id = @tenant;
        COMMIT TRANSACTION;
        SET @done = 1;
    END TRY
    BEGIN CATCH
        IF XACT_STATE() <> 0 ROLLBACK TRANSACTION;
        -- The retryable set. Everything else is re-thrown, immediately.
        IF NOT ERROR_NUMBER() IN (1205, 3960, 3961, 41302, 41304, 41305)
            THROW;
        SET @attempt += 1;
        -- jitter, from a value that is not predictable in advance
        WAITFOR DELAY '00:00:00.0' + CAST((RAND() * 50) AS varchar) + 'ms';
    END CATCH
END;
--
-- Two things that are SQL Server specific and both are common defects:
--   * SET XACT_ABORT ON is not optional. Without it, an error inside a TRY leaves the
--     transaction open and in an uncommittable state, and the next BEGIN TRANSACTION
--     fails with error 3902, which looks like a different problem entirely.
--   * SCOPE_IDENTITY() is correct and @@IDENTITY is not: @@IDENTITY returns a value from
--     the last statement of the whole session, including triggers on other tables.
--
-- A lock timeout, so that a blocked transaction becomes an error the loop can see:
--   SET LOCK_TIMEOUT 3000;   -- milliseconds; -1 is the default, which is infinite
--   and 1205 is then raised instead of waiting forever holding a connection.
-- OPTIMISTIC CONCURRENCY. The right default for a web application, and the pattern is
-- identical on all three engines except for the token.
--
-- The lost update this prevents, without any of it:
--   session 1: SELECT status, total FROM sales_order WHERE order_id = 1;  -- 'placed', 100.00
--   session 2: SELECT status, total FROM sales_order WHERE order_id = 1;  -- 'placed', 100.00
--   session 1: UPDATE ... SET status='paid';                            -- commits
--   session 2: UPDATE ... SET total_excl_tax = 95.00, status='paid';     -- commits
--   result: session 2 overwrote session 1's status with a value derived from a
--   stale read, and nothing anywhere reports an error.

-- (a) PostgreSQL, using the system column xmin. Zero schema change, and it is a
--     transaction id, so it is stable for the life of the row version.
--
--   client: SELECT order_id, xmin::text AS token, status, total_excl_tax FROM ...
--   client: UPDATE sales_order SET total_excl_tax = $1, version = version + 1
--          WHERE order_id = $2 AND xmin = $3::xid
--   rowcount = 0 means someone else changed it: re-read and re-decide.
UPDATE sales_order
   SET status = 'paid', total_excl_tax = 95.00
 WHERE order_id = :id AND xmin = :token'::xid;
-- Caveat: xmin is a 32-bit id and wraps, and it is compared with the = operator, so
-- a token from a long time ago can theoretically collide. A version column has no
-- such caveat and is visible in the schema. Use xmin when you cannot add a column,
-- and a version column everywhere else.
--
-- (b) SQL Server, with rowversion. 8 bytes, system-maintained, changes on every
--     update, cannot be written to, and is the correct answer on this engine.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD rowver rowversion NOT NULL;
--
--   SET ROWVERSION is the legacy syntax and is a different, deprecated type. The
--   token is binary(8) and must be sent as bytes, not as a string.
UPDATE dbo.sales_order
   SET status = 'paid', total_excl_tax = 95.00
 WHERE order_id = @id AND rowver = @rowver;
--
-- (c) Portable, explicit, and the one to use when the client is not the only writer:
-- a version integer, incremented in the same statement as the change.
UPDATE sales_order
   SET total_excl_tax = :total, version = version + 1
 WHERE order_id = :id AND version = :version;
-- 0 rows affected = a conflict, and the caller re-reads, recomputes and retries.
-- The client compares versions to be sure, and to detect ABA: if the version went
-- 7, 8, 9 and back to 7 because of a compensating update, the client's token is
-- stale in a way the number alone does not show. A monotonic counter does not have
-- that problem; a timestamp does, which is why a version integer beats updated_at.
--
-- The rule: the check and the write are ONE statement. A SELECT followed by an UPDATE
