-- with the version in a WHERE clause is correct; a SELECT, a comparison in the
-- application, and an UPDATE without the condition is not, and no isolation level
-- makes the second form correct.
-- PESSIMISTIC LOCKING, and the two rules that make it survivable.
--
-- Rule 1: lock in a global order. Every transaction acquires its locks sorted by a
-- stable key, so two transactions can never hold-and-wait on each other. This is the
-- only deadlock prevention that actually works, and it costs a sort in the
-- application.
SELECT * FROM inventory
 WHERE sku = ANY (:skus')
 ORDER BY sku                    -- the lock order IS the key order
   FOR UPDATE;
-- Without the ORDER BY, the plan decides the order, and the plan can change with
-- statistics, and a deadlocking deadlock appears after a data distribution shift.
--
-- Rule 2: never hold a lock across a call to anything else. A lock held during an HTTP
-- request, a message publish or a sleep is a deadlock waiting for a network timeout,
-- and it is also the most common cause of idle-in-transaction.
--
-- The two lock-wait options, and the difference between them:
SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL
    FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;      -- take what is free, leave the rest. A queue.
SELECT * FROM inventory WHERE sku = :sku'
    FOR UPDATE NOWAIT;         -- fail immediately if held. An interactive edit.
-- The default is neither: block until the other transaction finishes, holding a
-- connection. In a request handler that is a latency spike with no error, and
-- under load it is the pool exhaustion that precedes the outage.
--
-- MySQL has neither keyword. The equivalent of NOWAIT is innodb_lock_wait_timeout
-- set to a small value; the equivalent of SKIP LOCKED is a locking read inside a
-- loop that catches error 1205, which is worse and which is why SKIP LOCKED was
-- added. Always set innodb_lock_wait_timeout deliberately: the default of 50 seconds
-- is longer than most HTTP timeouts, so the caller has already given up by the time
-- the database returns.
SET SESSION innodb_lock_wait_timeout = 5;

Decision. The reference model uses optimistic concurrency with a version integer for the order, subscription and payment tables, and pessimistic locking only for the aggregate-root rows that enforce a set-level invariant — the tenant_usage row and the outbox claim. The reason is arithmetic: the order path is 1 000 writes per second on rows that are touched by one user each, so a version column costs one integer per write and detects every conflict; pessimistic locking on the same path would add a lock to 1 000 transactions a second, each of which can block a request thread for the duration of somebody else’s transaction.

Test the retry path, because it is the part that only runs under contention. A concurrency test that opens two connections, holds both transactions open, commits one and asserts that the other raises a retryable error and then succeeds — run it on every engine, in CI, permanently. A retry loop that is wrong in the direction of not retrying fails as a production anomaly, and one that retries too much fails as an outage, and neither is covered by a functional test.

7.5 Idempotent writes, upsert semantics and the outbox

Three problems that are usually solved in the application and belong in the schema: making a retry safe, making a concurrent create not duplicate, and making a committed change and a published message atomic with each other. Each has a per-engine form and each has a specific way of being done wrong.

-- IDEMPOTENCY. A payment API must survive a client retry, and the client cannot tell
-- a timeout from a failure, so it will retry. The schema makes the retry harmless.
CREATE TABLE idempotency_record (
    tenant_id        bigint NOT NULL,
    idempotency_key  varchar(128) NOT NULL,
    -- The hash of the request body. Same key, different body is a client bug, and
-- silently returning the first response would hide it.
    request_hash     bytea NOT NULL,
    -- The response, stored. A retry returns this rather than re-executing, so the
-- response is byte-identical rather than merely equivalent.
    response_status  int,
    response_body    jsonb,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, idempotency_key),
    created_at        timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT ck_idem_response CHECK (
        (response_status IS NULL) OR (response_status BETWEEN 200 AND 299))
);

-- The write, in one transaction. The unique constraint is the arbiter, so two
-- concurrent retries of the same request produce one payment, not two.
INSERT INTO idempotency_record (tenant_id, idempotency_key, request_hash)
VALUES (:tenant, :key', digest(:body', 'sha256'))
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, idempotency_key) DO NOTHING
RETURNING true AS i_was_first;

-- If i_was_first, do the work and store the response. If not, the row exists:
--   response_status IS NULL  -> another attempt is in flight: return 409 Retry-After
--   response_status = 201     -> return the stored response, byte for byte
-- and if request_hash differs from the stored one, return 422 and log it: the
-- same key with a different payload is a client defect, not a retry.

-- The two details that make it correct rather than merely present:
--   1. the record is inserted in the SAME transaction as the payment, so a rollback
--      of the payment also removes the claim and the next retry is first again;
--   2. the record is retained for at least as long as the client might retry, which is
--      longer than anyone believes: 24 hours for a payment API is a common minimum,
--      and a purge job that removes the records is what makes the guarantee expire.
-- UPSERT, per engine, and the specific defect of each form.
--
-- PostgreSQL: ON CONFLICT. The most precise of the three, because the conflict target
-- is explicit and the SET clause can be conditional.
INSERT INTO tenant_usage (tenant_id, active_users, order_lines)
VALUES (:tenant, 1, 0)
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id) DO UPDATE
   SET active_users = tenant_usage.active_users + 1
 WHERE tenant_usage.active_users < :seat_limit';   -- conditional, and the
                                          -- WHERE makes the violation a
                                          -- no-op instead of an error
-- Two defects to know:
--   * a multi-row INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE that touches the same key twice
--     raises "ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE command cannot affect row a second time". The fix
--     is to aggregate the input first, which is also faster.
--   * DO NOTHING does not tell you whether it did nothing, unless you add RETURNING,
--     which returns zero rows. Checking the row count is the portable way.
-- MERGE exists from PostgreSQL 15 and has the same limitations, more syntax, and a
-- history of surprising behaviour on the WHEN MATCHED side. Prefer ON CONFLICT.
--
-- MySQL: INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE. The conflict target is IMPLICIT: any
-- unique key can trigger it, so a duplicate on the wrong key takes the UPDATE branch.
-- That is a correctness hazard on a table with two unique constraints.
INSERT INTO tenant_usage (tenant_id, active_users, order_lines)
VALUES (:tenant, 1, 0)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    active_users = tenant_usage.active_users + 1;
-- The VALUES() function is DEPRECATED from 8.0.20 in favour of a row alias:
INSERT INTO t (a, b) VALUES (1, 2) AS new
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE b = new.b + 1;
--
-- DO NOT USE REPLACE INTO. It is DELETE followed by INSERT, which:
--   * fires DELETE triggers and not INSERT triggers, in the wrong order;
--   * cascades to every child row, deleting and re-inserting them;
--   * loses the row identity, breaking every foreign key that references it;
--   * and can violate a foreign key from a table that happens to reference the row.
-- It is a data-loss operation that reads like an upsert, and it has been in MySQL
-- for twenty years.
--
-- SQL Server: MERGE. It is the most expressive of the three and the one with the most
-- documented defects: a MERGE combined with an INSERT trigger has produced wrong
-- results, HOLDLOCK is required and forgotten, and the statement has a unique plan
-- cache behaviour that surprises people. The defensible alternative for the two
-- common cases:
UPDATE t SET b = src.b
  FROM src JOIN t ON t.id = src.id
 WHERE t.b <> src.b;
INSERT INTO t (id, b)
SELECT src.id, src.b FROM src
 WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM t WHERE t.id = src.id);
-- Two statements, so a race between them is possible -- which is why they must be
-- wrapped in SERIALIZABLE, or run against a key-range lock:
--   SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;  or
--   SELECT * FROM t WITH (UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK) WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM src);
-- If MERGE is used, MERGE ... WITH (HOLDLOCK) INTO is not a documented combination; put
-- the hint on the target in a subquery, and test it under concurrency.
-- THE OUTBOX. The problem: a database commit and a broker publish cannot be atomic,
-- because they are two systems. The three candidate designs:
--
--   A. publish inside the transaction     -- a broker failure rolls back a valid order
--   B. publish before the commit           -- a commit failure publishes an event for
--                                            an order that does not exist
--   C. write an outbox row in the same transaction, relay afterwards  <-- this one
--
-- C gives at-least-once delivery with no dual-write problem. It is not exactly-once
-- and it cannot be, without a distributed transaction between the database and the
-- broker, which nobody should want.
--
-- The relay, and the three properties it must have:
--   1. claim atomically, so two relays do not publish the same row
--   2. publish BEFORE marking published, so a crash between them duplicates
--   3. be ordered per aggregate, so two events for the same order cannot arrive swapped
WITH claimed AS (
    SELECT outbox_id FROM outbox
     WHERE published_at IS NULL
       AND created_at < now() - INTERVAL '2 seconds'   -- grace: skip rows a
                                                              -- peer is mid-flight on
     ORDER BY outbox_id
     LIMIT 200
       FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED)
UPDATE outbox o
   SET published_at = clock_timestamp(), attempts = o.attempts + 1
  FROM claimed c
 WHERE o.outbox_id = c.outbox_id
RETURNING o.outbox_id, o.aggregate_id, o.topic, o.payload, o.attempts;
-- The rest of the relay, and the three failure modes that produce incidents.
--
-- 1. Ordering. The broker gives no ordering guarantee across partitions, and this
--    relay reads in outbox_id order, which is insertion order per database. Two
--    events for the same aggregate can therefore be reordered in flight. Fix: partition
--    the topic by aggregate_id, and give the consumer a per-key serialiser.
--
-- 2. Poison messages. A payload that no consumer can process is retried forever and
--    blocks the partition. Fix: a dead-letter topic after N attempts, an alert on the
--    dead-letter depth, and a replay tool. The attempts column above is what the
--    policy reads.
--
-- 3. Consumer idempotency, which is not optional once delivery is at-least-once.
--    The consumer needs its own dedup key. The cheapest correct one is to make the
--    consumer's effect idempotent, which for a projection means an upsert on the natural
--    key rather than an insert, and for a side effect means a dedup table:
CREATE TABLE consumer_dedup (
    consumer     text NOT NULL,
    message_id   uuid NOT NULL,
    processed_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (consumer, message_id)
);
INSERT INTO consumer_dedup VALUES ('billing', :message_id')
ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;   -- 0 rows = already processed, ack and move on

-- Keeping the outbox small. It grows at the publish rate and shrinks at the
-- relay rate, and the difference is the metric that says whether the relay is
-- keeping up. Without a delete job, the table is an unbounded archive of every
-- event ever emitted, which is a write cost and a GDPR liability.
--
-- Delete in batches, oldest first, on the same partial index the relay uses:
DELETE FROM outbox
 WHERE outbox_id IN (
     SELECT outbox_id FROM outbox
      WHERE published_at IS NOT NULL
        AND published_at < now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
      ORDER BY published_at LIMIT 10000)
  RETURNING 1;
-- 7 days is a defensible replay window and it is a business decision, not a technical
-- one. State it in the runbook.
--
SELECT COUNT(*) AS backlog, MIN(created_at) AS oldest,
       now() - MIN(created_at) AS max_lag
  FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL;
-- Alert on max_lag, not on COUNT. A backlog of 200 000 rows that drains in 2 seconds
-- is healthy; a backlog of 12 that is 4 hours old means the relay died on Tuesday.
--
-- When NOT to use an outbox: if change data capture from the write-ahead log is
-- already deployed (Debezium, sqlserver cdc, MySQL binlog, pgoutput). It captures
-- the same guarantee with no application code, at the cost of a tool that must be
-- operated. The decision is whether the team will operate one more thing, and the
-- answer is usually yes only when several services need the events.

Requirement. The outbox is a schema requirement, not an application pattern, for one reason: it is the only way to make the state change and the event atomic, and atomicity is a property of the transaction. An application that inserts an outbox row after committing its business change has re-introduced the dual-write problem with an extra table, and the window between the two statements is where the events are lost. Put the insert inside the transaction, or use log-based capture.

Every outbox needs three alerts wired on day one: relay lag over 30 seconds, any message with attempts above 5, and dead-letter depth above zero. All three are silent conditions by construction — a relay that fails does not throw an exception, it just stops publishing, and the failure is observed by the downstream consumer noticing that nothing is arriving.

7.6 Read-your-writes and the replica lag trap

The last correctness property, and the one that is hardest to test, because the write succeeds on one node and the read succeeds on another and both are correct in isolation. This section covers the three ways to guarantee it, the three ways each engine measures lag, and the failure that follows from reading a replica that is about to be promoted.

-- THE PROBLEM, stated precisely.
--
--   POST /orders        -> primary, commits, returns 201 with order_id 4711
--   GET  /orders/4711   -> replica, 120 ms behind, returns 404
--
-- The client is correct to retry the read. The read is correct for its node. The
-- system as a whole has told the user their order does not exist, immediately after
-- accepting it. In a checkout flow this is a duplicate-order incident; in a
-- dashboard it is a support ticket; in a webhook receiver it is a retry storm.
--
-- There are exactly three honest solutions, and everything else is a heuristic.
--
-- 1. Route the read to the primary. Simple, always correct, costs the read replica.
--    Correct for the read-after-write request itself; wrong for the NEXT request from a
--    different connection, which is the case that actually matters.
-- 2. Sticky routing. A session, a user or a tenant is pinned to the primary for N seconds
--    after any write. Cheap and effective; needs a per-user or per-session timestamp
--    somewhere, and it must be a REDIS or a database row, not process memory.
-- 3. A replication token, returned by the write and sent with the read. Precise, no
--    pinning, and the only one that survives a failover. This is the right one.

-- PostgreSQL: the write returns its WAL position, and the read waits for the
-- replica to have replayed at least that far. The standby reports where it is.
-- on the PRIMARY, in the write path, after COMMIT:
SELECT pg_current_wal_lsn() AS commit_lsn;
-- return commit_lsn to the client as a response header
--
-- on the REPLICA, in the read path, before serving the read:
--   SELECT pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() >= $1::pg_lsn;
-- and if it is false, wait, up to a bound, then serve from the primary. In practice:
SELECT pg_last_wal_replay_lsn(), pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp();
--
-- The precise form, using a logical replication barrier. The primary records a
-- message into the WAL; the replica applies messages in order, so once it has seen
-- this message it has seen the commit that preceded it. This is exact, and it works
-- with any number of replicas, which pg_current_wal_lsn() polling does not.
--   primary:  SELECT pg_logical_emit_message(true, 'routing', :request_id);
--   replica:  waits for the message, then serves the read
-- Caveat: the replica must be consuming that plugin's output, and the emission is a
-- WAL record, so it costs a little on the write path.
-- MySQL: the same idea with GTIDs, and it is the cleanest implementation of the three.
--
-- on the PRIMARY, capture the GTID of the commit:
SELECT @@GLOBAL.gtid_executed AS commit_gtid;
-- return it to the client
--
-- on the REPLICA, before serving the read, wait for it to be applied:
SELECT WAIT_FOR_EXECUTED_GTID_SET('uuid:4711-4799', 2); -- 2 = second timeout
-- Returns 0 on success, 1 on timeout. The replica compares that GTID set against its
-- own gtid_executed, which is updated only when a transaction has been APPLIED, not
-- merely received. That is exactly the guarantee read-your-writes needs, and it is
-- why GTID is the right foundation for it.
--
-- Requires gtid_mode = ON, which is the setting for every modern MySQL deployment and is
-- worth checking before designing around it.
SELECT @@gtid_mode, @@enforce_gtid_consistency, @@binlog_format;

-- SQL Server: an LSN token plus the availability group.
--
-- Read routing is the primary mechanism and it is an application intent, not a URL:
--   ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly  sends the session to a secondary that is online
--   and applies AG Secondary routing rules, which route by replica if there is one.
--
-- The token form, for a read that must follow a write:
--   the write returns sys.fn_xact_... no: the commit LSN is available from the
--   transaction itself, and the durable form is:
SELECT current_lsn FROM sys.dm_tran_database_snapshot;  -- not this
--
-- The value that works is per-replica and is the one to compare against:
SELECT replica_server_name, synchronization_state_desc, sync_state_desc,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), last_hardened_lsn) AS hardened,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), last_received_lsn)   AS received,
       last_commit_time
  FROM sys.dm_hadr_database_replica_states
 WHERE is_local = 1 OR database_id = DB_ID();
-- Read from a secondary only once last_received_lsn is at or beyond the token the
-- write returned. last_hardened_lsn is stronger still: it means the secondary has
-- fsynced it, so a failover there would not lose it.
--
-- A synchronous commit is the blunt alternative: every commit waits for a secondary to
-- acknowledge, so the secondary is never behind and read-your-writes is free. It
-- costs one network round trip per commit and reduces write throughput to that of
-- the slowest replica. Section 1.5 prices it.
-- MEASURING LAG, and the three ways each engine reports it, two of which are wrong.
--
-- PostgreSQL, the correct version. pg_stat_replication.replay_lag is the time spent
-- replaying, which is ZERO when the standby is idle, and therefore reports no lag on an
-- idle replica while the replica is an hour behind. Measure the WAL POSITION instead:
SELECT client_addr, application_name, state, sync_state,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), sent_lsn) AS sent,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), write_lsn) AS written,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), flush_lsn) AS flushed,
       CONVERT(varchar(30), replay_lsn) AS replayed,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)) AS replay_gap_bytes,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn) AS replay_gap_bytes_raw
  FROM pg_stat_replication;
-- On the STANDBY, the honest number is the same subtraction from the other side:
--   received:  pg_last_wal_receive_lsn() - pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()
--   applied:   pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() - pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp()
--   write:     pg_last_wal_receive_lsn() - pg_last_wal_write_lsn()
-- A replica can be receiving the WAL perfectly and not applying it for an hour because
-- of a long-running query (max_standby_streaming_delay cancels it) or because of a
-- recovery conflict. Both look like "replication is fine" from the primary.
--
-- The conflict counters, which is how a query killed on the standby announces itself:
-- recovery conflicts: queries cancelled on the standby by max_standby_*_delay
SELECT datname, conflicts
       stats_reset FROM pg_stat_database_conflicts;
--
-- MySQL: Seconds_Behind_Source is the time since the LAST EVENT, not a lag. On an
-- idle primary it grows without bound while the replica is perfectly current.
-- It is also NULL during a restart, and it is documented as unreliable. Use the GTID
-- sets:
-- on the source:   SELECT @@global.gtid_executed;   -- the full executed set
-- on the replica: SELECT @@global.gtid_executed;   -- the set it has applied
-- and the difference is the true set of transactions the replica is missing, which can
-- be computed with GTID_SUBSET() rather than estimated:
SELECT GTID_SUBTRACT('uuid:1-1000', @@GLOBAL.gtid_executed) AS missing_from_replica;
--
-- SQL Server: the same shape. sync_state_desc = SYNCHRONIZED means the secondary has
-- hardened everything, which is the guarantee you want; SYNCHRONIZING means it has
-- not. performance_schema.replication_applier_status_by_worker gives per-worker
-- positions, and the per-database LSNs above give the byte-level gap.
--
-- MySQL applier, for the same reason:
SELECT channel_name, service_state, last_queued_transaction,
       last_queued_transaction_end_queue_timestamp, last_applied_transaction,
       last_applied_transaction_end_apply_timestamp
  FROM performance_schema.replication_applier_status_by_worker;
-- THE FAILURE THAT FOLLOWS FROM THE TRAP: a read from a replica that is then promoted.
--
--   t=0     a write is committed on the primary
--   t=0.2   a read is served from a replica that is 400 ms behind   -- 404, or stale data
--   t=0.5   the primary fails; the replica is promoted
--   t=0.5   the 400 ms of writes that had not been replicated are GONE, permanently
--
-- A read from a replica is therefore a claim that the read was answered from a copy
-- that is not the system of record, and that claim is falsified the moment the replica
-- becomes the system of record. The two are the same thing, and a design that routes
-- reads to replicas is a design that accepts a small, bounded probability of data loss
-- in exchange for read capacity.
--
-- Four properties that make that trade explicit rather than accidental:
--
--   1. State the RPO the asynchronous replication implies (section 1.5), and state
--      it in the same document as the SLO that depends on the replica reads.
--   2. Never serve a write acknowledgement from a replica, and never serve a read that
--      follows a write for that same entity from a replica (section 7.6 first table).
--   3. Make the replica read a distinguishable thing: a separate connection string, a
--      separate port, a separate pool, and a metric. A read that looks like a primary
--      read is the one nobody audits.
--   4. At failover, stop the application. A promoted replica that accepts writes while the
--      application is still writing to a primary hostname is a split brain, and the
--      reconciliation afterwards is a data-loss incident rather than a failover.

-- The connection pool discipline that makes all of this work, and that is skipped
-- constantly:
--
--   * a transaction MUST use one connection. Two connections in one transaction means
--     the two halves see different snapshots, and on PostgreSQL the second half is at
--     READ COMMITTED regardless of what the first half set, because SET TRANSACTION
--     applies per transaction.
--   * a session setting MUST be applied on every checkout from the pool, not once at
--     connection creation. statement_timeout, lock_timeout, transaction isolation,
--     time zone and search_path are all per session, and a pool that sets them once
--     produces behaviour that depends on which connection a request happened to get.
--   * one request, one connection, no holding an idle connection between a write and a
--     read, because the routing decision is made per checkout and a read after a
--     release may land on a different node than the write did.
StrategyPrecisionCostSurvives failoverUse for
Read-your-write routing to the primaryExact for the same request; none for the next oneRead capacity on the primaryYesThe default. Everything else is an optimisation of this
Sticky routing for N secondsBounded by NA timestamp per user or session, somewhere durableYes, if the stamp is durableUI flows where the next request follows a write: checkout, settings, wizard steps
Replication tokenExactA round trip to the replica per readYes, and it is the only option that also survives a replica being re-seededAnything with a correctness requirement: billing, permissions, provisioning
Eventual consistency, acceptedNoneNoneYesDashboards, search indexes, recommendation feeds — and only where the domain can tolerate it in writing, not only in a design document

Caveats. Two traps that are specific to connection pools and that account for most read-your-writes incidents in practice. First, a pool with more than one replica connection will serve two reads of the same row from two different replicas with two different lags, so two components on one page can disagree. Second, a retry that moves a read to a different connection moves it to a different node, so a sticky-session mechanism implemented in the pool is defeated by any client-side retry. Both are solved by making the routing decision from the token in the request rather than from the connection’s identity.

Exercises 7

  1. Reproduce the write-skew failure from section 7.3 on all three engines with two sessions, using each engine’s default isolation and then SERIALIZABLE. Record which engine failed, which raised a retryable error, and which silently produced the inconsistent state.
  2. Write a retry loop for each engine that retries only the correct error codes, and prove it by injecting each of five errors: two retryable and three not. The non-retryable cases must surface immediately, and a test must assert that they do rather than assuming it.
  3. Add an idempotency record to a payment path in a schema you can reach. Fire six concurrent requests with the same idempotency key and assert that exactly one payment exists and that all six responses are byte-identical. Then send the same key with a different body and record what the system does.
  4. Build the outbox relay, kill the process between publishing a message and marking the row published, and record the resulting duplicate. Then show that a consumer with a dedup table absorbs it, and measure the relay’s maximum lag under a synthetic write burst of 10 000 events per second.
  5. Measure replication lag correctly on each engine you have access to — WAL position, GTID set difference, and LSN respectively — and compare each with the metric the tool reports by default. Record the case in which the default metric reports no lag while the true lag is minutes.
  6. Implement read-your-writes with a replication token on one engine. Write the test: write on the primary, read through the replica immediately, and assert the read sees the write. Then increase the write rate until the replica falls a second behind, and confirm the test still passes for a token-ful read and fails for a token-less one.

8 · Migrations and Schema Evolution

8.1 Schema change as the highest-risk operation8.2 Expand and contract as a deployable sequence8.3 The three-phase rename, with real DDL8.4 Online DDL: what each engine will do without a lock8.5 Batched backfills and adding NOT NULL without a rewrite8.6 Migration tooling, transactionality and the review checklist

A schema change is the only routine operation that can take a healthy, fully redundant, correctly indexed production database offline while every individual statement in it returns success. This chapter covers the risk model, the expand-and-contract pattern that turns a rename into four boring deploys, the online-DDL capability of each engine stated precisely, batched backfills that finish in bounded time, and the migration tools with their materially different transactional guarantees.

8.1 Schema change as the highest-risk operation

Rolling back an application is one command. Rolling back a schema change is a separate, later, differently-shaped operation, and on a large table it may not be possible at all. That asymmetry is the entire reason this chapter exists.

The failure mode is not that DDL is hard to write. It is that the destructive DDL and the reversible DDL are the same statement. Consider the most common schema change in a SaaS product: ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN external_ref text is safe in every engine. The next sentence in the same pull request — DROP COLUMN external_ref — is irreversible, and once it has been applied the data is gone and no rollback of the binary will bring it back. The pull request that contained both was reviewed as a unit, and the two statements have opposite risk profiles.

OperationReversibleBlocks writesWrites every rowCan be done online
ADD COLUMN nullable, no default✓ drop the column✓ briefly, ACCESS EXCLUSIVE or ALTER lock✗✓ on all three
ADD COLUMN with a volatile default✓✓ for the duration of the rewrite✓✗ on PostgreSQL below 11; ✓ from 11 with a non-volatile default
DROP COLUMN✗ data is destroyed✓✓ (marks pages UNUSED, does not reclaim)✓, and cheap, but the cost is paid later by VACUUM FULL or a cluster rewrite
DROP INDEX✓ recreate from DDL✗✗✓
CREATE INDEX non-concurrently✓ drop it✓ for the whole build✓ read only✓ PostgreSQL CONCURRENTLY; ✓ MySQL ALGORITHM=INPLACE; ✓ SQL Server ONLINE=ON (Enterprise only)
Adding a foreign key✓ drop the constraint✓✓ validation scan, plus a lock on the referenced tablePartially — MySQL pt-online-schema-change or foreign_key_checks=0; PostgreSQL NOT VALID then VALIDATE CONSTRAINT
Changing a column type, e.g. text → varchar(64)✓ only if widening✓ for the rewrite✓✗ on all three without a shadow-column technique
Adding a CHECK constraint✓✓✓ scan✓ via NOT VALID (PostgreSQL) or WITH NOCHECK (SQL Server) then validate
Adding a NOT NULL to an existing column✓✓✓ full table scan, and a rewrite on several engines✓ if the column was added NOT NULL from the start, otherwise not directly
Renaming a table or column✓ in name only✓ exclusive lock✗ metadata only, but every dependent view, function, trigger and ORM mapping is invalidated✗ — this is the case section 8.3 exists for

The second reason schema change is the highest-risk operation is that it is the one operation whose cost is not visible in a test environment. A CREATE INDEX on a 400-row staging table takes 4 ms. The same statement on a 900-million-row payment_event table takes 41 minutes and reads 60 GB into the buffer pool, evicting the working set and pushing every concurrent query’s response time up by an order of magnitude for the duration. Nothing about the statement changed. The row count is the entire difference between a no-op and an outage, and the only defence is to measure against a production-shaped dataset.

The third reason is that DDL participates in the transaction system in ways application code does not, and the three engines resolve this differently enough that a migration written for one is a hazard on another. On PostgreSQL and SQL Server, DDL is transactional: it takes the schema lock, does the work, and rolls back cleanly if the transaction aborts. On MySQL, DDL causes an implicit commit — the running transaction is committed before the DDL executes and the DDL is not rolled back if a later statement fails. A migration script that assumes transactional DDL will leave a partially applied schema on MySQL, and the recovery path is a hand-written compensating script rather than a rollback.

-- Probe: what is holding the lock your DDL needs, right now?
-- PostgreSQL 18. Queue the ALTER in a second session and watch who it waits behind.
SELECT a.pid,
       a.state,
       a.wait_event_type,                                  -- Lock is the one that matters here
       now() - a.xact_start AS xact_age,
       now() - a.query_start AS query_age,
       left(a.query, 90) AS query,
       pg_blocking_pids(a.pid) AS blocked_by
  FROM pg_stat_activity a
 WHERE a.datname = current_database()
   AND a.backend_type = 'client backend'
   AND a.pid <> pg_backend_pid()
 ORDER BY a.xact_start NULLS LAST;

-- MySQL 9.x. data_locks / data_lock_waits are the 8.0 replacement for
-- information_schema.innodb_locks, which was removed in 8.0.
SELECT r.trx_id            AS waiting_trx,
       r.trx_mysql_thread_id AS waiting_thread,
       r.trx_query           AS waiting_query,
       b.trx_mysql_thread_id AS blocking_thread,
       b.trx_query           AS blocking_query
  FROM performance_schema.data_lock_waits w
  JOIN information_schema.innodb_trx r ON r.trx_id = w.REQUESTING_TRX_ID
  JOIN information_schema.innodb_trx b ON b.trx_id = w.BLOCKING_TRX_ID;

-- SQL Server 2025. sys.dm_exec_requests carries the wait type; a DDL
-- waiting on a schema-modification lock shows RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE or LCK_M_*.
SELECT r.session_id,
       r.status,
       r.command,
       r.wait_type,
       r.wait_time,
       r.blocking_session_id,
       r.wait_resource,
       r.query_time / 1000.0 AS wait_seconds
  FROM sys.dm_exec_requests r
 WHERE r.session_id <> @@SPID
   AND r.database_id = DB_ID()
 ORDER BY r.total_elapsed_time DESC;

The three queries are deliberately different in what they return. PostgreSQL gives you the blocking PID chain directly via pg_blocking_pids, which is the single most useful function in incident response and has no exact equivalent elsewhere. MySQL requires a two-table self-join because the wait graph is exposed as a separate table rather than a column. SQL Server gives you blocking_session_id but not the chain, so a blocking chain longer than one requires walking it yourself with a recursive CTE over sys.dm_exec_requests.

Decision rule. Treat every DDL statement as if it takes an exclusive lock on the table for its full duration, and only then improve it using the engine-specific allowances in section 8.4. A migration that is safe under the pessimistic assumption is always safe; a migration that is safe only because of an allowance is one engine upgrade away from an outage. Where the pessimistic assumption is unacceptable — a 900-million-row table and a 41-minute build — the answer is not a cleverer DDL statement but the shadow-table technique in section 8.3.

8.2 Expand and contract as a deployable sequence

Expand-and-contract replaces a single breaking change with a sequence of changes that are individually backward compatible, so that for the entire window in which the old and new code are both deployed, both schemas are sufficient to serve both.

The pattern has three phases and the middle one has an arbitrary duration. Expand adds the new shape without removing the old. Migrate moves data and shifts reads. Contract removes the old shape, but only after evidence that nothing references it. The critical property is that every phase is independently deployable and independently revertible, and that the application tolerates any prefix of the sequence. The corollary is that the old column may be written by nothing and read by nothing for weeks before it is dropped, and that this is not waste — that period is the window during which a rollback is still cheap.

-- The worked change: rename sales_order.status to sales_order.order_state,
-- and change its domain from free text to a checked enumeration.
-- Four deploys. Each is separately deployable and separately revertible.

-- Deploy 1: EXPAND. The new column exists, is nullable, and is not yet
-- authoritative. The old column is untouched and still fully live.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    ADD COLUMN order_state text NULL;

-- A trigger keeps the two columns in step for as long as both are live.
-- It is deleted in deploy 4, and it is the only piece of machinery that
-- makes the intermediate states safe for concurrent writers.
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION sales_order_state_sync() RETURNS trigger
LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $fn$
BEGIN
    IF NEW.order_state IS NOT NULL AND NEW.status IS NOT NULL
       AND NEW.order_state <> NEW.status THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'order_state and status disagree: % vs %',
              NEW.order_state, NEW.status;
    END IF;
    IF NEW.order_state IS NOT NULL THEN
        NEW.status := NEW.order_state;
    ELSE
        NEW.order_state := NEW.status;
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $fn$;

CREATE TRIGGER trg_sales_order_state_sync
    BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE ON sales_order
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION sales_order_state_sync();

-- Deploy 2: MIGRATE, part one. Backfill, in batches. See section 8.5.
UPDATE sales_order
   SET order_state = status
 WHERE order_id BETWEEN 1 AND 50000
   AND order_state IS NULL;

-- Deploy 2: MIGRATE, part two. Shift reads to the new column, behind a
-- flag. Both columns still exist, so the flag can be flipped back instantly.
-- (The application-level read is shown in the exercises; the DDL that makes
-- the flip safe is the constraint below.)
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    ADD CONSTRAINT ck_sales_order_order_state
    CHECK (order_state IN ('placed','paid','shipped','cancelled','refunded')) NOT VALID;

-- Deploy 3: CONTRACT begins. Prove the old column is dead before touching it.
SELECT count(*)
  FROM pg_stat_statements
 WHERE query LIKE '%status%'
   AND query LIKE '%sales_order%'
   AND calls > 0
   AND stats_since > now() - interval '14 days';

-- Deploy 4: CONTRACT completes. Drop the synchronisation machinery first,
-- then the old column. Order matters: dropping the trigger first means the
-- two columns can diverge during the window, which is a signal, not a loss.
DROP TRIGGER trg_sales_order_state_sync ON sales_order;
DROP FUNCTION sales_order_state_sync();
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    DROP COLUMN status;

The RAISE EXCEPTION in the trigger is not defensive decoration. During deploy 2 the application writes both columns for some requests and only one for others, depending on which code path ran. A trigger that silently picks one value over the other would let the two diverge, and the divergence would be discovered weeks later by a reconciliation report. A trigger that refuses the write converts a silent data-quality defect into a loud, immediately reproducible error at the moment it happens.

The rollback asymmetry that trips teams. After deploy 3 the old binary can no longer be deployed, because it writes a column that no longer exists. The window of free rollback closes at deploy 3, not at deploy 4. Teams routinely assume the window closes at the end and discover otherwise during an incident. Write the closing date into the migration itself as a comment, and set an automated check that fails if the contract phase has not run within its stated window — an undeployed contract is a permanent tax, and in the case of a text column dropped on PostgreSQL it is also a permanent bloat debt because the space is not returned until the table is rewritten.

The trigger in deploy 1 is the one component that is easy to get wrong at scale. A row-level trigger on the highest-write table in the system executes on every write, including writes that touch neither column, unless the trigger function declares its columns with WHEN (...). On a table taking 40 000 writes per second, an unnecessary function call is a measurable tax and a lock-ordering hazard, because the trigger runs inside the writer’s transaction and holds the row lock a moment longer. The narrowed form is BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OF order_state, status ON sales_order, with a WHEN clause carrying the null tests. Where the engine allows it — SQL Server with a filtered DML trigger, MySQL 9 with a BEFORE UPDATE trigger that checks NEW against OLD first — the same narrowing applies.

A sequence that also fixes a bug. Expand-and-contract is not only for renames. Adding a column, adding a table, widening a constraint, introducing a new enumeration value, splitting one table into two, and adding a non-nullable column with a backfill are all the same pattern applied to different shapes. The test of whether a change is correctly decomposed is simple: can the currently deployed binary run against the schema as it stands after every individual step? If the answer is no at any point, the sequence is wrong, regardless of how carefully the steps were described.

8.3 The three-phase rename, with real DDL

A rename looks like one operation and is actually four, because the new name must exist, the data must exist under both names, the reads must move, and only then may the old name go. The intermediate state is a table with both a status and an order_state column, which is redundant and is the point.

The three-phase rename is named for its three deploys and has four DDL phases, because the backfill is separated from the column addition. Phase one adds the new column. Phase two backfills it. Phase three switches reads and writes. Phase four removes the old column. The redundancy is temporary and it is bounded by a date, and it is the only structure in which a rename is reversible at every step.

What makes the naive ALTER TABLE ... RENAME COLUMN dangerous is not the lock, which is brief and metadata-only on all three engines. It is the dependency invalidation. A rename invalidates every view, materialised view, function, trigger, check constraint, foreign key, statistics object and ORM mapping that names the column, and in different ways on each engine:

EngineViewsFunctions / triggersORM mappingCan it be fully automatic?
PostgreSQL 18Rewritten automatically if the view’s stored parse tree can be adjusted; a view referencing another view is followed recursively, and pg_depend is consultedNot rewritten. The body is stored as text for LANGUAGE plpgsql and as a parse tree for LANGUAGE sql; the plpgsql case fails at execution, not at rename time, which is the worst possible failure modeNot at all; the mapping is in application code✗ — pg_depend gives you the list, but you must fix every plpgsql function by hand and re-validate
MySQL 9.xRewritten automatically; a view is stored as an unparsed SHOW CREATE VIEW string and is re-parsed on renameRewritten for triggers; stored routines are not rewritten and fail at next executionNot at all✗ — and there is no dependency view, so the list must be reconstructed from information_schema
SQL Server 2025Not rewritten; the schema-bound reference is checked at creation and the view becomes unresolvable, which is visible immediately at sys.sql_expression_dependenciesNot rewritten; the object is marked untrusted or fails at executionNot at all✗ — but sys.sql_expression_dependencies and sys.dm_sql_referencing_entities enumerate the dependents precisely, which makes this the easiest of the three to audit
-- Phase 1: the new name exists. Nullable, no default, no constraint.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN order_state text NULL;

-- Phase 2: backfill. Batched, resumable, and written so that a row that is
-- updated concurrently during the backfill is caught by the trigger, not lost.
-- The whole loop is in section 8.5; this is the statement inside it.
UPDATE sales_order
   SET order_state = status
 WHERE order_id > :cursor
   AND order_id <= :cursor + :batch
   AND order_state IS NULL;

-- Phase 2b: the constraint is added NOT VALID. On PostgreSQL this takes a
-- brief exclusive lock but performs NO scan of existing rows. Rows written
-- after this statement are checked; existing rows are checked on validation.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    ADD CONSTRAINT ck_so_order_state
    CHECK (order_state IN ('placed','paid','shipped','cancelled','refunded')) NOT VALID;

-- Validation takes a SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE lock: it does not block reads
-- and does not block writes. It may run for as long as the table takes to scan.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    VALIDATE CONSTRAINT ck_so_order_state;

-- Phase 3: NOT NULL without a table rewrite. See section 8.5 for the
-- full three-statement sequence and why NOT VALID is not available here.
--
-- Phase 4: drop the old name, and only then drop the machinery.
ALTER TABLE sales_order DROP COLUMN status;

-- The same four phases, MySQL 9.x. The DDL is one statement at a time because
-- each causes an implicit commit; a failed ALTER leaves the schema exactly here.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN order_state VARCHAR(32) NULL ALGORITHM=INSTANT;
UPDATE sales_order SET order_state = status WHERE order_state IS NULL LIMIT 10000;
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    MODIFY COLUMN order_state VARCHAR(32) NOT NULL ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;
ALTER TABLE sales_order DROP COLUMN status, ALGORITHM=INSTANT;

-- The same four phases, SQL Server 2025. WITH (ONLINE = ON) is Enterprise
-- only; on any other edition the statement fails immediately rather than
-- silently taking a lock, which is at least a safe failure.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD order_state NVARCHAR(32) NULL
    WITH (ONLINE = ON, DATA_DELETION = ON);   -- DATA_DELETION = OFF is the default and self-join is rare
UPDATE sales_order SET order_state = status WHERE order_state IS NULL;
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    ADD CONSTRAINT ck_so_order_state CHECK (order_state IN (N'placed',N'paid',N'shipped',N'cancelled',N'refunded')) WITH NOCHECK;
ALTER TABLE sales_order ALTER COLUMN order_state NVARCHAR(32) NOT NULL;
ALTER TABLE sales_order DROP COLUMN status;

Three engine divergences in that listing are worth naming individually. MySQL’s ALGORITHM clause is an assertion, not a hint. If the requested algorithm is not available, the statement fails rather than falling back — which is exactly what you want, because a silent fallback is a lock you did not budget for. SQL Server’s WITH NOCHECK is a persistent lie. Unlike PostgreSQL’s NOT VALID, which is a transient state that must be cleared, NOCHECK marks the constraint as untrusted and that marking survives until an explicit WITH CHECK CHECK CONSTRAINT — and the optimiser will subsequently assume the constraint holds, producing a wrong plan on any table that actually violates it. Use it only when a concurrent VALIDATE is scheduled, and record that fact in the migration. SQL Server’s DATA_DELETION = OFF default means a self-join is required for many online index operations, and that self-join is where the operation quietly becomes offline.

Renaming across engines is not symmetric, and the asymmetry is a reason not to. sp_rename on SQL Server refuses to rename a column if any object depends on it, including a non-schema-bound view, and returns a message naming the dependents. PostgreSQL renames unconditionally and defers the failure. MySQL renames unconditionally and may not tell you. Of the three, only SQL Server makes the rename a conversation rather than a leap of faith. Where a rename is genuinely required, the portable sequence is always the four-phase one above, never the single statement.

8.4 Online DDL: what each engine will do without a lock

"Online" is not a property of an operation. It is a property of an operation on a particular engine at a particular version on a particular table, and the honest formulation is a table of exactly which lock is taken and for how long.

All three engines have made large improvements here, and all three still have cases that require a rewrite or an exclusive lock. The vocabulary is also inconsistent, which is why teams get into trouble: PostgreSQL has locks you can wait out and a small set of operations that are metadata-only; MySQL has an ALGORITHM enum that you assert; SQL Server has an ONLINE hint that is edition-gated. The correct engineering response is to state, per migration, which of the three is being relied upon and what happens when the assumption is wrong.

Operation on a large tablePostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.x (InnoDB)SQL Server 2025
Add nullable column✓ ACCESS EXCLUSIVE held for milliseconds only; no table scan; catalog-only✓ ALGORITHM=INSTANT; also INPLACE. Nothing after the position is moved✓ WITH (ONLINE = ON); Enterprise only. Adds a nullable column without a rewrite
Add column with a default✓ only if the default is not volatile. A constant or now() is stored in the catalog and the value is materialised on read. random() is evaluated per row and rewrites the table✓ INSTANT for a column added at the end, since 8.0.29. A column inserted in the middle is at best INPLACE with a table rebuild✓ not — a default constraint with a non-NULL value requires a size-of-data operation. Add the column nullable, backfill, then add the default
Drop column✓ metadata-only; the space is not reclaimed. Attaches a DROPPED attribute so the column is skipped by SELECT *✓ INSTANT; from 8.0.29 the InnoDB redo format records dropped columns without a rebuild✓ metadata only. Space is reclaimed at the next page split or heap compaction
Create index✓ CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY. Cannot run inside a transaction block, may fail and leave an INVALID index that must be dropped and retried, and takes a brief SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE lock that blocks other DDL✓ ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE; a brief exclusive metadata lock at start and end. ALGORITHM=COPY blocks writes for the whole build and is the default for several index types✓ CREATE INDEX ... WITH (ONLINE = ON); Enterprise only. On Standard and Web it fails instantly. All supported index types in 2025 are online-capable
Change column type✗ full rewrite with ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, unless the change is binary-coercible — widening varchar(n) to varchar(m) is catalog-only✗ ALGORITHM=COPY; INPLACE is supported only for a small set of VARCHAR length increases✗ size-of-data operation. ALTER COLUMN on a non-LOB column is online in Enterprise with restrictions on NULL and default changes
Add foreign keyPartially — ADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID skips the scan, then VALIDATE CONSTRAINT runs a SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE validation. It also takes locks on the referenced table to install the triggerPartially — foreign_key_checks = 0 skips validation entirely, including for existing rows, which is a correctness decision not a performance one✓ WITH NOCHECK then CHECK CONSTRAINT; online on Enterprise
Add check constraint✓ NOT VALID then VALIDATE CONSTRAINT — no blocking of reads or writes at any point✓ ALGORITHM=INPLACE; the constraint is checked for new rows only until a repair validates it✓ WITH NOCHECK then WITH CHECK CHECK CONSTRAINT
Set NOT NULL on an existing column✗ full scan under ACCESS EXCLUSIVE. Section 8.5 gives the workaround✗ INPLACE is permitted and rebuilds the table without copying data twice, but it takes a metadata lock for the duration✗ unless the column has a filtered unique index proving non-nullability, in which case it is catalog-only
Add a partition✓ attaching a new partition takes a short lock; existing data is not moved. DETACH PARTITION ... CONCURRENTLY avoids the long lock but leaves the range unfilled✗ ALTER TABLE ... ADD PARTITION reorganises; ALTER TABLE ... EXCHANGE PARTITION is the online equivalent✓ SWITCH to an aligned partition is metadata-only and the standard technique for range lifecycle
-- PostgreSQL 18: the three online forms, and how to verify which one you got.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN source_channel text NULL;
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN imported_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now();  -- catalog-only: now() is STABLE, not VOLATILE
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ix_sales_order_placed_tenant
    ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC);

-- CONCURRENTLY builds the index in two table scans with the work split,
-- waits for transactions that could see either snapshot, and can leave an
-- INVALID index behind. INVALID indexes are maintained on write and never
-- used for reads: pure cost. Find them and fix them.
SELECT n.nspname AS schema, c.relname AS index_name,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(c.oid)) AS size,
       i.indisvalid, i.indisready
  FROM pg_index i
  JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
  JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace
 WHERE NOT i.indisvalid AND n.nspname NOT IN ('pg_catalog','information_schema')
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(c.oid) DESC;

-- MySQL 9.x: always assert ALGORITHM and LOCK. If the assertion cannot be
-- honoured the statement errors instead of quietly locking the table.
ALTER TABLE payment_event
    ADD COLUMN processor_ref VARCHAR(64) NULL ALGORITHM=INSTANT;
ALTER TABLE payment_event
    ADD INDEX ix_payment_event_tenant_created (tenant_id, created_at)
    ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;
ALTER TABLE payment_event
    MODIFY COLUMN amount DECIMAL(14,2) ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;

-- The dry run: run the ALTER with ALGORITHM=INSTANT against a clone and read
-- the "Note" line. "ALGORITHM=INSTANT is not supported" means the real run
-- will either fail or, worse, be written without the clause.
SET @sql = 'ALTER TABLE payment_event ADD COLUMN processor_ref VARCHAR(64) NULL, ALGORITHM=INSTANT';
PREPARE stmt FROM @sql;
EXECUTE stmt;
EXECUTE stmt;
DEALLOCATE PREPARE stmt;

-- SQL Server 2025: ONLINE = ON is Enterprise. On Standard it fails at parse,
-- which is a feature. Verify the edition before writing the migration.
SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('Edition') AS edition,
       CASE WHEN SERVERPROPERTY('Edition') LIKE '%Enterprise%'
            THEN 'ONLINE = ON available'
            ELSE 'ONLINE = ON will raise error 1833' END AS online_ddl;

ALTER TABLE payment_event
    ADD processor_ref NVARCHAR(64) NULL WITH (ONLINE = ON);
CREATE INDEX ix_payment_event_tenant_created
    ON payment_event (tenant_id, created_at DESC)
    WITH (ONLINE = ON, SORT_IN_TEMPDB = ON, MAXDOP = 0);

The PREPARE / EXECUTE pair in the MySQL block is a real technique, not an illustration. MySQL DDL cannot be run inside a transaction, so it cannot be rolled back for a rehearsal; but it can be prepared and executed as a statement, which means the whole migration can be run against a restored clone of production inside an explicit session, and the engine’s Note output tells you which algorithm it actually selected. On a table where the rehearsal picks ALGORITHM=COPY instead of INPLACE, the production run would have blocked writes for the duration of a full copy. This is the only reliable way to discover that before the maintenance window.

The 8 GB rule of thumb, and why it exists. A rewrite or rebuild of a table under roughly 8 GB will usually complete inside a maintenance window, and a rebuild above it usually will not. The number is not physical — it is a statement about how long you are willing to hold a metadata lock while a long queue of queries builds up behind it. On a busy system, a 200 ms lock is survivable and a 20 s lock is an outage, because the queue of lock waiters behind an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE request on PostgreSQL blocks every subsequent query on that table, including pure readers. If a rebuild is genuinely required on a large table, use pgroll or gh-ost rather than holding the lock.

The metadata lock queue is the mechanism nobody plans for. On PostgreSQL, a request for ACCESS EXCLUSIVE does not jump the queue. It queues behind whatever conflicting lock is outstanding, and every subsequent lock request on that table — including a ACCESS SHARE from a plain SELECT — queues behind the pending ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, because granting them would starve the writer. One long-running report started thirty seconds before your migration therefore turns a 200 ms metadata operation into a 30-minute outage, and the queue is invisible unless you look for it. The mitigation is a lock_timeout on every migration, set low, so the DDL gives up rather than accumulating a queue of blocked readers.

8.5 Batched backfills and adding NOT NULL without a rewrite

A backfill is a bulk operation with a latency profile. Executed as a single statement it produces a long transaction, a long lock, an unbounded amount of undo or redo, and a replication stream that every replica must apply before it can be promoted — so it defeats the very replication you built for availability.

The correct shape is always the same: commit frequently, select a bounded range, sleep, repeat. The batch size is a tuning parameter with a real optimum rather than "as small as possible". Very small batches mean a large number of round trips and a very long total wall clock, during which the table is in a mixed state for longer. Very large batches mean long transactions, which on PostgreSQL prevent VACUUM from cleaning up the dead tuples the update creates — and on every engine they lengthen the window in which locks are held and undo or rollback segments are pinned. The usual starting point is 1 000 to 10 000 rows, and it is worth measuring rather than assuming.

-- Batched backfill, PostgreSQL 18. The loop lives in the migration runner,
-- not in the database, so that it can be paused, resumed and observed.
--
-- Properties that matter, in order of importance:
--   1. Bounded transaction: each batch commits, releasing locks and vacuum's
--      ability to reclaim the dead tuples this UPDATE creates.
--   2. Monotonic cursor on the primary key, so a resumed run never re-reads.
--   3. Throttle, so the backfill never exceeds a stated share of capacity.
--   4. statement_timeout as a circuit breaker: a batch that is going wrong
--      aborts instead of running for an hour.

DO $$
DECLARE
    v_batch   int     := 5000;
    v_sleep   interval := '50ms';
    v_cursor  bigint  := 0;
    v_max     bigint;
    v_rows    int;
BEGIN
    SELECT max(order_id) INTO v_max FROM sales_order;
    LOOP
        v_cursor := v_cursor + v_batch;

        SET LOCAL lock_timeout        = '2s';
        SET LOCAL statement_timeout   = '60s';
        SET LOCAL idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s';

        UPDATE sales_order
           SET order_state = status,
                total_excl_tax = total_excl_tax   -- no-op: forces the row version to be
         WHERE order_id >  v_cursor - v_batch
           AND order_id <= v_cursor
           AND order_state IS NULL;

        GET DIAGNOSTICS v_rows = ROW_COUNT;
        EXIT WHEN v_cursor >= v_max OR v_rows = 0;

        -- Jitter, not a fixed sleep: 5 000 concurrent workers that all sleep
        -- 50 ms wake in lockstep and produce exactly the burst the backfill
        -- was meant to avoid.
        PERFORM pg_sleep(v_sleep * (0.5 + random() * 1.0));
    END LOOP;
    RAISE NOTICE 'backfill complete through order_id %', v_cursor;
END $$;

-- NOT NULL without a rewrite. There is no NOT VALID for NOT NULL on any
-- engine, because a NOT NULL constraint is not an implication the planner can
-- use before every row has been checked. The three-statement workaround:
--
--   (a) prove non-nullability with a CHECK that the planner CAN use, because it
--       is only ever added to rows that already satisfy it;
--   (b) attach NOT NULL, which PostgreSQL then takes from the validated CHECK
--       rather than from a scan; and for pre-12 versions a pg_squeeze away.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    ADD CONSTRAINT ck_so_order_state_notnull
    CHECK (order_state IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID;
ALTER TABLE sales_order VALIDATE CONSTRAINT ck_so_order_state_notnull;
-- Now the planner knows the column is non-null; the SET NOT NULL is a catalog edit.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ALTER COLUMN order_state SET NOT NULL;

The three-statement NOT NULL sequence is the standard PostgreSQL 12+ workaround and it is exact. PostgreSQL 12 added attnotnull derivation from a validated CHECK (col IS NOT NULL) constraint specifically so that SET NOT NULL on an already-validated column is a catalog operation with no table scan. On PostgreSQL 18 this works. On MySQL and SQL Server it does not, and the alternatives are worse: MySQL’s MODIFY ... NOT NULL with ALGORITHM=INPLACE rebuilds the table’s metadata but the operation still needs to hold a metadata lock, and SQL Server’s ALTER COLUMN ... NOT NULL is a size-of-data operation unless the column carries a filtered unique index. The genuinely portable answer on all three is the shadow column: add a new column that is NOT NULL from birth, backfill it, switch reads, and never set NOT NULL on an existing column at all. Section 8.3 does exactly this.

PropertySingle-statement backfillBatched backfill
Transaction duration41 minutes on a large table< 1 s per batch
Locks heldRow locks on every touched row for the whole duration; any long reader in the way stalls the entire backfillRow locks on one batch at a time
Undo / rollback growthPins all undo for the duration. On PostgreSQL this also prevents VACUUM from removing any dead tuple anywhere in the database for 41 minutesBounded by the batch
ReplicationOne large transaction. Asynchronous replicas fall behind by its full duration; a synchronous replica blocks the primary until it commitsMany small transactions, each cheaply applied
Killable?Only by waiting for it, or by pg_terminate_backend and a full rollback of 41 minutes of workBetween batches, with all work so far committed
Failure blast radiusEverythingOne batch; the cursor makes resume exact
What it costs the userA latency cliff on the whole tableA small, steady, throttled tax

Add the index before the backfill, and think about which index. The batching pattern above scans on the primary key and filters on order_state IS NULL, which is fine while the untouched rows are still sparse in the table. If the filter column is not indexed, every batch performs a sequential scan of the whole table to find the remaining nulls, and the cost per batch is constant rather than decreasing — the backfill gets slower per row processed as it progresses. The fix is either a partial index on the rows that still need work, or a scan of the primary key range with the null test as a cheap filter. A partial index that is dropped after the backfill completes is the cleanest option and is a normal, reviewable part of the migration.

Backfill through the outbox if the backfill has side effects. A backfill that changes a column from which a derived value is computed must also update whatever the derivation produced, or the two will disagree until the next legitimate write. If the derivation is maintained by a trigger, the backfill fires it naturally. If the derivation is maintained by application code, the backfill silently desynchronises the two, and the repair is a second full scan. The check that catches this is a reconciliation query comparing the derived column against the source, run immediately after the backfill completes and recorded in the migration’s own verification step.

8.6 Migration tooling, transactionality and the review checklist

A migration tool is a ledger. Its job is to answer two questions — which migrations have run against this database, and in what order — and its secondary job is to make the SQL reviewable. Everything else it does is optional, and the optional parts are where the differences between tools live.

The property that actually matters when a migration fails halfway through is transactional DDL. Where DDL is transactional, a failed migration rolls back to a clean state and the tool’s repair or undo command has a coherent starting point. Where it is not — MySQL, and MySQL only, of the three engines in this guide — a failed migration leaves the schema in an intermediate state, and recovery is a hand-written compensating statement. No tool can fix this; a tool can only refuse to pretend. The tools differ in how honestly they report it.

ToolModelTransactional DDLIdempotence / repairWhere it is genuinely wrong to use it
FlywayVersioned plain SQL files with a checksum per fileFollows the engine. On PostgreSQL and SQL Server a file is one transaction; on MySQL each statement commitsChecksum mismatch on a changed file is a hard error, which is the correct behaviour. repair re-aligns the ledger after a manual fix and is an audited operationWhen the migration needs to express application logic. Flyway has no branching, no conditionals and no variables beyond placeholders
LiquibaseXML, YAML, JSON or formatted SQL changelog with change types and preconditionsSame as above. runInTransaction: false is required per-change for CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY and for anything MySQL runs on MySQLPreconditions and runAlways exist but the changelog grows an implicit state; large changelogs are hard to review in a diffWhen the logical model is the source of truth and the changelog is being hand-maintained in parallel — the two will diverge
AlembicPython upgrade/downgrade functions generated from ORM metadataFollows the driver and the engine. op.execute() for raw SQL inherits the engine’s behaviourAutogenerate produces the diff between models and the database, which is a genuine drift detector and the best argument for the tool. The downgrade function is generated too, which is how most teams end up with a downgrade that does not actually reverse a data migrationWhen index, partition and constraint choices matter. The generated DDL is a starting point that is almost always edited by hand, at which point the ORM is no longer generating it and the drift detection is the only thing left
EF Core migrationsC# Up / Down methods, snapshot file as the model of recordFollows the provider. suppressTransaction is required per-migration for online operationsThe __EFMigrationsHistory table plus a model snapshot gives idempotent migrate. The snapshot is a serialised model and is the single best drift detector available for an ORM-coupled applicationWhen the application is not the owner of the schema, or when a second team owns it. EF will then try to reconcile its snapshot against a schema it did not create
Prisma MigrateSQL generated from the Prisma schema, with a shadow database for diffingEngine-dependent. Prisma explicitly warns that MySQL DDL is not transactional and will not roll backVery strong drift detection: the shadow database is a full clone compared before applying. Weak review: the generated SQL is not usually read, and it is frequently inadequate for the physical designFor the same reason as EF, and additionally when the Prisma schema is used to drive production DDL — the physical design is exactly what it cannot express
sqitchDeploy / verify / revert as first-class, with dependencies between changesPer-deploy, engine-dependent, and the verify step is a first-class user-written assertionThe only tool in this table where verification is a first-class concept rather than a comment. sqitch verify runs after every deploy and fails the pipeline if the schema is not in the expected stateNever — the cost is a steeper learning curve and a changelog that is harder to generate from an ORM
-- Flyway: the shape of a versioned migration, and the two things that must
-- be declared rather than assumed.
--
-- V014__add_order_state.sql

-- PostgreSQL and SQL Server: the file runs in one transaction. If any
-- statement fails, the whole file rolls back and the ledger is untouched.
-- MySQL: each statement commits. A failure at line 30 leaves lines 1-29
-- applied, and the ledger row is NOT written, so a re-run re-executes
-- line 1 and fails on "column already exists". The migration must therefore be
-- written defensively, or split so that each file is one atomic idea.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ADD COLUMN order_state text NULL;

-- The pre-migration size gate. A migration that would rewrite a 900M-row
-- table is stopped here rather than at 3am.
--
--   SELECT count(*) FROM sales_order;   -- abort above 200 000 000

-- The lock gate. Every migration sets a short lock_timeout so a queued DDL
-- never accumulates a queue of blocked readers behind it.
SET lock_timeout = '3s';
-- MySQL: SET SESSION lock_wait_timeout = 3;
-- SQL Server: SET LOCK_TIMEOUT 3000;

-- The dead-work check. An index this migration creates must not already
-- exist from a previous partial run.
DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY IF EXISTS ix_sales_order_placed_tenant;

-- Alembic: raw SQL inside a Python migration, with the transactional
-- behaviour stated explicitly rather than inherited by accident.
--
-- def upgrade() -> None:
--     with op.get_context().autocommit_block():   # required: CONCURRENTLY
--                                         # cannot run in a transaction
--         op.execute("CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ix_so_placed_tenant "
--                   "ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)")
--     op.add_column("sales_order", sa.Column("order_state", sa.Text(),
--                                     nullable=True))
-- def downgrade() -> None:
--     with op.get_context().autocommit_block():
--         op.execute("DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY IF EXISTS ix_so_placed_tenant")
--     op.drop_column("sales_order", "order_state")
--
-- The downgrade above reverses the DDL. It does not reverse the backfill, and
-- it cannot: the data written by the backfill into order_state is not
-- recoverable once the column is dropped. This is why the three-phase rename
-- exists -- it puts the irreversible step in its own migration, at the end,
-- after the point of no return has been passed deliberately and visibly.

-- sqitch: deploy, verify, revert. The verify step is the review checklist,
-- executed, and its failure fails the deploy.
--
-- @dev:verify SELECT 1 FROM pg_constraint WHERE conname = 'ck_so_order_state'
-- @dev:verify SELECT count(*) FROM sales_order WHERE order_state IS NULL;  -- must be 0
-- @prod:verify SELECT count(*) FROM sales_order WHERE order_state IS NULL; -- must be 0
-- @prod:verify SELECT indexrelid::regclass FROM pg_index WHERE NOT indisvalid;
-- @prod:verify  -- must return zero rows
-- @prod:verify  SELECT 1 FROM sales_order s
-- @prod:verify   WHERE s.order_state NOT IN ('placed','paid','shipped','cancelled','refunded')
-- @prod:verify   LIMIT 10;
-- @prod:verify
-- @prod:verify -- The ledger is only written after verify succeeds.

The ORM owns the logical model; the migration owns the physical design. Never let one tool own both. The most common failure in this area is an ORM autogenerating a migration that adds a column, creating the column at the wrong position in the table, omitting an index because indexes are not part of the object model, and silently applying a cascade rule that a hand-written migration would never have chosen. The rule that survives contact with production is: the ORM generates the column list, a human writes the constraints, indexes, partition clauses and algorithm assertions, and a CI check compares the two and fails on any difference. The alembic check command, EF Core’s migrations has-pending-model-changes, and Prisma’s migrate diff all perform exactly this comparison, and all three are the single highest-value migration-related CI step available.

The review checklist is short and the discipline is that it is executed rather than read. Six questions, in the order they should be asked of a migration under review:

#QuestionWhy it is askedHow to verify
1What lock does this take, for how long, and at what row count?It is the only question whose answer changes between staging and productionName the table’s current row count from the metrics system. If the statement is a rewrite and the row count is above the threshold from section 8.4, the review stops here
2Does this run in a transaction on every target engine?Determines whether a partial failure is recoverable by re-runningState the engine and the answer in the migration file. On MySQL, one atomic idea per file
3Can this be re-run safely after a partial failure?Re-runnability is what makes a failed migration an inconvenience rather than an incidentIF NOT EXISTS / IF EXISTS where available; on MySQL and SQL Server, a preceding existence check in a SELECT because IF EXISTS for ADD COLUMN is not portable
4Does this lock or block on anything a long transaction might hold?Section 8.4's metadata-queue failureEnumerate the tables it touches and check for long-running readers or writers on them in the last 30 days
5Is there a rollback, and is it complete?A downgrade that does not reverse a data change is worse than none, because it is believedRun the downgrade against a restored production-shaped snapshot, not against staging
6What assertion proves it worked, and has that assertion run?Verification is what makes a migration trustworthy rather than merely appliedA sqitch verify block, a post-deploy check step, or a query with a recorded expected result. "It applied without error" is not verification

Exercises 8

  1. Take the highest-volume table in a schema you can reach. Run every statement in the table in section 8.4 against a production-shaped copy and record, per engine, the algorithm actually chosen, the lock taken, the duration, and the peak replica lag during the operation. Produce a one-page table from the results and mark every cell that was not what the documentation predicted.
  2. Implement the three-phase rename from section 8.3 on sales_order.status → order_state, including the synchronising trigger. Then kill the application mid-sequence at each of the four deploys and roll back. Assert that the old binary runs correctly against the schema as it stands at that point, and record the exact deploy at which rollback stops being possible. Compare that deploy to the one a team would have predicted.
  3. Add a NOT NULL column to a 50-million-row table by each of the three methods — direct, shadow column, and the validated-CHECK trick — and measure duration, lock hold time, and the transaction log or WAL volume each produced. Explain, from the measurements, why the direct method is not merely slow but operationally dangerous.
  4. Write a batched backfill with a monotonic cursor and jitter, and prove resumability: kill the process at three different points and confirm the final row count and the absence of gaps. Then run four concurrent instances of the same backfill against the same table and show that the jittered version finishes in bounded time while a fixed-sleep version produces a burst that pushes p99 latency past its SLO.
  5. Measure replication lag during a single-statement backfill of the same data volume as your batched version. Record the maximum lag on an asynchronous replica and confirm that a promotion during the single-statement backfill would lose the batch. Write the runbook line that prevents this and test it by attempting the promotion.
  6. Adopt the six-question review checklist for one real migration. Time the review. Then deliberately submit two bad migrations — one with a full rewrite on a large table and one with a non-idempotent ADD COLUMN — and confirm the checklist catches both without the reviewer needing engine knowledge. Fix what the checklist misses.

9 · Query Design and Performance

9.1 Query shape before query plan9.2 Reading the plan: three analysers, three vocabularies9.3 The estimated-versus-actual gap is the finding9.4 Operators, side by side, and what each one costs9.5 N+1 at the SQL layer and its three fixes9.6 Pagination, batch size, projection and plan reuse

Performance work is a measurement discipline before it is an optimisation discipline. This chapter covers how to read a plan on each engine, why the difference between the estimate and the reality is the single most informative number in the output, how to recognise and repair the N+1 at the SQL layer, and the four query-writing habits — projection, ordering, pagination and preparation — that pay for themselves before any index is touched.

9.1 Query shape before query plan

Before any plan is read, one question must be answered from the access-pattern inventory of chapter 1: how many rows does this statement touch, and how many does it return. A plan is a description of a specific cardinality, and a plan is only interpretable against the cardinality it was built for.

A query is not slow or fast in the abstract. It is slow relative to the number of rows it must examine, and the two diverge by orders of magnitude when the access path is wrong. The diagnostic that precedes every plan analysis is therefore arithmetic: take the cardinality of the driving table from the inventory, multiply by the fan-out of each join, and compare the result to the row count the statement is supposed to return. When those two numbers differ by more than about two orders of magnitude, the cause is a missing or unusable index, a wrong join type, or a predicate that is not sargable — and the plan will only tell you which of the three.

Access pattern (AP)Statement shapeRows examinedRows returnedRatio that is acceptable
AP-04 order historyOrders for one tenant, newest first, 50 per page≈ 50 with a composite index on (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)50≤ 3×
AP-04 without the composite indexSame statement, tenant filter onlyAll rows for the tenant — for the largest tenant, 4.1 million5082 000× — this is the bug
AP-07 invoice runAggregate every sales_order_line for orders placed last month, across all tenants≈ 30 million, and it must be all of them12The ratio is irrelevant; this belongs on a replica or in the warehouse
AP-11 plan lookupJoin subscription to plan to render a dashboard tile2 rows12×
AP-14 order detailOrder header, its lines, and the payment events for the order1 + 4 + 31 order, 7 rows≈ 1×

The shape ratio deserves one more rule, because it is the number most often misread: a ratio ≥ 100 on a statement whose result set is small is a defect, not a characteristic of the data. The second half of query shape is the structure of the statement, which determines how many plans it needs. Four properties matter, and each has a specific failure:

  • Number of distinct shapes. A statement with a parameter that appears in an optional predicate produces a different plan for WHERE status = 'x' and for WHERE status = 'x' AND created_at > ?. On PostgreSQL a prepared statement with five optional filters may have five plans; on MySQL 9 the equivalent effect is a ref_or_null access path appearing in EXPLAIN and a Range checked for each record note appearing in the log. This is why a "single query" is frequently a family of queries.
  • Number of joins. The optimiser’s search space grows superlinearly. PostgreSQL uses dynamic programming up to join_collapse_limit (default 8) and switches to a genetic algorithm beyond it; MySQL has optimizer_search_depth (default 62); SQL Server has a hard limit of 64 tables per query and gives up with a warning. Past roughly eight tables in a join, the plan is no longer a search result but a heuristic, and a hand-written join order that the optimiser could not find is often correct.
  • Correlated subqueries. A subquery in the SELECT list that references the outer row executes once per outer row unless the planner decorrelates it. It usually does, on all three engines, and it usually does so by turning the join into something the access path cannot serve. Seeing a correlated subquery in a plan is a signal to write the join explicitly.
  • Aggregation before and after the join. Aggregating before a join and then joining the small result is almost always cheaper than joining and then aggregating. GROUP BY tenant_id on sales_order filtered to one day, then joined to tenant, beats the reverse. Section 3.5's denormalisation is this principle applied to storage.
-- The shape question, answered by the database rather than by reasoning.
-- pg_stat_statements normalised the query, reports its own row estimate,
-- and reports the real one. The three columns to compare are calls,
