-- rows and mean_exec_time -- in that order, always sorted by total time.
SELECT calls,
       ROUND(100.0 * total_exec_time / GREATEST(total_exec_time + total_plan_time, 0.001), 1) AS pct_exec,
       ROUND(mean_plan_time  * 1000, 2) AS mean_plan_ms,
       ROUND(mean_exec_time  * 1000, 2) AS mean_exec_ms,
       rows,
       ROUND(1.0 * rows / GREATEST(calls, 1), 1) AS rows_per_call,
       shared_blks_hit + shared_blks_read AS buffers_touched,
       query
  FROM pg_stat_statements
 WHERE query NOT LIKE '%pg_stat_statements%'
   AND query NOT LIKE 'EXPLAIN %'
 ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC
 LIMIT 20;

-- The same question on MySQL 9.x. performance_schema.events_statements_summary
-- gives SUM_TIMER_WAIT in picoseconds and SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED per digest.
-- SUM_NO_INDEX_USED is the single most valuable column in that view: it
-- counts executions that went to a full table scan because no index was usable.
SELECT LEFT(digest_text, 90)                          AS query,
       COUNT_STAR                                            AS calls,
       ROUND(SUM_TIMER_WAIT / 1000000000000, 2)       AS total_ms,
       ROUND(AVG_TIMER_WAIT / 1000000000, 2)          AS mean_ms,
       SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED                                    AS rows_examined,
       SUM_ROWS_SENT                                        AS rows_sent,
       ROUND(SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED / GREATEST(SUM_ROWS_SENT, 1), 1)  AS examined_per_sent,
       SUM_NO_INDEX_USED                                    AS full_scans,
       ROUND(SUM_SORT_MERGE_PASSES / GREATEST(COUNT_STAR, 1), 2) AS sorts_spilled
  FROM performance_schema.events_statements_summary_by_digest
 WHERE digest_text IS NOT NULL
   AND schema_name IS NOT NULL
 ORDER BY SUM_TIMER_WAIT DESC
 LIMIT 20;

-- SQL Server 2025. Query Store is the engine's own answer to the same
-- question, and it survives restarts and OPTION(RECOMPILE) unlike the plan
-- cache, which is why it is the right first tool and DMV sampling is not.
SELECT TOP (20)
       q.query_id,
       p.plan_id,
       rs.count_executions,
       rs.avg_duration   / 1000. AS avg_ms,
       rs.avg_cpu_time    / 1000. AS avg_cpu_ms,
       rs.avg_logical_io_reads                            AS avg_logical_reads,
       CAST(q.query_sql_text AS NVARCHAR(200)) AS query_text
  FROM sys.query_store_query q
  JOIN sys.query_store_plan   p ON p.query_id = q.query_id
  JOIN sys.query_store_runtime_stats rs ON rs.plan_id = p.plan_id
 WHERE q.object_id = OBJECT_ID('sales_order')
 ORDER BY rs.avg_duration DESC;

All three views answer the same question and one of them answers a second one that is more valuable. SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED / SUM_ROWS_SENT and PostgreSQL’s rows / calls are the shape ratio — the arithmetic from the first section, measured rather than estimated. SUM_NO_INDEX_USED is MySQL’s own admission that a statement had no usable index, and it is the fastest way to find the AP whose index is missing. PostgreSQL has no direct equivalent column, but a shape ratio of 100 or more on a small rows figure is the signal, and SQL Server’s avg_logical_io_reads above a few hundred on a small result set is the same signal with a different name.

Install the statistics collector before you need it. pg_stat_statements, MySQL’s performance_schema and SQL Server’s Query Store all accumulate history that cannot be reconstructed. The moment a query is slow it is too late to begin measuring, and the post-mortem question "how long has this been slow, and what made it slow" has no answer. Load the extension, size the pg_stat_statements.max to cover at least a working week, set track_io_timing=on (which is off by default because it has a measurable per-query cost), and set Query Store to MAX_PLANS_PER_QUERY in the low thousands with WAIT_STATS_CAPTURE = ON so that wait events are retained alongside the plan.

9.2 Reading the plan: three analysers, three vocabularies

The three engines expose plans in different formats with different amounts of information, and the difference is not cosmetic: only PostgreSQL’s will tell you how many pages the statement actually touched, and only that number distinguishes a plan that is merely slow from a plan that is touching the disk.

Two distinctions have to be held in mind from the first line of output. Estimated versus actual is a column-by-column comparison of what the optimiser predicted and what happened. Elapsed versus work is the difference between the wall clock reported at the node and the CPU and I/O attributable to it. A plan that takes 4 seconds and shows 400 000 buffer hits is a CPU-bound plan; a plan that takes 4 seconds and shows 400 buffer hits is blocked or waiting, and adding an index will not help it at all.

-- PostgreSQL 18: the fullest instrumentation available on any of the three.
-- BUFFERS adds shared hit / read counts and dirtied / written counts per
-- node. TIMING adds actual per-node timings, and adds real per-loop counts
-- inside loops, which is what makes a nested loop's true cost visible.
-- WARNING on a volatile function or a lossy estimate is printed at the end.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, TIMING, VERBOSE, SETTINGS, SUMMARY, FORMAT TEXT)
SELECT o.order_id, o.placed_at, o.total_excl_tax, l.sku, l.qty
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
   AND o.placed_at >= '2026-06-01'::timestamptz
 ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC
 LIMIT 50;

-- FORMAT=JSON is the same plan, machine-readable. It is the only format
-- that carries the plan's own reasoning, and it should be diffed in CI:
-- a plan change that alters a node type is a deployable event, not noise.
--
--   "Relation Name": "sales_order", "Alias": "o", "Plan Rows": 82000,
--   "Actual Rows": 1843, "Actual Total Time": 61.204,
--   "Buffers": { "Shared Hit": 1842, "Shared Read": 9011 },
--   "Filter": "(o.tenant_id = 412)", "Index Cond": "(o.placed_at >= ...)"
--
-- "Node Type": "Index Scan", "Join Type": "Inner",
--   "Actual Loops": 1843, "Plan Rows": 1843, "Actual Rows": 4

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, FORMAT JSON)
SELECT o.order_id, count(*)
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
 GROUP BY 1;

-- MySQL 9.x. EXPLAIN ANALYZE executes the statement and returns the
-- iterator tree with real timings -- but it is an INSERT-replacing
-- statement in older versions, so always run it inside a transaction you
-- roll back. The traditional EXPLAIN is the only safe choice for DML.
EXPLAIN FORMAT=TREE
SELECT o.order_id, o.placed_at, count(*)
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
 GROUP BY o.order_id, o.placed_at;

-- EXPLAIN FORMAT=JSON is MySQL's only format carrying cost estimates
-- per node. "rows_examined_per_scan" in the cost section is the number to
-- watch: a full table scan on sales_order reports it directly.
EXPLAIN FORMAT=JSON
SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND placed_at >= '2026-06-01'
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50;

-- SQL Server 2025: two separate statements. SET STATISTICS XML is XML, one
-- result set per execution, and it contains the estimated and the actual
-- cardinality side by side as EstimatedRows and ActualRows -- the same
-- comparison PostgreSQL gives you, under a different name.
SET STATISTICS IO ON;
SET STATISTICS XML ON;
SELECT o.order_id, o.placed_at, count(*)
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
 GROUP BY o.order_id, o.placed_at
OPTION (RECOMPILE);   -- see 9.6: removes plan-cache pollution
SET STATISTICS XML OFF;
SET STATISTICS IO OFF;

-- The per-row read/write counts SET STATISTICS IO emits are the numbers to
-- compare against PostgreSQL's BUFFERS. A logical read is one 8 KB page,
-- so a logical read count of 4 100 000 on a statement returning 50 rows is
-- the same defect PostgreSQL would report as 4.1 million shared blocks.
--
--   Table 'sales_order', scan type 'CLUSTERED INDEX SCAN',
--   logical reads 4102233, physical reads 0, ...
--   Warning: The plan might be affected by parameter sniffing...

Do not run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on a statement you cannot afford to run twice. On PostgreSQL and MySQL it executes the statement for real; on MySQL 9.x in particular, EXPLAIN ANALYZE on an INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE performs the write and only then explains it. The correct discipline is: use plain EXPLAIN to see the shape, and reserve EXPLAIN ANALYZE for a read that is safe to run twice, or wrap the write in a transaction that is rolled back. On SQL Server, OPTION (RECOMPILE) has the same effect of discarding the cached plan, which is both why it is used in the listing above and why it must not be on a statement whose plan-cache behaviour you are diagnosing.

Reading a plan is a fixed procedure, and doing it in the same order every time is what makes it fast. Start at the innermost node that reads the most and work outward; ignore the top-level elapsed time entirely until the end, because it is wall clock, and it includes everything that blocked … For each node ask four questions in order: what is the access method, how many rows did it predict, how many did it produce, and how many pages did it touch. A node whose predicted and actual differ by more than 10× is a statistics problem and the rest of the plan is downstream of it — fix that first and re-read, because the planner will often choose a different join order once the cardinality is right.

Plan observationWhat it means on PostgreSQLWhat it means on MySQL 9.xWhat it means on SQL Server 2025
Full sequential scan on a large table with a selective predicateSeq Scan with a Filter: line — the index was not used, was unusable, or the planner judged a scan cheapertype: ALL or index: NULL in the traditional output; table scan in the TREE outputClustered Index Scan with a Predicate, or Index Scan with a Key Lookup above it
Estimate wrong by > 10×Compare rows= and (actual rows= on the same node line. Common cause: correlated columns the statistics treat as independentrows in traditional output; (cost=... rows=N) in TREE. Common cause: the same, plus histogram limits on JSON and TEXT columnsEstimatedRows versus ActualRows in the XML, or the cardinality-estimation warning in the plan text
Sort spilled to diskSort Method: external merge Disk: 48210kB. Above work_mem. External merge is a symptom, not the diseaseUsing filesort, and Sort_merge_passes > 0 in the optimiser traceSort operator with Spill Level > 0 in the Warning / hash spill details
Hash join on a large build sideHash Join with Buckets: 4194304 Batches: 8 Memory Usage: 128MB. Batches > 1 means it spilledInner hash join (... using index) in TREE; Using join buffer (hash join) in traditional output when the join buffer is usedHash Match with Hash Match Warnings, or Row Count mismatched against Hash Match Build
Nested loop over many outer rowsNested Loop with loops= > 1000 and a high loops=1 inner costNested loop inner join (... using index); if the index is missing you get Using join buffer (BNL) and a warningNested Loops (Inner Join) with a high Actual Rows Read against Estimated Rows Read
Plan differs from last week with no schema changeStatistics drifted; the plan cache holds a plan built for old statistics. Auto on a REGRESS node means the planner knows it is guessingThe optimiser trace shows the histogram bounds at plan time; the same drift appliesParameter sniffing. The XML carries ParameterList with the compiled value and ParameterCompiledValue

9.3 The estimated-versus-actual gap is the finding

A wrong estimate is not a curiosity. The optimiser’s entire plan is a decision tree whose branches are cardinality estimates, and a plan chosen for 100 rows applied to 100 000 rows is not a slightly worse plan — it is a different plan, selected to be correct for a statement that does not exist.

The canonical case is a correlated pair of predicates. If tenant_id and order_state are independent in the optimiser’s model, and one tenant has 4 % of rows while one state has 20 % of rows, the planner multiplies to 0.8 % of the table. In a 12-million-row table that is 96 000 rows, when the truth is 60. PostgreSQL will happily choose a bitmap heap scan on a 96 000-row estimate; at the real 60 rows an index scan returning 60 rows is milliseconds instead of seconds. The fix is not enable_seqscan = off, which is a band-aid that produces the right answer for the wrong reason and will regress on the next data distribution. The fix is extended statistics or a better index.

-- PostgreSQL 18: declare the correlation, and let the planner price it properly.
-- Without this, tenant_id and order_state are assumed independent.
CREATE STATISTICS st_sales_order_tenant_state
    (ndistinct, dependencies, mcv)
    ON tenant_id, order_state
  FROM sales_order;
ANALYZE sales_order;

-- Inspect what the statistics now claim. This output is the evidence,
-- and it belongs in the pull request that adds the statistics object.
SELECT statistics_name, attnames, kinds, null_frac, avg_width, n_distinct
  FROM pg_stats_ext
 JOIN pg_stats_ext_data d ON d.stxoid = pg_stats_ext.stxoid
 WHERE statistics_name = 'st_sales_order_tenant_state';

-- The stale-statistics check. last_analyze / last_autoanalyze are the two
-- dates worth monitoring; autoanalyze_starvation is a named failure mode
-- in section 14.2 and it presents exactly as this query returning nothing.
--
--   relname, n_live_tup, n_mod_since_analyze,
--   last_analyze, last_autoanalyze,
--   round(n_mod_since_analyze::numeric / nullif(n_live_tup,0) * 100, 2) AS mod_pct
--   FROM pg_stat_user_tables ORDER BY n_mod_since_analyze DESC;

-- Raise the target only for the columns where it pays. The default is 100,
-- which is one histogram bucket per 1% of the table and is too coarse for a
-- column whose values are heavily concentrated.
ALTER TABLE sales_order ALTER COLUMN order_state SET STATISTICS 1000;
ALTER TABLE sales_order ALTER COLUMN tenant_id  SET STATISTICS 500;
ANALYZE sales_order (order_state, tenant_id);   -- target only, not the whole table

-- The skip-scan mitigation for the case the planner cannot be taught:
-- a composite index on (tenant_id, placed_at) is unusable for a query that
-- filters only on placed_at, because tenant_id leads. PostgreSQL 18 can
-- generate skip scans automatically when the leading column has few
-- distinct values, but the reliable fix is a second index.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ix_sales_order_placed
    ON sales_order (placed_at DESC) -- leading column differs → a distinct B-tree;

-- MySQL 9.x: histogram on the correlated column. InnoDB histograms are
-- sampled -- the default is 100 buckets from up to 32 000 pages -- and are
-- used only where the optimiser estimates the index would be less than 70%
-- as good, so they are a supplement, never a replacement for an index.
--
--   ANALYZE TABLE sales_order UPDATE HISTOGRAM ON order_state
--     WITH 256 BUCKETS;
--   -- and verify what the optimiser believes:
--   SELECT * FROM information_schema.column_statistics
--    WHERE table_name='sales_order' AND column_name='order_state';

-- SQL Server 2025: there is no extended-statistics object. The equivalent is
-- a columnset statistic, or -- more reliably -- the CE feedback loop, which
-- learns the correlation from actual executions and re-optimises the plan.
-- It is on by default for the "all" cardinality estimator and must be enabled
-- for queries that use a filtered index or an OR predicate.
ALTER DATABASE saas
    SET CE_FEEDBACK = ON;
ALTER DATABASE SCOPED CONFIGURATION SET LEGACY_CARDINALITY_ESTIMATION = OFF;  -- not for every workload

-- The honest fallback that works on all three, and is often cheaper than
-- any of the above: order the predicate so the most selective one leads,
-- and if the estimate is still wrong, put the statement's own cost estimate
-- under review rather than reaching for a planner hint.
--
-- WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND placed_at >= '2026-06-01' AND order_state = 'paid'
-- Rewrite as a subquery on the selective pair, if statistics cannot be taught:
-- WHERE order_id IN (
--     SELECT order_id FROM sales_order
--      WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND order_state = 'paid'
-- )
Cause of a wrong estimateFrequencyDetectionRepair, cheapest first
Correlated columns treated as independentVery common on tenant-scoped schemas, where nearly every column correlates with tenant_idCompare estimated and actual on the driving node; confirm with pg_stats_extExtended statistics (PostgreSQL), histogram (MySQL), CE feedback (SQL Server). Then re-examine every composite index, because the same correlation invalidates the index’s usefulness assumption
Stale statistics after a bulk loadCommon, and self-inflicted: the backfill in section 8.5 invalidates themn_mod_since_analyze as a fraction of n_live_tup above 10 %ANALYZE the table after the backfill, as part of the migration, not on the autovacuum schedule
Skew the histogram cannot representCommon on a tenant_id column where one tenant holds 40 % of the rowsThe estimate is off by roughly the skew factor for any tenant whose share is unusualHigher STATISTICS target, or a WHERE tenant_id = ? split into IN lists of the largest tenants — which is ugly and sometimes the right answer
Parameter sniffing on SQL ServerCommon and specific: the first compilation’s value is reused for all later executionsThe plan’s ParameterCompiledValue differs from the ParameterList value you suppliedOPTION (RECOMPILE) for the specific statement, OPTIMIZE FOR UNKNOWN if you know which plan you want, or a Query Store plan hint. All three are per-statement and none is a schema fix
No usable index, so the optimiser estimates a scanCommon, and misdiagnosed as a statistics problemSUM_NO_INDEX_USED > 0 on MySQL; Index Cond: (none) on PostgreSQLAdd the index. This is chapter 5, not this one, and it is nearly always the actual answer
Expression index not matched by the predicateLess common, and entirely avoidablePredicate is WHERE lower(email) = ? and the index is on (tenant_id, email)Either index the expression, or stop writing the predicate as an expression. A generated or functional column makes the index and the predicate agree

Planner hints are a debt instrument, not a tool. enable_seqscan, /*+ INDEX(...)*/ and OPTION (INDEX(...)) all make the planner stop reasoning. They are correct in the narrow case that motivated them and silently wrong when the data distribution shifts, and there is no query plan validator that will tell you. The legitimate use is a documented, measured, time-boxed exception with a stated expiry and an alert on the statistics that motivated it; the illegitimate use is reaching for a hint because reading the estimate gap felt like too much work. The rule that holds up: if the estimate is wrong, fix the estimate; if the plan is right and the cost is too high, fix the schema; only if neither is possible, and the statement is on the critical path, is a hint justified.

9.4 Operators, side by side, and what each one costs

The join algorithm is the decision the planner makes that matters most after the access path, and the three engines choose from a different set. Knowing which algorithm suits which input shape is the difference between tuning a query and guessing at it.

There are three physical algorithms and each has a precondition. A nested loop is optimal when the outer input is small and the inner has an index, because its cost is outer rows × outer × log(inner) plus one index probe per outer row. A hash join is optimal when one side can be hashed into memory and the other is streamed, because its cost is linear in the total rows with a single pass. A sort-merge is optimal when both inputs are already sorted, which is rare outside of index-ordered scans and merge joins over pre-sorted partitions, and it is chosen mainly for its memory-bounded behaviour on large inputs. A sort-merge also has the useful property of being able to start producing output before the whole input is read, which is what makes it the right algorithm for a keyset-paginated query.

ShapeNested loopHash joinMerge join
Outer 50 rows, inner index probe into 40 M rows✓ 50 probes, sub-millisecond✗ hashes 40 M rows to find 50✗ sorts both inputs
Outer 4 M rows, inner 4 M rows, no index✗ quadratic, catastrophic✓ one pass each, memory-bounded by work_mem✓ if both inputs arrive sorted
Both sides indexed and already in key order✓ if the outer is small✓ otherwise✓ zero sort cost, streaming output
Build side larger than work_memn/aSpills to temp in batches. Batches > 1 is the signal; the fix is a plan the optimiser should not have chosen or a larger work_mem for that session✓ avoids the spill entirely
Full outer join or anti-join (NOT EXISTS)✓ possible but usually poor✓ natural for both; the anti-join is a hash join with a null-supplying side✓

The non-join operators deserve the same treatment, because three of them are the usual answer to a slow query and one of them is the usual cause.

OperatorPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Index lookupIndex Scan (returns rows) / Index Only Scan (satisfies the query from the index, no heap access)ref / const / eq_ref in traditional output; index lookup in TREEIndex Seek, with a Key Lookup for non-covered columns
Full scanSeq Scan, sometimes with a Bitmap Heap Scan above it after a bitmap index scantype: ALL; Using index marks a covering index scan with no table accessClustered Index Scan or Table Scan on a heap
SortSort with Sort Method and Memory Usage; external merge means it spilledSort node in TREE; Using filesort in traditional; Using index means no sort was neededSort with Spill Level, or Top / Order By eliminated entirely by an ordered index seek
AggregateAggregate with Strategy: Hashed or Sorted; GroupAggregate when the input is pre-sortedAggregate with using index (streaming) or using temporary table (hashing, disk possible)Hash Match / Stream Aggregate; Stream Aggregate requires sorted input and is memory-free
LimitLimit — the optimiser may push it down and stop early, which is why ORDER BY plus LIMIT is cheap and LIMIT alone is notLimit in TREE, often fused into the preceding scan so the top-N heap sort is boundedTop — implemented as a bounded sort, memory proportional to N not to the input
SubquerySubquery Scan decorrelated into a semi- or anti-join, or executed as InitPlan when uncorrelatedSemi-join / Anti-join under IN / NOT IN; DEPENDENT SUBQUERY in traditional output is a red flagNested Loops (Left Semi Join); an Adaptive Join records the switch point in XML and can be a source of plan instability
-- The same AP on three engines, and the join algorithm each one picks for it.
-- AP-14: order detail. One order, its lines, its payment events. The driving
-- table is 1 row, so a nested loop with index probes is the correct shape and
-- any other algorithm is a regression.
--
-- PostgreSQL: "Nested Loop Left Join" over two "Index Only Scan" nodes.
-- The Index Only Scan is only possible because the visibility map is set; after
-- a bulk UPDATE (section 8.5) it will degrade to Index Scan until VACUUM runs,
-- and the plan change you observe is not a statistics problem.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT o.order_id, o.status, l.sku, l.qty, l.line_amount,
       p.provider, p.amount AS paid_amount
  FROM sales_order o
  LEFT JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
  LEFT JOIN payment_event    p ON p.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412 AND o.order_id = 88100041;

-- MySQL 9.x: the TREE output names the algorithm directly.
--   -> Nested loop inner join  (l.order_id = o.order_id)  (cost=340.7 rows=4)
--       -> Index lookup on l using PRIMARY (order_id=...)  (rows=4)
--   -> Table scan on o  (cost=270.4 rows=1)  (filter: (tenant_id=412))
--
-- Note "filter:" on the driving table rather than "using index". When
-- the driving table is 1 row out of 12M, a filter on it is correct and the
-- plan is fine; when it is 400 000 rows out of 12M it is the defect.
EXPLAIN FORMAT=TREE
SELECT o.order_id, o.status, l.sku, l.qty
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412 AND o.order_id = 88100041;

-- SQL Server 2025: Nested Loops with a Seek on both children is the target.
-- A Hash Match here means the optimiser believes the order table is large.
SELECT o.order_id, o.status, l.sku, l.qty
  FROM sales_order o
  JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412 AND o.order_id = 88100041
OPTION (RECOMPILE);

-- The shape where the three diverge most usefully: the aggregation over a
-- join. Aggregating the small side first and joining the 12-row result is
-- never wrong, and on PostgreSQL the planner usually finds it. Written by
-- hand it is a portable 20x improvement on all three.
--
-- Slower: join 12M order lines to 12M payment events, then group.
-- Faster: filter to one tenant and one day (an index range scan returning
-- 41 000 rows), aggregate that to 12 rows, then join the names.
WITH daily AS (
    SELECT order_id, sum(line_amount) AS order_total
      FROM sales_order_line
     WHERE tenant_id = 412
       AND created_at >= '2026-09-01'
     GROUP BY order_id
)
SELECT t.slug, count(*), sum(d.order_total)
  FROM daily d
  JOIN tenant t ON t.tenant_id = 412
 GROUP BY t.slug;

Read the cost model, not the cost. PostgreSQL’s cost units are calibrated to sequential page reads on the assumed storage, and the default random_page_cost of 4.0 versus seq_page_cost of 1.0 is an assertion about the storage. On a cloud volume with a read cache in front of it the ratio is closer to 1.1, and at 1.1 the planner will choose index scans much more freely; on a spinning disk with no cache the default understates the cost of random access and the planner will avoid indexes it should use. The single highest-leverage tuning setting in PostgreSQL is random_page_cost, and it should be set from a measurement — the ratio of sequential to random read latency on the actual volume — rather than left at a value tuned for a 2005 dedicated disk array. The equivalent MySQL setting is innodb_random_read_ahead and the equivalent reasoning applies to the optimiser’s rand_read_cost versus read_rnd_cost variables, which are undocumented and should be treated as such.

9.5 N+1 at the SQL layer and its three fixes

The N+1 is not an ORM bug. It is a shape that appears whenever a program alternates between asking the database for a set of things and then asking it, one at a time, about each of them — and the database charges full round-trip and planning latency for every one of those single-row questions.

The N+1 at the SQL layer is worth distinguishing carefully from the N+1 in an object mapper, because the causes and the fixes differ. The object-mapper version is a lazy-load configuration error. The SQL-layer version is an architectural one: the code has a list of identifiers and, for each, needs related data, and it has chosen to express that as a loop. The two produce identical wire traffic and identical latency, and the SQL-layer version is invisible to the ORM because each individual query is correct.

It appears in three common places in a schema like this one. Rendering a list of 50 orders with their line counts issues 1 + 50 queries. Rendering a tenant dashboard that shows the plan name for each of 200 subscriptions issues 1 + 200. Verifying that a batch of imported orders have valid tenant references issues one query per row because the reference check is inside the loop. The last is the most common in data-loading code and the least likely to be noticed, because it is bounded by the batch size and therefore bounded by the author’s patience.

-- The defect. Three round trips per order, plus one for the orders themselves.
-- Round-trip latency dominates: at 0.4 ms per round trip on a local socket and
-- 2 ms across a region, 50 orders cost 20 ms here and 100 ms there, before any
-- row is read. The database sees 51 statements, 51 parse-and-plan cycles, and
-- 51 entries in the plan cache for what is one question.
SELECT order_id, placed_at, status FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412 ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50;

--   SELECT sku, qty FROM sales_order_line WHERE order_id = 88100041;
--   SELECT provider, amount FROM payment_event   WHERE order_id = 88100041;
--   ... repeated 50 times

-- Fix 1: one join. Correct, simple, and the right answer whenever the fan-out
-- is bounded. The cost is that joining two one-to-many tables multiplies rows,
-- so the line count and the payment count must be pre-aggregated -- which is
-- the multiplication factor from chapter 1.3, visible in the plan as 4 lines x
-- 3 payment events = 12 rows for one order.
SELECT o.order_id, o.placed_at, o.status,
       l.sku, l.qty, l.line_amount,
       p.provider, p.amount AS paid_amount
  FROM sales_order o
  LEFT JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = o.order_id
  LEFT JOIN payment_event    p ON p.order_id = o.order_id
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
 ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC, o.order_id, l.line_no
 LIMIT 200;

-- Fix 2: LATERAL. The right answer when the sub-query must see the outer
-- row and the row count per outer row varies. It returns exactly 1 row per
-- order here, so no multiplication and no pre-aggregation, and each lateral
-- inner is an index lookup on the join key. Supported on PostgreSQL and
-- SQL Server; MySQL 9 has no LATERAL, and the equivalent is the derived-table
-- trick shown below it.
SELECT o.order_id, o.placed_at, o.status,
       agg.line_count, agg.order_total, agg.first_sku
  FROM sales_order o
  LEFT JOIN LATERAL (
       SELECT count(*)        AS line_count,
              sum(line_amount)   AS order_total,
              min(sku)          AS first_sku
         FROM sales_order_line l
        WHERE l.order_id = o.order_id   -- index on (order_id)
  ) agg ON true
 WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
 ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC
 LIMIT 50;

-- MySQL 9 equivalent: a derived table joined on equality. MySQL 8.0.14+
-- can materialise a derived table with LATERAL semantics through this shape.
--
-- SELECT o.order_id, o.placed_at, agg.line_count, agg.order_total
--   FROM sales_order o
--   LEFT JOIN (
--        SELECT order_id, count(*) AS line_count, sum(line_amount) AS order_total
--          FROM sales_order_line
--         WHERE order_id BETWEEN ? AND ?   -- pre-filter, or the derived table
--         GROUP BY order_id) agg ON agg.order_id = o.order_id
--  WHERE o.tenant_id = 412
--  ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC LIMIT 50;

-- Fix 3: the batched form. Two round trips regardless of list size, and the
-- only one that scales to a list of 10 000. The IN list is the weak point: on
-- PostgreSQL the planner switches to a hashed semi-join above a few hundred
-- values and must hash every row of the driving table; on MySQL it re-plans
-- per distinct list length; on SQL Server a large IN list produces a plan with
-- 10 000 seeks and a compile time proportional to the list length. Past a few
-- hundred identifiers, a temporary table plus a join is faster on all three.
-- Step 1: the identifiers, already in hand.
CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE wanted_order (order_id bigint PRIMARY KEY)
    ON COMMIT DROP;
INSERT INTO wanted_order (order_id)
SELECT order_id FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC
 LIMIT 50;

-- Step 2: everything about those 50 orders, in one statement.
SELECT w.order_id, l.sku, l.qty, l.line_amount, p.provider, p.amount
  FROM wanted_order w
  LEFT JOIN sales_order_line l ON l.order_id = w.order_id
  LEFT JOIN payment_event    p ON p.order_id = w.order_id
 ORDER BY w.order_id, l.line_no;

-- The test that finds the defect, and it costs nothing to run in CI:
-- count distinct statements per request. One request, one query, is the goal.
-- pg_stat_statements delta over a fixed window, grouped by the digest.
--
--   SELECT calls, rows, left(query, 60) FROM pg_stat_statements
--    WHERE query LIKE '%sales_order_line%' ORDER BY calls DESC LIMIT 10;
-- A digest whose call count equals the number of orders rendered in the
-- window is the N+1, named and quantified.
FixRound tripsWorks whenFails whenEngine portability
Single join1Fan-out is small and bounded; the consumer wants a flat row setTwo one-to-many tables in the same query — the row count is the product, and a three-way join of order → line → payment gives 4 × 3 = 12 rows per orderUniversal
LATERAL1The sub-query must see the outer row; the per-outer-row count varies; exact one-row-per-outer semantics are neededMySQL has no LATERAL at any 9.x version. The derived-table workaround requires a pre-filter, which is only equivalent if the range is known in advancePostgreSQL, SQL Server. Not MySQL
Batched IN or temp table2–3The identifier list is known; the result set is large; the work is in a data-loading or export pathList length above a few hundred, where the plan degrades on all three enginesUniversal, with the temp-table form preferred above 200 values
Pre-aggregated cache0–1The same sub-query is issued repeatedly with the same outer rows — a rendered page revisited, a webhook replayNever for correctness-critical reads. Chapter 10 covers when this is safeUniversal

The variant that hides from every tool: the N+1 inside a trigger or a stored procedure. A PL/pgSQL function that loops over an array issuing an UPDATE per element is one statement to the application’s connection, one plan, one entry in pg_stat_statements — and 5 000 round trips to the storage engine, each with its own WAL record and its own replication amplification. A cursor-based FOR loop inside a function has the same property. The detection is a per-call timing anomaly: the same query taking 4 ms at 1 000 rows and 900 ms at 50 000 rows is linear-plus, and the line-level profile in PL/pgSQL shows the loop. The repair is a set-based statement inside the function, which is faster and shorter than the loop it replaces.

Make the N+1 a compile error rather than a review finding. Three static checks catch the overwhelming majority: a lint rule that fails on a repository call inside a loop, an architecture-test that asserts a per-request statement budget, and a proxy-level metric that alerts when the ratio of statements to requests for a given route exceeds a threshold. The third is the most valuable because it catches the cases the first two do not see — the lazy load, the dynamically built query, the ORM’s own eager-loading that degenerates under a nullable relationship. Chapter 14 covers where that metric lives.

9.6 Pagination, batch size, projection and plan reuse

Four habits that cost nothing, are portable across all three engines, and eliminate more production performance problems than any index: select the columns you need, order the rows you return, paginate by key rather than by offset, and reuse the plan.

Projection. SELECT * is not merely inelegant. It makes the plan sensitive to columns nobody reads, it defeats every covering index the moment one column is added, it prevents index-only scans on MySQL and SQL Server entirely, and it is the reason a table with a 4 KB metadata column behaves differently from a table without one. The rule is that a projection list is written out, always, and that a code generator is the only acceptable way to write it.

Ordering. LIMIT without ORDER BY returns an arbitrary set of rows from an arbitrary point in the access path, and on all three engines the arbitrariness is not even stable between executions. The rows on page 2 may overlap page 1, or skip entirely. This is not a theoretical defect: a reporting export paginated this way silently loses rows, and the loss is invisible because the total is not checked. The related trap is LIMIT with an ORDER BY on a column that is not in the index — the engine must read every matching row and sort them before discarding all but n. A Top operator is O(n) memory; a Sort before it is O(m log m) where m is everything that matched.

Pagination. Offset pagination asks the database to find and discard offset rows before returning the next page. At page 1 000 000 of a table that has churned, the engine walks a million index entries and a million dead tuples, so page n costs n and the cost grows with every page request — which means the deepest pages are both the slowest and the most likely to be requested, because that is where a user who searched rather than scrolled ends up. Keyset pagination asks for rows strictly after the last key seen, and costs the same on every page. The cost of keyset is that jumping to an arbitrary page number is no longer supported, and a stable total order requires a tiebreaker column.

Batch size. A batch is a tradeoff between round trips and transaction size, and both terms are measurable. Too small and the per-statement overhead dominates; too large and the lock hold time, the transaction log volume and the replication lag all grow. The useful measurement is the curve of throughput against batch size, and it has a maximum that is usually somewhere between 500 and 5 000 rows depending on the row width and the engine.

-- Projection: the covering index only works if the list matches. This plan uses
-- Index Only Scan; adding status to the SELECT, or the column existing in the
-- index already, is the whole difference between an index read and a heap read.
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ix_sales_order_tenant_placed
    ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)
    INCLUDE (order_id, status, total_excl_tax);
-- MySQL: covering is automatic; the index must contain every selected column.
--   CREATE INDEX ix_so ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id, status);
--   -- and the plan says "Using index" instead of "Using where".
-- SQL Server: INCLUDE columns do not participate in the key but live in the
--   leaf, which is the same idea under a different keyword.
--   CREATE INDEX ix_so ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)
--     INCLUDE (order_id, status, total_excl_tax) WITH (ONLINE = ON);

-- Keyset pagination. The tiebreaker is mandatory: placed_at is not unique,
-- -- and without it, two rows with the same timestamp can straddle a page
-- -- boundary and one of them is skipped or repeated forever.
SELECT order_id, placed_at, status, total_excl_tax
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412
   AND (placed_at, order_id) < ('2026-09-14T11:02:17Z'::timestamptz, 88100041)
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC, order_id DESC
 LIMIT 50;

-- MySQL 9.x: row-value comparison is supported from 8.0 and is the cleanest
-- form; the equivalent is a lexicographic expansion, which is what the optimiser
-- rewrites the row-value form into anyway.
SELECT order_id, placed_at, status
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412
   AND (placed_at < '2026-09-14 11:02:17' OR (placed_at = '2026-09-14 11:02:17' AND order_id < 88100041))
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC, order_id DESC
 LIMIT 50;

-- SQL Server 2025: OFFSET/FETCH is the standard form and the tuple comparison
-- is not available, so the expansion is mandatory. The ORDER BY is not
-- optional: without it, OFFSET is an error, which is a good design decision.
SELECT TOP (50) order_id, placed_at, status
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412
   AND (placed_at < '20260914T110217'
        OR (placed_at = '20260914T110217' AND order_id < 88100041))
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC, order_id DESC;

-- Offset pagination, shown so the degradation can be measured. Force the
-- optimiser to materialise every skipped row: OFFSET 2000000 on a table with
-- 6% dead tuples means walking 2 million index entries and 2 million dead heap
-- tuples, and the "Rows Removed by Filter" line in the plan is the evidence.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT order_id, placed_at FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC
 OFFSET 2000000 ROWS FETCH NEXT 50 ROWS ONLY;

-- Batch size, measured. The curve has a maximum; find it rather than guessing.
-- PostgreSQL: a prepared statement executed with a varying batch parameter.
PREPARE batch_insert (bigint, numeric, text) AS
    INSERT INTO payment_event (tenant_id, order_id, amount, provider, created_at)
    VALUES ($1, 88100041, $2, 'stripe', now());
-- Run 10 000 rows in batches of 50, 200, 1 000, 5 000; record rows/s,
-- mean statement latency, total WAL bytes, and peak replica lag.

-- Plan reuse. PostgreSQL: plan_cache_mode. auto re-plans after five
-- executions, which is right for most workloads. force_custom_plan
-- re-plans every execution -- correct for a statement whose selectivity varies
-- wildly with the parameter (a tenant filter) and expensive in CPU.
-- force_generic_plan is the opposite and is usually catastrophic here.
SET plan_cache_mode = 'auto';
SET plan_cache_mode = 'force_custom_plan';   -- per-session, for the dashboard query

-- PostgreSQL generic plan trap, measured: a plan cached for tenant_id = 1
-- (0.001% of rows) is reused for tenant_id = 99000 (4% of rows) and is 400x
-- slower than a custom plan for it. Auto mode avoids this by re-planning
-- after five executions; observe it in pg_prepared_statements.
SELECT name, generic_plans, custom_plans,
       ROUND(100.0 * generic_plans / GREATEST(generic_plans + custom_plans, 1), 1)
         AS pct_generic
  FROM pg_prepared_statements
 ORDER BY generic_plans DESC;

-- MySQL 9.x: the analogous problem is optimiser_switch usage, and the
-- tuning knob is optimizer_switch='plan_cache_mode=server' in 8.0.28+,
-- which makes each prepared statement's plan adapt to the parameter like
-- PostgreSQL's auto. Without it MySQL re-plans every execution, which
-- is correct and costs parse-and-optimise time on every call.
SET optimizer_switch = 'plan_cache_mode=server';
--
-- SQL Server 2025: parameter sniffing is the named problem and OPTION(RECOMPILE)
-- is the named answer, at the cost of a compile per execution. Apply it to the
-- specific statement, not the procedure, or the whole procedure loses plan reuse.
--
--   OPTION (RECOMPILE)              -- always re-plan: correct, costs a compile
--   OPTION (OPTIMIZE FOR (@tenant_id UNKNOWN))  -- always use the average-density plan
--   OPTION (PARAMETERIZATION PLAN PER VALUE)   -- 2022+; a plan per distinct value
HabitCost when omittedDetectionFix
Explicit projection listCovering index unusable; heap read per row; network and deserialisation cost proportional to row width rather than to need; breaks on every ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN because a mapped class that expects 14 columns gets 15Compare returned columns to used columns in pg_stat_statements; watch for the deploy-time failure where a SELECT * mapper stops matching a new column shapeWrite the list. If it is generated, generate it from the schema at build time, not at query time
Explicit total orderNon-deterministic results, rows skipped or repeated across pages, exports that silently lose rowsDifferential test: run the same paginated query 50 times and compare the concatenated identifier setsAlways ORDER BY, always with a unique tiebreaker as the final key
Keyset paginationPage n costs O(n); deep pages are seconds; under churn, rows are lost between pages regardlessRows Removed by Filter in the plan; p99 latency that is a straight line in page depthTuple comparison on (sort key, unique key), or the lexicographic expansion on engines without it
Measured batch sizeEither round-trip-bound or transaction-bound, and usually both on different workloads at different times of dayThroughput-versus-batch curve; p99 commit latency as a function of batch sizeFind the knee; set a floor of 100 and a ceiling of 5 000 and make the ceiling a per-statement timeout guard
Reused plansEither a stale plan reused against new data distribution, or a re-parse per executionPostgreSQL: pg_prepared_statements generic vs custom ratio. SQL Server: Query Store, comparing recompiles to executions. MySQL: Com_stmt_prepare versus Com_stmt_executeDefault to adaptive behaviour on all three, and use force_custom_plan or OPTION(RECOMPILE) only on the named statements where the selectivity genuinely varies

Cursor pagination for large exports, and the one thing that breaks it. Keyset pagination is correct and cheap for interactive use. For an export of four million rows, the right structure is a server-side cursor or a keyset loop with a bounded batch, both of which hold a transaction open for the duration on PostgreSQL and therefore block VACUUM for its duration. The correct export does not hold a transaction: it commits every batch and resumes from the last key, exactly as the backfill in section 8.5 does. What breaks both approaches is a concurrent write to the sort key — a backdated order inserted with a placed_at earlier than the last key already read will be picked up and may be exported twice, and the fix is a placed_at no earlier than the export’s start time, or a snapshot on a replica that was not replicating the export’s window.

Exercises 9

  1. Take the top ten statements by total time from pg_stat_statements, performance_schema or Query Store. For each, compute the shape ratio from section 9.1 and identify the access pattern from chapter 1 it serves. Produce a table mapping every statement to an AP, and flag any statement that serves no AP — those are the ones nobody designed for.
  2. Reproduce the estimate-gap failure from section 9.3 on a table with correlated tenant_id and order_state columns. Record the estimate and the actual on the driving node, create the extended statistics object or histogram, ANALYZE, and re-read the plan. Record the before and after in a single table, and note whether the join order changed as well as the cost.
  3. For one endpoint of your own, count the statements per request. If it is greater than one, identify the fix from section 9.5 that applies, implement it, and measure the improvement in both statement count and p99 latency. Then verify that the ORM’s lazy loading has not simply moved the N+1 into a second query that is harder to see.
  4. Measure offset pagination at depths 1, 1 000, 10 000, 100 000 and 1 000 000 on a table with realistic bloat, recording wall time, buffers touched and Rows Removed by Filter. Then implement keyset pagination and repeat. Present the two curves and identify the depth at which offset becomes unusable — the answer is a design constraint, not a curiosity.
  5. Trigger the generic-plan trap: prepare a tenant-scoped query, execute it once for the smallest tenant, then a thousand times for the largest, and record the latency of the first execution and of the thousandth. Compare with plan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan and with force_generic_plan. Determine which setting is correct for your workload and write the evidence into the configuration file.
  6. Find a query in a codebase you can reach that has a correlated subquery, a SELECT *, or a LIMIT without an ORDER BY. Fix it, add a regression test that asserts the deterministic page contents, and measure. Add the deterministic-pagination test to CI so the defect cannot return.

10 · Caching and Read Performance

10.1 The cache stack, browser to database10.2 Cache-aside and correct invalidation10.3 Cache keys, versioning and the key:version technique10.4 Stampedes, negative caching and jitter10.5 TTL as a correctness decision; entity versus query caching10.6 Redis, APCu and HTTP caching compared

A cache is a replica of a fact that someone else may change. Every difficulty in caching follows from that one sentence: the cache is always correct at the moment it is written and always potentially wrong afterwards, and the only question is how long it stays right and what happens in the window where it does not. This chapter covers the layer stack, the invalidation pattern that is actually correct, key design, stampede protection, and the honest accounting of staleness that a user-facing system has to make.

10.1 The cache stack, browser to database

Five layers sit between the user and the row, each with a different invalidation authority, a different cost per hit, and a different staleness window. Putting data in the wrong layer is the most common and most expensive caching mistake, and the reason it is expensive is that the wrong layer is the one that is hardest to invalidate.

The layers in ascending order of staleness tolerance and descending order of proximity:

LayerWho controls itCost per hitTypical stalenessRight forWrong for
1. Browser cache (Cache-Control, ETag)Your HTTP response header, subject to the user clearing itZero — no network at allWhatever TTL you set; can be minutes or a dayStatic assets, immutable content, a page the user will look at twice in a sessionAnything where the same person sees two different values in the same session
2. CDN / edge cacheYou, via a surrogate key or a purge APIVery low, ~10–40 ms from a nearby point of presenceUsually seconds; purge is not instantaneous everywhereAnonymous reads of public data; large static payloads; edge-rendered HTML for unauthenticated usersAuthenticated content, unless the cache key includes a signature of the authorisation
3. Reverse-proxy / HTTP cacheYou, via headersLow, sub-millisecond on the same hostSeconds, or a stale-while-revalidate windowThe same as the CDN, for traffic that does not leave the regionNon-idempotent requests — never cache anything but GET and HEAD at this layer
4. Shared cache (Redis, Memcached)Your application code, explicitly0.2–1 ms, plus a serialisation costWhatever your invalidation logic guarantees — from zero to unboundedExpensive aggregate reads shared across processes; reference data; anything where a sub-second window is acceptableSingle-row reads of a table that is small enough to serve from the connection pool anyway
5. In-process cache (APCu, Symfony cache, a Go sync.Map)Your application code, plus a deployment to clear itMicrosecondsAt most the lifetime of the process, unless invalidatedPer-process hot data, configuration, feature-flag snapshots, anything read on every request by every processAnything that must be coherent across a fleet — because there is no such mechanism, only a timeout
6. Materialised view / rollup tableThe database’s own maintenance machineryIndex-scan speed, plus a refresh costOne refresh interval, exactlyAggregates over data too large to aggregate on demand, at a stated refresh lagAnything where a minute of staleness is unacceptable — the refresh is the staleness, always

The rule that decides the layer is the size of the thing being cached relative to the cost of producing it. If a query takes 2 ms and returns 40 bytes, caching it at layer 4 or 5 is a net loss: the serialisation, the network round trip and the eviction bookkeeping cost more than the query. If a query takes 900 ms and returns 4 MB, caching it is transformative. The crossover is usually around a 10× ratio of production cost to retrieval cost, and it is worth measuring rather than assuming, because the retrieval cost of an in-process string is about 100 ns while the retrieval cost of a Redis round trip is about 400 µs.

A second rule decides the layer, and it is the one most often broken: invalidation difficulty increases as you go down the stack. A browser cache is invalidated by a response header on the next request. A CDN is invalidated by a purge call. A shared cache is invalidated by a DEL from application code that knows the key. A materialised view is invalidated by a refresh the database runs. There is no layer at which the answer to "what must be deleted when this row changes" is easier to compute than the answer at layer 4 — and at layer 4 the answer is computable in application code, which is why most production systems converge there.

-- Layer 3: HTTP caching, declared by the server. Vary must include every input
-- that changes the representation, or two users will receive each other's
-- cached representation. This is the single most consequential header here.
--
-- Anonymous plan pricing: public, identical for everyone, changes monthly.
-- max-age=300 and s-maxage=3600 say: the browser may reuse for 5 minutes,
-- any shared cache may reuse for an hour. s-maxage is the CDN's instruction
-- and the browser ignores it.
--
--   Cache-Control: public, max-age=300, s-maxage=3600, stale-while-revalidate=600
--   ETag: "plan-2026-09-a1c4"
--   Vary: Accept-Encoding

-- Authenticated dashboard: private means no shared cache, and the ETag makes
-- the revalidation conditional and free. must-revalidate stops a stale
-- response being served after it expires without a check.
--
--   Cache-Control: private, no-cache, must-revalidate
--   ETag: "W/<hash of tenant_id + subscription.version + query>"
--   Vary: Authorization, Accept-Encoding

-- no-store is the correct answer for anything containing personal data, and it
-- is also the correct answer for anything you cannot describe the staleness of.

-- Layer 4: the shared cache key. Namespace, tenant, entity, version, variant.
-- The tenant is in the key on every read of tenant data; its absence is the
-- single most common cross-tenant data leak in a cached multi-tenant system.
SELECT 't', tenant_id, plan_code, plan_version FROM subscription
 WHERE tenant_id = 412;

-- Layer 6: a rollup table, maintained by the database rather than by the
-- application. This is the right answer when the query is a scan of
-- millions of rows that reduces to a few hundred, and the staleness budget
-- is a stated interval rather than an accident.
CREATE TABLE tenant_daily_spend (
    tenant_id     bigint NOT NULL,
    spend_on      date NOT NULL,
    order_count   int NOT NULL,
    spend_amount  numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, spend_on)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (spend_on);

-- Recomputed, not incrementally maintained. The idempotent UPDATE makes it safe
-- to re-run after a failure, which an incremental trigger-maintained counter is not.
INSERT INTO tenant_daily_spend (tenant_id, spend_on, order_count, spend_amount)
SELECT o.tenant_id,
       o.placed_at::date,
       count(DISTINCT o.order_id),
       sum(o.total_excl_tax)
  FROM sales_order o
 WHERE o.placed_at >= current_date - 2
 ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, spend_on) DO UPDATE
    SET order_count  = EXCLUDED.order_count,
        spend_amount = EXCLUDED.spend_amount;

The layer that is almost always missing. Between the application and the database there is almost always a connection pool, and a connection pool is a cache of connections — which means a cache of session state, temporary tables, advisory locks and SET variables. Chapter 14.5 covers the two failure modes this produces, and both are severe: a variable set on one connection and read from another, and a prepared statement that the server no longer recognises because it was prepared on a different backend. Neither is a hypothetical; both are a direct consequence of treating a pooled connection as a session.

10.2 Cache-aside and correct invalidation

Cache-aside — the application looks in the cache, and on a miss goes to the database and then populates the cache — is the pattern to use, because it is the only one where the cache failure degrades to normal database performance rather than to an outage.

Write-through, where the cache is updated in the same operation as the database, sounds safer and is not: it makes the cache write a participant in the correctness of the database write, so a cache failure becomes a database failure, and it gives you a two-write problem with no distributed transaction. Write-behind, where the cache is written first and the database asynchronously, is faster and loses data on failure. Cache-aside with an explicit invalidation on write is slower by one cache read on the miss path and is the only one of the three whose failure mode is a performance regression.

With cache-aside, the correctness question is entirely the invalidation question, and it must be answered exhaustively or not at all. The question is: given this write, which cache keys are now wrong? There are four sources of wrongness and all four must be enumerated for the write path that has been implemented:

SourceExample in this schemaKeys affectedDetection
The entity’s own keysales_order.status changesorder:{id}Trivial and the only one teams get right
Every query that reads the entityAny cached order list for tenant 412, tenant dashboard, monthly spendUnknown, and unbounded, unless the cache is per-entityThis is the hard one. See section 10.5 for the per-entity resolution
Every query that reads a related entityChanging order_state invalidates a cached count of unpaid orders for the tenant, a cached first-line-SKU summary, the tenant’s rollup rowAgain unknown unless the read set is declaredRequires a declared read set. See the note below
Every query that reads a denormalised copyChanging sales_order.total_excl_tax invalidates every cached view of tenant_daily_spend containing that dayBounded and knowable, because the denormalisation was a deliberate design decisionThe invalidation ledger from section 3.6, if it was kept
-- Cache-aside, written out in full, with the invalidation enumerated rather
-- than assumed. The comment block is the actual deliverable of this
-- pattern: the code is twenty lines and the read set is the design.

--
-- Cached reads that consult sales_order, declared explicitly:
--   order:detail:{tenant_id}:{order_id}      -- the order and its lines
--   order:list:{tenant_id}:{cursor}         -- a page of the order list
--   tenant:dashboard:{tenant_id}           -- counts, plan, spend-to-date
--   tenant:spend:{tenant_id}:{date}        -- the rollup row
-- Consequently, an UPDATE to any sales_order row invalidates all four
-- key families for that tenant. Section 10.3 replaces this enumeration with
-- a version counter so the enumeration is not required.
--
--   Cache GET order:detail:412:88100041
--   -- miss:
--   row := SELECT ... FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = ? AND order_id = ?
--   if row not found:
--       SET order:detail:412:88100041 MISSING 30s     -- negative cache, 10.4
--   else:
--       SET order:detail:412:88100041 <serialised row> 300s
--   -- hit: return the deserialised row
--
--   On UPDATE sales_order SET status = ? WHERE tenant_id = ? AND order_id = ?:
--   1. Commit the database transaction first. The cache is never invalidated
--      before the data is durable, because a crash between the two would
--      leave a cache permanently disagreeing with a database that never changed.
--   2. DEL order:detail:{tenant}:{order}
--   3. DEL tenant:dashboard:{tenant}
--   4. DEL tenant:spend:{tenant}:{placed_at::date}
--   5. Delete every order:list:{tenant}:* -- which is a SCAN, and is the reason
--      section 10.3 exists. A version counter on the list family replaces it
--      with a single INCR.

-- The failure mode of the DEL-first ordering, stated as SQL so it is
-- unambiguous. In a write-through cache this is a two-phase commit without
-- a coordinator, and the recovery is a reconciliation job that nobody writes.
BEGIN;
UPDATE sales_order
   SET status = 'shipped', version = version + 1
 WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND order_id = 88100041;
-- DEL the four key families here and the transaction rolls back on conflict:
-- the cache says "shipped", the database says "paid", and the cache has a
-- 300-second TTL during which the user is shown a state that never existed.
COMMIT;
-- Only now, outside the transaction, invalidate.

-- The transactional outbox from section 7.5 is the correct carrier for the
-- invalidation, because it makes "the data was committed" and "the cache was
-- invalidated" the same durable fact, retried until both succeed, and
-- observable when neither has happened.
INSERT INTO outbox (topic, aggregate_type, aggregate_id, tenant_id, payload, created_at)
VALUES ('order.status_changed', 'sales_order', 88100041, 412,
        '{"from":"paid","to":"shipped"}', now());

The read set must be known, and if it is not, the cache is a guess. This is the honest statement of the cache-aside problem and it is not solvable by better code. If you cannot enumerate every query that reads sales_order, you cannot invalidate them, and the only remaining correctness mechanism is a TTL — which means a bounded staleness, not correctness. The two acceptable resolutions are: cache only per-entity keys, so the read set is one key per write by construction; or declare the read set, keep it in the same repository as the queries, and make a new cached query fail review until its invalidation is written down. Teams that take the second option and maintain the declaration find that the declaration is also the best documentation of the schema’s actual access patterns they will ever have, because the declaration is derived from real reads rather than from the access-pattern inventory of chapter 1, which was a prediction.

Invalidate by event, not by call site, and let the event be the audit trail. Every write emits a domain event into the outbox; one consumer turns each event into a set of cache deletions. The advantage is not elegance, it is that a write path that forgot to invalidate becomes a missing event rather than a silent inconsistency, and a test asserting "every order write produces an order.status_changed event" catches it. It also means the invalidation logic lives in exactly one place rather than being duplicated in every repository method, and that a bulk backfill from section 8.5 automatically invalidates rather than silently producing a stale cache for 300 seconds.

10.3 Cache keys, versioning and the key:version technique

The key:version technique replaces the invalidation problem with a monotonic counter, so that a write is one INCR and a read is one extra GET, and nothing has to know what depends on what.

The idea is that each cache key family is suffixed with a version number, and the version lives in one place — a database row, a Redis key, or a process-local cache. Reads compose the real key as family:{identity}:{version}. Invalidation increments the version. The old entries are no longer reachable and expire on their own TTL. The consequence is that invalidation is constant cost and constant knowledge: the writer needs to know only that it must increment version 3, not that four other keys exist.

-- The version table. One row per cache key family, per scope. This is the
-- complete invalidation contract: a write increments the families it can
-- possibly affect, and nothing else is required to know about any other family.
CREATE TABLE cache_version (
    scope        text NOT NULL,      -- 'tenant' or 'global'
    scope_id     bigint NOT NULL,   -- tenant_id, or 0 for global
    family       text NOT NULL,      -- 'order_detail', 'order_list', 'dashboard', 'spend'
    version      bigint NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
    PRIMARY KEY (scope, scope_id, family)
);

-- Read path: two lookups (version, then value) instead of one. Batch them
-- where possible -- a single MGET, or a Lua script, or a local micro-cache with
-- a 1s TTL on the version itself.
--
--   ver := GET  cachever:tenant:412:order_detail           -- 1
--   raw := GET order_detail:412:88100041:1
--   if raw is nil:
--       row := SELECT ...
--       ver := GET cachever:tenant:412:order_detail       -- re-read: it may
--                                                        -- have moved while
--                                                        -- the query ran
--       SET order_detail:412:88100041:{ver} <row> 300s
--       return row
--   return raw
--
-- The re-read of the version after the database read is not optional. Without
-- it, a write that commits during the query writes the row at version 1 and
-- stores it at version 2, where it will be read as authoritative -- a value
-- that was stale at the moment it was cached and will persist for the full TTL.

-- Write path: one INCR, applied after the database commit, via the outbox
-- consumer so that a crash between the two is retried rather than lost.
--
--   INCR cachever:tenant:412:order_detail      ← after the commit, never before
--   INCR cachever:tenant:412:order_list
--   INCR cachever:tenant:412:dashboard
--
-- The entries written at the previous version are unreachable. They are not
-- deleted; they expire. On a hot family with a 300s TTL and 4 000 writes per
-- minute, a busy tenant accumulates 4 000 * 300 = 1.2M unreachable keys, so the
-- eviction policy is doing real work and maxmemory-policy must be allkeys-lru
-- rather than a noeviction that turns memory pressure into write failures.

--
-- The version counter in the database, for the case where Redis is the cache
-- and the database is the source of truth for the version. INCR in the database
-- is a row update, so it must be idempotent-safe and must be in the same
-- transaction as the write, or versioned from the outbox sequence instead.
UPDATE cache_version
   SET version = version + 1
 WHERE scope = 'tenant' AND scope_id = 412 AND family = 'order_detail';

-- Key composition, and the rules that make a key safe. Note that none of the
-- components are attacker-controlled without normalisation, and that the
-- tenant is present on every tenant-scoped key.
--
--   <family>:<scope>:<scope_id>:<discriminator>:<version>
--
--   order_detail:tenant:412:88100041:1
--   order_list:tenant:412:2026-09-14:7
--   spend:tenant:412:2026-09-01:1
--
-- Rules:
--   1. The tenant identifier is in the key. Always. No exceptions, including
--      for keys that are currently only read by one tenant.
--   2. The discriminator is normalised and length-bounded: a key built from a
--      raw query string is a memory-exhaustion vector and a collision source.
--   3. A schema version prefix is included so that a deploy which changes the
--      shape of the cached object cannot read an object of the old shape.
--      Without it, a rolling deploy returns deserialisation errors at the rate
--      the cache turns over -- which is to say, for hours.
--   4. No key encodes anything the database already guarantees is unique
--      without also encoding the tenant, because uniqueness is scoped and
--      the key space is not.
Invalidation strategyCost of a writeCost of a readKnowledge required of the writerFailure mode
Explicit DEL of named keysOne round trip per key family; a SCAN if any key family is a wildcardOne GETComplete read set — every query that reads the entityA query added without its invalidation produces a permanently stale key until TTL
Tag-based / surrogate keysOne SADD per key on write, one SMEMBERS plus DEL per tag on invalidateOne GET plus tag bookkeepingComplete read set, expressed as tag membership rather than as key namesTag sets grow without bound and SMEMBERS becomes a blocking operation on a large tag
key:versionOne INCR per affected familyOne GET for the version, one for the value — batched, or the version is cached locally for 1 sWhich families, not which keysUnreachable keys accumulate until TTL; memory pressure if the eviction policy is wrong
Write-throughThe cache write is on the critical path and in the failure pathOne GETComplete read set, plus a serialisation format for every shapeA cache failure becomes a request failure; two-write problem with no coordinator
TTL only, no invalidationZeroOne GETNone — and that is the entire costBounded staleness equal to the TTL, everywhere, unconditionally. Acceptable only where that is genuinely acceptable

Versioning is not free, and the cost is on the read path. The technique adds a lookup to every read, and if that lookup is a Redis round trip it has roughly doubled the cost of a cache hit over a bare GET. Three mitigations, in increasing order of correctness. Cache the version in-process for 100 ms to 1 s, which bounds the staleness added by the version to that interval and is usually the whole answer. Batch the version and the value into one MGET, which requires composing the key before the version is known and so only works for the families whose version is predictable. Use a Lua script on Redis, which runs both operations server-side and eliminates the interleaving entirely — the correct answer for the read-after-invalidation race in the previous section, and the reason a Redis cluster deployment should budget for scripting.

10.4 Stampedes, negative caching and jitter

A cache stampede is the event where a popular key expires and every concurrent request for it misses simultaneously, producing a thundering herd against the database. It is not a gradual degradation; it is a step change, and on a query already near the database’s capacity it is an outage with a one-second duration.

The mechanism is worth being precise about, because the defences differ. When a key’s TTL expires, N requests already in flight all observe the miss. Each opens its own database connection, each runs the query, each waits. The database sees N times the normal load for that statement. The TTL then means the herd repeats every cycle. Two aggravating factors make it worse: a shared cache makes N large across every process in the fleet, and a popular key — a hot tenant’s dashboard — is by definition the one with the most concurrent readers.

The defences are layered and each is cheap:

DefenceMechanismCostFailure if not applied
Probabilistic early expiryEach reader independently decides to refresh early with probability p × (time since expiry). Spreads the refresh over the TTL windowOne EXPIREAT comparison per read, or a stored expiry timestamp in the valueEffective for most keys with no coordination at all. Weak for a key with thousands of concurrent readers, where the collision probability is still high
Jittered TTLTTL is base ± random(base/10) rather than a constantZeroA fleet that populated a key together expires it together. Jitter is the cheapest fix and it should always be on, even with the others
Request coalescing / single flightThe first miss takes a lock; concurrent misses wait on it, then re-read the key and find it populatedA lock key, a short wait, and a lock TTL longer than the query’s worst caseCollapses the herd to one query. The lock must have a TTL or a crashed holder deadlocks the key until eviction
Stale-while-revalidateReturn the stale value immediately and refresh in the backgroundServing a value past its TTL, deliberatelyCorrect for a bounded window, and the only defence that also protects against a database outage. Section 10.6 covers the HTTP form
Negative cachingCache the absence of a row for a short TTLOne extra key, and a shorter TTL than the positive caseA missing row is a cache miss forever, so every request for a non-existent order is a full database query. This is the most common stampede in practice, because a bot enumerating order IDs is an attack that looks like a stampede
-- Single-flight with a lock, a lock TTL, and a wait that re-reads rather than
-- blocking on the lock. The wait must be bounded and the waiter's re-read
-- must happen after the lock is observed as free, or the herd is merely relocated.
--
-- function GetOrder(ctx, tenantID, orderID):
--     key := "v3:order_detail:tenant:412:88100041"
--     if raw := GET(key); raw != nil: return decode(raw)
--
--     lockKey := key + ":lock"
--     if SET(lockKey, myToken, NX EX 10):        -- 10s, longer than the worst query
--         defer DEL_IF_TOKEN(lockKey, myToken)    -- never delete another holder's lock
--         row := SELECT ... FROM sales_order ...
--         if row == nil:
--             SET(key, "MISSING", 30)            -- negative cache, short TTL
--             return notFound
--         ver := GET("cachever:tenant:412:order_detail")
--         SET(key + ":" + ver, encode(row), 300 + rand.Intn(60))   -- jitter
--         return row
--
--     // We are a waiter. Bounded wait, then re-read.
--     for i := 0; i < 20; i++:
--         sleep(50ms + rand.Intn(50))           -- jitter on the poll interval
--         if raw := GET(key); raw != nil: return decode(raw)
--     // The holder is slow or dead. Fall through and query the database ourselves
--     // rather than failing: correctness beats protecting the database from a
--     // 1-second burst, and the herd has already been reduced to the waiters
--     // that exceeded the bound.
--     return SELECT ...

-- The probabilistic early-expiry variant, in the form a Lua script must take
-- so that the compare and the refresh are one atomic step server-side. Reading
-- the value, deciding in the client, and then writing, leaves a window in which
-- two clients both decide to refresh.
--
-- KEYS[1] = value key, KEYS[2] = lock key
-- ARGV[1] = ttl seconds, ARGV[2] = now (ms), ARGV[3] = early-expiry probability per 10s
-- local raw    = redis.call('GET', KEYS[1])
-- if not raw then return nil end
-- -- The value carries its own expiry so the client does not need a second call.
-- local expiresAt = tonumber(string.match(raw, '"exp":(%d+)'))
-- local age       = (tonumber(ARGV[2]) - expiresAt + tonumber(ARGV[1])*1000) / 1000
-- if age >= tonumber(ARGV[1]) then return raw end              -- genuinely expired
-- if math.random() < tonumber(ARGV[3]) then
--     if redis.call('SET', KEYS[2], '1', 'NX', 'EX', 10) then
--         return {raw, 'REFRESH'}                                  -- this caller refreshes
--     else
--         return raw                                               -- someone else does
--     end
-- end
-- return raw

-- Negative caching, and the one place it is dangerous. A MISSING for a
-- genuinely absent row is correct. A MISSING written because the read went to
-- -- a read replica with lag (section 11.3) is a lie that outlives the write by
-- 30 seconds, and it is the interaction of these two features that produces
-- "the order I just created is not there". Never serve a negative cache entry
-- from a replica, and never negative-cache a read that was served by a replica.

-- The stampede detector. This is the alert that fires when a hot key expires
-- -- and the database query count for one statement goes to its normal peak in
-- under a second, which is the shape of the event and nothing else produces it.
SELECT calls, ROUND(calls / GREATEST(stats_reset, 1), 2) AS calls_per_sec, query
  FROM pg_stat_statements
 WHERE query LIKE '%tenant:dashboard%'
    OR query LIKE '%WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND placed_at%'
 ORDER BY calls DESC;

A lock-based single flight without a lock TTL is a distributed deadlock. If the process holding the lock is killed — a deploy, an OOM kill, a lost connection — the lock key remains until the cache’s own eviction reaches it, and every subsequent request for that key waits, then times out, then falls through and queries the database anyway, which is the herd you were trying to prevent plus a pile of timed-out requests. Every lock key must have a TTL longer than the query’s worst case and shorter than the value’s TTL. The related hazard is deleting a lock you no longer hold: the DEL must be conditional on the token, or a slow holder that finally returns will delete the lock a newer holder has taken, and the mutual exclusion is gone.

Warm the cache on deploy, and watch the hit rate as a first-class SLO metric. A rolling deploy replaces every process, and a cold fleet has a hit rate of zero and a database load equal to the full traffic for as long as the keys take to repopulate — which for a large key set is minutes. Warming the popular keys during the deploy, before the new fleet takes traffic, converts that into a background cost. The metric to alert on is not the hit rate itself, which is noisy and slow-moving, but the database query rate for the statements that have a cache: a sudden rise to a value above the pre-deploy baseline means the cache is not doing its job, and that is a much more actionable signal than "hit rate dropped from 94 % to 61 %".

10.5 TTL as a correctness decision; entity versus query caching

A time-to-live is not a performance setting. It is a statement about how long a user may be shown a value that is no longer true, and choosing it is a product decision dressed as a configuration value.

The honest question for a TTL is: if a user is shown this stale value, what is the worst thing that happens? The answers are not all the same, and they determine both the TTL and whether a cache is appropriate at all.

Cached valueWorst consequence of stalenessAppropriate TTLAlternative to a short TTL
Plan catalogue and pricingA customer sees a price that was withdrawn. Recoverable, embarrassing5–60 min, or version-invalidate on price changeVersion the family on every plan write; the TTL is then only a safety net
Tenant dashboard totalsA user sees yesterday’s revenue. Recoverable, and they usually know why30–300 sRollup table at layer 6, where the staleness is an explicit refresh interval
Order status for displayA user is shown an order as paid when it is refunded. Money and trust0 — invalidate on write, or 0 s with stale-while-revalidate bounded to 2 sRead from the primary. This row is the reason caching is dangerous
Permission / entitlement checkA user is shown a feature their plan does not include, or is denied one it does. Security-relevant0 for revocation; short for grantFail closed: cache the grant, never cache the denial, and re-check on the critical action
Reference data: countries, currencies, tax ratesA stale currency list. Nearly harmless1–24 h, version-invalidated on writeNearly always fine
Full-text search resultsA user cannot find a document that exists. Recoverable, confusing60–600 sAccept it and label the timestamp; search staleness is expected by users

The entity-versus-query decision follows from the same analysis, and it is really a decision about the shape of the invalidation problem.

Per-entity caching caches one row, or one row plus its owned children, under a key derived from the primary key. The invalidation set for a write is exactly one key, by construction, and there is nothing to enumerate. The cost is that the query that reads many entities does one GET per entity — a hundred round trips for a hundred-row list — so per-entity caching is only viable when the entity count per query is small, or when the values are held in a per-request local map so the hundred reads become one batched MGET.

Per-query caching caches the result set under a key derived from the query’s parameters. It is far better for the hundred-row list: one round trip, and the database is not touched at all. Its invalidation set is every query that could have produced a different result, which is exactly the read-set problem from section 10.2. It is only safe where the read set is declared, or where the version technique from section 10.3 makes the enumeration unnecessary.

The resolution used in practice is to use both, at different granularities, and be explicit about which is which. Per-entity for the small hot objects — a tenant, a plan, a subscription — where the invalidation set is genuinely one key. Per-query with a version for the large lists and aggregates, where the version makes the invalidation a constant-cost INCR. And a per-request local memo for anything a single request reads more than once, which costs nothing and eliminates the intra-request repetition that makes a hundred-entity list a hundred round trips.

-- Per-entity, with a per-request local memo. The memo is the part that makes
-- per-entity viable for list-shaped reads: 50 entities, 1 MGET, 1 database
-- round trip on a full miss, 0 on a full hit.
--
-- function LoadOrders(ctx, tenantID, cursor, limit):
--     ver  := GET("cachever:tenant:412:order_list")
--     keys := ["v3:order_list:tenant:412:" + cursor + ":" + ver]
--     if raw := MGET(keys); raw[1] != nil: return decode(raw[1])
--
--     rows := SELECT ... FROM sales_order
--                 WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND (placed_at, order_id) < ...
--                 ORDER BY placed_at DESC, order_id DESC LIMIT 50
--     // Per-entity, loaded in one batch and memoised for the request's lifetime
--     // so the render loop does not issue 50 individual lookups.
--     ids := pluck(rows, "order_id")
