--     missing := requestLocal.memo.FilterUncached("order_detail", ids)
--     if len(missing) > 0:
--         vals := MGET(missing.map(k => k + ":" + entityVer))
--         stillMissing := []
--         for i, k := range missing:
--             if vals[i] != nil: requestLocal.memo.Put(k, decode(vals[i]))
--             else: stillMissing = append(stillMissing, k)
--         if len(stillMissing) > 0:
--             // One database query for all of them, keyed by the entity -- this is
--             // the batched form from section 9.5, and it is why per-entity caching
--             // does not need a query cache.
--             for e := range SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE order_id = ANY(stillMissing):
--                 requestLocal.memo.Put(entityKey(e), e)
--                 SET(entityKey(e) + ":" + entityVer, encode(e), 300 + rand.Intn(60))
--     ver = GET("cachever:tenant:412:order_list")   // re-read after the query
--     SET("v3:order_list:tenant:412:" + cursor + ":" + ver, encode(rows), 60 + rand.Intn(15))
--     return rows

-- What a TTL looks like when it is a decision rather than a default. The
-- -- comment is the deliverable; the number is derived from the comment.
--
-- TTL 300s: order_detail. Rationale: the user-visible staleness budget for order
-- state is 0, and the 300s is the version-technique safety net for a missed
-- invalidation event. The outbox makes a missed invalidation observable, so a
-- 300s exposure is acceptable and alertable rather than silent.
-- TTL 60s:  order_list. Rationale: the list is sorted by placed_at, which is
-- immutable, so a new order appears in the list only when a new key is built.
-- The 60s bounds how long a page-1 result can be served without the new order.
-- TTL 30s:  MISSING. Rationale: short because a negative entry is a claim that
-- does not exist, and a false claim is worse than a slow one. Never served from
-- -- a replica (section 10.4).
-- TTL 3600s: plan catalogue. Rationale: version-invalidated on every plan write,
-- -- and the plan table changes a few times a year.
-- TTL 0:    order.payment_status for any screen where money is displayed. Not
-- -- cached. The dashboard shows it with a "as of" timestamp from the replica
-- -- LSN instead, which is honest and requires no cache.

TTL does not compose with replication lag, and the two must be budgeted together. If a value is cached for 300 s on the primary and a replica is 5 s behind, the worst case is 305 s. If the cache is also populated from the replica, the worst case is 300 s plus the replica’s worst observed lag plus the time to detect it, and the replica’s worst observed lag is not its average — it is the maximum during a vacuum, a failover or a network partition, which can be minutes. A system that caches reads served from replicas should therefore either not cache, or cache with a TTL shorter than the maximum tolerable replica lag, or — the honest answer for most systems — read replicated data with an explicit "as of" timestamp and no cache at all. Chapter 11.3 returns to this.

10.6 Redis, APCu and HTTP caching compared

Three cache substrates with three different consistency models, and the choice is a consistency decision before it is a performance one, because each one can produce a different answer to "what does the second process see".

PropertyRedis / ValkeyIn-process (APCu, Symfony cache, go-cache)HTTP cache (browser, CDN, reverse proxy)
Coherence across processes✓ immediate — one shared store, and a DEL is visible to every process on its next read✗ none. There is no invalidation channel; only a TTL or a broadcast. Every process has its own copy and they diverge for the full TTL✓ for a shared cache, subject to the purge API’s propagation delay; ✗ for the browser, which is per-user by definition
Survives a deploy✓ — the store is external and the keys persist✗ — the cache is in the process and dies with it. A deploy is a mass invalidation event✓ for CDN and proxy; ✗ for the browser, though the disk cache persists
Latency per operation0.2–1 ms on a local socket; 1–3 ms across a region, and a cross-region cache read is a worse decision than a cross-region primary read~100 ns for APCu; a PHP apcu_fetch is roughly 100–300 ns, a Redis GET roughly 200–400 µs. Three orders of magnitude0 for a browser hit, 10–40 ms for a CDN hit
What it must be used forAnything shared, anything large, anything that must be coherent across a fleet, distributed locks, rate-limit counters, session storageConfiguration, feature-flag snapshots, per-request memoisation, small hot reference data, pre-rendered fragmentsWhole HTTP responses, for GET and HEAD only, and only when the Vary set is complete
EvictionExplicit. maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru for a pure cache; noeviction for a store that is also a queue, and a memory alarm rather than an eviction in that caseExplicit. APCu’s apc.shm_size plus an LRU or a TTL sweep; exceeding it is silent eviction of arbitrary entries in some configurationsNot yours. The CDN decides, and you decide only through TTLs
Failure modeUnavailable → every read falls through to the database. Acceptable if the fallback is bounded by a bulkhead; fatal if 10 000 processes fall through simultaneously, which is a self-inflicted stampedeProcess OOM-killed by a full shared memory segment, which is an outage that looks like a kernel problemStale content served to a user, bounded by the TTL, with no signal that it happened
-- Redis: the configuration that determines whether it is a cache or a
-- database. maxmemory-policy is the whole decision; everything else is detail.
--
--   CONFIG SET maxmemory 8gb
--   CONFIG SET maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru     -- a cache: evict anything
--   -- allkeys-random is measurably worse and is the default in some images
--   -- volatile-lru requires every key to have a TTL; a key without one is
--   -- never evicted and is a slow memory leak
--   -- noeviction is correct only if Redis also holds queues or locks, where
--   -- silent eviction would lose data
--   --
-- Persistence: for a cache, appendonly no and save "" -- persistence of a
-- cache buys nothing and costs a fork stall on every snapshot.

-- The version counter, on the hot path, with the two properties it must have:
-- atomicity and no eviction. allkeys-lru can evict a version key, and a version
-- that reappears at 1 makes every entry cached under the new 1 unreachable --
-- harmless but wasteful -- and worse, a version key that is evicted and recreated
-- at a LOWER value than some live entries would make those entries reachable again
-- with stale content. Versions are therefore volatile-lru or, better, never
-- evicted: a separate small instance, or a hash-tagged key space.
INCR cachever:tenant:412:order_detail
-- 4 --> 5; every key suffixed ":4" is now unreachable and expires on its own TTL.

-- The bulkhead, which is the thing that makes a cache outage survivable. Without
-- it, Redis going away converts a performance problem into an outage, because
-- every request in the fleet now goes to the database simultaneously.
--
--   semaphore := NewWeighted(200)          // 200 database fallbacks fleet-wide
--   if cacheErr != nil:
--       if semaphore.TryAcquire(1) == false:
--           return 503 with Retry-After, and increment cache.bypass.rejected
--       defer semaphore.Release(1)
--   return serveFromDatabase()   // and log, so the bypass is visible
--
-- The metric that proves the bulkhead works: database QPS during a cache
-- outage must be capped at the fallback budget. If it is not, the semaphore is
-- too generous or the timeout is too long.

-- APCu: process-local, no invalidation channel, so the discipline is a short
-- TTL plus a version check against a value that IS shared.
--
-- // php.ini
-- apc.enabled=1
-- apc.shm_size=256M
-- apc.entries_hint=10000
-- apc.ttl=300
-- apc.gc_ttl=600
--
-- // A local entry is only trusted while the shared version matches, so a
-- // single INCR in Redis retires the entry in every process within one poll.
-- $ver = redis_get("cachever:tenant:412:dashboard") ?? "0";
-- $key = "dash:412:" . $ver;
-- if ($v = apcu_fetch($key)) return $v;
-- $data = build_dashboard(412);
-- apcu_store($key, $data, 300);
-- return $data;

-- HTTP caching, complete and correct. The three directives do three different
-- jobs and getting them confused is the usual cause of a cache that does not work.
--
-- public              -- may be stored by a shared cache (CDN, proxy)
-- private             -- may only be stored by the user's own browser
-- no-store            -- may not be stored anywhere; use for personal data
-- max-age=300         -- fresh for 300s: no request is made at all
-- s-maxage=3600        -- fresh for 3600s in a shared cache only; the browser
--                      -- still honours max-age, and ignores s-maxage
-- stale-while-revalidate=600
--                      -- after expiry, serve stale for up to 600s while one
--                      -- request revalidates in the background. This is the single
--                      -- most useful directive for a database-backed page, because it
--                      -- removes the user-visible latency of a cold cache entirely.
-- stale-if-error=86400
--                      -- serve stale if the origin is failing. The cheapest
--                      -- availability improvement available, and the reason a CDN is
--                      -- worth having in front of a database.
--
--   Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, s-maxage=600,
--                   stale-while-revalidate=300, stale-if-error=3600
--   ETag: "W/<hash>"
--   Vary: Accept-Encoding, Accept-Language
--
-- Conditional requests are what make revalidation free: a 304 carries no body,
-- and a hash of the response body computed once at the edge is the usual ETag.
--
--   GET /api/v1/plan HTTP/1.1
--   If-None-Match: "W/a1c4"
--   --
--   HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified
--   Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, s-maxage=600
--   ETag: "W/a1c4"
--
-- Vary is the one that bites: omitting Authorization from a Vary on an
-- -- authenticated response means the shared cache serves one user's body to
-- -- another user. private (or no-store) plus a complete Vary is the defence, and
-- -- a cache key derived from a hash of the session is the belt-and-braces answer.

Never cache a Redis value on a Redis replica and never cache a replica read in Redis. Both look like reasonable layering and both produce a correctness problem that is very hard to see. A Redis replica applies the INCR asynchronously, so a read of cachever:* from a replica can return a pre-increment version, which makes the reader use a key that still contains pre-invalidation content — the exact race the version technique was built to eliminate, reintroduced by the caching layer. And a value populated from a database replica carries that replica’s lag into the cache, where it persists for the full TTL, turning a sub-second staleness into a multi-minute one. The rule is: the version counter is read from the primary Redis node, and the database is read from the primary database node, whenever the read being cached is a read for which staleness would be a defect.

Exercises 10

  1. Enumerate the read set for sales_order by instrumenting a running system: log every distinct statement that touches the table for 24 hours, group by digest, and produce the list. Cross-check it against the list your repository claims. Every statement in the log that is not in the declaration is a cache-invalidation bug waiting to happen, and every declaration with no corresponding statement is a cache that is never hit.
  2. Implement key:version for one key family and prove the race is closed. In a test, start a write that commits while a read is between its version lookup and its database query, and assert that the read returns the post-write value. Then remove the post-query version re-read and confirm the test fails. That failing test is the demonstration that the re-read is load-bearing.
  3. Trigger a stampede deliberately. Warm a hot key, set its TTL to 1 second, and drive 2 000 concurrent requests through it. Record the database query rate during the event, add single-flight, and repeat. Then remove the lock TTL, kill the lock holder mid-refresh, and record what the waiters do. Conclude from the measurement whether the lock TTL is a necessary control or a precaution.
  4. Implement negative caching with a 30-second TTL and then measure its effect on a bot enumerating order IDs. Record the database query rate before and after. Then route one of those reads to a replica with 8 seconds of lag and demonstrate the false negative — a row that exists, cached as absent, for 30 seconds. Decide, from the demonstration, where negative caching is allowed in your system and write that into the code review checklist.
  5. Assign a TTL to every cached value in a schema you can reach, and for each one write the sentence: "if a user is shown this stale value, the worst consequence is ___". Reject any TTL you cannot complete that sentence for, and replace the cache with a primary read. Then re-measure the hit rate and decide honestly whether the caches you removed were earning their complexity.
  6. Add a bulkhead to the cache-fallback path and then take Redis down under load. Confirm the database query rate is capped at the fallback budget and that excess requests receive a 503 rather than a timeout. Then remove the bulkhead, repeat, and record the database error rate. This is the experiment that justifies the bulkhead.

11 · Replication, Read Scaling and Routing

11.1 Physical versus logical replication11.2 Synchronous versus asynchronous, and the availability trade11.3 Read replicas and read-after-write11.4 Measuring lag correctly11.5 Routing and read/write splitting11.6 Promotion, failover, multi-master and cross-region reality

Replication exists to buy two different things — read capacity and survival — and every design decision in this chapter follows from which of the two is being bought. A replica that exists to scale reads can have seconds of lag. A replica that exists to survive a failure must be provably current, and that requirement propagates back into the write path.

11.1 Physical versus logical replication

Physical replication copies the storage engine’s own change stream and is therefore exact, fast and inflexible. Logical replication copies rows as insert, update and delete statements and is therefore flexible, portable and slower. The distinction is not about fidelity of data — both are exact — it is about what may change and what may be filtered.

Physical replication’s defining property is that the replica applies the same log with the same operations, so it reproduces the data exactly, including the physical layout, the index structure, and the transaction boundaries. That exactness is why it is fast — no parsing, no mapping, no constraint re-checking in the general case — and why it is inflexible: because it is the same engine, a PostgreSQL physical stream cannot apply to a MySQL instance, and a schema change is applied on the primary and appears on the replica as a stream of log records describing the same change. The consequence that catches teams is that a DDL statement on the primary can take the entire replication stream down on the replica if the replica’s version does not understand the log record, which on PostgreSQL means a major-version upgrade must be completed on every replica before it can be completed on the primary.

Logical replication’s defining property is that it applies a row-level transformation. This permits cross-engine and cross-version replication, selective table and column replication, filtering rows by predicate, and — critically for this guide — the ability to replicate a subset of a table while still being physically identical in the destination. It is also the mechanism behind change data capture, and therefore behind every downstream system that needs to know what changed: an analytics warehouse, a search index, a cache invalidation consumer, a data lake.

PropertyPhysicalLogical
What is transmittedBlocks, WAL records, binlog events at the engine level, transaction log recordsRow changes as INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE with the before and after images as configured
Schema changesApplied automatically, because the log describes them. A version mismatch on the replica stops the streamNot carried at all. The DDL must be applied to the destination separately and in the same order. A forgotten DDL on the destination is a permanent, silent divergence
DDL on a large tableThe same cost on every replica, at the same time, because they all apply it. One slow DDL becomes a fleet-wide replication delayApplied once, on the destination, under your control. The publication’s DDL handling in PostgreSQL 18 can propagate some DDL, and it is still the newest and least exercised feature in this area
Table and column filtering✗ impossible✓ publish_via_partition_root and the publication’s column list on PostgreSQL; --replicate-do-db, --replicate-rewrite-db and --replicate-do-table on MySQL
Row filtering✗✓ WHERE clause in the publication’s FOR TABLE on PostgreSQL; --replicate-filter on MySQL, evaluated on the source
Cross-engine✗✓, via an intermediate: MySQL to Kafka to PostgreSQL, or a CDC connector. The source engine’s row format must be understood by the destination’s tooling, and this is where most cross-engine pipelines break
Conflict on the destinationImpossible — the destination is a byte-level copy and applies operations, not decisionsPossible, and the reason logical replication is not a multi-master technology: an UPDATE or DELETE that finds no matching row is either an error or a no-op depending on configuration, and both are silent by default
Apply latencyMilliseconds to low seconds at the same region; tens of milliseconds across a region, dominated by network round trips because the protocol is often serialisedSeconds at the same region, because the apply is a statement and the source holds the transaction open until at least one subscriber acknowledges in row-by-row mode
Sequential consistencyFull, and freeNot guaranteed unless both the initial sync snapshot and the subsequent apply are coordinated. A subscriber can see a row that was updated after the snapshot but before the subscription started, then apply the update again — a lost-update window of minutes
Cost to the sourceMinimal. WAL shipping is nearly free; synchronous replication adds an fsync on the primary’s commit pathMaterial. The publication decodes the log, formats rows, and in row-by-row mode holds the transaction open for each subscriber’s acknowledgement, which adds the subscriber’s network round trip to the writer’s latency
Use it forRead replicas, standbys, high availability, disaster recoveryZero-downtime major version upgrades, selective replication of a subset of tables, cross-region analytics, and change data capture
-- PostgreSQL 18: logical replication, the full setup, and the two settings that
-- decide whether a subscriber can ever be trusted to be caught up.
--
-- On the source:
CREATE PUBLICATION saas_pub
    FOR TABLE sales_order, sales_order_line, payment_event
    WITH (publish = 'insert, update, delete',
           publish_via_partition_root = true);

-- Subscriptions are created with CREATE SUBSCRIPTION ... WITH (create_slot = false)
-- and the slot is copied across separately. Creating the slot on the subscriber
-- and then subscribing is the documented procedure precisely so that a failed
-- initial sync does not lose the WAL that would have to be replayed to catch up.
CREATE SUBSCRIPTION saas_sub
    CONNECTION 'host=primary.internal port=5432 dbname=saas user=replicator'
    PUBLICATION saas_pub
    WITH (copy_data = true, create_slot = false, enabled = false, slot_name = 'saas_sub');
ALTER SUBSCRIPTION saas_sub ENABLE;

-- The two settings that are not defaults and that decide correctness:
--
-- 1. On the SUBSCRIBER: wal_receiver_status_interval (default 10s) is how
--    stale the lag measurement can be. A 10s interval means your best
--    possible reading of "how far behind is the replica" is 10 seconds old.
-- 2. On the PUBLICATION: publish_via_partition_root. With it false, changes
--    made through a partitioned parent arrive at the subscriber as changes to
--    the partition, and a subscriber without the partitions sees nothing.
ALTER SYSTEM SET wal_receiver_status_interval = '1s';
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_logical_replication_workers = 8;   -- default 4
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_sync_workers_per_subscription = 4;  -- default 2

-- The table-level settings that must be on both sides for a working
-- subscription, and which are a routine cause of a silent non-start:
--   wal_level = logical
--   max_wal_senders > number of physical + logical subscribers
--   max_replication_slots >= max_wal_senders   (a slot whose consumer has
--                                           -- vanished pins WAL forever and
--                                           -- fills the disk: section 14.2)
--   pg_hba.conf permits replication for the replicator role

-- MySQL 9.x: the equivalent, with the flag that produces row images.
-- binlog_format=ROW is the default and binlog_row_image=FULL is the default,
-- but both are commonly overridden. binlog_row_image=MINIMAL is faster and
-- makes the apply fragile: an update on a table with no indexed
-- --where -- value is not replicated at all, which is data loss.
SET GLOBAL binlog_row_image = 'FULL';
--
-- CREATE PUBLICATION saas_pub FOR TABLE saas.sales_order, saas.sales_order_line
--   WITH (publish='update,delete');
-- CHANGE REPLICATION SOURCE TO SOURCE_HOST='primary.internal', SOURCE_USER='repl',
--   SOURCE_PASSWORD='...', SOURCE_LOG_FILE='binlog.000142', SOURCE_LOG_POS=4;
-- CHANGE REPLICATION SOURCE TO SOURCE_AUTO_POSITION=1;   -- GTID preferred
--
-- The apply thread, and the one setting that makes it correct:
-- replica_parallel_workers. With 1 (the default) the apply is strictly serial,
-- which makes the subscriber a bottleneck well before the network is one.
SET GLOBAL replica_parallel_workers = 8;
-- With more than one worker, a transaction touching two rows handled by
-- -- different workers can deadlock against itself, which is why
-- -- replica_preserve_commit_order must be ON: it stops the applier from
-- -- finishing transaction N+1 before N, which is what the subscriber's own
-- -- transactional guarantees depend on.
SET GLOBAL replica_preserve_commit_order = ON;

-- SQL Server 2025: transactional replication for a read scale-out. Note the
-- distinction from the other two: SQL Server's replication is a set of
-- distributor- and subscriber-side agents, not a log and an apply worker, and
-- its failure modes are agent-configuration failures rather than slot failures.
--
-- sp_adddistributor @security_mode = 1, @password = '...';  -- once per instance
-- EXEC sp_addsubscription ... @subscriber = 'reader-1', @subscription_type = 'PULL';
-- sp_addpushsubscription_agent @publication = 'saas_pub', @subscriber = 'reader-1';
--
-- The read-committed snapshot setting on the SUBSCRIBER, without which a reader
-- -- query blocks every writer on the subscriber, and the subscriber is a
-- -- replica so nobody expects writes, and so nobody notices until it is a
-- -- latency incident on a dashboard.
ALTER DATABASE saas_reader SET READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE;
ALTER DATABASE saas_reader SET SNAPSHOT ISOLATION ON;

Change data capture is logical replication with a consumer, and it is the foundation of everything downstream. Once row changes leave the database as a stream, the same stream can drive an analytics warehouse, a search index, a cache invalidation subscriber, a data lake, and an audit archive — each an independent consumer with its own offset, replayed independently, none of them able to affect the write path. This is a materially better architecture than the alternative, where each of those consumers is implemented as a scheduled query against the primary or as a trigger writing to a table. The reason it is better is the isolation: a broken search indexer replays from its own offset and cannot slow down a transaction, and its lag is measurable in seconds. The prerequisites are the ones above, plus a schema-change discipline that treats DDL as an event: because logical replication does not carry DDL, every schema change must be applied to every subscriber in the correct order, before or with the data that depends on it.

11.2 Synchronous versus asynchronous, and the availability trade

Synchronous replication trades write availability for zero data loss. Asynchronous replication trades a bounded data loss for write availability. There is no third option, and the whole design question is which failure the business can survive.

The mechanism is worth stating precisely because it is often described loosely. In synchronous replication the primary does not commit until at least one synchronous standby has acknowledged receipt — on PostgreSQL, a flush of the WAL to the standby’s disk; on MySQL, the acknowledgement of the binlog event; on SQL Server, the commit acknowledgement through the distributor. A synchronous standby that is slow therefore makes the primary slow, and a synchronous standby that is down makes the primary unable to commit, unless the configuration specifies otherwise. The configuration that specifies otherwise is the one that matters.

PostgreSQL’s synchronous_standby_names accepts a priority list, and synchronous_commit can be set to remote_apply, on or local per session. The ANY n (standby_a, standby_b) form means "any n of these", which is the setting that gives both zero-loss and availability: with two standbys, requiring either one keeps commits succeeding when one is down, at the cost of losing the redundancy during that window. The failure mode of the naive configuration is severe and well documented: a synchronous standby in a different region adds a round trip to every commit, and a partition takes the primary down entirely because it cannot distinguish "the standby is down" from "the standby is slow". ANY 1 over two in-region standbys plus an asynchronous cross-region standby is the common production answer, and it costs one in-region round trip — usually under a millisecond.

ConfigurationData loss on primary failureWrite latency costBehaviour when a standby is downUse when
Asynchronous, no standbysAll writes since the last checkpoint or moreZeron/aNever, in production. This is a development configuration
Asynchronous with a replicaEverything the replica has not yet applied — measured in seconds, unbounded during a vacuum or a large DDLZeroNo effect on writesRead replicas, and any disaster-recovery posture that accepts a bounded loss
Synchronous on / remote_apply, one standbyZero, if the standby has flushedOne round trip to the standby — 0.3 ms in-region, 15–40 ms cross-regionWrites block until the standby returns, unless the configuration excludes itA single-region system where a commit must never be lost
Synchronous ANY 1 (a, b), two standbysZero while at least one is upOne in-region round tripWrites continue; the redundancy is temporarily oneThe production default for a system that cannot lose an acknowledged write
Quorum commit across three or moreZero while a majority is upOne round trip, plus a small coordination costWrites continue with two of threePostgreSQL has no native quorum commit; this is built with two synchronous_standby_names entries in a priority list, which is not the same thing and is weaker
Synchronous cross-regionZero15–40 ms added to every commit, and p99 governed by the distance’s variance rather than its meanA partition stops writes completelyRarely. A 20 ms write latency is a 20 ms write latency for every user, forever, to protect against a failure that may never occur
-- PostgreSQL 18: the production configuration. Two in-region standbys, any one
-- satisfies the commit, plus a cross-region standby that is asynchronous.
-- The cross-region standby is deliberately NOT in synchronous_standby_names.
ALTER SYSTEM SET synchronous_standby_names = 'ANY 1 (standby_a, standby_b)';
ALTER SYSTEM SET synchronous_commit = 'on';       -- flush, not remote_apply
ALTER SYSTEM SET wal_keep_size = '4GB';
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_wal_size = '16GB';
ALTER SYSTEM SET archive_mode = on;
-- SELECT pg_reload_conf();

-- remote_apply vs on, and the difference is a cross-region round trip.
--   on          : the standby has fsynced the WAL. Commit waits for one disk write there.
--   remote_apply: the standby has replayed it. Commit waits for replay, which is
--                -- slower and further behind, and the only thing it buys is that a
--                -- failover loses nothing. Since a failover replays WAL anyway, the
--                -- incremental guarantee is small and the latency cost is real.
-- Default to 'on' and rely on WAL replay at failover time.

-- Proof that it is synchronous, and which standby is currently the one
-- satisfying commits. sync_state = 'sync' for one, 'quorum' for the rest.
SELECT client_addr, application_name, state, sync_state,
       sent_lsn, write_lsn, flush_lsn, replay_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)) AS replay_lag
  FROM pg_stat_replication
 ORDER BY sync_state, replay_lsn;

-- MySQL 9.x: the same idea, with one critical difference -- WAIT_FOR_EXECUTED_GTID
-- SET makes a replica synchronous, and a synchronously-replicated replica that
-- is down will BLOCK the primary for innodb_wait_timeout seconds, which
-- defaults to 50 and is a fifty-second stall for every writer. The
-- mitigation is to lower innodb_wait_timeout and use semisynchronous
-- replication with a timeout rather than a hard wait.
CHANGE REPLICATION SOURCE TO
    SOURCE_HOST = 'standby-a.internal', SOURCE_USER = 'repl',
    SOURCE_AUTO_POSITION = 1,
    SOURCE_SSL = 1,
    GET_SOURCE_PUBLIC_KEY = 1;
--
INSTALL PLUGIN rpl_semi_sync_master SONAME 'semisync_master.so';
SET GLOBAL rpl_semi_sync_master_enabled = 1;
SET GLOBAL rpl_semi_sync_master_timeout = 1000;   -- ms, then fall back to async

-- The SQL Server 2025 equivalent: @synchronous_commit. This is per
-- operation, and 'remote_apply' has exactly the same replay-vs-flush
-- trade as PostgreSQL's. Note that SQL Server's synchronous replication runs
-- through a Distributor, so there is an additional hop and a second component
-- whose failure also blocks commits.
--
-- sp_addpushsubscription_agent @publication='saas_pub', @subscriber='standby-a',
--     @sync_type = 'automatic', @sync_mode = 'async';
-- sp_changedistributor_agent @password='...', @polling_interval=5;
--
-- COMMIT WITH (DELAYED_DURABILITY = ON) is the durability trade in the other
-- -- direction: a commit is not durable until the log is flushed, and marking a
-- -- small transaction as delayed removes the log flush from its commit path. A
-- -- crash can then lose it. Appropriate for a high-volume, individually
-- -- low-value write; never for a payment authorisation.

The trade is not symmetric and the asymmetry runs one way. A synchronous replica that is down costs you availability, every second it is down, in the form of writes that are refused or delayed. An asynchronous replica that is down costs you nothing until the moment the primary also fails, at which point the loss is everything it had not applied. The first is a continuous, measurable, certain cost. The second is a single, rare, unbounded one. Teams that choose asynchronous almost always make the right call, and teams that choose synchronous because "we cannot lose data" are frequently losing far more data through refused writes and abandoned checkout flows than they ever would have through a failover. The decision should be made in terms of what the business does at 3am, and the answer for a payment system is usually: synchronous within the region, asynchronous across it, and a documented, tested, time-boxed procedure for reconciling whatever the cross-region standby missed.

11.3 Read replicas and read-after-write

A read replica is a cache with unbounded capacity, no invalidation protocol and a latency that is not under your control. It is enormously valuable and it introduces exactly one class of bug — the read that must see a write that has just committed — and that class of bug is entirely preventable if the routing decision is made from the request rather than from the connection.

The replica is fast because it is not doing the work of writing: it is applying a log. The reads it serves are therefore ordinary MVCC reads against a snapshot that is old, and the oldness is a number that varies with load, with vacuum, with a network blip and with an operator running a large DDL. The consequence is that no fixed delay is a safe delay: waiting 100 ms after a write is correct on Tuesday and wrong on the day someone rebuilds an index on the primary.

-- The three ways to fix read-after-write, and what each one costs.
--
-- Fix 1: route the read to the primary. Correct, trivially. Costs the read
-- -- capacity the replica exists to provide, for as long as the token is
-- -- valid. A token TTL of 5 seconds means every user who writes pays 5
-- -- seconds of primary-only reads, which on a write-heavy workload is most
-- -- of the traffic.
--
--   primary := route(token != "", token)
--   if primary: read from primary
--   else:      read from replica
--
-- Fix 2: wait for the replica to reach a known position. Exact, and it requires
-- -- carrying a position rather than a timestamp. This is the only fix that
-- -- is correct across a failover, and it is what the engines' native
-- -- mechanisms do.
--
--   PostgreSQL hot_standby_feedback + pg_last_wal_replay_lsn(), polled by the
--   application through a small side channel: a token holding the commit LSN,
--   and a read that waits until SELECT pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() >= token
--   with a bounded timeout, after which it reads from the primary.
--
--   MySQL: WAIT_FOR_EXECUTED_GTID_SET('uuid:1-4711', 1) -- waits up to 1 second
SELECT WAIT_FOR_EXECUTED_GTID_SET('3E11FA47-71CA-11E1-9E33-C80AA9429562:1-4711', 1)
         AS replicated;

--   SQL Server: the transaction's commit timestamp compared with the replica's
--   -- last received. SQL Server exposes this through the Availability Group's
--   -- LSN, and the only supported application-level mechanism is a routing
--   -- decision: SQL Server has no statement-level "wait for my commit to land".

-- Fix 3: make the read tolerant. The cheapest and the most frequently correct.
-- If the UI can render "processing" for the 300 ms until the replica catches
-- -- up, the problem is a design problem rather than an infrastructure one, and
-- -- the fix is a state machine rather than a routing rule.
--
--   order := GET order:detail:412:88100041        -- the cache, from ch10
--   if order == nil:
--       order := read(order_id, route = REPLICA)
--       if order == nil && recentlyWrote(order_id):
--           order := read(order_id, route = PRIMARY)   // bounded fallback
--       cache(order, shortTTL)

-- The interaction that produces the bug nobody diagnoses: negative caching from
-- a replica. A read goes to a replica, the replica has not applied the insert,
-- the read returns nothing, and a MISSING is cached for 30 seconds. The user
-- -- now has a negative cache entry asserting that an order they just created does
-- -- not exist, for half a minute, caused by a replica that was 1.2 seconds behind.
-- Rule, and it is absolute: a negative cache entry is never written from a
-- -- replica read, and a MISSING that is read from a replica is never cached at all.

-- The lag measurement that makes the routing decision possible, on the replica.
SELECT now() - GREATEST(pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp(), '2000-01-01') AS replay_delay,
       pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() AS replay_lsn,
       CASE WHEN pg_last_wal_receive_lsn() = pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()
            THEN 'streaming'
            ELSE 'receiving but not replaying: query conflict, or a long txn' END AS state
  FROM pg_stat_wal_receiver;

Read replicas are for a specific kind of read, and using them for others is a design error. The reads that belong on a replica are the ones that tolerate staleness by nature: a reporting query over last month, a search index refresh, an analytics extract, a cache warm, a background job’s enumeration. The reads that belong on a primary are the ones where the user is about to act on the answer: a checkout total, a permission check, a quantity availability check, a confirmation page. The distinction is not technical and it is not a matter of latency tolerance — it is a matter of whether a stale answer leads to a wrong action. A replica serving the first kind is what makes a replica worth having. A replica serving the second kind is how a customer buys four seats when three are available.

11.4 Measuring lag correctly

Replication lag has at least four definitions, they disagree, and the one your monitoring tool reports by default is usually the least useful of them. The measurement must be stated in terms of the thing that matters — position, not time — and the disagreement between the definitions is itself a diagnostic.

The definitions are: time lag, the wall-clock difference between now() and the timestamp of the last transaction the replica has applied; byte lag, the difference in log position; apply lag, the difference between the last byte received and the last byte applied, which distinguishes network delay from replay delay; and visible-data lag, the amount of staleness a reader would actually experience, which is the only one a user experiences. Time lag is a proxy for visible lag that degrades exactly when replay is fast, because a burst of transactions all stamped at the same second creates a time lag that jumps and then falls while the actual position difference is constant.

-- PostgreSQL 18: all four definitions, from the primary, in one query. Compare
-- sent_lsn to flush_lsn to replay_lsn and the picture is complete.
SELECT application_name,
       client_addr,
       state,
       sync_state,
       sent_lsn, flush_lsn, replay_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn))      AS total_byte_lag,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, flush_lsn))       AS network_lag,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(flush_lsn, replay_lsn))      AS apply_lag,
       write_lag, flush_lag, replay_lag,                            -- reported intervals, often 0
       now() - reply_time                                          AS since_last_reply
  FROM pg_stat_replication
 ORDER BY pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) DESC;

-- The view that most tools read, and the one that lies. pg_stat_replication_
-- --lag is the interval since the last message received from the standby, and
-- -- in synchronous mode it is 0 constantly because the primary is blocked
-- -- waiting for exactly that message. It measures the health of the connection,
-- -- not the staleness of the data.
--
--   SELECT * FROM pg_stat_replication_lag;  -- DO NOT use as a lag SLO

-- Time lag, the definition everyone quotes, and why it is the least useful.
-- This is pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp(), and it is zero when the replica is
-- -- idle with nothing to replay, which is indistinguishable from "perfectly
-- -- caught up" in a monitoring system. A replica that is 40 minutes behind
-- -- an idle primary reports a time lag of zero.
SELECT extract(epoch FROM now() - pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp()) AS time_lag_seconds;

-- Byte lag, the honest one: the difference between what the primary has and
-- -- what the replica has replayed. Convert to seconds with the primary's
-- -- current WAL generation rate, measured over a window, not assumed.
--
--   SELECT pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS bytes_behind;
--   -- bytes_behind / (bytes_per_second over the last 60s) = seconds behind

-- MySQL 9.x: three positions, and the difference between them is the whole story.
--   Retrieved_Gtid_Set   -- what the replica has received from the source
--   Executed_Gtid_Set    -- what the replica has applied
-- A gap between them means the applier is behind, not the network.
SELECT CHANNEL_NAME,
       SERVICE_STATE,
       LAST_QUEUED_TRANSACTION,
       LAST_QUEUED_TRANSACTION_END_QUEUE_TIME,
       LAST_APPLIED_TRANSACTION,
       LAST_APPLIED_TRANSACTION_END_APPLY_TIME,
       -- Seconds the applier is behind. This is the useful one.
       TIMESTAMPDIFF(SECOND, LAST_APPLIED_TRANSACTION_END_APPLY_TIME, NOW()) AS apply_lag_seconds,
       -- Retransmission count > 0 means the network is losing packets and the
       -- -- byte lag is not the binding constraint.
       (SELECT SERVICE_STATE FROM performance_schema.replication_connection_status
         WHERE CHANNEL_NAME = r.CHANNEL_NAME) AS io_thread_state
  FROM performance_schema.replication_applier_status_by_coordinator r;

-- The old, wrong metric: Seconds_Behind_Source in SHOW REPLICA STATUS. It
-- -- compares a source timestamp to the replica's IO thread timestamp and
-- -- reads 0 whenever the replica's IO thread is idle, which is most of the time.
-- It also reads a large non-zero value during a single long transaction that
-- -- has committed on the source and is not yet visible to the applier, which is
-- -- a false alarm caused by correct MVCC behaviour.
--
--   SHOW REPLICA STATUS\G
--   --   Seconds_Behind_Source: 0        <- meaningless most of the time
--   --   Exec_Source_Log_Pos: 4 812 993  <- the real one
--   --   Retrieved_Gtid_Set / Executed_Gtid_Set -- the better one

-- SQL Server 2025: the correct measurement for an Always On Availability Group.
-- log_send_queue_size and log_replay_queue_size are in bytes, are never
-- -- zero-idle, and are exactly the two halves of the definition. Divide by the
-- -- measured log generation rate for seconds.
SELECT ag.name                                   AS ag_name,
       ar.replica_server_name,
       ars.is_local,
       drs.synchronization_state_desc,
       drs.log_send_queue_size                          AS send_bytes,
       drs.log_replay_queue_size                        AS replay_bytes,
       drs.end_of_log_record_time,
       DATEADD(SECOND, DATEDIFF(SECOND, drs.end_of_log_record_time, SYSUTCDATETIME()), 0)
                                                          AS replay_delay_seconds,
       drs.availability_mode_desc
  FROM sys.dm_hadr_database_replica_states drs
  JOIN sys.availability_replicas ar
    ON ar.replica_id = drs.replica_id
  JOIN sys.availability_groups ag
    ON ag.group_id = drs.group_id
 ORDER BY drs.log_replay_queue_size DESC;

-- The four failure modes, and the measurement that distinguishes them. Without
-- -- this decomposition, "the replica is behind" is not actionable.
--
--   1. Network.         send_bytes growing, replay_bytes flat,  IO thread restarting.
--   2. Receive but not replay. send_bytes flat, replay_bytes growing, one long
--      -- transaction holding a snapshot open, or a query conflict with hot_standby_feedback.
--   3. Apply throughput. Both growing together, replay rate < send rate. The
--      -- replica's I/O or CPU is saturated. Section 4.6 covers this.
--   4. Replay is current, the reader is not. replay_lsn == sent_lsn, but the
--      -- connection is routed to the wrong node, or hot_standby_feedback is off
--      -- and the replica is refusing to apply a conflict-cancelling query, which
--      -- -- shows as log_replay_queue_size 0 with replay_lsn stuck.
--
-- Case 4 is the one that presents as "no lag reported" while readers see
-- -- minutes-old data, and it is the reason the byte measurement must be
-- -- taken against the primary's current position, never read from the replica.
MetricReads 0 whenWhat it actually measuresVerdict
Time since last replayed transactionThe replica is idle with nothing to replayNothing useful. A replica 40 minutes behind an idle primary reports 0✗
Seconds behind source (MySQL)The IO thread is idle, which is most of the timeIO thread position, not applier position✗
pg_stat_replication_lag on PostgreSQLIn synchronous mode, constantlyTime since the last status message, which synchronous mode is engineered to make recent✗
Log position difference (bytes)Never, unless the WAL has been recycled and the LSN has wrappedThe exact amount of committed data not yet applied✓
Queue size (SQL Server availability group)NeverBytes sent and bytes awaiting replay, separately✓
GTID set difference (MySQL)NeverWhich transactions are received and which are executed, as sets✓

Turn byte lag into an SLO by measuring the rate, and alert on the derived seconds. A byte threshold is meaningless: 100 MB behind at 2 MB/s is 50 seconds, and 100 MB behind at 200 MB/s is half a second. The measurement that converts is the log generation rate over a trailing window, which is a simple derivative of the primary’s own position, and the derived metric is bytes_behind / bytes_per_second — seconds of visible staleness, which is what the user experiences and what the SLO should be written in. This also makes the metric comparable across a quiet Sunday and a Monday morning peak, and it is the form a read-your-writes token TTL can be set from.

11.5 Routing and read/write splitting

Read/write splitting is a connection-routing problem sitting on top of a correctness problem, and it must be solved from the request, not from the connection, because a pooled connection has no identity that survives a transaction boundary.

Two tools dominate. PgBouncer sits in front of PostgreSQL and multiplexes thousands of client connections onto a small number of server connections, and it also routes by database name — which is how read replicas are usually reached, because PgBouncer routes a connection called saas_read to whichever host that alias points at, and a failover changes the alias. ProxySQL does the same for MySQL and adds considerably more: hostgroups, query rules, replication lag awareness, and a writer / reader split that can be changed at runtime. Both are worth the operational complexity at a certain scale; both are premature below it.

-- PgBouncer: the configuration that makes read/write splitting correct, and
-- -- the one line that makes prepared statements work.
--
-- ; transaction mode is the only mode that gives real multiplexing, and it
-- ; is incompatible with session-level state. See section 14.5.
--
-- [databases]
-- saas        = host=primary.internal  port=5432  dbname=saas pool_size=40
-- saas_read   = host=replica-1.internal port=5432  dbname=saas pool_size=120
-- saas_read   = host=replica-2.internal port=5432  dbname=saas pool_size=120
--
-- [pgbouncer]
-- ; 1 = session, 2 = transaction, 3 = statement. Transaction mode is the
-- ; -- default recommendation and the only one that scales past a few hundred
-- ; -- clients, because a connection is held only for the duration of a
-- -- transaction.
-- pool_mode = transaction
-- ; max_client_conn is 5 000–20 000; max_db_connections is what the server's
-- ; -- connection limit can survive. The ratio is the whole point of the tool.
-- max_client_conn = 10000
-- max_db_connections = 200
-- ; max_prepared_statements is 0 in older releases, and with it at 0 in
-- ; -- transaction mode every server-side prepared statement is invalidated the
-- ; -- moment the server connection is handed to another client. Set it to a
-- ; -- positive number from PgBouncer 1.21 onwards.
-- max_prepared_statements = 200
-- ; server_reset_query must run between clients in transaction mode, or a SET
-- ; -- done by one client leaks into the next. DISCARD ALL is the correct value
-- ; -- and it is not free: it runs on every transaction boundary.
-- server_reset_query = DISCARD ALL
-- ; server_reset_query_always = 0   -- run it only when the server connection
-- ; -- -- changes, which is what you want with reset-on-release
-- ; server_lifetime = 3600          -- recycle server connections hourly, to bound
-- ; -- -- the damage of a connection-level leak
-- ; server_idle_timeout = 600
-- ; reserve_pool_timeout = 2       -- -- seconds a client waits before the
-- ; -- -- reserve pool is opened under pressure
-- ; ignore_startup_parameters = extra_float_digits,options
--
-- ; auth_type = scram-sha-256 and auth_user/ auth_query are the only
-- ; -- supported way to do per-user authentication through a pool, because the
-- ; -- server connection is shared and the session user is not preserved.

-- ProxySQL: hostgroups, and the rules that make the split lag-aware.
--
-- INSERT INTO mysql_servers (hostgroup_id, hostname, port) VALUES
--   (1, 'primary.internal', 3306), (2, 'replica-1.internal', 3306);
--
-- -- reader_hostgroup is where an unmatched SELECT goes. Everything that is not
-- -- a SELECT -- INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, DDL, SHOW, SET -- stays on the writer,
-- -- because the default rule is to use writer_hostgroup for anything not matched.
-- INSERT INTO mysql_query_rules (rule_id, active, match_digest, destination_hostgroup)
-- VALUES (1, 1, '^SELECT', 2), (2, 1, '.*', 1);
--
-- -- The exception list. These must go to the writer even though they are reads,
-- -- and forgetting one of them is the read-after-write bug in its purest form:
-- --   SELECT ... FOR UPDATE / FOR SHARE       -- takes locks
-- --   SELECT last_insert_id(), uuid(), now(), cur_timestamp()  -- nondeterministic
-- --   SELECT ... INTO                            -- writes a variable
-- --   SELECT from a temporary table              -- session state
-- --   CALL, and any stored procedure, which is opaque to the rule matcher
-- --   SELECT nextval('seq')                     -- a sequence is writer-local
-- INSERT INTO mysql_query_rules (rule_id, active, match_pattern, destination_hostgroup)
-- VALUES (10, 1, '^SELECT .* FOR UPDATE', 1), (11, 1, '^SELECT LAST_INSERT_ID', 1),
--        (12, 1, '^SELECT .* INTO', 1), (13, 1, '^CALL', 1), (14, 1, 'NEXTVAL', 1);
--
-- -- Lag awareness, and why it is the feature that matters. With it enabled,
-- -- ProxySQL refuses to send a read to a replica whose lag exceeds
-- -- max_replication_lag, and falls back to the writer. Without it, the split
-- -- is a fixed decision made at configuration time and the system degrades
-- -- silently during exactly the incident when it matters.
-- UPDATE mysql_global_variables SET variable_value='2000' WHERE variable_name='max_replication_lag';
-- UPDATE mysql_replication_hostgroups SET max_replication_lag=2000 WHERE hostgroup_id=2;

-- The application-side rule, which is the part no proxy can do for you. The
-- -- decision is made from a token in the request and carried in a separate
-- -- connection, because the pooled connection has no identity.
--
-- write := POST /api/v1/orders
--   txn := primary.Begin()          // always the writer alias
--   ... insert ... commit
--   token := mint(lastCommitPosition, ttl = max(stalenessBudget, 5s))
--   Set-Cookie: rrt=<token>; Path=/; Max-Age=<ttl>
--
-- read := GET /api/v1/orders/88100041
--   if token := cookie(rrt); token != "":
--       waitForReplica(token.position, timeout = 500ms)
--       alias = token.position reached ? reader : primary
--   else:
--       alias = reader
--   rows := open(alias).Query(...)   // a NEW connection, chosen by alias, not the
--                                // -- one that happened to be in the pool
--
-- The three ways this is done wrong:
--   1. SELECT ... FROM primary WHERE ... decided by a session variable set on a
--      -- pooled connection. Another request gets that connection, reads the wrong
--      -- -- node, and the variable is the only evidence. This is the single most
--      -- -- common multi-tenant cross-tenant-adjacent incident in this area.
--   2. A sticky connection per user. Two tabs, two requests, two connections, and a
--      -- failover strands one of them on a dead node with no way to notice.
--   3. Routing on the request path only. A background job that reads through the
--      -- primary because it did not go through the HTTP router is fine until the
--      -- -- primary is the thing that is overloaded.
ScaleTopologyDo you need a proxy?Why
< 50 connectionsOne primary, one replica, direct connectionsNo. The application holds its own pool and picks the aliasA proxy adds a component whose failure is a total outage, and it solves a problem the application can solve in twenty lines. Below about 200 concurrent connections the PostgreSQL process-per-connection model is not the bottleneck
200–2 000 connectionsPrimary plus two replicas behind PgBouncer or ProxySQLYesMultiplexing becomes necessary: each PostgreSQL backend is a process, and 2 000 of them is 2 000 processes competing for memory and for the lock manager’s shared arrays
2 000–50 000 connectionsMultiple primaries behind a proxy, with the proxy doing the read split and the lag checkYes, and the proxy becomes a critical component that needs its own availability storyAt this scale the proxy is also the natural place to enforce per-tenant rate limits, which section 13.5 needs anyway
Any scale, async analyticsApplication writes a change stream, a warehouse reads from itNo — this is a different system, and it should beChapter 11.1: the reporting load should not be on the operational database at all, and the isolation the change stream provides is the point

A proxy in the write path must not be a single point of failure, and the usual design makes it one. PgBouncer in transaction mode is a stateless process and two of them behind a load balancer with health checks is the correct deployment; it is also common to deploy one, and it is then an outage with no fallback. If a proxy is in the path, it gets the same treatment as the database: a second instance, a health check that tests an actual query, and a documented manual bypass. The health check matters specifically: a PgBouncer that is running but cannot reach the primary answers TCP connect successfully and will sit in the load balancer taking requests it cannot serve, which is worse than being down because it is not removed from rotation.

11.6 Promotion, failover, multi-master and cross-region reality

Replication gives you a copy. It does not give you a primary, an election, a routing table or a consistent story about what happens when the primary is unreachable — and the difference between the two is the difference between having a backup and having a failover capability.

Failover is a coordinated operation with an irreversible moment in it: the moment the last possible writer commits. Everything before that moment can be reversed. This is why failover is not a query, and why a system that treats it as one — an operator running pg_ctl promote without knowing the state of the other replicas — is running a coin toss with a database. The tooling exists on all three engines, and the tooling’s job is to prevent the operator from being the one who makes the decision.

-- PostgreSQL 18: managed failover with Patroni and etcd, which is the answer for
-- Kubernetes and the answer for a hand-rolled setup. Patroni's job is to make the
-- -- irreversible moment a quorum decision rather than a human judgement.
--
-- SELECT application_name, state, sync_state, replay_lsn FROM pg_stat_replication;
-- SELECT pg_is_in_recovery();   -- must be false on a promotable node
-- --
-- -- Patroni REST API, which is what an operator or a runbook actually calls:
--   POST /primary     -- trigger a controlled failover (the reversible moment)
--   POST /restart     -- restart a member
--   GET  /primary      -- who is the leader, per etcd's consensus
--   GET  /cluster      -- every member, its role, its lag, and its health
--
-- -- The safety property that must be verified, not assumed. Before promoting a
-- -- candidate, confirm it has replayed the primary's last position:
SELECT pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() AS replayed,
       pg_last_wal_receive_lsn() AS received,
       -- The primary's final position, obtained from the old primary's last
-- -- archive or from a monitoring system, NOT from a table on the replica.
       '0/9A3B1C8'::pg_lsn                  AS target,
       pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() >= '0/9A3B1C8'::pg_lsn AS safe_to_promote
  FROM pg_stat_wal_receiver;

-- MySQL 9.x: manual promotion, which is genuinely manual and is why
-- Orchestrator, Patroni for MySQL, or a cloud managed service is used. The
-- -- sequence that must not be skipped:
--
--   1. On the OLD primary: STOP REPLICA; SET GLOBAL read_only = ON;
--      --    Read-only is the fence. Without it, a partitioned old primary
--      --    continues accepting writes that the new primary will never see, and the
--      --    divergence is discovered weeks later by a reconciliation report.
--   2. On the CANDIDATE: STOP REPLICA;
--   3. Read the coordinates from the old primary and apply them:
CHANGE REPLICATION SOURCE TO SOURCE_HOST = 'replica-1.internal', SOURCE_LOG_FILE = 'binlog.000142', SOURCE_LOG_POS = 4812993;
START REPLICA;
SHOW REPLICA STATUS\G   -- Exec_Source_Log_Pos must reach 4812993
STOP REPLICA;
RESET REPLICA ALL;
SET GLOBAL read_only = OFF;
--   4. Repoint every application connection, and invalidate the read-only
--   --   assumption: the cached plan that was built against a read-only replica
--   --   is now wrong on a writable node.

-- SQL Server 2025: an Always On Availability Group performs a planned failover
-- -- with a defined sequence, and an automatic failover only when the group is
-- -- configured for it. ALLOWED_FAILOVER_OVERRIDE and FAILOVER_TARGET are the
-- -- two settings that decide who is permitted to be the target.
ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP saas_ag
    SET AVAILABILITY GROUP ON WITH (
        FAILOVER_TARGET = N'saas-replica-1',
        ALLOWED_FAILOVER_OVERRIDE = N'');

-- Multi-master, honestly. The two engines' multi-master features are useful in
-- -- specific cases and are traps in general, and the reason is not
-- -- technical but economic: they make a conflict possible that no local rule
-- -- can resolve correctly.
--
-- MySQL Group Replication: works for the case where every member is a replica
-- -- of the others and the workload is write-local: two datacentres, each with a
-- -- local primary, writing to users in its own region. The certification layer
-- -- -- serialises conflicting transactions, so a genuine conflict costs a
-- -- -- round trip between regions, which is why the group is only viable for
-- -- -- low write rates and low conflict probability. It is a good fit for a
-- -- -- read-heavy, write-local system and a bad fit for a write-heavy one.
--
-- PostgreSQL BDR: logical multi-master with conflict resolution by rule --
-- -- last update wins, insert-ignore, target-win, or a custom conflict handler --
-- -- applied in the apply worker. This resolves conflicts by <em>choosing one</em>,
-- -- not by preventing them. A last-update-wins resolution on a column two
-- -- regions edited concurrently silently discards one of the edits, and no
-- -- -- conflict count in any monitoring system will tell you which one.
--
-- Where multi-master traps you, concretely:
--   1. A "unique" constraint is not unique across masters. Two regions insert
--      -- the same email for one tenant, both commit locally, and the constraint
--      -- -- holds on both nodes. The duplicate exists until a detection sweep finds it,
--      -- -- which is to say never.
--   2. A foreign key is not enforced across masters. An order referencing a
--      -- -- tenant that is being deleted in the other region is valid locally.
--   3. A serial sequence is not a serial sequence. Two nodes issue the same
--      -- nextval, and the primary key is either not a constraint or a violated one.
--      -- -- The fix is a UUIDv7 or a snowflake, and the migration of existing
--      -- -- primary keys to one is a chapter-8 project in itself.
--   4. Read-your-writes across regions is not merely difficult; it is a distributed
--      -- consensus problem, because the token must be issued by a node that has
--      -- seen the write, and after a conflict resolution it may not have.
--   5. There is no synchronous mode that is synchronous across regions without
--      -- paying the round trip, so the availability claim is weaker than the
--      -- -- latency cost suggests.
--
-- Cross-region read latency, measured rather than assumed. This is the query
-- -- that establishes the number every architecture decision depends on.
SELECT 1 AS n -- warm
UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 1
-- 64 round trips. Divide the elapsed time by 64 for a round trip, which is the
-- -- number a TLS handshake amortises over but a query does not.
--
-- Result: London to Frankfurt 2.1 ms, London to Dublin 0.9 ms, London to Virginia
-- 69 ms. At 69 ms, a request that makes 8 sequential round trips — the N+1 of
-- section 9.5, with 8 rows — has a floor of 552 ms that no amount of caching or
-- indexing removes. This is the number that makes an N+1 a regional outage
-- rather than a performance problem.

Exercises 11

  1. Measure replication lag four ways on a real replica — time lag, byte lag, apply lag and the tool’s default metric — during three scenarios: steady load, a 40-minute VACUUM, and a large index build on the primary. Produce a chart of all four and identify the scenario in which the default metric reports no lag while the visible staleness is minutes. Write the monitoring configuration that reports the derived seconds correctly.
  2. Configure logical replication from a primary to a subscriber for sales_order and payment_event. Then change the schema on the primary without touching the subscriber and observe what happens to the stream. Document the exact failure, and write the DDL-ordering rule that would have prevented it. Add a check to CI that compares the primary and subscriber schemas after every migration.
  3. Reproduce the read-after-write failure: write on the primary, read through the replica immediately, and record how many times the write is not visible. Then implement a position-based wait and confirm the failure disappears. Then deliberately exceed the wait timeout and confirm the read falls back to the primary rather than returning a stale answer.
  4. Demonstrate the negative-cache-plus-replica interaction from section 11.3. Read a just-created order from a replica 2 seconds behind, confirm the MISS, and measure how long the false negative persists. Then implement the two rules — no negative cache from a replica, no MISSING cached from a replica read — and verify the interaction is closed.
  5. Fail over a replication topology under load, using the managed tooling on one engine and the manual sequence on another. Record the time from primary loss to the new primary accepting writes, the amount of data lost under synchronous and under asynchronous configurations, and whether any client observed a write that was subsequently not present. Then re-run with the old primary still reachable but fenced, and confirm the fence held.
  6. Measure the cross-region round trip to every region you operate in, and compute the latency floor of your top ten endpoints by multiplying the round trip by the number of sequential round trips each requires. Identify the endpoints where the floor exceeds 200 ms, and decide for each whether the answer is fewer round trips, a regional read replica, or accepting the latency.

12 · Data Retention, Lifecycle and Deletion

12.1 Retention as a design requirement12.2 Time partitioning to make deletion cheap12.3 Soft versus hard delete, and the legal dimension12.4 Erasure, cascade obligations and anonymisation12.5 TTL, automatic expiry and archival tiers12.6 Tombstones, integrity after deletion, and the backup problem

Every row is a liability with a shelf life, and a schema that has not decided what that shelf life is will be asked to decide it under legal or commercial pressure at the worst possible moment. This chapter covers retention as a design requirement, the physical mechanism that makes deletion cheap, the difference between a soft delete and a real one, the cascading obligations of a right-to-be-deleted request, and the fact that a backup is a copy of personal data with the same obligations attached.

12.1 Retention as a design requirement

Retention is not a policy document. It is a set of decisions that determine the primary key space, the partitioning strategy, the index design, the legal exposure and the storage cost, and every one of those decisions is expensive to change later.

The reason retention belongs in the design phase rather than the operations phase is that it is a physical constraint. A table of order lines that must retain seven years and a table of API call records that must retain 90 days have different row counts, different growth rates, different partition keys, different index selectivity, and different vacuum or defragmentation behaviour — and a query that joins them must survive one of them being much smaller. Deciding the retention period in month one, and discovering in month thirty that the 90-day table is 4 % of the database and the 7-year table is 96 %, is a migration involving every query that touches both.

Data classExamples in this schemaTypical retentionSet byConsequence of getting it wrong
Transactional recordssales_order, sales_order_line, payment_event6–10 years (tax) to 7 years (statutory limitation)Finance and legal, per jurisdictionToo short and you cannot answer a tax audit. Too long and every query pays for rows nobody will read
Personal dataapp_user.email, app_user.status, any address or profile fieldUntil the relationship ends, plus a short tail for supportPrivacy law — GDPR erasure, CCPA deletion, and equivalentsToo long and every storage medium including backups is a compliance exposure. This is the one where the penalty is a percentage of global turnover
Audit trailaudit_eventOften longer than the data it describes, because it is the evidence of what happenedCompliance regimes: SOX, PCI DSS, financial services rulesDeleting the audit trail alongside the data destroys the evidence and is a separate compliance failure from the one the retention period was addressing
Technical telemetryRequest logs, query logs, slow query samples7–30 daysEngineering, for capacity and debuggingRetaining longer fills disks, raises the storage cost of every backup, and is itself a personal-data exposure when logs contain identifiers
Derived and recomputableMaterialised rollups, cached responses, denormalised columnsAs short as the recomputation takesEngineeringToo long is pure waste. Too short is a performance problem. Both are cheap to fix, which is why this is the easiest row in the table
Aggregated statisticsTenant spend per day, feature usage countsIndefinite, with the ability to reduce resolution over timeProduct, for cohort analysisGranular personal data aggregated is often no longer personal data; that reclassification is a legal determination, not an engineering one, and it should be recorded

Two properties of retention interact with the physical design and are worth stating before the mechanism. The first is that the volume of data within the retention window is the working set of every query that must respect it, so retention and index and partition design are the same decision viewed from three angles. The second is that the volume of data outside it is still stored — it is simply never queried — and that stored-but-never-queried data has a real cost: it is in every full backup, it is in every replica, it is bloat for the storage system, and it is a personal-data exposure surface that no query will ever reveal.

-- The retention register, as a table rather than a document. This is the artefact
-- that the schema, the partitions, the lifecycle jobs and the legal page all
-- derive from, and having it in the database means a drift check is a query.
CREATE TABLE retention_policy (
    table_name     text NOT NULL,
    data_class     text NOT NULL,
    retain_months  int  NOT NULL,
    basis          text NOT NULL,     -- 'tax-year', 'gdpr-erasure', 'sox-404', 'debug'
    jurisdiction   text NOT NULL,     -- 'EU', 'UK', 'US-CA', '*'
    partition_col  text,
    legal_hold      boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    PRIMARY KEY (table_name, data_class)
);

INSERT INTO retention_policy VALUES
  ('sales_order',        'transactional', 84,  'statutory-limitation', '*',     'placed_at', false),
  ('sales_order_line',   'transactional', 84,  'statutory-limitation', '*',     'created_at', false),
  (-- payment_event is retained for two independent reasons and the LONGER one,
  ('payment_event',      'transactional', 84,  'pci-dss-3.2',         '*',     'created_at', false),
  ('app_user',           'personal',      0,   'gdpr-erasure',         'EU',    NULL,        false),
  ('audit_event',        'audit',         120, 'sox-404',             'US',    'created_at', false),
  ('query_log',          'telemetry',     1,   'debug',               '*',     'captured_at', false);

-- The drift check: what is actually stored versus what the register says
-- -- should be stored. A gap of more than one partition is a finding,
-- -- and it is a query that belongs in a scheduled job rather than in an
-- -- audit, because the audit finds it after the fact.
--
-- SELECT c.relname AS table_name,
--        min(part_boundary) AS oldest_partition,
--        max(part_boundary) AS newest_partition,
--        r.retain_months, r.basis
--   FROM pg_class c
--   JOIN retention_policy r ON r.table_name = c.relname
--   LEFT JOIN pg_inherits i ON i.inhparent = c.oid
--  GROUP BY c.relname, r.retain_months, r.basis;

-- The legal hold, which overrides every policy above and is the reason the
-- -- hold flag lives in the register and not in a wiki. A legal hold on a
-- -- table must survive every lifecycle job, every retention job and every
-- -- partition drop, and the only way to guarantee that is for the job to
-- -- consult this table on every run rather than to be told not to run.
CREATE TABLE legal_hold (
    hold_id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    subject_type  text NOT NULL,     -- 'tenant', 'user', 'table'
    subject_id    text NOT NULL,
    matter_ref    text NOT NULL,     -- the case or request reference
    placed_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    placed_by     text NOT NULL,
    released_at   timestamptz,
    CHECK (released_at IS NULL OR released_at > placed_at)
);
CREATE INDEX ix_legal_hold_subject ON legal_hold (subject_type, subject_id)
     WHERE released_at IS NULL;   -- partial: only active holds are read

--
-- The erasure query, and why a legal hold makes it a join rather than a
-- -- DELETE. A hold on one row of one table for one subject suspends the
-- -- erasure obligation for that subject's rows in every table, so the
-- -- erasure job must ask about holds before it erases anything. This is the
-- -- single most commonly missed control in a right-to-be-deleted implementation.
SELECT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM legal_hold
     WHERE subject_type = 'tenant'
       AND subject_id = '412'
       AND released_at IS NULL
) AS under_legal_hold;

Retention is per-jurisdiction, and a single global period is a compliance bug. A SaaS product with tenants in the EU, the UK and California has three different erasure regimes with different triggers, different exceptions and different timelines, and the intersection — the shortest period, the most aggressive erasure — is the only safe default. The design consequence is that retention must be a property of the row, or of the tenant, not of the table. A global DELETE FROM payment_event WHERE created_at < now() - interval '7 years' is simple and wrong, because it cannot express that this tenant’s data is subject to a hold and that tenant’s is not. The partition boundary and the hold table together make the job a decision rather than a timestamp comparison, and that is the correct shape.

12.2 Time partitioning to make deletion cheap

A DELETE of a million rows is a million dead row versions, a vacuum obligation, a replication stream that a replica must apply before it can be promoted, and a table that has not got smaller. A DROP PARTITION is a catalog operation that reclaims the space immediately on all three engines and costs nothing.

The comparison is the whole argument for partitioning. A DELETE does not remove rows from the table; it marks them dead, and the space is reclaimed only when vacuum runs and only for pages where every row happens to be dead. It writes every deleted row to the WAL or the binlog or the transaction log, which a replica must apply — so a large delete is a large replication event, and a synchronous standby is blocked for its duration. It holds row locks for its duration, so a long delete on a hot table blocks a long transaction on that table. A DROP PARTITION by contrast unlinks a relation from the catalog; on PostgreSQL it is a fast catalog operation and the files are unlinked, on MySQL it drops the tablespace files, and on SQL Server it moves the data out and deallocates it. None of it is proportional to the number of rows removed, because none of it visits the rows.

PropertyDELETE of 20 M rowsDROP PARTITION of the same data
TimeMinutes to hours, proportional to row countMilliseconds, proportional to the number of partitions
Space reclaimed immediatelyNo — only after vacuum, and only fully where pages become entirely deadYes, on every engine
WAL / binlog / log volumeOne record per deleted row. This is the reason a delete is the most expensive kind of writeNone. Nothing is written
Replication effectThe replica applies 20 M deletes and falls behind by minutes; a synchronous standby blocks the primary for the durationNone. A synchronous standby is not involved at all
LocksRow locks for the duration; a long transaction that blocks vacuumAn ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on the parent for the catalog operation — milliseconds
Foreign keys referencing itMust be disabled or deferred, and every referencing table must be updatedMust be dropped or repointed first, but the check is a catalog lookup
RecoveryNot possible. The rows are gone and there is no undo outside a backupRecoverable from a backup or, for DETACH, the partition is intact and can be reattached
Query impactEvery query touching the table now has dead tuples to skip, until vacuumNone

The consequence for design is that a table with a deletion obligation should be range-partitioned on the timestamp that governs the obligation, with the partition interval chosen so that one interval is a manageable unit of work. Monthly is the usual choice for a transaction table and daily for a high-volume event table. The interval is a compromise: smaller partitions mean more catalog entries and a longer partition list to plan against — the planner must consider every partition that the predicate might touch, and hundreds of partitions on a statement with no partition key predicate is a measurable cost — while larger partitions mean each DROP is a longer catalog operation and the default partitions must be created further ahead.

-- Range partitioning on the retention clock. Monthly partitions for a transaction
-- table, with a DEFAULT partition so that a write with a timestamp outside
-- -- the declared range cannot fail. The DEFAULT is a safety net that must be
-- -- monitored, because it silently accumulating rows is a retention failure.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    placed_at      timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    status         text NOT NULL,
    total_excl_tax numeric(14,2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    PRIMARY KEY (placed_at, order_id),   -- partition key must lead
    CONSTRAINT fk_so_tenant FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id) REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);

CREATE TABLE sales_order_2026_09 PARTITION OF sales_order
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-09-01') TO ('2026-10-01');
CREATE TABLE sales_order_2026_10 PARTITION OF sales_order
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-10-01') TO ('2026-11-01');
CREATE TABLE sales_order_default  PARTITION OF sales_order DEFAULT;

-- Indexes on a partitioned table. On PostgreSQL an index on the parent is
-- -- created on every partition and the parent index is attached, so there
-- -- is exactly one index definition and no drift is possible.
CREATE INDEX ix_so_tenant_placed ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC);

-- The lifecycle job. Create ahead, drop behind, and never use DELETE.
--
-- 1. Create the next two months' partitions. A missing partition is an
-- --   INSERT failure, so the create-ahead horizon is a hard availability
-- --   requirement, and running out of it is a page.
--
-- 2. Attach the new month's data BEFORE dropping the old month, so the table
-- --   is never missing a period. Never the other way round.
--
-- 3. Check the legal_hold table for the period being dropped, and skip if held.
--
-- 4. Detach, then drop. Detach is metadata-only and makes the operation
-- --   reversible; CONCURRENTLY avoids the ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock at the cost of
-- --   leaving a gap that must be filled before reattaching.
ALTER TABLE sales_order DETACH PARTITION CONCURRENTLY sales_order_2019_08;
DROP TABLE sales_order_2019_08;          -- catalog + unlink; instant regardless of size

-- The MySQL form, and the exchange that makes it non-blocking. ALTER TABLE ...
-- -- DROP PARTITION is itself metadata-only since 5.7, but adding one is a
-- -- table rebuild, which is why a staging table plus EXCHANGE exists.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_2026_11 LIKE sales_order;
--
-- ALTER TABLE sales_order PARTITION BY RANGE COLUMNS (placed_at) (
--   PARTITION p202609 VALUES LESS THAN ('2026-10-01'),
--   PARTITION p202610 VALUES LESS THAN ('2026-11-01'),
--   PARTITION pmax    VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE)   -- no DEFAULT in MySQL;
-- );  -- pmax is the equivalent safety net and the same monitoring problem
--
-- ALTER TABLE sales_order EXCHANGE PARTITION p202611 WITH TABLE sales_order_2026_11;
-- -- A metadata-only swap, but the tables must have identical structure
-- -- INCLUDING indexes, and any foreign key on a partitioned MySQL table
-- -- must be absent, which in practice means no foreign keys on partitioned
-- -- tables at all in MySQL 9.x. That is a real constraint of the engine and it
-- -- should be a design input, not a discovery.
ALTER TABLE sales_order DROP PARTITION p201908;

-- SQL Server 2025: SWITCH is the mechanism, and it is metadata-only. The target
-- -- must have an identical column layout, identical constraints including
-- -- defaults, and a CHECK constraint that matches the partition boundary exactly,
-- -- or the SWITCH fails. That CHECK constraint is the part everyone forgets.
--
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_2019_08 (LIKE sales_template);
-- ALTER TABLE sales_order_2019_08 ADD CONSTRAINT ck_bound
--   CHECK (placed_at >= '2019-08-01' AND placed_at < '2019-09-01');
-- ALTER TABLE sales_order SWITCH PARTITION p201908 TO sales_order_2019_08;
-- DROP TABLE sales_order_2019_08;
--
-- SWITCH requires the partitions to be ALIGNED, which for a clustered
-- -- index means the partitioning scheme of the index must match the table,
-- -- and for a columnstore-indexed table means every nonclustered index must be
-- -- aligned as well. Unaligned, SWITCH is not available at all.
--
--   CREATE INDEX ix_so ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC)
