--     ON (placed_at)   -- note the partition scheme on the index itself
--     WITH (ONLINE = ON, DATA_COMPRESSION = PAGE);

-- The measurement that justifies the partition. Before and after, on the same
-- -- data, the same clock:
--
--   DELETE FROM sales_order WHERE placed_at < '2019-09-01';  -- 20,418,203 rows
--   --   Time: 1h 47m 02s.  WAL: 3.9 GB.  Replica lag: 1h 44m.
--   --   Table size after: unchanged.  Index bloat: +4%.
--   DROP TABLE sales_order_2019_08;                               -- same rows
--   --   Time: 84 ms.  WAL: 0.  Replica lag: 0.
--   --   Table size after: 4.1 GB smaller.  Index bloat: unchanged.

A partitioned table has a cost that is paid on every query, and it is not only planning. The partition key must lead every unique constraint, which for a tenant-scoped schema is awkward: if the primary key is (order_id) and the partition key is placed_at, the key must become (placed_at, order_id), and every foreign key that references it must include the partition key — which means a foreign key from sales_order_line to sales_order must carry placed_at as well as order_id, and that is a schema-wide decision made for a table-management benefit. On PostgreSQL the cost is lower because the planner prunes partitions at plan time from the predicate, and the practical limit is a few hundred partitions. On MySQL the cost is the absence of foreign keys, which is severe. On SQL Server the cost is the alignment requirement on every index. The rule is that partitioning is for tables that are large and have a deletion or archiving obligation, and for any other table it is a cost paid for nothing.

12.3 Soft versus hard delete, and the legal dimension

A soft delete — a deleted_at column that hides a row rather than removing it — is a decision about whether the row is still the customer’s data. If the answer is yes, a soft delete is a failure to comply, because the data is still stored, still replicated, still in every backup, and still visible to anyone with database access.

The reason soft delete is so widely and badly used is that it is trivially easy to add and it solves a real problem: the accidental deletion of a record someone needed. But it solves that problem by converting a data-protection problem into a data-retention problem, and the second one has a legal dimension the first does not. A row marked deleted_at = now() is still a row containing a customer’s email address. Under GDPR, under CCPA, under the UK GDPR, and under most sectoral rules, that is personal data that has not been erased, and the fact that the application does not display it is irrelevant to the obligation. Hidden is not erased: a row marked deleted_at is stored personal data, which is a categorically different thing (≠) from data that has been deleted.

MechanismWhat it protectsWhat it costsLegal standingUse when
Hard deleteNothing — the data is goneReferential integrity, audit history, accidental-deletion recoveryComplies. This is erasureAn erasure obligation, or a data class with no retention requirement
Soft delete (deleted_at)Accidental deletion; the row is recoverable for the TTL of the columnEvery query needs a predicate; every unique constraint needs a partial variant; the data is still personal dataDoes not comply. It is a hidden retention, not an erasureNever as the mechanism for an erasure request. Acceptable for a business-logical deletion such as a cancelled-but-not-deleted subscription, where the customer relationship continues
Soft delete with a short hard-delete deadlineAccidental deletionThe same, plus a jobComplies after the deadline, and the deadline is the actual retention periodWhere a short recovery window is genuinely wanted — a 30-day trash — and the job is monitored
Tombstone (row removed, marker kept)Referential integrity and replication correctnessAlmost nothing: the marker is tiny and contains no personal dataComplies. The personal data is gone; only the fact that a row existed remainsAlways, as a companion to a hard delete of a row that other rows reference
Anonymisation (overwrite in place)The analytical value of the record without the personal dataBreaks history; an overwritten row is no longer the record that happenedComplies if the identifying fields are genuinely non-reversible and the residual data is not re-identifiableAggregated or behavioural data where the aggregate matters and the person does not
Pseudonymisation (replace the identifier with a token)Joins across datasets, without retaining the identityRequires the mapping to be held somewhere, separately protected — and that mapping is the re-identification keyComplies only if the mapping is held separately with its own access control, and the token is not derived reversibly from the identifierAnalytics pipelines, where two datasets must be joined on a person
-- Soft delete done correctly, which means correctly *for its actual purpose*:
-- -- a business-logical state, not an erasure. A cancelled subscription is
-- -- not a deleted one; it still exists, it still has a plan, and the
-- -- relationship's legal basis continues for the retention tail.
ALTER TABLE subscription
    ADD COLUMN cancelled_at timestamptz;

-- Every unique constraint on a soft-deleted table needs a partial variant, and
-- -- this is where soft delete usually breaks: the constraint still holds for
-- -- deleted rows, so a customer's email cannot be reused after they leave,
-- -- which is a bug report about "cannot re-register with my old address".
ALTER TABLE app_user
    DROP CONSTRAINT uq_user_email,
    ADD CONSTRAINT uq_user_email_live UNIQUE (tenant_id, email)
        WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;
-- MySQL has no partial indexes: a partial unique constraint is not expressible
-- -- at all. The MySQL pattern is a generated column that is NULL when deleted,
-- -- because MySQL unique constraints allow unlimited NULLs, and a NULL never
-- -- collides. This is the standard workaround and it is not a hack.
--
-- ALTER TABLE app_user
--   ADD COLUMN email_live VARCHAR(255)
--     GENERATED ALWAYS AS (IF(deleted_at IS NULL, email, NULL)) STORED,
--   ADD UNIQUE KEY uq_user_email_live (tenant_id, email_live);
-- SQL Server: a filtered unique index, which is the direct equivalent.
--   CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_user_email_live ON app_user (tenant_id, email)
--     WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

-- The soft-delete query hazard, and why the fix is a view and not a reminder.
-- Every statement in the codebase must add the predicate. Reminders do not
-- -- survive contact with a new query written under deadline.
--
-- ALTER TABLE app_user ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;   -- ch13.2
-- CREATE POLICY app_user_tenant ON app_user USING (
--     tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::bigint
--     AND deleted_at IS NULL);
-- 
-- With the policy in place, the predicate is enforced by the engine and a query
-- -- that forgets it returns nothing rather than another tenant's rows. Without
-- -- it, forgetting it is a data breach. This is the actual argument for RLS,
-- -- -- and it is a correctness argument, not a defence-in-depth one.

-- Hard delete, done in an order that keeps the database consistent at every
-- -- statement boundary rather than only at COMMIT. A child-first order means
-- -- -- no intermediate state violates a foreign key.
BEGIN;
DELETE FROM payment_event     WHERE tenant_id = 412;
DELETE FROM sales_order_line WHERE tenant_id = 412;
DELETE FROM outbox            WHERE tenant_id = 412;
DELETE FROM sales_order      WHERE tenant_id = 412;
DELETE FROM subscription     WHERE tenant_id = 412;
DELETE FROM app_user         WHERE tenant_id = 412;
INSERT INTO tenant_tombstone (tenant_id, slug, erased_at, reason, event_count)
VALUES (412, 'acme', now(), 'erasure-request', 184023);
DELETE FROM tenant           WHERE tenant_id = 412;
COMMIT;

Erasure and audit are in tension, and the resolution is a distinction between identity and fact. An audit trail that records "user 8 4412, tenant 412, order 88 100 041, status changed from paid to refunded at 14:02:17" is evidence of something that happened, and it is also a record that user 8 441 2 exists. Most regimes handle this by requiring the audit record to be retained while permitting the identifying fields to be removed, because the evidentiary value survives the pseudonym. The engineering consequence is that an audit table’s identity fields must be separable from its fact fields, which means the design should put the actor identifier in a column that can be nulled independently of the event description, and it should be known in advance — retrofitting that separation means rewriting a table that a regulator may already have an opinion about. The alternative, retaining the identifier and claiming an exemption, is defensible in some jurisdictions and must be a documented legal position rather than an engineering one.

12.4 Erasure, cascade obligations and anonymisation

A right-to-be-deleted request is a graph problem, not a DELETE. The subject is a row; the data is a connected component that may span tables, partitions, caches, search indexes, log files, analytics exports and backups, and each of those has a different mechanism and a different timeline.

The graph must be walked exhaustively, and the walk is the design. The tempting approach — find every table with a foreign key to app_user, and recurse — finds the tables the schema knows about and misses the ones the schema has forgotten: a TEXT column holding a JSON blob that contains a user identifier, a log table with no foreign key, an export in object storage, an entry in a search index, a value in a Redis cache, a row in a data warehouse, a copy in a backup. The enumeration must therefore be a maintained artefact, and the artefact’s correctness is what a regulator tests.

-- The personal-data register. Like the retention register in 12.1, this belongs in
-- -- the database, because a document is a document and a table can be joined
-- -- to the erasure job, diffed against the live schema, and tested.
CREATE TABLE personal_data_register (
    location        text PRIMARY KEY,   -- 'db:saas.public.app_user.email'
    subject_column  text NOT NULL,
    erasure_method text NOT NULL,      -- see the table above
    cascade         boolean NOT NULL,   -- true: erased with the subject automatically
    covered_by_backup boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    owner           text NOT NULL,
    CHECK (erasure_method IN ('hard-delete','nullify','anonymise','pseudonymise','tombstone','legal-hold'))
);

INSERT INTO personal_data_register VALUES
  ('db:saas.public.app_user.email',          'email',            'hard-delete',  true,  true, 'platform'),
  ('db:saas.public.app_user.display_name',   'display_name',     'hard-delete',  true,  true, 'platform'),
  ('db:saas.public.sales_order.billing_address', 'billing_address', 'nullify',      true,  true, 'platform'),
  ('db:saas.public.audit_event.actor_user_id',   'actor_user_id',   'pseudonymise', true,  true, 'platform'),
  ('db:saas.public.app_user.metadata',        'metadata',        'anonymise',    true,  true, 'platform'),
  ('redis:cache:order_detail',                'serialised_row',  'hard-delete',  true,  false,'platform'),
  ('search:orders:412',                     'document',        'hard-delete',  true,  false,'platform'),
  ('s3://exports/daily/2026-09-14.csv',       'csv_columns',     'hard-delete',  false, true, 'analytics'),
    ('warehouse.public.fact_order.customer_key',  'customer_key',    'pseudonymise', false, true, 'analytics'),
  ('backup:pg_base/2026-09-01.dump',           'whole_dump',      'legal-hold',   false, true, 'infra');

-- The anonymous-row technique, which is a good deal subtler than
-- -- "replace the email with NULL". A stable, non-reversible token derived
-- -- from the subject's own key, stored once, and never in the same table
-- -- as the identity, so that joining the two reconstructs the identity --
-- -- -- which is permitted only if the mapping is separately protected.
UPDATE app_user
   SET email          = NULL,
       display_name   = NULL,
       -- A pseudonym that preserves the shape of the data for analytics
       -- (one row remains, counts still work) without preserving the person.
       email_pseudonym = 'u_' || encode(digest(user_id::text || '{erasure-salt-v1}', 'sha256'), 'hex'),
       metadata       = NULL,
       status         = 'erased',
       erased_at      = now()
 WHERE user_id = 84412;

-- The erasure job, in the order the operations must happen. The order is
-- -- the whole design, and the reason it is written out in full is that
-- -- every one of these steps is individually forgettable.
--
--  1. Record the request. An erasure request that is not recorded cannot be
-- --    evidenced as honoured, and "we cannot show our erasure logs" is itself a
-- --    finding. Keep: request id, subject, timestamp, identity of the
-- --    requester, verification evidence. Never keep: the data itself.
--  2. Check for a legal hold. A held subject is not erased; the request is
-- --    recorded as deferred with a reason and reviewed on a schedule.
--  3. Propagate to every system with a 'cascade = true' row, in dependency order,
-- --    each step recorded with its own completion timestamp. Retry each; never
-- --    mark the request complete while any step is outstanding.
--  4. Erase from object storage and the warehouse, which are not transactional
-- --    with anything and cannot be part of the database transaction.
--  5. Erase from the operational database, in one transaction (12.3).
--  6. Bump the cache-version families from chapter 10 so no cached copy
-- --    survives, and delete the search index documents.
--  7. Wait for the replica lag to be confirmed zero, then mark the request
-- --    complete. A replica still holding the row is a copy that has not been
-- --    erased, and "we deleted it" is not true until the copy is gone.
--  8. Let the backup cycle expire the backup that contains it (12.6). Record
-- --    the earliest date on which the backup containing this data is destroyed,
-- --    and report that date to the requester. Most regimes require exactly that.

--
-- The progress table, which is what makes step 3 survivable.
CREATE TABLE erasure_request (
    request_id     uuid PRIMARY KEY,
    subject_type   text NOT NULL,
    subject_id     text NOT NULL,
    received_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    due_at         timestamptz NOT NULL,      -- received_at + 30 days
    completed_at   timestamptz,
    backup_expires_at timestamptz,           -- step 8, reported to the subject
    UNIQUE (subject_type, subject_id, received_at)
);
CREATE TABLE erasure_step (
    request_id     uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES erasure_request(request_id),
    location       text NOT NULL,             -- a key from personal_data_register
    status         text NOT NULL,             -- pending, running, done, failed, deferred-hold
    attempts       int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    last_error     text,
    completed_at   timestamptz,
    PRIMARY KEY (request_id, location)
);

Anonymisation that is reversible by a database query is not anonymisation. The common failure is a pseudonym derived from the identifier with a function whose inverse is trivial: 'user_' || user_id, or md5(email) without a secret salt, or a name that encodes a postcode. A regulator’s test is whether the subject is reasonably likely to be re-identified, and a salt that lives in the same database fails that test because the database operator can reverse it. The salt must be a secret held in a key manager, must not be a column, and must be versioned so that a rotation does not break the joins in the analytics pipeline that depend on the old pseudonym — which is itself a data-retention obligation for the old mapping. Where the pseudonym is only needed for aggregate statistics rather than for joining, the better answer is to aggregate first and never pseudonymise at all: a per-tenant, per-day order count is not personal data, and the moment you make it one by attaching a user identifier you have created a problem you did not have.

12.5 TTL, automatic expiry and archival tiers

Automatic expiry moves the retention decision from a scheduled job to a property of the row, which removes a whole class of human error and introduces a different one: rows that expire before anyone expected.

TTL columns are natively supported in two of the three engines and absent in the third, and the difference is architectural rather than cosmetic. A native TTL is enforced by the storage engine as part of normal maintenance, so it costs nothing when it is not being enforced, it is batched rather than per-row, and — critically — it is asynchronous. A row is not deleted at its TTL instant; it is deleted when a maintenance pass reaches it, which on PostgreSQL is a heap pass whose frequency is a ttl_ setting and on Cloudflare-style stores is a background sweep. Every design consequence follows: a row is visible after its TTL, a query must tolerate a row that should be gone, and the effective retention period is the TTL plus the sweep interval plus the replication lag.

-- PostgreSQL 18: a native TTL column. Added in 18 and the reason this section
-- -- can start with it. The TTL column is declared with a length unit and the
-- -- engine deletes the row when the age exceeds it. Note what this does NOT
-- -- do: it does not cascade, it does not fire triggers, and it does not write
-- -- a WAL record per deleted row in the way a DELETE would. The deletion is
-- -- -- performed as a batch operation and is cheaper, but it is still WAL.
CREATE TABLE session_token (
    token_hash   bytea PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id    bigint NOT NULL,
    user_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    issued_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    TTL interval NOT NULL          -- expires 30 days after issue
) WITH (ttl = '30 days');

-- The default TTL for the table, and a per-table override for the rows that
-- -- need a different one. Refreshed on update, which is what a sliding
-- -- session needs and what a fixed-expiry token must avoid.
CREATE TABLE api_token (...) WITH (ttl = '90 days', ttl_refresh = 'true');

-- The rows past their TTL are still there until the sweep reaches them, so
-- -- the observable TTL is longer than the declared one:
SELECT count(*) AS expired_but_present,
       max(age('now', issued_at)) AS oldest_expired
  FROM session_token
 WHERE issued_at < now() - interval '30 days';

-- MySQL 9.x: no native TTL. The two options, and they are not equivalent.
--
-- Option 1, an event scheduler. Simple, and it runs on one node, so a replica's
-- -- -- scheduler also fires and the DELETE is replicated, producing duplicate
-- -- -- work and, with row-based replication, a replicated DELETE for a row that
-- -- -- was already gone. Guard with a single-writer check or disable the
-- -- -- scheduler on replicas.
--
-- CREATE EVENT purge_session_tokens ON SCHEDULE EVERY 1 HOUR DO
--   DELETE FROM session_token WHERE issued_at < NOW() - INTERVAL 30 DAY
--     LIMIT 10000;
--
-- Option 2, a partitioning job with DROP PARTITION, per 12.2. More machinery,
-- -- -- and the only one whose cost does not grow with the number of rows expiring.

-- SQL Server 2025: no native TTL either, and a job is required. The form to
-- -- use is a batched DELETE with an OUTPUT clause, so that the erasure is
-- -- recorded in an audit table in the same statement and the same transaction --
-- -- which is what makes it evidence rather than a claim.
--
-- DELETE TOP (10000) FROM session_token
--  OUTPUT deleted.token_hash, deleted.user_id, SYSDATETIME()
--    INTO audit_token_purge (token_hash, user_id, purged_at)
--  WHERE issued_at < DATEADD(DAY, -30, SYSUTCDATETIME());

-- Archival tiers, and the rule that decides which tier a row goes to. The rule
-- -- is not age alone; it is age plus whether the row is still queried.
--
--   Tier 0  hot      : <em>now</em>            → primary, NVMe, index-resident
--   Tier 1  warm      : 7–90 days         → primary, SSD, larger storage class
--   Tier 2  cold      : 90 days – 2 years → replica or a second instance, object-store-backed
--   Tier 3  archive    : 2–7 years        → compressed columnar, in object storage,
--                    queried by a restore-then-query, never online
--   Tier 4  destroy    : > retention     → gone, with a tombstone and a log line
--
-- Querying an archive is the part that is usually unplanned. The three options,
-- -- with their real costs:
--
-- 1. Restore the partition to a scratch database and query it. Correct, exact,
-- --   and slow: a 40 GB restore is minutes, and an auditor asking one question
-- --   should not wait 20 minutes. A scratch instance with a pre-warmed data
--   one scratch instance kept warm with an already-restored copy,
--   so the common case is seconds rather than a restore. The cold case is
--   still a full restore and should be quoted honestly in the runbook.

-- 2. Parquet in object storage, queried by DuckDB, Athena, BigQuery or Spark.
--   -- The right answer for most analytics and for an auditor's question, and it
--   -- -- requires the archive to be written in a columnar format from the start,
--   -- -- which is a decision to take before the first partition is archived.
--   -- Reading back a relational dump to produce it six months later is a project.
--
-- 3. A federated query across tiers, which is what a data-lakehouse table format
-- -- gives you and which is a different architecture rather than a feature.

Archival is a format decision made years before it is needed. A partition that is archived as a PostgreSQL or MySQL dump has to be restored by a database to be read, which means a database, which means storage, which means a restore time proportional to the partition size. A partition that is archived as Parquet can be read by a single process on a laptop, queried without a schema server, and read by twenty tools. The cost of choosing Parquet at the start is a conversion step in the archival job and a loss of transactional guarantees in the archive — and that loss is acceptable precisely because the archive is by definition outside the transaction. The mistake is to defer the decision, because after two years of dumps the conversion is a project and the archive is inaccessible in the meantime.

12.6 Tombstones, integrity after deletion, and the backup problem

Deleting a row that other rows reference creates a choice: break the reference, refuse the delete, or record the fact that the row existed. The third is the only one that keeps a distributed system correct across time, and it is called a tombstone.

The problem is specific. A sales_order_line references a sales_order. Erase the order for data-protection reasons and the lines are erased too, in the same transaction, in one database — and everything is consistent. But the erasures propagate through a change stream to a search index, an analytics warehouse and a cache, and those consumers apply changes independently and at their own pace. The search index applies the order deletion before the line deletion, and for the intervening seconds it has an order document with no lines and an orphaned line document that references a document which no longer exists. A consumer that resolves references by a join on read will produce a dangling reference; a consumer that resolves them eagerly will fail. The tombstone is the marker that says "this row existed and is now gone", and it is what lets a consumer distinguish deleted from not yet seen — a distinction it cannot make otherwise.

-- The tombstone, in the operational database. It carries no personal data: the
-- -- tenant slug and email are erased above precisely so this table can hold
-- -- the identifier without becoming a retention problem of its own.
CREATE TABLE tombstone (
    entity_type    text NOT NULL,
    entity_id      text NOT NULL,
    tenant_id      bigint,
    deleted_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    origin_node    text NOT NULL,        -- which node emitted it, for multi-master
    PRIMARY KEY (entity_type, entity_id, deleted_at)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (deleted_at);
-- Tombstones are retained for a bounded window -- long enough that every
-- -- consumer has certainly seen one, which is a function of the slowest
-- -- consumer's maximum outage tolerance, and therefore a number that must be
-- -- derived from a stated consumer SLO rather than guessed at 30 days.

-- The consumer side, where the tombstone earns its keep. A change stream
-- -- without tombstones cannot distinguish "deleted" from "not yet replicated",
-- -- so a full re-sync is the only way to become correct, and a full re-sync is
-- -- a project that gets triggered by an incident.
--
--   for event in stream:
--       if event.op == "delete":
--           search.delete(event.entity_type, event.entity_id)
--           warehouse.mark_deleted(event.entity_type, event.entity_id, event.deleted_at)
--       else:
--           if warehouse.is_deleted(event.entity_type, event.entity_id):
--               // The row was deleted and has now been resurrected. This is a
--               // -- legitimate outcome: an erasure followed by a restore from a
--               // -- backup taken before the erasure. Decide explicitly.
--               warehouse.record_resurrection(event, reason='restored-from-backup')
--           search.index(event)

-- The referential-integrity question that partitioning answers badly. A
-- -- foreign key from a non-partitioned table to a partitioned table must
-- -- include the partition key, so every referencing table carries the
-- -- timestamp. The alternative is to have no foreign key, and the reason
-- -- that is frequently the right answer is worth stating precisely: a foreign
-- -- -- key is a guarantee about a write, not about history. Dropping the parent
-- -- -- row to satisfy a retention policy is not a write that the constraint is
-- -- -- meant to protect against, and the constraint has to be relaxed to permit it.
--
-- The two lawful options, both of which need saying out loud:
--   (a) Keep the FK, and delete the parent LAST, after every child. This is
--       -- the 12.3 order, and it works. The cost is that a partition drop
--       -- requires dropping and recreating the FK, which is metadata-only but
--       -- -- is a window in which the constraint does not exist.
--   (b) Drop the FK on the archiving table and enforce the relationship in the
--       -- lifecycle job, which is a batch job that must be correct.

-- THE BACKUP PROBLEM. A backup is a copy of personal data. It is in scope, it
-- -- is retained, and it is where erasure obligations are most often
-- -- quietly unfulfilled, because the operational database was correctly erased
-- -- and the dump from 41 days ago still contains the row.
--
-- The lawful position, and it is the only one that is both defensible and
-- -- operable: you may retain a backup containing erased data until that
-- -- backup ages out of the retention cycle, PROVIDED you (i) do not restore it
-- -- -- except as part of disaster recovery, (ii) apply the erasure again on
-- -- -- restore, in the restore runbook, as a mandatory step, and (iii) tell the
-- -- -- data subject the date on which the last copy will be destroyed.
-- -- Clause (ii) is the one that is missed, and its absence is a genuine finding:
-- -- a restored backup reintroduces erased personal data into a live database.
--
-- The restore runbook must therefore include, as a numbered step, before the
-- -- database is allowed to serve traffic:
--
--   R7. Apply all erasure requests received after the backup's timestamp, and
-- --     all retention policies, before the instance is added to the service.
--   R8. Verify: SELECT count(*) FROM erasure_request
--           WHERE received_at > '<backup timestamp>' AND completed_at IS NULL;  -- must be 0
--
-- The backup inventory, because you cannot honour a backup-retention
-- -- commitment if you cannot enumerate the backups.
CREATE TABLE backup_inventory (
    backup_id       text PRIMARY KEY,
    taken_at        timestamptz NOT NULL,
    kind            text NOT NULL,        -- full, incremental, wal-archive, snapshot
    storage_uri     text NOT NULL,
    encryption_key_id text NOT NULL,      -- per-tenant keys, ch13.6
    expires_at      timestamptz NOT NULL,
    -- Which database objects it contains, so that a retention commitment
--   can be answered without restoring anything.
    -- tenant_ids_covered   text[],   -- NULL means "all tenants"
    -- latest_erasure_id    uuid      -- the erasure_request this backup predates
    CHECK (expires_at > taken_at)
);
--
-- And the query that answers the question an auditor actually asks: "what is
-- -- the earliest date at which all copies of this subject's data are gone?"
SELECT r.request_id,
       max(b.expires_at) AS all_copies_gone_after,
       max(b.expires_at) - r.received_at AS worst_case_window,
       count(*) AS backups_containing_subject
  FROM erasure_request r
  JOIN backup_inventory b
    ON b.taken_at > r.received_at - interval '7 days'
   AND b.expires_at > r.received_at
 WHERE r.subject_id = '84412' AND r.completed_at IS NOT NULL
 GROUP BY r.request_id, r.received_at
 ORDER BY all_copies_gone_after DESC;

Backup retention is a data-retention decision and it is usually made by someone else. The default retention on a managed database service is commonly 35 days, chosen for operational reasons by a platform team that is not thinking about personal data. Thirty-five days is a long time to hold an erased person’s email address, and on a system with 35-day backup retention an erasure request can be honoured operationally in ten minutes and still leave a copy in production for a month. The two mitigations, and both are needed: keep the operational retention aligned with the legal commitment, and put the expiry calculation from the query above into the erasure response, so the commitment made to the data subject is a number derived from the actual backup inventory rather than an aspiration. A commitment of "we have deleted your data" when a backup still holds it for 26 more days is not a small imprecision; in a written response to a subject access request it is a false statement.

Exercises 12

  1. Build a retention register as a table for a schema you can reach. Populate it from the schema by inspecting every column and classifying it. Then diff it against the columns the schema actually has and reconcile the two lists — every column the register does not mention is a retention decision that was not made, and the exercise is worth doing because that list is never empty on a real system.
  2. Measure the difference between the two deletion mechanisms on a table with 20 million rows. Record wall time, log volume, replica lag, table size before and after, and the time until the space is actually reclaimed. Then convert the DELETE into a partitioned DROP PARTITION and repeat. Use the numbers from section 12.2 as a hypothesis and report whether they held.
  3. Implement a soft delete on a table with a unique constraint and demonstrate the two failures it introduces: a deleted row still blocking a reuse of the same value, and a query that forgets the predicate. Then move the predicate into a row-level security policy and demonstrate that the second failure is now a correctness property of the engine rather than a discipline of the codebase.
  4. Build a personal-data register by grepping the schema for every column holding a name, an email, an address, a free-text field and a JSON or text blob, and classify each. Then enumerate the systems outside the database that hold a copy — caches, search indexes, object storage, the warehouse, log files, exports — and add them. Confirm the list against personal_data_register and note how many rows the register that shipped with the system was missing.
  5. Implement an erasure request end to end, including the per-step progress table, the legal-hold check, the propagation to every non-cascade location, and the wait for confirmed replica lag. Then measure the wall clock to full completion and to the point at which all backups containing the data will have expired. The second number is the one that goes in the response to the subject.
  6. Restore a backup into a scratch instance and verify that the restore runbook’s erasure-reapplication step actually works. Add a test to CI that restores the most recent backup nightly, replays the erasure log, and asserts that no erased identifier is present. Then test the scenario the runbook exists for: a restore onto a failed primary, with the erasure steps deliberately skipped, and record what you find.

13 · Multi-Tenancy

13.1 Three models, compared on six axes13.2 Row-level security and why it is worth its cost13.3 Tenant resolution, precedence and the trust boundary13.4 Per-tenant configuration, entitlements and quotas13.5 The missing predicate, noisy neighbours and rate limits13.6 Export, offboarding, migration and per-tenant keys

Multi-tenancy is a set of isolation decisions that a schema makes structurally rather than a feature that an application adds. Every row in this schema carries a tenant_id, and the presence of that column is the commitment: from this point, a single missing predicate is a data breach, and the design must make that single missing predicate either impossible or loud.

13.1 Three models, compared on six axes

Shared schema, schema per tenant, and database per tenant are three answers to the same question — where does the boundary between customers live — and each is the right answer for a different set of constraints. The question is not which is best; it is which set of constraints you have.

AxisShared schema (one table, tenant_id column)Schema per tenant (one schema, same server)Database per tenant (one database, one connection)
IsolationLogical only. A missing predicate leaks. RLS makes the leak require a deliberate act, which is a materially higher barStrong. A search_path decides the schema, and a schema’s grants are a real boundary. A connection that names the wrong schema reads the wrong tenant’s data, but the blast radius is one schema, not the tablePhysical. A connection cannot see another database at all. A compromise of the server means everything, but a compromise of the application means one tenant
Cost per tenantNear zero. Storage, index and buffer pool are shared, so a tenant with 200 rows costs 200 rowsLow. Storage and indexes are shared per database; the overhead is catalog and per-schema planning metadataHigh. Every tenant has a minimum database footprint — on SQL Server roughly 30–50 MB of system catalogs plus log files, on MySQL 100–200 MB including redo and undo. One thousand tenants is 150 GB of overhead before a single row
Operational costOne schema to migrate, one set of statistics, one set of plans. Migrations are fast because the largest table is the sum of all tenants', which is also its weaknessOne migration per schema, or a loop. At 2 000 tenants a single ALTER is 2 000 catalog transactions, and a VACUUM, an index rebuild and an ANALYZE must be run per schemaOne migration per database, each with its own connection, its own log, its own backup, its own monitoring. At 1 000 tenants this is 1 000 deployment units and the migration tooling from chapter 8 becomes the dominant operational burden
Migration blast radiusOne statement, all tenants, one lock. The 41-minute index build from section 8.4 applies to every tenant at once — and a failure affects everyoneOne statement per schema, executed in a loop with a throttle. A failure affects one tenant; the loop can skip and retry. Migration duration is proportional to tenant countSame as schema-per-tenant, plus connection and storage overhead per tenant, plus a much longer total wall clock
Noisy neighbourWorst. One tenant’s 900-million-row table is one tenant’s 900-million rows in everyone’s table. Mitigated only by partitioning and by rate limits, both of which are advisoryBetter. Separate schemas have separate indexes, so a large tenant’s index is not a large index in everyone else’s table. The buffer pool is still sharedBest. Separate databases have separate buffer pools, separate memory grants, separate log files and separate IOPS allocation if they are on separate volumes. Resource governance is possible without cooperation from the application
Restore and exportBoth are WHERE tenant_id = ? on a full table, and a restore is all-or-nothing. Point-in-time recovery is a single timeline for everyone, which is convenient and also means one customer’s PITR is everyone’s PITRRestore is all-or-nothing per database. Export is pg_dump -n tenant_412, which is fast and completeRestore and export are per tenant and genuinely trivial, which is the strongest argument for this model for a product that must offer customers a copy of their own data
Connection costOne pool. The connection count is a function of load, not of tenant countOne pool per database, and a connection names a schema. PgBouncer’s search_path rewriting makes this one poolOne pool per tenant. At 1 000 tenants with 20 connections each you need 20 000 connections or a proxy per tenant, and PgBouncer cannot route to 1 000 databases cheaply without a routing table that is itself an operational artefact
CeilingLimited by the largest tenant’s data in a shared table, and by a single table’s index count on PostgreSQL (32 767 total, 2 000 per table for CREATE INDEX)Limited by the number of relations the planner will consider and by catalog size; comfortable to a few thousand tenantsLimited by the operational team. Past a few hundred tenants the migration tooling, the backup policy and the monitoring cardinality become the constraint, not the database
Choose it whenTenants are small relative to each other and similar in shape; the team is small; isolation requirements are contractual rather than regulatory; you may need to change the model laterThere are a few large tenants and many small ones; per-tenant configuration is a real requirement; isolation must be demonstrable to an auditor; the tenant count is bounded in the hundredsIsolation is a contractual or regulatory requirement; tenants have very different sizes; the product sells data export as a feature; the tenant count is bounded and the team can operate the fleet

Almost every real system ends up in a hybrid, and the hybrid is the honest answer rather than a compromise. The pattern that works is: shared schema for the long tail, schema-per-tenant or database-per-tenant for the named accounts that contractually require it, and a mechanism for promoting a tenant from one to the next. The mechanism is the part that is usually absent, and its absence is what turns a hybrid into a technical debt with a deadline. Promoting a tenant is not a data copy — it is a data copy plus a routing change, a key change, a cache-namespace change, a migration-ledger change and a monitoring change, and section 13.6 treats it as the project it is.

-- The shared schema, and the four decisions that make it defensible. Each is
-- -- cheap now and expensive later.
--
-- Decision 1: tenant_id is NOT NULL, in every table, with no default. A nullable
-- -- tenant_id is a row that belongs to nobody and that no predicate can
-- -- partition, and it is invisible to every query.
--
-- Decision 2: tenant_id leads every index that serves a tenant-scoped access
-- -- pattern. An index on (placed_at) alone is useless for a tenant-scoped query
-- -- because it has to filter the tenant out of every row it visits.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    order_id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    placed_at      timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    status         text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'placed',
    currency       char(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'GBP',
    total_excl_tax numeric(14,2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    version        int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    CONSTRAINT ck_so_total CHECK (total_excl_tax >= 0)
) PARTITION BY LIST (tenant_id);   -- see the note below
CREATE INDEX ix_so_tenant_placed ON sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC) INCLUDE (order_id, status);
CREATE INDEX ix_so_tenant_status ON sales_order (tenant_id, status) WHERE status <> 'placed';

-- Decision 3: every unique constraint is tenant-scoped. A global unique on
-- -- email would make two tenants unable to register the same address, which is
-- -- both a functional bug and a leak of one tenant's data to another through an
-- -- error message.
ALTER TABLE app_user ADD CONSTRAINT uq_user_tenant_email UNIQUE (tenant_id, email);
-- The same applies to every foreign key: a FK that does not include tenant_id
-- -- permits an order in tenant 412 to reference a subscription in tenant 413.
ALTER TABLE sales_order
    ADD CONSTRAINT fk_so_subscription
    FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, subscription_id) REFERENCES subscription (tenant_id, subscription_id);
-- This requires a UNIQUE (tenant_id, subscription_id) on the parent. It is
-- -- the composite-key form of tenant isolation, and it is enforced by the engine
-- -- rather than by a predicate that can be forgotten. It is the single most
-- -- valuable constraint in a multi-tenant schema.
ALTER TABLE subscription ADD CONSTRAINT uq_sub_tenant_id UNIQUE (tenant_id, subscription_id);

-- Decision 4: the tenant context is set once per transaction, by the connection
-- -- layer, from the request -- never by a query. Everything in 13.3 follows from
-- -- this line.
SET LOCAL app.tenant_id = '412';
SET LOCAL app.tenant_slug = 'acme';
SET LOCAL app.user_id = '84412';

-- Partitioning by tenant: powerful, and the hot-tenant problem from section 4.4
-- -- makes it a trap at scale. A LIST partition per tenant gives physical
-- -- separation and cheap per-tenant maintenance, and it costs:
--   - a partition per tenant, and PostgreSQL's practical ceiling is a few hundred
--     -- to a couple of thousand relations for a workable plan time.
--   - every new tenant is a DDL operation, which means a migration, which means
--     -- a lock. Onboarding stops being an INSERT.
--   - a tenant whose data is dropped cannot be dropped without dropping the
--     -- partition, which is good, and which also means the DROP is a catalog
--     -- -- operation that is fast, which is also good.
--   - HASH partitioning by tenant_id is the alternative: fixed partition count, no
--     -- DDL per tenant, and a hot tenant still concentrates in one partition, so it
--     -- buys index locality and not resource isolation.
--
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_p412 PARTITION OF sales_order FOR VALUES IN (412);
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_p413 PARTITION OF sales_order FOR VALUES IN (413);
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_ph00 PARTITION OF sales_order FOR VALUES WITH (
--     MODULUS 64, REMAINDER 0);  -- the hash form, 64 fixed partitions
--
-- The practical compromise for a system with a long tail: HASH partition into a
-- -- fixed number of partitions, and move the handful of large tenants out to
-- -- dedicated partitions when they cross a threshold. Moving is an ATTACH, a
-- -- backfill and a DETACH, which is the 8.3 four-phase rename applied to a table.
--
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_p99000 PARTITION OF sales_order FOR VALUES IN (99000);
-- -- and the migration from hash to dedicated is:
-- ALTER TABLE sales_order DETACH PARTITION sales_order_ph07;
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_ph07_isolated (LIKE sales_order INCLUDING ALL);
-- INSERT INTO sales_order_ph07_isolated SELECT * FROM sales_order_ph07 WHERE tenant_id = 99000;
-- DELETE FROM sales_order_ph07 WHERE tenant_id = 99000;   -- the 8.5 batched form
-- -- and the tenant-specific partitions:
-- CREATE TABLE sales_order_t99000 PARTITION OF sales_order FOR VALUES IN (99000);
-- ATTACH PARTITION sales_order_t99000 FOR VALUES IN (99000);  -- metadata only
-- DROP TABLE sales_order_ph07;   -- now empty
SharedSchema per tenantDatabase per tenant
Tenants10 – 100 00010 – 2 00010 – 500
Provisioning latencyOne INSERT, ~1 msCREATE SCHEMA + grants, ~50 msCREATE DATABASE + migrate, seconds to minutes
Migration for 1 000 tenants1 statement, 41 min1 000 statements, ~20 min with a throttle1 000 connections, ~4 h with a throttle, and a fleet-wide lock risk
Per-tenant encryption key✗ not at the storage layer; application-layer onlyPartial, with a per-schema tablespace✓ native, per-database or per-volume
Per-tenant restore / PITR✗ all or nothing✗ all or nothing per database✓ genuinely per tenant
Per-tenant resource limitAdvisory only: statement_timeout, quotas, connection capsAdvisory, plus separate indexes✓ memory grants, IOPS, log rate governors
Best-fit tenant profileLong tail, small, similarMid-market with per-tenant config needsFew large accounts, regulated, or data-export-driven

Decide the model by deciding what you will do when it is wrong. The question is not "which model is best for my current tenant mix" but "which model can I migrate away from, and at what cost". Shared schema is the most migratable, because the data has no physical boundary to break: promoting a tenant to a dedicated database is a data copy, and the application change is a routing table. Database-per-tenant is the least migratable, because a tenant that must move to shared requires a reverse copy out of an environment you control less well. A useful heuristic: start shared, build the promotion path from day one — even if you never use it — because the path is what makes the current choice cheap rather than merely defensible.

13.2 Row-level security and why it is worth its cost

Row-level security moves the tenant predicate from the query into the engine. That is the entire value proposition and it is worth a measurable cost, because the alternative is a class of bug whose detection requires exhaustive testing and whose consequence is a regulatory disclosure.

The cost of RLS is real and specific. On PostgreSQL the policy is evaluated as an additional filter for every row the query would otherwise return, and when the policy predicate is not index-backed the planner must either evaluate it per row or add a sub-plan — and the plans with RLS enabled are measurably worse than the plans without, particularly for queries that already have a tenant predicate, where the predicate is evaluated twice. On MySQL 9.x the same is true and the setting is sql_mode-independent, but MySQL’s implementation is per-table and requires every table to have a policy or be exempted. On SQL Server 2025 the mechanism is a security policy with a predicate function, and the predicate function is a scalar UDF whose inlining behaviour determines whether the query is optimised at all — the difference between an inlined and a non-inlined predicate is a factor of ten on a large scan.

-- PostgreSQL 18: the complete RLS setup for this schema, and the three details
-- -- that are each independently sufficient to make it not work.
ALTER TABLE sales_order      ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE sales_order_line ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE app_user         ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
--
-- Detail 1: ENABLE is not FORCE. Without FORCE, the table owner bypasses every
-- -- policy. Your application connects as the owner -- because that is what the
-- -- default provisioning does -- so ENABLE alone gives you a policy that is not
-- -- applied to the connection that matters most. FORCE applies the policy to the
-- -- -- owner as well, and it is the setting that makes the guarantee real.
ALTER TABLE sales_order FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;

--
-- Detail 2: the tenant context is a session variable, so it must be set inside a
-- -- transaction that ends, and the variable must not survive the pooled connection.
-- -- current_setting with missing_ok = false raises if it is unset, which is the
-- -- -- behaviour you want: an unset context is a hard error, not an empty result
-- -- -- set, and not a full table read.
CREATE POLICY sales_order_tenant_isolation ON sales_order
    USING       (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::bigint)
    WITH CHECK (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::bigint);
--
-- USING governs what rows are visible to SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE. WITH CHECK
-- -- governs what rows may be written by INSERT and UPDATE. Omitting WITH CHECK is
-- -- -- the classic gap: an application can read only its own rows and can insert
-- -- -- into any other tenant, which is a write-side breach that a read-only test
-- -- -- will never find.
--
-- Detail 3: a policy on a table is bypassed by a view owned by a superuser unless
-- -- the view is created WITH (security_invoker = true). A reporting view built on
-- -- sales_order that is owned by a role with BYPASSRLS reads every tenant. This is
-- -- the single most common way a correctly configured RLS schema leaks, because
-- -- the tables are all correct.
CREATE VIEW v_order_report WITH (security_invoker = true) AS
    SELECT tenant_id, placed_at::date AS d, sum(total_excl_tax) AS total
      FROM sales_order GROUP BY 1, 2;

-- The cost, measured. With the policy in place, a tenant-scoped query that
-- -- already has the predicate evaluates it twice. Confirm the plan still uses the
-- -- composite index and has not degraded to a filter, and if it has, the fix is
-- -- an index whose leading column is the policy's column, which is already the
-- -- design from 13.1.
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT order_id, placed_at, total_excl_tax
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE tenant_id = 412
 ORDER BY placed_at DESC LIMIT 50;
--
--   Index Scan using ix_so_tenant_placed on sales_order
--     Index Cond: (tenant_id = 412)
--     Filter: (tenant_id = (current_setting('app.tenant_id')::bigint))  -- evaluated once per
--                                                                                -- row, but on
--                                                                                -- -- 50 rows
--   Planning Time: 0.412 ms   Execution Time: 0.089 ms
--
-- The Filter line is the visible cost of the guarantee. On a 50-row page it is
-- -- -- noise. On a 12-million-row scan it is the difference between 0.1 s and 40 s,
-- -- -- which is why the cross-tenant administrative query needs a role with
-- -- -- BYPASSRLS and a logged, separately-audited access path.

-- MySQL 9.x: no RLS. The equivalent is a VIEW per table with a tenant predicate,
-- -- and it has the same two weaknesses RLS has: the base table is still reachable,
-- -- -- and a developer can query the base table by mistake. What MySQL offers
-- -- -- instead is a much stronger tool for the cases that matter most.
--
-- CREATE VIEW v_sales_order AS
--   SELECT * FROM sales_order WHERE tenant_id = CAST(@@session.app_tenant_id AS UNSIGNED);
--
-- The strong tool is per-schema privilege, which is the schema-per-tenant model:
--   CREATE USER 'app_t412'@'%' IDENTIFIED BY '...';
--   GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON tenant_412.* TO 'app_t412'@'%';
--   -- and the application connects as the tenant's own user. A query against any
--   -- other schema is a permission error, not an empty result. This is a stronger
--   -- boundary than a predicate, and it costs a connection-pool-per-credential.

-- SQL Server 2025: a security policy with a predicate function. Inlining is the
-- -- -- whole performance question: an inlined scalar UDF is folded into the query
-- -- -- plan, a non-inlined one is called per row and is catastrophic on a scan.
--
-- CREATE SCHEMA sec; GO
-- CREATE FUNCTION sec.fn_tenant(@tenant_id AS BIGINT)
--   RETURNS BIT WITH SCHEMABINDING
--   AS RETURN SELECT CAST(SESSION_CONTEXT(N'AppTenantId') AS BIGINT) = @tenant_id; GO
-- CREATE SECURITY POLICY sec.pol_sales_order
--   ADD FILTER PREDICATE sec.fn_tenant(tenant_id) ON sales_order
--   WITH (STATE = ON); GO
--
-- SESSION_CONTEXT survives a pooled connection in a way that a plain SET does not,
-- -- and it can be marked read-only for the connection's lifetime:
--   EXEC sp_set_session_context @key = N'AppTenantId', @value = 412, @read_only = 1;
-- -- @read_only = 1 means the application cannot change it mid-transaction, which
-- -- -- removes a class of bug that RLS on PostgreSQL does not have. Reset it with
-- -- -- sp_set_session_context @key = N'AppTenantId', @value = NULL.
QuestionPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Native row-level security✓ ENABLE / FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY with USING and WITH CHECK✗. Per-schema grants, or per-tenant views over a session variable✓ RLS, or a security policy with a predicate function
Context mechanismSET LOCAL app.tenant_id — transaction-scoped, so it cannot leak across a pooled connectionUser-defined session variable, or the connection’s user identitySESSION_CONTEXT with @read_only = 1, or EXECUTE AS
Does the owner bypass?Yes, unless FORCE is set. This is the default and it silently disables the guaranteeYes, always, if the user is the ownerYes, unless the predicate is applied; security_policy covers the owner, a security bar view with security_invoker does not
Views bypass?Unless created WITH (security_invoker = true) and owned by a non-superuserUnless the view has its own predicateUnless created WITH (security_invoker = true)
Index-friendly?✓ if the policy’s column leads an index. The planner uses the policy as an additional restriction and can use an index for itDepends entirely on the view’s predicate, which is the same situation as an RLS policy✓ if the predicate function is inlined. Check with sys.dm_exec_query_stats or the plan XML’s ScalarOperator node
Administrative bypassA role with BYPASSRLS, granted to nobody in the application, used by an audited break-glass pathThe owner account, whose use must be audited separatelyEXECUTE AS to a privileged login, audited by SQL Server audit
Testing the guaranteeSet the context, query without any predicate, assert only own rows. Repeat per roleConnect as the tenant user and query the base table; assert a permission errorSet SESSION_CONTEXT and query; assert only own rows. Also assert that a read-only context cannot be reset by the application

RLS is a control on the read path, and it does nothing about the write path unless WITH CHECK is present. A policy declared with only USING restricts what a role can see and modify, and says nothing about what it may insert. An application with a bug that omits tenant_id from an INSERT, or that takes it from the request body, will successfully write into another tenant’s table, and the resulting row will then be invisible to the tenant that owns it — which means the data is lost, unreported and invisible, rather than merely misplaced. The test for this is a single integration test that attempts an insert with a mismatched tenant identifier and asserts that it raises, and it is the highest-value test in a multi-tenant suite.

13.3 Tenant resolution, precedence and the trust boundary

The tenant identifier arrives in the request, and where the application decides to read it from is the single most security-relevant line of configuration in a multi-tenant system. Every mechanism has an attack, and the defence is a precedence order and a single point where the resolution happens.

The available mechanisms, in the order they should be trusted: the connection’s own authenticated identity, which is a property of the TLS certificate or the signed token and cannot be forged by the client; the host name, which the client controlled and the server verified; the path segment, which the client controls entirely; a header, which the client controls entirely and which is trivially spoofable by anything that is not your edge; and a body or query parameter, which the client controls entirely and which should never be used. The order below is the order of trust, and the rule is that a lower-trust mechanism may only be used when the higher-trust ones are absent, and only after the request is authenticated.

-- The resolution order, and the reason for each rung. Read the "if absent" column
-- -- as the specification: this is what the code must do, not what it may do.
--
--  1. mTLS certificate subject CN or SAN          trust: authenticated, unforgeable
--  2. Host header, matched against tenant.slug     trust: client-supplied, server-verified
--                            against a uniqueness constraint
--  3. X-Tenant-Id header                        trust: client-supplied, unverified
--  4. Path segment /api/t/{slug}/orders           trust: client-supplied, unverified
--  5. ?tenant_id=412                             trust: never acceptable
--
-- The rule that makes the ladder safe: a lower rung may only be consulted if
-- -- every higher rung is definitively absent. "Definitively absent" means the
-- -- header is not present at all -- not that it is empty, not that it parses,
-- -- and not that it failed to match a tenant. A present-but-invalid value is
-- -- a 400, never a fallback to the next rung.

--
-- The table the resolution depends on. Note the constraints: slug is unique
-- -- globally (so a host header resolves to exactly one tenant), and the reserved
-- -- names are rejected so that a tenant cannot claim an infrastructure hostname
-- -- and intercept traffic intended for the platform.
CREATE TABLE tenant (
    tenant_id      bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
    slug           text NOT NULL,
    name           text NOT NULL,
    status         text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'active',
    created_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT uq_tenant_slug UNIQUE (slug),
    CONSTRAINT ck_tenant_slug_format CHECK (slug ~ '^[a-z0-9]([a-z0-9-]{1,61}[a-z0-9])?$'),
    CONSTRAINT ck_tenant_slug_reserved CHECK (slug NOT IN (
        'www','api','app','admin','static','cdn',
        'mail','smtp','status','support','docs'))
);

--
-- The resolution, written out. The cache is per-process and short, and it is
-- -- keyed on the raw host so that two tenants cannot collide.
--
-- func resolveTenant(r *http.Request) (tenantID int64, err error) {
--     // 1. Certificate, if the connection used mTLS.
--     if r.TLS != nil && len(r.TLS.PeerCertificates) > 0 {
--         return tenantFromCN(r.TLS.PeerCertificates[0].Subject.CommonName)
--     }
--
--     // 2. Host. Strip the port and normalise, then match against slug.
--     host := strings.ToLower(strings.Split(r.Host, ":")[0])
--     if host == appBaseDomain {
--         return 0, ErrNoTenant          // the marketing site: no tenant context
--     }
--     if slug, ok := strings.CutSuffix(host, "."+appBaseDomain); ok {
--         return lookupSlug(slug)         // no cache miss is an error, never a fallback
--     }
--
--     // 3. Header, only if the Host carried no tenant at all.
--     if v := r.Header.Get("X-Tenant-Id"); v != "" {
--         id, err := strconv.ParseInt(v, 10, 64)
--         if err != nil { return 0, ErrBadTenant }   // present but invalid: 400
--         return id, nil
--     }
--
--     // 4. Path.
--     if id, ok := pathTenant(r.URL.Path); ok { return id, nil }
--
--     return 0, ErrNoTenant
-- }
--
-- The binding step, immediately after resolution and before any query. This is the
-- -- only place app.tenant_id is ever set, and it is inside a transaction so that
-- -- -- the value cannot survive the pooled connection (ch14.5).
--
-- func WithTenant(ctx context.Context, r *http.Request, fn func(*sql.Tx) error) error {
--     t, err := resolveTenant(r)
--     if err != nil { return err }
--     db, err := pool.BeginTx(ctx, nil)
--     defer db.Rollback()
--     // Never interpolate: SET does not accept a bind parameter, so the value is
--     // -- passed as a text literal and the function takes an int64, not a string.
--     if _, err := db.Exec("SELECT set_config('app.tenant_id', $1, true)",
--                        strconv.FormatInt(t, 10)); err != nil { return err }
--     if err := fn(db); err != nil { return err }
--     return db.Commit()
-- }

-- The cross-check that closes the remaining gap. A token issued to a user of
-- -- tenant 412 must not authenticate against a request that resolved to tenant
-- -- 413, and the check is a single indexed lookup that happens on every request:
SELECT 1
  FROM app_user u
  JOIN tenant t ON t.tenant_id = u.tenant_id
 WHERE u.user_id = 84412
   AND u.status = 'active'
   AND t.tenant_id = 413;   -- must return 0 rows for a cross-tenant token

The header is the weakest rung and the one teams start at, and it is the one that leaks. X-Tenant-Id is trivially set by any client, and if the edge does not strip it then it is a request parameter with a security-sensitive name. The specific failure: a support tool, a mobile client, or a test harness sends X-Tenant-Id: 412 to reach another customer’s data while presenting a valid token for tenant 99000, and nothing in the stack objects. The mitigations, in order: the edge must delete the header from inbound requests before setting its own; a cross-region API gateway or a service mesh makes this automatic and is the right place for the rule; and the token’s own tenant claim is always cross-checked against the resolved tenant, which is the mitigation that holds even when the header stripping is forgotten. The cross-check costs one indexed lookup and is worth more than the header stripping, because it fails closed.

Resolution is a trust decision and it belongs at the edge, not in the repository. The failure mode of resolving per query is that different code paths resolve differently — one uses the header, one uses the path, one uses a claim from the token — and the paths disagree in exactly the cases where it matters. The rule is that resolution happens once, at the boundary, its result is carried in the context, and every downstream component reads it from there. A component that can reach the tenant identifier from anywhere other than the context is a component that can be called with the wrong tenant, and there is no static check that will find it.

13.4 Per-tenant configuration, entitlements and quotas

Per-tenant configuration is where a shared-schema model earns or loses its simplicity. A column on tenant is one row read on every request; a table of overrides is a query; a per-tenant sidecar is a network hop. The number of configuration dimensions grows with the product, and the resolution cost grows with it.

-- The three-tier configuration model, and the reason for the middle tier. A single
-- -- table of (tenant_id, key, value) requires a query per configuration read and
-- -- -- has no type safety and no default. A column per dimension on tenant is fast
-- -- -- but every new dimension is a migration on the tenant table and a change to
-- -- -- every code path that reads tenant.
--
-- Tier 1: the plan, which is already a row. plan_code on the subscription points at
-- -- it, and it carries the entitlements and the quotas as columns, because a plan is
-- -- -- shared by many tenants and its shape is owned by the product team.
CREATE TABLE plan (
    plan_code        text PRIMARY KEY,
    name             text NOT NULL,
    monthly_price    numeric(12,2) NOT NULL,
    seat_limit       int NOT NULL,
    api_call_quota   bigint NOT NULL,
    -- Entitlements as a JSON document, versioned by the plan_code. A boolean
    -- -- per feature would mean a column per feature and a migration per feature,
    -- -- -- forever, and every one of them a chance to forget a check.
    entitlements     jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'::jsonb,
    CHECK (jsonb_typeof(entitlements) = 'object')
);

-- Tier 2: the tenant override. A sparse table, read only when a feature is not
-- -- on by default, and it is the tier that makes a sales-driven exception
-- -- -- ("we will turn on SSO for you next quarter") a data change rather than a
-- -- -- code change.
CREATE TABLE tenant_override (
    tenant_id    bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES tenant(tenant_id),
    key          text NOT NULL,
    value        jsonb NOT NULL,
    expires_at   timestamptz,      -- a trial entitlement expires itself
    granted_by   text NOT NULL,
    reason       text,
    created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, key)
) PARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id);
CREATE INDEX ix_override_live ON tenant_override (tenant_id, key)
    WHERE expires_at IS NULL OR expires_at > now();  -- not valid; see 9.3

-- The resolution, in order, with the cache in front of all of it. The important
-- -- property is that the whole resolved set is one row-shaped value, so the hot
-- -- -- path is a single cache read and a single database read on a miss.
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION resolve_config(p_tenant bigint)
RETURNS jsonb LANGUAGE sql STABLE AS $fn$
    SELECT coalesce(
        (SELECT jsonb_object_agg(o.key, o.value)
           FROM tenant_override o
          WHERE o.tenant_id = p_tenant
            AND (o.expires_at IS NULL OR o.expires_at > now())),
        '{}'::jsonb)
    || (SELECT p.entitlements
          FROM subscription s
          JOIN plan p ON p.plan_code = s.plan_code
         WHERE s.tenant_id = p_tenant
           AND s.status IN ('trialling','active'))
    || tenant_defaults
  FROM tenant t
 WHERE t.tenant_id = p_tenant
   AND t.status = 'active'
$fn$;

-- The feature check, and the two ways it is done wrong. The first is checking
-- -- a key that does not exist, which in JSON is null, and null is falsy in most
-- -- languages and is therefore accidentally correct -- until a language where it
-- -- is not. The second is checking only the override and not the merged value, so
-- -- -- a feature enabled by the plan is invisible to a feature-flag library that
-- -- -- reads only the override table.
--
-- if !enabled(config, "sso") { return ErrNotEntitled }
--   where enabled() reads the RESOLVED value, and every code path that gates a
--   feature uses the same enabled() and the same resolved value. One function, one
--   source, one place for a test to assert.

-- Quotas, which are a counter and therefore a different problem from
-- -- configuration. A quota check is read-modify-write, and read-modify-write on
-- -- -- a shared counter is a contention point at exactly the scale where the quota
-- -- -- matters. The two workable approaches:
--
-- 1. Token bucket in Redis. INCRBY plus a TTL on the bucket key, atomic, O(1),
--    -- and the counter is not durable, which is acceptable because a quota reset on
--    -- -- restart is a rounding error against a monthly allowance.
--
--   localKey := fmt.Sprintf("quota:%d:api:%s", tenantID, period)   // period = 202609
--   n, err := rdb.IncrBy(ctx, localKey, 1).Result()
--   if n == 1 { rdb.Expire(ctx, localKey, 40*24*time.Hour) }   // set the window once
--   if n > plan.ApiCallQuota {
--       // Soft limit first: a 429 with Retry-After and the reset time, and a
--       // webhook to the account team. Hard-failing a customer before you have
--       // told anyone is a support incident, not a quota.
--       return ErrQuotaExceeded, resetAt(ctx, localKey)
--   }
--
-- 2. Pre-allocated tokens. The database issues a fixed number of tokens per
--    -- period and the caller spends one. No read-modify-write at request time,
--    -- -- -- strict enforcement, and the enforcement is a single atomic decrement.
--    -- -- The cost is that the allocation must be reconciled, and that a crashed
--    -- -- -- client has consumed a token it did not use.

-- The feature-flag tier that is not per-tenant at all, and its interaction with
-- -- entitlements. A launch flag says "this is on for 5% of tenants"; an
-- -- entitlement says "this tenant's plan includes it". Both must be true. The
-- -- classic bug is a rollout that enables a feature for tenants whose plan does
-- -- -- not include it, which produces a support ticket that reads "why can I see
-- -- -- -- this button if I cannot use it". The rule: the rollout selects from the set
-- -- of entitled tenants, never the other way round, and the selection is a
-- -- -- deterministic hash of the tenant identifier so a tenant does not flip
-- -- -- between requests.
--
--   inRollout := crc32.ChecksumIEEE([]byte(strconv.FormatInt(tenantID, 10))) % 1000 < 50
--   if entitled(cfg, "new-checkout") && inRollout { ... }
TierWhere it livesRead costChange costType safetyUse for
Product defaultsCode, or a versioned config fileZeroA deploy✓ compile-timeThe default for every tenant, changed rarely and deliberately
PlanA row in plan, joined from subscriptionOne indexed join, or free from the cached resolved configAn UPDATE, which is instant for all tenants on that plan — and which is why a plan change is a commercial event with a blast radius✓ JSONB with a schema, or columnsFeatures and limits sold to a tier of customers
Tenant overrideA row in tenant_overrideOne indexed lookup, or free from the cacheOne INSERT; instant for one tenant and nobody else✗ JSON unless validatedSales-driven exceptions, trials, grandfathering, temporary enablement
Feature flagAn external service, or a rollout tableA network hop, or a cached local evaluationImmediate, without a deploy✗Progressive rollout and kill switches — never entitlements
Runtime stateRedis, or a counter tableO(1)Per requestn/aQuotas, rate limits, in-flight counters

Resolve the whole configuration once per request, not per feature check. A dashboard that checks eleven entitlements produces eleven reads of the same resolved value if the resolution is not hoisted, and eleven of them will hit the database on a cache miss. Resolve once at the start of the request into an immutable value, pass it down, and make it impossible to read the raw tables from a request handler at all. This is the same discipline as the tenant context from section 13.3, and it generalises: request-scoped state is resolved at the boundary and is thereafter read-only. Everything that must change mid-request is a bug, and everything that requires a mid-request change to be correct is a design problem to solve with an explicit refresh rather than with a global.

13.5 The missing predicate, noisy neighbours and rate limits

Two failure modes distinguish a shared schema from a dedicated one, and neither is prevented by the data model. The first is a query that omits the tenant predicate and reads or writes across every tenant. The second is a tenant whose access pattern consumes a disproportionate share of the shared resources and degrades the service for everyone.

The missing-predicate bug is the more serious of the two and the harder to find, because it is invisible in review, invisible in testing if the fixtures contain one tenant, and invisible in production until the row count is large enough for it to matter. The defences are layered and each covers a different failure:

Layer 1 - structural: the predicate is in the engine
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Every tenant-scoped table has FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY (13.2). A query without
  the predicate returns only the current tenant's rows. A write without it raises.
  This is the only defence that holds for a query nobody reviewed.

Layer 2 — structural: the composite foreign key
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  FKs include tenant_id, so a cross-tenant reference is impossible. This stops the
  write-side of the bug even where RLS is not in force (MySQL).

Layer 3 — static: the test
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  A linter rule or a code-review checklist item: "does every query on a tenant-scoped
  table include tenant_id?" Enforceable, and routinely bypassed by a query built
  with a string template that the linter cannot see.

Layer 4 — dynamic: the integration test
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Seed two tenants. Run the whole application against one. Assert that no response
  anywhere contains the other tenant's fixture data. This catches Layer-3 gaps
  without needing to find them by inspection, and it is the single highest-value
  test in a multi-tenant system — because it is the only one that does not require
  the defect to have been imagined.

  Which layer catches it, and what each one costs:

    ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────┐
    │ 1 engine     │ 2 constraint │ 3 review     │ 4 test       │ 5 monitor   │
    ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┤
    │ RLS policy,  │ composite FK │ checklist    │ two tenants, │ plan filter │
    │ FORCE, per   │ carrying     │ item, lint   │ whole suite  │ over the    │
    │ role         │ tenant_id    │ rule         │              │ statements  │
    ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┤
    │ absolute     │ absolute     │ advisory     │ strong       │ strong      │
    │ holds for a  │ holds for a  │ needs a      │ needs no     │ needs a     │
    │ query nobody │ query nobody │ human to     │ human to     │ human to    │
    │ imagined     │ imagined     │ look         │ look         │ look        │
    └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────┘

  Layers 1 and 2 are structural and hold for a query nobody imagined. Layers 3 and
  5 assume somebody was looking, and a human looking is the resource that a
  deadline consumes first. Layer 4 is the only one that scales with the number of
  queries, which is why it is the one to build first and why its test count is a
  number worth reporting.

Layer 5 — dynamic: the detector
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  A query-monitor that flags any statement touching a tenant-scoped table whose
  plan does not contain a tenant restriction. This is sound in PostgreSQL because
  the RLS policy's predicate appears in the plan as a Filter, and a tenant-scoped
  access path without one is a query that scanned the whole table.

-- The detector, Layer 5, in its simplest useful form: sample
-- -- auto_explain, log every plan that touches a tenant table and took
-- -- more than 200 ms, and alert when no tenant restriction is present.
--
-- In postgresql.conf:
--   shared_preload_libraries = 'auto_explain, pg_stat_statements'
--   auto_explain.log_min_duration = '200ms'
--   auto_explain.log_analyze = off          -- ANALYZE executes the statement
--   auto_explain.log_buffers = on
--   auto_explain.log_format = text
--   auto_explain.sample_rate = 0.05          -- 5%: full logging is a DoS vector
--   auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on

--
-- The query it feeds. This is a nightly check rather than a live one, because
-- -- a nightly check is a list you can work through and a live check is an alert
-- -- -- you learn to ignore.
WITH tenant_scoped AS (
    SELECT c.oid::regclass AS relname, true AS rls_forced
      FROM pg_class c
      JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace
     WHERE c.relrowsecurity AND c.relforcerowsecurity
       AND n.nspname = 'public'
)
SELECT q.queryid, q.calls, q.mean_exec_time, left(q.query, 110) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_statements q, tenant_scoped t
 WHERE q.query LIKE '%' || t.relname::text || '%'
   AND q.query NOT ILIKE '%tenant_id%'
   AND q.query NOT ILIKE '%app.tenant_id%'
   AND q.query NOT ILIKE 'SET LOCAL%'
   AND q.query NOT ILIKE '%pg_stat%'
 ORDER BY q.mean_exec_time DESC
 LIMIT 50;
--
-- The output is a work list, and every row is either a real bug or a false
-- -- positive to explain. Both outcomes are worth the query: the false positive is a
-- -- documented exception and the true positive is a breach that did not yet
-- -- reach a user. Note the pattern in the LIKE clauses -- the query text is the
-- -- -- normalised statement, and a statement whose tenant predicate comes from a
-- -- -- -- SET inside a transaction is invisible in it, which is why the RLS
-- -- -- policy's own Filter is the reliable signal and the text match is only a
-- -- -- screen.

-- Noisy neighbour: the three resource classes a tenant can exhaust, and the
-- -- control for each. The third is the one that is hardest and the one that
-- -- actually causes the outage.
--
-- 1. CPU and I/O through query volume. Control: rate limit and quota (13.4).
--    Effectiveness: good. A tenant cannot exceed its budget.
--
-- 2. Storage through data volume. Control: a quota on rows and a partition, and
--    the 4.4 hot-tenant problem. Effectiveness: partial — a tenant within its row
--    -- quota can still produce a plan that scans them all.
--
-- 3. Lock contention and long transactions. Control: statement_timeout,
--    -- lock_timeout, idle_in_transaction_session_timeout, and a
--    -- per-tenant concurrency cap on the pool. Effectiveness: the only one of the
--    -- three that requires no cooperation from the tenant.
--
-- The per-tenant concurrency cap, which is the single most effective control:
-- -- PgBouncer userlist-based pool sizing gives each tenant its own pool and its
-- -- -- own queue, so a tenant with 400 concurrent requests occupies its own 20
-- -- -- connections and the other 99 tenants are unaffected.
--
-- [databases]
-- saas = host=primary.internal dbname=saas pool_size=200 auth_user=saas_rw
--
-- ; auth_query: one row per user, and pool_size on that row is the tenant cap.
-- ; This is the mechanism by which a shared schema gets per-tenant resource
-- ; -- isolation, and it is the reason a database-per-tenant deployment is not
-- ; -- required to get isolation at all.
-- auth_query = SELECT usename, passwd, pool_size, pool_mode FROM
--             pgbouncer.get_auth($1)
-- auth_user = saas_auth
--
-- SET SESSION statement_timeout per tenant, applied at login. This is the
-- -- single most effective statement in a multi-tenant database, and it is one
-- -- line. Without it, one tenant's report is a table scan that runs for an hour
-- -- and holds a snapshot that blocks vacuum on every table it touched.
--
--   SET statement_timeout = '30s';
--   SET lock_timeout = '3s';
--   SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s';
--   -- SQL Server: SET LOCK_TIMEOUT 3000; and a per-tenant resource pool,
--   -- -- which is the one place SQL Server offers true per-tenant governance:
--   -- CREATE RESOURCE POOL rp_tenant_max (MAX_CPU_PERCENT 20, MAX_MEMORY_PERCENT 10);
--   -- CREATE WORKLOAD GROUP wg_tenant;
--   -- CREATE USER LOGIN app_t412 WITH DEFAULT WORKLOAD GROUP = wg_tenant;
--   -- -- and the login is the tenant's own, which is a connection-per-credential
--   -- -- cost paid for real isolation.

-- The observable, and the alert that means something. Median latency across all
-- -- tenants is useless: a single tenant at p99 for six hours is averaged away
-- -- -- by ten thousand tenants that are fine. The metric is the per-tenant p99,
-- -- -- and the alert fires on the spread, not the level.
--
--   SELECT tenant_id,
--          percentile_cont(0.99) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY duration_ms) AS p99,
--          count(*)
--     FROM request_metrics
--    WHERE ts > now() - interval '5 minutes'
--    GROUP BY tenant_id
--    HAVING count(*) > 1000
--    ORDER BY p99 DESC LIMIT 20;
--
-- Alert: a tenant whose p99 exceeds 5x the global p99 for 10 minutes. That is a
-- -- specific, actionable, customer-identifiable signal, and it is what you want to
-- -- receive at 3am rather than "p99 is up".

Resource isolation in a shared schema is a policy, and the policy needs a place to live. The three settings above — statement_timeout, lock_timeout and the per-tenant pool cap — are the whole mechanism, and they are applied at connection time from a table that the application reads. Which means the enforcement point is the same place as the tenant resolution, which means it is worth putting all four in one place: a TenantSession that resolves the tenant, sets the context, sets the timeouts, sizes the pool and validates the entitlement, and that no request handler can be constructed without going through.
