Every one of those four is a control, and four controls implemented in four places is three controls, because the fourth will be the one that is missing in the code path written under deadline.

13.6 Export, offboarding, migration and per-tenant keys

The four operations that a multi-tenant system must support and that are always deferred: giving a tenant its data, removing a tenant completely, moving a tenant between isolation models, and holding a key that only the tenant’s own administrators can use. Each is a project, and each becomes an emergency the first time a customer asks for it.

-- Export. The requirements are unhelpfully specific and every one of them is a
-- -- design input: complete, in an open format, portable to the customer's own
-- -- systems, without our identifiers, and delivered for data that is larger than
-- -- a memory buffer and a request timeout.
--
-- The shape: an object-store prefix per export, one file per table as CSV, one
-- -- manifest, signed download URLs with a short expiry, and a job with a
-- -- resumable cursor. The keyset loop from 9.6, not OFFSET, and committed per
-- -- batch so the export does not hold a transaction for its duration.
--
--   cur := startKey
--   for {
--       rows := SELECT * FROM sales_order
--                 WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND order_id > cur
--                 ORDER BY order_id LIMIT 10000
--       if len(rows) == 0 { break }
--       write(rows)            // append to s3://exports/412/<uuid>/sales_order.csv
--       cur = rows[len(rows)-1].order_id
--       // Not a consistent snapshot: rows inserted during the export with a higher
--       // order_id ARE included, and rows deleted during it are NOT. State that in the
--       // manifest. A snapshot would hold a transaction open for the whole export
--       // and block vacuum, which is worse than a slightly inconsistent export.
--   }
--
-- The audit export is a different problem and is driven by a law rather than a
-- -- customer: a regulator or a law-enforcement request, with a legal hold, a
-- -- chain of custody, and a format nobody specified. The audit_event table is
-- -- append-only by design here, which is what makes it exportable at all.

-- Offboarding. Twenty-four steps, and the reason it is written out in full is
-- -- that the three systems that are not in the database are the three that are
-- -- -- forgotten. Run it as a job with a state machine, never as a script, and
-- -- -- make every step idempotent so a re-run after a failure is safe.
--
--  1. Freeze: status = 'offboarding'. All writes rejected, reads still served.
--  2. Final export to the tenant's designated address, and a written confirmation.
--  3. Revoke every credential: sessions, API keys, OAuth grants, mTLS certs,
--     -- SSO connections, webhooks, and the per-tenant encryption key's grant.
--  4. Erase or export the caches and search indexes (12.4).
--  5. Erase the data in the operational database, in the child-first order (12.3).
--  6. Erase or anonymise in the warehouse, in object storage, and in the logs.
--  7. Wait for confirmed replica lag of zero on every node.
--  8. Destroy the per-tenant encryption key, and record the key's ARN and the fact
--    -- of destruction as an audit event. With a cloud KMS, ScheduleKeyDeletion
--    -- -- with a 7–30 day window, so a mistake is recoverable for a stated period.
--  9. Wait for the backup expiry window, and report the date (12.6).
-- 10. Remove the PgBouncer user, the database role, the schema, the tenant's pool.
-- 11. Drop the tenant's partitions, reclaiming the storage immediately (12.2).
-- 12. Write a tombstone (12.6) and a final audit event with a row count, and
--    -- retain the tenant's slug in the tombstone so that the identifier is not
--    -- -- reusable and so the erasure is evidenced.

-- Promoting a tenant from shared to dedicated. The three-phase pattern from
-- 8.3 applied to a table, and the routing change is the part with no rollback.
--
-- Deploy 1: create the destination and copy. A dump-and-restore for a small
-- -- tenant, or a pg_dump -Fc | pg_restore for a large one. COPY, not logical
-- -- -- replication: a one-off transfer, and logical replication of a single
-- -- -- tenant's rows needs a publication with a WHERE clause, which is a
-- -- -- mechanism for a permanent subscription.
-- --
-- --   pg_dump -Fc -t sales_order -t sales_order_line --filter 'tenant_id=412'
--   pg_restore -d tenant_412 --clean --if-exists
-- --
-- -- Then run a reconciliation query and require zero differences before proceeding.
-- -- This is the step that is skipped and it is the step that catches a dump that
-- -- -- silently omitted a row.
--
-- Deploy 2: dual-write. The application writes to the shared table and to the
-- -- dedicated database, and reads from the shared one. The write is idempotent on
-- -- -- the destination's own key, so a retry is safe.
--
-- Deploy 3: switch reads. The routing table gains a row and the switch is atomic.
-- -- Both schemas remain valid and the dual-write continues, so the switch is
-- -- reversible for the length of the rollback window.
--
-- Deploy 4: stop the shared-table writes for that tenant, wait for the lag to
-- -- confirm zero, then drop the rows from the shared table. This is the point of no
-- -- return and it should be the only irreversible step.
--
-- Deploy 5: remove the dual-write path, one deploy later. Removing it in the same
-- -- deploy as the switch means a rollback re-enables a write path with a table
-- -- -- whose data is now behind the dedicated copy.

--
-- The routing table, which is the whole of the multi-model abstraction. One row
-- -- per tenant says where its data is, and every read and write path consults it.
-- -- The cache is per-process with a short TTL and is invalidated by the same
-- -- -- outbox event that performs the promotion.
CREATE TABLE tenant_placement (
    tenant_id      bigint PRIMARY KEY,
    placement      text NOT NULL,     -- 'shared' | 'schema' | 'database'
    locator        text NOT NULL,     -- schema name, or dsn
    state          text NOT NULL,     -- 'stable'|'dual-write'|'migrating'
    promoted_at    timestamptz,
    CHECK (placement IN ('shared','schema','database'))
);
--
-- The reason this table exists rather than a configuration file: a migration of
-- -- one tenant is a data change with a long-lived consequence, and a config file
-- -- -- does not have a transaction, a history, or an audit trail. This one does.
--
-- Per-tenant encryption keys. The honest ladder of options, in increasing
-- -- isolation and increasing operational cost:
--
-- 1. Application-layer envelope encryption. A key per tenant in a KMS; the data key
--    -- is wrapped by the tenant's key; the ciphertext lives in a normal column.
--    -- Everything works: indexes, queries, backups. The plaintext exists in the
--    -- -- database, so a database compromise reveals it, and a query on an
--    -- -- encrypted column is a decrypt, which is why only a few columns are
--    -- -- encrypted and never one that is searched or sorted.
--
-- 2. Transparent data encryption with a per-tenant key. MySQL's
--    -- encryption_key_id per tablespace, and a per-tenant tablespace; PostgreSQL's
--    -- LUKS dm-crypt on a per-tenant tablespace mounted at the OS level. The
--    -- -- plaintext is never on disk unencrypted, and the database cannot read it,
--    -- -- which also means no backup is readable without the key.
--
-- 3. Per-tenant database on a separately encrypted volume. Isolated by a storage
--    -- boundary rather than an application boundary, and the only option that
--    -- -- survives a database-administrator-level compromise for tenants that
--    -- -- need it to.
--
-- The KMS integration, and the three properties that matter. Key creation per
-- -- tenant is one API call and is fine to 5 000 tenants. Key *rotation* is the
-- -- problem: a data key is wrapped at write time and unwrapped at read time, so
-- -- rotating the wrapping key without re-wrapping every data key makes the older
-- -- -- data unreadable. Re-wrapping is a batch job over the data-key table with a
-- -- -- dual-key read window, and it is the same exercise as a schema migration
-- -- -- with the same discipline: expand, migrate, contract.
--
--   -- AWS KMS, and the three calls that matter:
--   CreateKey(KeyUsage='ENCRYPT_DECRYPT', Tags=[{Key='tenant',Value='412'}])
--   GenerateDataKey(KeyId=cmk, KeySpec='AES_256')   -- returns plaintext + ciphertext
--     -- store only the ciphertext; use the plaintext to encrypt the field and discard it
--   ScheduleKeyDeletion(KeyId=cmk, PendingWindowInDays=30)
--     -- 7–30 days. The window is what makes an accidental erasure recoverable,
--     -- -- and it is why step 8 of offboarding is not irreversible.

Offboarding that leaves a key, a cache or a warehouse row is not offboarding, and each of those is a separate system with a separate owner. The database row is the easy part. The hard parts are: the per-tenant encryption key, which by design nothing else can erase; the warehouse copy, which a nightly job re-populates from a change stream if the stream’s retention window has not closed; the search index, which has no expiry of its own; the CDN cache, which serves a cached page to an unauthenticated edge until its TTL; and the log files, which are usually on somebody else’s retention policy. The mitigation that works is not a checklist — checklists are for the three systems the author knew about — it is the personal-data register from section 12.4, because a register that is diffed against reality in a scheduled job finds the system nobody remembered, and the diff is a query.

Exercises 13

  1. Enable row-level security on a multi-tenant schema and prove each of the four ways it can be silently ineffective: without FORCE, without WITH CHECK, through a view owned by a superuser, and through a role with BYPASSRLS. For each, write the test that fails, then fix it and show the test passing. The point of the exercise is the four failing tests, which is what the guarantee actually rests on.
  2. Seed two tenants with distinguishable fixture data and run the entire application’s test suite against one of them, asserting that no response contains the other tenant’s data. Count the failures — every one is a missing tenant predicate — and convert them into the static-analysis rules and the code-review checklist items that would have caught them.
  3. Build the tenant-resolution ladder from section 13.3, including the reserved-slug constraint, and test the attack cases: a cross-tenant X-Tenant-Id header with a valid token for another tenant, a host header naming a reserved slug, a path segment naming a slug that does not exist, and a token whose tenant claim disagrees with the resolved tenant. Each must fail closed with a specific error, and each test must assert the specific error rather than merely a non-200.
  4. Simulate a noisy neighbour. Give one tenant a query that scans its whole dataset, run it alongside traffic from fifty other tenants, and measure the p99 of the other fifty. Then apply each of the three controls from section 13.5 individually and re-measure: rate limit, statement_timeout, and a per-tenant pool cap. Produce the three numbers and identify which control bought the most.
  5. Implement the dual-write and switch procedure for a tenant promotion, and then perform it in the wrong order — switch reads before reconciliation, drop the shared rows before the lag is confirmed zero — and record what breaks in each case. Then run the correct order and measure the total elapsed time and the point at which the rollback window closed.
  6. Add the per-tenant encryption key lifecycle to a schema: key creation at provisioning, dual-key read during rotation, and scheduled deletion at offboarding. Then attempt to read a tenant’s encrypted column after offboarding, using a copy of the backup taken before it, and confirm that the data is unrecoverable. That last step is the only one that proves the design.

14 · Reliability, Observability and Failure Modes

14.1 Stating an SLO the database can be held to14.2 The failure catalogue14.3 What to instrument, and which engine answers what14.4 Slow query logging: sampled versus full14.5 Connection pooling and the prepared-statement trap14.6 Backup is not a restore, and the 3am runbook

Reliability is a property of the whole system and the database is where it is most often lost, because the database is the component whose failure is silent until it is total. This chapter covers the SLO, the failure catalogue with the engine-specific views that detect each entry, what to log and at what sampling rate, the two connection-pooling defects that account for a large fraction of production incidents, and the capacity arithmetic that has to be done before the disk is full.

14.1 Stating an SLO the database can be held to

An SLO for a database-backed system is a statement about the database’s behaviour under load, not about its uptime. A database that is up and rejecting connections at capacity has 100 % uptime and zero availability, and an uptime metric cannot tell the difference.

The useful SLO is composed from four numbers, and each is measurable from the engine’s own instrumentation with no additional tooling. Latency, expressed as the proportion of requests under a threshold, because a mean hides the users who are suffering. Throughput, expressed as commits per second and as queries per second, because a system can be slow at any volume and fast at none. Error rate, and the classification matters — a timeout is a capacity signal and a serialization failure is a concurrency signal, and treating them as one number is why error-rate alerts are set to thresholds that are either useless or deaf. And connection utilisation, which on PostgreSQL is max_connections and on SQL Server is the connection count against the service’s memory allocation, and which is the single best leading indicator there is.

LayerIndicatorDefinitionMeasured byWhy this layer
SystemRequest success ratioProportion of requests returning a correct 2xx within 2 s, over a 28-day windowLoad balancer or APM, per routeThe only number the business can act on. Everything below it is a diagnostic
SystemLatency at three percentilesp50, p99, p99.9 of end-to-end request durationAPM. p99.9 is the one that correlates with support ticketsp50 tells you about the typical user; p99.9 tells you about the users who write the complaints
DatabaseCommit throughputCommits per second, sustained and per-tablePostgreSQL: xact_commit delta. MySQL: Questions / Com_. SQL Server: Batch Requests/secA sudden fall with stable input means something is blocking. The graph shape matters more than the value
DatabaseQuery latency, per statement classp99 of pg_stat_statements.mean_exec_time by digest, sampledAll three engines, nativelyIsolates the regression to a statement class rather than to the system
DatabaseConnection utilisationActive + idle connections against the limit, and idle-in-transaction separatelyPostgreSQL: pg_stat_activity. MySQL: Threads_connected. SQL Server: sys.dm_exec_connectionsThe leading indicator. Connections are consumed by leaks long before throughput or latency moves, because the leak is a slow accumulation
DatabaseReplication lagByte lag divided by measured log rate, in secondsSection 11.4Directly determines the staleness a reader can observe, which is an availability property for any read-scaled system
DatabaseStorage headroomBytes free, and days of growth at the current rateEvery engine, plus the storage layerDisk full is the failure that has no partial mitigation. The alert is a projection, not a threshold
DatabaseError budgetBudget consumed = 1 − (success ratio / objective)DerivedTurns an SLO into a release policy, which is the only way an SLO changes behaviour
-- The four signals, measured once, from each engine. This block is the one to
-- -- run when something is wrong and you do not yet know what.
--
-- PostgreSQL 18. Five numbers, in the order they are most often diagnostic.
SELECT
  -- 1. Connection utilisation, with the dangerous category separated out.
  (SELECT current_setting('max_connections')::int)                             AS max_conn,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity)                                    AS total_conn,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE state = 'idle in transaction')                                 AS idle_in_txn,
  (-- An idle-in-transaction connection holds a snapshot and blocks vacuum.
  (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state = 'active'
                                          AND wait_event_type = 'Lock')                  AS waiting_on_lock,
  (-- 2. Longest-running transaction. The single most useful number in any
  (-- -- database incident: it is the usual cause of the vacuum blockage and
  (-- -- it is invisible on every other graph.
  (SELECT max(now() - xact_start) FROM pg_stat_activity
    WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL)                                       AS longest_xact,
  (-- 3. Dead tuples against the autovacuum threshold. Bloat is the
  (-- -- leading indicator of the next slow query, not a current problem.
  (SELECT sum(n_dead_tup) FROM pg_stat_user_tables)                          AS dead_tuples,
  (SELECT sum(cnt) FROM (SELECT count(*) cnt FROM pg_stat_activity
                        WHERE wait_event_type = 'IO' GROUP BY 1) s)      AS io_waiters,
  (-- 4. Checkpoint pressure: checkpoints requested versus timed. A high
  (-- -- requested count means the checkpoints are not keeping up with the
  (-- -- write rate, which is a max_wal_size problem before it is a disk problem.
  (SELECT checkpoints_timed - checkpoints_req FROM pg_stat_bgwriter)              AS cp_forced,
  (-- 5. Cache hit ratio, and the honest caveat: above 99% it tells you the
  (-- -- working set fits, and below 90% it tells you it does not. In between it
  (-- -- tells you almost nothing, and tuning on it is cargo cult.
  ROUND(100.0 * sum(heap_blks_hit) / GREATEST(sum(heap_blks_hit) + sum(heap_blks_read), 1), 2)
    AS heap_hit_pct;

--
-- MySQL 9.x. Note that Threads_connected rising with no change in Threads_running
-- -- is the pool leaking, not the database being busy.
--
--   SHOW GLOBAL STATUS WHERE Variable_name IN (
--     'Threads_connected','Threads_running','Max_used_connections','Aborted_connects',
--     'Innodb_buffer_pool_wait_free','Innodb_row_lock_waits','Innodb_row_lock_time',
--     'Innodb_log_waits','Created_tmp_disk_tables','Slow_queries','Queries','Com_stmt_prepare',
--     'Com_stmt_execute','Open_files');
--
--   -- Innodb_buffer_pool_wait_free rising: the buffer pool cannot hold the working
--   -- set and the flush thread is behind. Section 6.6, and the fix is more RAM
--   -- or a smaller working set, in that order.
--   -- Created_tmp_disk_tables: a disk sort or a disk hash join. Section 9.4.
--   -- Innodb_log_waits: the redo log is too small for the write rate, and every
--   -- -- commit is waiting. Fix innodb_redo_log_capacity before anything else.

--
-- SQL Server 2025. Three DMV queries, and they answer three different questions.
--
-- 1. What is the database waiting on, right now, by accumulated wait time.
--    sys.dm_os_wait_stats is cumulative since startup and is the correct basis
--    for a differential reading, not for a raw sort.
SELECT TOP (10) wait_type, waiting_tasks_count,
       total_wait_time_ms / 1000. AS total_wait_s,
       max_wait_time_ms,
       signal_wait_time_ms / NULLIF(total_wait_time_ms, 0) AS pct_signal
  FROM sys.dm_os_wait_stats
 WHERE wait_type NOT IN ('SLEEP_TASK','SLEEP_SYSTEMTASK','WAITFOR',
                     'CLR_SEMAPHORE','BROKER_EVENTHANDLER','TRAFFIC_GENERAL_WAITCMD')
   AND total_wait_time_ms > 0
 ORDER BY total_wait_time_ms DESC;

-- 2. Signal time versus total time. A wait type that is 95% signal time is
-- -- the runnable queue: the server is saturated and the fix is capacity or a
-- -- plan. A wait type that is 5% signal and 95% resource is external: a slow
-- -- disk, a slow network, or a slow lock. The distinction is the single most
-- -- useful number in SQL Server diagnostics and it is routinely ignored.
--
-- 3. The blocking chain, as a recursive query. sys.dm_exec_requests gives the
-- -- immediate blocker only; a chain of three is invisible without recursion.
--
-- WITH chain AS (
--   SELECT r.session_id, r.blocking_session_id, r.wait_type, r.wait_time,
--          CAST(NULL AS int) AS depth, 1 AS hops
--     FROM sys.dm_exec_requests r
--    WHERE r.blocking_session_id <> 0
--   UNION ALL
--   SELECT c.session_id, r.blocking_session_id, r.wait_type, r.wait_time,
--          c.depth + 1, c.hops + 1
--     FROM chain c
--     JOIN sys.dm_exec_requests r ON r.session_id = c.blocking_session_id
--    WHERE c.hops < 10
-- )
-- SELECT session_id, blocking_session_id, wait_type, wait_time, depth
--   FROM chain ORDER BY depth DESC;

--
-- The SLO, written as a configuration value rather than a document, so that the
-- -- alerting rules are generated from it and cannot drift from it.
CREATE TABLE slo_objective (
    service          text PRIMARY KEY,
    window_days      int NOT NULL,
    target_ratio     numeric(4,3) NOT NULL,   -- 0.999
    -- The database-level objectives, each of which is a query against a
    -- -- specific view. These are what a database engineer is paged on.
    p99_query_ms     numeric(8,1) NOT NULL,
    max_replication_lag_s numeric(8,1) NOT NULL,
    conn_utilisation_pct  numeric(4,1) NOT NULL,   -- page at 80%
    storage_headroom_days numeric(6,1) NOT NULL,  -- page at 14
    -- Fast burn and slow burn: the standard multi-window multi-burn alert, and
    -- -- the difference between a page at 3am and a page next week. A 2% budget
    -- -- consumed in 1 hour is 2%/1/24 of the monthly budget per hour, so it
    -- -- -- burns 14.4x faster than sustainable.
    fast_burn_window_mins int NOT NULL,
    fast_burn_rate       numeric(4,1) NOT NULL,     -- 14.4
    slow_burn_window_hours numeric(6,1) NOT NULL,   -- 6
    slow_burn_rate        numeric(4,2) NOT NULL     -- 1.0
);

Measure the SLO at the edge and the SLO for the database separately, and never define one in terms of the other. An end-to-end success ratio that includes the database is the right business number. A database-level objective — p99 statement latency, connection utilisation, replication lag — is the right engineering number, and the two are not the same measurement of the same thing. The reason this matters is error budgets: a 99.9 % end-to-end objective with a 99.9 % database objective is not a coherent plan, because the database contributes its own unavailability on top of everything else, and the composed objective is 99.8 %. Stating the composed number up front is what makes the budget mean something.

14.2 The failure catalogue

Eight failures account for the overwhelming majority of database incidents, and each has a specific signature, a specific detection query and a specific first action. A runbook that contains only these eight is worth more than a comprehensive one, because a comprehensive one is not read.

#FailureSignatureDetectionFirst actionPrevention
1Connection exhaustionError 53300 "too many clients" or a p99 timeout spike with a flat throughput graph. Latency rises while throughput does notPostgreSQL: count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity against max_connections. MySQL: Threads_connected / Max_used_connectionsFind the leak, not the load. A leaked connection is idle in transaction or sleep with a growing ageA pooler with max_client_conn far above the server’s limit; idle_in_transaction_session_timeout; per-route pool caps
2Lock contention and deadlockLatency rises, CPU falls. Many sessions in a wait state, none consuming CPUPostgreSQL: pg_locks joined to pg_stat_activity, or pg_blocking_pids. MySQL: data_lock_waits. SQL Server: blocking_session_idCancel the oldest blocking transaction. It is almost always the one holding a lock while doing work outside the databaseConsistent lock ordering; no user-interaction inside a transaction; lock_timeout so a waiter fails fast
3Replication lagReads are stale; writes are fine. Replica CPU and I/O near zeroByte lag divided by log rate (section 11.4)Check the split first: is the applier behind, or is the data simply being sent and not applied? They have different causesAsync within the region, monitored lag with a derived-seconds SLO, lag-aware routing
4Autovacuum starvationDead tuples grow without bound. Queries that were 50 ms become 5 s. Bloat indexes begin to fail DDL with disk errorsPostgreSQL: pg_stat_user_tables, n_dead_tup versus n_live_tup, and the autovacuum_vacuum_* *_threshold settings against the actual rate of change. MySQL: the InnoDB history list length in information_schema.innodb_metricsRun a manual VACUUM (ANALYZE) on the affected table, with a low vacuum_cost_delay so it does not itself become the loadPer-table autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor for the hot tables; a long-running transaction budget; idle_in_transaction_session_timeout
5BloatTable and index size far exceeds the data. A vacuum reduces neither. Reindex is the only curePostgreSQL: pgstattuple free space, and the ratio of pg_relation_size to n_live_tup * row_width. MySQL: information_schema.TABLES data versus index versus data-freeReindex, or pg_squeeze / VACUUM FULL in a maintenance window, and fix the causeHigher fillfactor on tables updated in random order; partition drops rather than deletes (section 12.2); avoid indexed column updates
6Statistics driftA query that was fast became slow with no schema change. The plan’s join order changedPostgreSQL: n_mod_since_analyze. MySQL: innodb_stats_persistent_sample_pages and the last update time. SQL Server: the CE feedback record and a plan change in Query StoreANALYZE the table, then re-read the plan before touching an indexExplicit ANALYZE after every bulk load and backfill; monitor the modification ratio, not just the last-analyze date
7Plan-cache pollutionMemory grows on the server; a specific statement’s latency varies by 100x depending on which parameter arrived firstPostgreSQL: pg_prepared_statements generic/custom ratio, and plan time as a fraction of total. SQL Server: recompiles per second from Query Store and the plan cache node countsApply the per-statement remedy from section 9.6: force_custom_plan or OPTION(RECOMPILE)Separate statements for the hot and cold access paths rather than one statement with an optional predicate
8Disk fullEverything stops, at once, on every engine. The error is unambiguous and there is no partial degradationBytes free projected against the growth rate, alerted as days of headroom rather than as a percentageNothing helps except freeing space. Extend the volume if the storage layer permits it — that is the one action that works at 3amRetention (chapter 12), capacity planning (14.6), and an alert at 14 days of headroom rather than at 90 % full
-- The detection queries for the four that are not caught by a general-purpose
-- -- dashboard, because each has a silent phase and each is cheap to run.
--
-- 4. Autovacuum starvation. Run hourly. The important column is the ratio, not
-- -- the absolute count, because the threshold is relative to the table.
SELECT schemaname, relname,
       n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       ROUND(n_dead_tup::numeric / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1), 3) AS dead_ratio,
       n_mod_since_analyze,
       ROUND(n_mod_since_analyze::numeric / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1), 3) AS stale_ratio,
       ROUND(pg_total_relation_size(relid) / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1)) AS bytes_per_row,
       last_autovacuum, last_autoanalyze,
       -- The four tables where vacuum is currently running. A long-running vacuum
       -- -- on a large table is normal; a long-running vacuum that is not progressing
       -- -- is blocked by a replication slot or an old snapshot, and that is a finding.
       (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_progress_vacuum v
         WHERE v.relid = c.oid) AS vacuum_running
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables c
 WHERE n_dead_tup > 10000
    OR n_dead_tup::numeric / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1) > 0.2
 ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 20;

-- The replication-slot check, which is the mechanism behind a whole class of
-- -- "the disk is full and nothing is using the space" incidents. A slot whose
-- -- consumer has gone pins WAL until the disk fills, and it does so silently.
SELECT slot_name, active, active_pid,
       CASE WHEN active
            THEN pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn))
            ELSE pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn))
        END AS -- retained for an inactive slot, this grows forever
       restart_lsn, confirmed_flush_lsn,
       ROUND(extract(epoch FROM now() - COALESCE(
              (SELECT confirmed_flush_lsn FROM pg_replication_slots r2
                WHERE r2.slot_name = s.slot_name), restart_lsn))::numeric / 3600, 1)
         AS age_hours
  FROM pg_replication_slots s
 LEFT JOIN pg_stat_replication r ON r.pid = s.active_pid
 ORDER BY pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn) DESC;
--
-- The alert: any slot retaining more than 4 GB, and any slot inactive for more
-- than an hour. Both are always wrong in a healthy system. max_slot_wal_keep_size
-- -- is the guard: it caps the damage by invalidating the slot rather than filling the
-- -- disk, and the cost of hitting it is that the subscriber needs a fresh snapshot,
-- -- -- which is a trade you should make deliberately rather than discover.
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_slot_wal_keep_size = '10GB';

-- 5. Bloat. pgstattuple is exact but slow and takes an ACCESS SHARE lock per
-- -- -- table, so it belongs in a maintenance window, not in a dashboard. The cheap
-- -- -- proxy that can run continuously:
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgstattuple;
SELECT relname,
       ROUND(tuple_percent)                                     AS tuple_pct,
       ROUND(dead_tuple_percent)                               AS dead_pct,
       pg_size_pretty(free_percent / 100 * pg_relation_size(relid))   AS free_space,
       -- A live-tuple count far below the estimate is the signature of a table that
       -- -- has been heavily updated and never vacuumed. Below 50% is a finding.
       ROUND(pg_relation_size(relid) / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1) / 1024) AS kb_per_live_tuple
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 LEFT JOIN LATERAL pgstattuple(relid) ON pg_relation_size(relid) > 100 * 1024 * 1024
 WHERE free_percent > 30
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(relid) * free_percent DESC LIMIT 20;

-- 6. Statistics drift, as a single query that names the tables at risk. The
-- -- threshold is 10% of the table changed since the last analyze, which is
-- -- higher than the default autovacuum threshold and therefore fires first.
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_mod_since_analyze,
       ROUND(100.0 * n_mod_since_analyze / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1), 1) AS pct_changed,
       ROUND(reltuples::numeric / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1), 2) AS stats_estimate_error
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 WHERE n_live_tup > 10000
   AND n_mod_since_analyze::numeric / GREATEST(n_live_tup, 1) > 0.1
 ORDER BY n_mod_since_analyze DESC LIMIT 30;
--
-- stats_estimate_error is the one to watch: reltuples is the planner's number and
-- -- n_live_tup is the count from the last vacuum. A ratio of 0.5 means the planner
-- -- -- believes the table has half the rows it has, and every cardinality estimate
-- -- -- derived from it is wrong by that factor.

--
-- 7. Plan-cache pollution, on PostgreSQL. generic_plans dominating means the
-- -- plan built for the first parameter is being reused for all of them, which is
-- -- -- correct only if the selectivity does not vary. mean_plan_time as a
-- -- fraction of mean_exec_time is the cost side of the trade.
SELECT name, generic_plans, custom_plans,
       ROUND(mean_plan_time / GREATEST(mean_plan_time + mean_exec_time, 0.0001), 3)
         AS pct_time_spent_planning
  FROM pg_prepared_statements
 WHERE generic_plans + custom_plans > 1000
 ORDER BY generic_plans DESC LIMIT 20;

--
-- 8. Disk, projected. Never alert on a percentage of a volume: a volume that
-- -- -- grows at 4 GB a day is 30 days from full at 80% and 370 days at 10%,
-- -- -- and the same percentage means something completely different on each. Alert
-- -- -- on days of headroom, and the projection uses a trailing 7-day rate so
-- -- -- that a bulk load does not produce a permanently pessimistic forecast.
--
--   WITH usage AS (
--     SELECT date_trunc('day', ts) AS d, sum(bytes_used) AS used
--       FROM storage_daily WHERE ts > now() - interval '14 days'
--      GROUP BY 1
--   ), rate AS (
--     SELECT (max(used) - min(used)) / 7.0 AS bytes_per_day FROM usage
--   )
--   SELECT round((capacity_bytes - max(used)) / NULLIF(bytes_per_day, 0)) AS days_headroom
--     FROM usage, rate;
--
-- The number that has to be on the same graph: how much of the growth is
-- -- retention-driven. A table whose growth is entirely inside its retention
-- -- window will plateau when the window is full; one that is not will not, and the
-- -- -- difference is a 3am conversation with the retention job's owner.

Failure 1 and failure 4 are the same failure wearing different clothes, and they are both caused by a transaction that will not end. An application opens a transaction, calls an external service, and waits. The connection is idle-in-transaction, holding a snapshot that prevents vacuum from reclaiming the dead tuples in every table the transaction has touched. The dead tuples accumulate, the tables bloat, the index scans slow, and eventually the connection pool is exhausted because every request now holds a connection while it waits on a service that is itself backed by the database. The symptom at 3am is "connection exhaustion"; the cause is a transaction that started twenty minutes ago and is blocked on an HTTP call. The defences are three settings and one code rule: idle_in_transaction_session_timeout (30 s), statement_timeout (30 s), lock_timeout (3 s), and no network call inside a database transaction. The fourth is a code-review item and it is the only one of the four that requires human discipline.

14.3 What to instrument, and which engine answers what

The three engines ship a complete, cumulative, always-on view of their own behaviour, and the majority of commercial database monitoring exists to re-present that data. Installing it is a matter of turning on the right counters; the judgement is in what to alert on and what to leave for a human to query.

Question you are askingPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
What is running right now?pg_stat_activity with wait_event, xact_start and pg_blocking_pidsinformation_schema.processlist and performance_schema.threadssys.dm_exec_requests joined to sys.dm_exec_sessions
What is it waiting for?wait_event_type — Lock, IO, Client, Activity. Client means the application is the problem, and that single field resolves a surprising number of incidentsperformance_schema.events_waits_current by EVENT_NAME, and sys.innodb_lock_waitssys.dm_os_waiting_tasks by wait_type, with signal versus resource time from dm_os_wait_stats
Which statements cost the most?pg_stat_statements, keyed by normalised digest, with total and mean time, rows, and buffer countsevents_statements_summary_by_digest, with the same plus SUM_NO_INDEX_USED and SUM_SORT_MERGE_PASSESQuery Store: sys.query_store_runtime_stats and sys.query_store_plan, with wait statistics and the plan itself
What does this statement actually do?EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS), auto_explain for production capture, log_planner_stats and log_parser_stats for planning overheadEXPLAIN FORMAT=TREE, EXPLAIN ANALYZE with a rollback wrapper, and the optimiser trace with --comment and SET optimizer_traceActual execution plan from Query Store, or SET STATISTICS XML ON. The graphical plan from SSMS is a rendering of the same data
How much is being locked and waited for?pg_locks by mode and granted, and pg_stat_database.deadlocks as a deltainformation_schema.INNODB_LOCKS replaced by performance_schema.data_locks; Innodb_row_lock_waits as a countersys.dm_tran_locks, and the lock-wait counters in perf_counter under Lock Waits/sec
How much space is where, and how full?pg_database_size, pg_total_relation_size, pg_ls_dir on the volume, and the WAL directory sizeinformation_schema.TABLES for data, index and free space, and the Innodb_data_file_path file sizessys.database_files, sys.dm_db_partition_stats, and sys.dm_db_log_space_usage
What is the optimiser’s view of the data?pg_statistic and pg_stats_ext; last_analyze and last_autoanalyze per tablemysql.innodb_table_stats and innodb_index_stats, plus the histogram tables in column_statisticssys.dm_db_stats_properties for last update, and the histogram for the CE
How is the cache doing?Shared buffer hit rate from pg_statio_user_tables, plus pg_buffercache in 18 for a per-page residency viewInnodb_buffer_pool_read_requests versus Innodb_buffer_pool_reads, per instance if the pool is split by instancesys.dm_os_buffer_descriptors and the Buffer Cache Hit Ratio performance counter, which is the one counter to distrust
Is replication healthy?pg_stat_replication with LSN differences; pg_stat_subscription for logicalperformance_schema.replication_applier_status_by_coordinator and GTID set differencessys.dm_hadr_database_replica_states with the two queue sizes
What changed in the plan, and when?No built-in history. pg_stat_statements has no plan archive; a plan regression is invisible unless you captured itNo built-in plan history either, which is the strongest argument for the optimiser trace and a CI plan-diffQuery Store is the only built-in plan history on any of the three, and it is the strongest single reason to choose SQL Server for a workload with unpredictable parameter distributions
-- The instrumentation, installed. Everything here is free and always-on; the work
-- -- is in turning on the two settings that are off by default for a reason,
-- -- and in accepting the small cost they carry.
--
-- PostgreSQL 18, postgresql.conf:
--
-- shared_preload_libraries = 'pg_stat_statements, auto_explain, pgstattuple'
-- pg_stat_statements.max = 10000            -- 10k digests: 1 GB of shared memory
-- pg_stat_statements.track = top          -- 'top' skips per-node detail; 'all' is
--                                           -- dramatically more expensive and is
--                                           -- not what you want in production
-- pg_stat_statements.track_utility = off
-- pg_stat_statements.max_size = 8192          -- KB per statement; bounds the
--                                           -- per-entry memory
-- track_io_timing = on                 -- OFF BY DEFAULT. Two clock reads per
--                                           -- -- I/O wait, and roughly 1-3%
--                                           -- -- overhead. Without it you cannot
--                                           -- -- separate a slow query from a
--                                           -- -- slow disk, ever.
-- track_activity_query_size = 4096
-- log_min_duration_statement = 500   -- the slow log; 14.4
-- log_checkpoints = on
-- log_lock_waits = on                  -- lock waits over a threshold,
--                                           -- with the duration and the query
-- deadlock_timeout = 200ms            -- log a statement that waits longer
--                                           -- -- for a lock; the single most
--                                           -- -- valuable logger in the list
-- log_temp_files = 0                  -- log every temp file and its size: the
--                                           -- -- cheapest way to find the sorts
--                                           -- -- and hashes that spilled (9.4)
-- log_autovacuum_min_duration = 0        -- see every vacuum and how long it took
--
-- The one query the engine does not provide and you must build: a
-- -- wait-event histogram over time. pg_stat_activity is a snapshot and
-- -- sampling it at one-second intervals from a monitoring agent is how a
-- -- five-minute query is distinguished from a five-second one, and the
-- -- difference is the whole diagnosis.
--
--   SELECT now() AS ts, wait_event_type, wait_event, state, count(*)
--     FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE backend_type = 'client backend'
--    GROUP BY 2,3,4;

--
-- MySQL 9.x, and the one setting that is off by default and should not be:
--
-- performance_schema = ON
-- performance_schema_instrument = '%=ON'
-- performance_schema_consumers_events_statements_history_long = ON
--   -- The long history is what lets you see the full shape of a statement
--   -- -- including its waits, per execution. OFF by default. Costly, and worth it.
-- performance_schema_consumers_statements_history = ON
-- performance_schema_consumers_events_statements_current = ON
-- table_statements_summary = ON
-- statements_performance_schema = ON        -- statements to the performance
--                                           -- -- schema rather than to slow log
-- slow_query_log = ON
-- long_query_time = 0.2
-- log_queries_not_using_indexes = OFF     -- ON is noise: it fires on every
--                                           -- -- legitimate full scan, which on
--                                           -- -- a reporting workload is all of them
-- min_examined_row_limit = 1000       -- pair it with the previous setting if
--                                           -- -- you want "scans of a big table
--                                           -- -- with no index" and nothing else
-- innodb_stats_persistent = ON
-- innodb_stats_persistent_sample_pages = 64  -- the accuracy/frequency trade; 64 is
--                                           -- -- the default, 512 is accurate and
--                                           -- -- -- expensive at ANALYZE time only
--
-- What to sample from performance_schema, and the sampling rate question,
-- -- -- answered by the same arithmetic as the slow log (14.4):
--
--   SELECT event_name, count_star, sum_timer_wait/1e12 AS total_s,
--          ROUND(avg_timer_wait/1e9, 2) AS avg_ms
--     FROM performance_schema.events_waits_summary_global_by_event_name
--    WHERE count_star > 0
--    ORDER BY sum_timer_wait DESC LIMIT 20;
--
--   -- The four that matter on every system, and what each one means:
--   --   wait/io/file/innodb/...      storage. High with high queue depth = the disk
--   --   wait/synchro/...              latch and lock internals. Rising with a flat
--   --   --                             throughput = a hot row or a hot index page
--   --   wait/lock/.../innodb_row_lock  row lock waits: section 14.2 failure 2
--   --   stage/sql/...                 time in the optimiser, not the executor

--
-- SQL Server 2025, and the DMVs that are worth their overhead.
--
-- ALTER SERVER CONFIGURATION SET XML SHOWPLAN_XML ON;   -- query store captures plans
-- ALTER SERVER CONFIGURATION SET SHOWPLAN_XML ALL ON;   -- live plans in sys.dm_exec_query_stats
-- ALTER DATABASE SCOPED CONFIGURATION SET MAXDOP = 0 PARALLEL_QUERY_WAIT = 0;
-- ALTER DATABASE saas SET QUERY_STORE = ON (
--     WAIT_STATS_CAPTURE = ON,          -- the reason to use it: wait statistics
--     --                                 per query, retained across restarts
--     QUERY_CAPTURE_MODE = AUTO,         -- captures the queries that actually
--     --                                 matter, and lets rare ones go rather
--     --                                 than evicting useful ones
--     MAX_PLANS_PER_QUERY = 20,
--     MAX_STORAGE_SIZE_MB = 2048,
--     CLEANUP_POLICY = (STALE_QUERY_THRESHOLD_DAYS = 30));
--
-- The three wait-time questions the DMVs answer, each in one query:
--
-- 1. What has the most total wait? dm_os_wait_stats, differentially (above).
-- 2. What has the most signal time? The same, sorted by signal_wait_time_ms --
--    this is the "the server is saturated" view.
-- 3. What has the most recent waits? dm_exec_query_stats.total_worker_time,
--    which is per query and is the one to graph over time.
--
--   SELECT TOP (20) qs.total_worker_time/1000. AS cpu_ms, qs.execution_count,
--          qs.total_elapsed_time/1000. AS elapsed_ms,
--          CAST(pa.value AS NVARCHAR(4000))
--     FROM sys.dm_exec_query_stats qs
--     CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(qs.sql_handle) st
--     CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_query_plan(qs.plan_handle) pa
--    ORDER BY qs.total_worker_time DESC;
--
-- The ratio of elapsed to CPU is the plan's efficiency. 1.0 is CPU-bound and
-- -- -- -- optimal. Above 10 the query is waiting for something and the thing is
-- -- -- -- usually I/O or a lock. This one ratio replaces a large amount of
-- -- -- -- plan reading, and it is the first number to look at in any incident.
--
--   SELECT qs.execution_count,
--          CAST(qs.total_elapsed_time * 1.0 / NULLIF(qs.total_worker_time,0) AS DECIMAL(6,2))
--            AS elapsed_per_cpu
--     FROM sys.dm_exec_query_stats qs
--    WHERE qs.total_worker_time > 1000
--    ORDER BY qs.total_worker_time DESC;

Instrument the wait, not the duration. Elapsed time answers "how long"; wait statistics answer "doing what instead of working". A query that takes four seconds and shows 3.9 seconds of ClientRead wait is not a database problem at all — the application is slow to send the next statement, usually because it is waiting on something else, and the database is the only component that can prove it. A query that takes four seconds and shows 3.9 seconds of IO wait is a storage or buffer-pool problem. A query that takes four seconds and shows 3.9 seconds of Lock wait is a concurrency problem. All three present to the user as the same 4-second timeout, and only wait statistics distinguish them, which is why enabling them is the first thing to do and why SQL Server’s WAIT_STATS_CAPTURE has no equivalent that is as cheap on the other two.

14.4 Slow query logging: sampled versus full

Full slow-query logging is unbounded, and the mechanism that makes it unbounded is the same one that makes it valuable: a statement that takes 30 seconds and is called 40 000 times an hour generates 40 000 log lines an hour, and a database under incident load is exactly when that happens.

The two failure modes are asymmetric. Under-logging means the incident you are diagnosing has no evidence. Over-logging means the logging itself becomes the load — a full plan or a stack of text for every statement over the threshold, at a rate proportional to the load, on the same machine that is already saturated. The right answer is a tiered design: cheap always-on summaries, expensive sampled detail, and a targeted capture that switches on during an incident rather than running continuously.

-- The tiered design, and the arithmetic that sets the thresholds. Three tiers,
-- -- three costs, and the middle one is the only one that is a judgement call.
--
--
-- Tier 1: aggregate, always on, essentially free. pg_stat_statements on every
-- -- engine already provides this: per digest, total and mean time, call count,
-- -- rows. It answers "which statements cost the most" for the entire window at no
-- -- per-execution cost. This is the tier that finds 95% of problems and it is
-- -- tier 1 in the table in 14.3, not in this one.
--
-- Tier 2: plan capture, sampled, the default choice. Capture the plan for 1 in N
-- -- statements over a duration threshold. The sample rate is chosen so that the
-- -- -- expected number of captured plans per day is in the hundreds, not the tens
-- -- -- of thousands: for a statement called 20 000 times a day, 1% is 200 plans,
-- -- -- 0.1% is 20, and 20 is too few to see a plan flip back and forth.
--
-- PostgreSQL, auto_explain with a sample rate:
--
--   auto_explain.log_min_duration = '500ms'
--   auto_explain.log_analyze = off          -- log_analyze = true EXECUTES the
--   --                                           -- statement to time it. In
--   --                                           -- -- production that is a second
--   --                                           -- -- execution of every slow
--   --                                           -- -- query. It is a debugging
--   --                                           -- -- tool, not a monitoring one.
--   auto_explain.log_buffers = on           -- with analyze=off, buffers are
--   --                                           -- the only per-node data available,
--   --                                           -- and they are the important part
--   auto_explain.log_timing = off
--   auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on
--   auto_explain.sample_rate = 0.02         -- 2%, and it is a per-execution
--   --                                           -- coin flip, not a modulo, so it
--   --                                           -- does not alias with a periodic
--   --                                           -- -- load pattern
--   auto_explain.log_format = json          -- machine-readable, and the
--   --                                           -- format the plan-diff in CI wants
--
-- MySQL 9.x, and the important subtlety that its slow log is not sampled --
-- -- it logs every statement over the threshold, and the only sampling available is
-- -- the min_examined_row_limit filter, which is a different thing entirely.
--   --
--   slow_query_log = ON
--   long_query_time = 0.5
--   log_slow_rate_limit = 100       -- MySQL 8.0.26+
--   --                                    -- the real sampling control: no more
--   --                                    -- than 1 log entry per 100 ms.
--   --                                    -- At 100ms it bounds the logging cost
--   log_slow_extra = ON                  -- includes rows examined and rows
--   --                                    -- sent, which is the shape ratio from 9.1
--   --                                    -- and is the single most useful column
--   log_slow_admin_statements = ON
--   min_examined_row_limit = 1000
--
-- SQL Server 2025: Query Store IS the sampled plan store, and it is better than
-- -- anything the other two offer, because it retains the plan of every distinct
-- -- plan for every query, keeps them across restarts, and lets you force a plan.
--   -- AUTO capture mode samples by frequency rather than by duration, and a query
--   -- that is slow 0.1% of the time may never be captured. The fix for a query
--   -- you already suspect is QUERY_CAPTURE_MODE = ALL, per query, temporarily.
--   --
--   ALTER DATABASE saas SET QUERY_STORE CLEAR ALL;
--   ALTER DATABASE saas SET QUERY_CAPTURE_MODE = ALL;
--   -- ... reproduce ...
--   --

--
-- Tier 3: targeted capture, switched on during an incident. This is the tier that
-- -- gets the plan you actually need, and it is switched on by a human who has
-- -- -- already decided which statement they are looking at.
--
-- PostgreSQL, the surgical version: log the plan for ONE query, matching on
-- -- text, without log_analyze. Note the anchor -- the leading whitespace -- which
-- -- stops the pattern matching every query containing the word.
--
--   ALTER SYSTEM SET session_preload_libraries = 'auto_explain';
--   -- in a single session:
--   SET auto_explain.log_min_duration = '100ms';
--   SET auto_explain.log_analyze = on;      -- safe HERE: one statement, one
--   SET auto_explain.log_buffers = on;       -- -- extra execution, and you have
--   SET auto_explain.log_format = json;      -- -- chosen the statement
--   SET auto_explain.log_nested_statements = on;
--   -- then run the suspect statement repeatedly

--
-- The plan-diff, in CI, which is the one that catches a regression before it
-- -- reaches production. Store the plan as JSON, normalise the volatile parts
-- -- -- -- (actual times, row counts, buffer counts, node costs), and compare the
-- -- structure: a change of node type, a new join algorithm, a lost index scan.
-- -- -- -- A plan that is 10% slower is not a finding; a plan that changed shape
-- -- -- -- is.
--
-- The four things to normalise before diffing, and each of them is a false
-- -- positive if forgotten:
--
--   Actual Total Time, Actual Rows, Actual Loops       -- vary with load
--   Shared Hit / Read, Dirtied / Written              -- vary with cache state
--   Startup Cost, Total Cost, Plan Rows                -- are the planner's own, and
--                                                  -- vary with statistics
--   Planning Time, Triggers                            -- environment
--
-- What survives normalisation and is therefore the signal:
--
--   Node Type                          Seq Scan -> Index Scan: a real change
--   Index Name, Index Cond, Filter      the index that is being chosen
--   Join Type, Strategy                 Hash Join -> Nested Loop: a real change
--   Sort Method, GroupAggregate vs      a memory decision
--     HashAggregate
--   Parent-Child Relationships          the join tree shape
--   Sort Key, Group Key                 the ORDER BY resolution
--
-- And the rule for the CI gate: a plan-shape change on a statement classified as
-- -- critical fails the build and requires a human to approve the new plan. This is
-- -- -- the only mechanism that reliably catches "it got slow because of a
-- -- -- PostgreSQL upgrade" or "it got slow because the data reached a new
-- -- -- cardinality threshold", both of which are invisible in every other test.

--
-- The three findings worth a whole pipeline, in the order they bite:
--
--   • the join algorithm changed      a cardinality threshold moved past a
--                                 boundary the planner prices differently
--   • the access method changed      a Seq Scan became an Index Scan, or the
--                                 index it chose is no longer the right one
--   • a filter replaced an index     a condition pushed from the access path
--                                 down into post-filter evaluation
--
-- A plan that is 10% slower is a metric, not a finding. A plan that changed
-- shape is a deployable event, and it is the only one of the three that
-- predicts a future incident rather than reporting a current one.

--
-- The log-retention problem, which is where slow logging actually goes wrong.
-- -- A slow log with no rotation fills a disk, and the disk is often the same
-- -- volume as the database, so a logging failure becomes failure 8 from 14.2.
-- -- Ship to a separate collector within seconds and keep the local file small.
--
-- logging_collector = on
-- log_directory = 'log'                     -- a volume with its own quota
-- log_rotation_age = '1d'
-- log_rotation_size = 256MB
-- log_truncate_on_rotation = on
-- log_statement = 'ddl'                    -- DDL always, and it is the
--                                            -- -- -- cheapest way to answer "who
--                                            -- -- -- changed the schema and when"
-- log_min_duration_statement = 500
--
-- Log retention itself is a data-retention decision (chapter 12): a slow
-- -- query log contains literal bind values on PostgreSQL and MySQL unless
-- -- log_parameter_max_length is set, and those values are frequently an email
-- -- -- address or a token. Two settings, and they are not defaults:
--
--   log_parameter_max_length = 0          -- 0 = do not log parameters at all.
--   --                                           -- The default (-1) logs them in
--   --                                           -- -- full, which is a personal-data
--   --                                           -- -- -- exposure in a file that is
--   --                                           -- -- -- shipped to a log aggregator
--   --                                           -- -- -- and retained for weeks
--   --                                           -- -- -- -- -- (12.1)
--   log_parameter_max_length_on_error = 0  -- but do log them for errors, where
--   --                                           -- -- they are the only way to
--   --                                           -- -- -- reproduce the failure
--   log_error_verbosity = default

A slow query log that records bind values is a personal-data store with none of the controls of one. On PostgreSQL and MySQL the parameterised query’s values are interpolated into the logged statement, so a log line reading WHERE tenant_id = 412 AND email = 'person@example.com' contains a personal identifier, in a file that is shipped to a log aggregator, indexed for search, retained for thirty days and accessible to everyone with log access — which on most teams is a much larger group than those with database access. Two settings fix it at negligible diagnostic cost: log_parameter_max_length = 0 for the general case, and log_parameter_max_length_on_error = 0 kept on for the one case where the values are the whole point. The residual problem is that an error’s logged values are still personal data in a log, so the log’s own retention policy becomes a data-retention obligation, and the 30-day default is once again a decision somebody else made.

14.5 Connection pooling and the prepared-statement trap

A connection pool is not a performance optimisation in the general case; it is a renaming of a connection, and the rename is where the bugs live. Two defects account for a disproportionate share of production incidents involving a pooled database, and both are consequences of treating a pooled connection as a session.

-- DEFECT 1: session state leaking between requests.
--
-- In transaction-mode pooling a server connection is handed to a different client
-- -- at every transaction boundary. Anything set on it by one client is visible to
-- -- the next. SET, SET LOCAL, temporary tables, advisory locks, prepared
-- -- statements, cursors, LISTEN registrations, and the row-level-security context
-- -- from 13.2 all belong to this list.
--
-- The three that cause incidents:
--
-- (a) app.tenant_id. SET LOCAL is transaction-scoped and so it IS correct in
-- --     transaction mode: it is discarded at COMMIT. SET -- without LOCAL --
-- --     is session-scoped and is NOT, and one <code>SET</code> without the keyword
-- --     is a cross-tenant data leak with a low-probability trigger and a
-- --     catastrophic outcome.
-- (b) A temporary table created with CREATE TEMP TABLE and no ON COMMIT DROP
-- --     persists on the server connection. The next client to receive it sees a
-- --     table it did not create, containing another tenant's rows.
-- (c) An advisory lock taken with pg_advisory_lock (session-scoped) and not
-- --     released holds it until the server connection is closed, and the pool
-- --     controls when that happens. pg_advisory_xact_lock is the correct call.
--
-- The mitigation is server_reset_query, which the pooler runs when a server
-- -- connection is returned. DISCARD ALL is the correct value and it is not free --
-- -- it runs on every release, or on every server-connection change with
-- -- server_reset_query_always = 0.
--
--   ; server_reset_query = DISCARD ALL
--   ; server_reset_query_always = 0
--
-- DISCARD ALL cannot run inside a transaction, and it cannot reset everything:
-- -- a prepared statement created with PREPARE is discarded; one created by the
-- -- extended protocol is not, and that is the next defect.

--
-- DEFECT 2: prepared statements and transaction-mode pooling. This is the one
-- -- that generates "prepared statement p1_does_not_exist" and "cached plan must not
-- -- change result type" at a rate proportional to the pool size, and it is
-- -- entirely a consequence of how the engines name prepared statements.
--
-- PostgreSQL, extended protocol. The client sends Parse with a statement name, the
-- -- server creates a PREPARED statement on THAT backend under THAT name, and the
-- -- name is a client-chosen string with no server-side uniqueness. PgBouncer in
-- -- transaction mode routes Parse to one backend and Bind to another, and the
-- -- second backend has never heard of the name.
--
--   ERROR: prepared statement "p1" already exists        -- another client used the name
--   ERROR: prepared statement "p1" does not exist          -- different backend
--
-- Three resolutions, in order of preference:
--
-- 1. PgBouncer max_prepared_statements > 0 (1.21+). The pooler tracks prepared
-- --    -- statements per server connection and replays the Parse when the backend
-- --    -- changes. This is the correct answer and it is available on PostgreSQL.
-- --
-- 2. Disable server-side prepared statements in the driver. libpq:
--      prepared_statements = never (in the connection string); node-postgres:
--      options=-c prepare_threshold=0  -- prepared statements are then
-- --      -- created and dropped around every execution, which costs a parse and a
-- --      -- -- plan per call and eliminates the plan-reuse benefit of 9.6
-- --
-- 3. Use SQL PREPARE rather than the extended protocol. Then the statement is
-- --    -- parsed by the server as SQL text and is DISCARDed by DISCARD ALL on
-- --    -- -- release, so it is self-cleaning -- and you pay a plan per execution,
-- --    -- -- because the generic plan is only cached per session.
--
--   PREPARE load_order (bigint) AS SELECT ... ;
--   EXECUTE load_order(412);
--
-- MySQL 9.x: the binary protocol's COM_STMT_PREPARE is server-side and per-session,
-- -- and the same problem exists. MySQL's answer is binlog-based statement
-- -- replication awareness: with binlog_format=STATEMENT or MIXED, a statement
-- -- that references a prepared statement is expanded to its text on the binlog, so
-- -- -- the replica is not affected. With binlog_format=ROW -- the default and the
-- -- -- correct choice -- the prepared statement is a client-side construct and the
-- -- -- applier on the subscriber creates its own. ProxySQL's MySQL protocol layer
-- -- -- handles this internally, which is one of the reasons to use it.
--
-- SQL Server 2025: sp_prepare and sp_execute are per-session objects with a handle,
-- -- and the same thing happens. The handle is an integer, so two clients on one
-- -- -- session collide on it. TDS does not multiplex, so this only arises with a
-- -- -- third-party pooler.

--
-- The pooling configuration, per engine, with the two numbers that matter: the
-- -- client-side maximum and the server-side maximum. Everything else is detail.
--
-- PostgreSQL, PgBouncer in transaction mode:
--   max_client_conn  = 10000      -- the application's total across all
--                              -- -- processes, which is what a
--                              -- -- -- container orchestrator will
--                              -- -- -- give you anyway
--   max_db_connections = 200       -- the actual PostgreSQL backends,
--                              -- -- and it must leave room for:
--   default_transaction_isolation = -- -- -- superuser_reserved_connections
--   --                               -- -- -- (3) + the replication
--   --                               -- -- -- connections + PgBouncer's
--   --                               -- -- -- -- own, so budget for it
--                              -- -- explicitly. 200 is comfortable;
--   --                               -- -- 10 000 is not.
--   pool_mode = transaction
--   max_prepared_statements = 200
--   server_reset_query = DISCARD ALL
--   server_lifetime = 3600
--
-- The formula for the server-side number, and it is not a matter of taste:
--   PG backends  = CPU_cores * 2 + effective_spindle_count
--   (the classic postgresql.conf comment's advice, and it is still roughly right)
--   PG backends <= max_connections - superuser_reserved - replication - maintenance
--   App pool    = PG backends / 2        -- half the budget, so that a
--                                     -- -- pooler failover, a failover
--                                     -- -- promotion, or a batch job
--                                     -- -- can have connections without
--                                     -- -- evicting the application
--   PgBouncer backends = PG backends - 1 (its own admin connection)
--
-- MySQL 9.x: max_connections is a per-instance global and is usually set high enough
-- -- to be a problem. The relevant interaction is that each connection has a per-session
-- -- memory footprint -- sort_buffers, join_buffer, read_buffer, net_buffer -- and
-- -- 1 000 connections at 8 MB each is 8 GB of memory that exists whether or not the
-- -- connections are used. Formula:
--   max_connections <= (RAM_for_connections / per_connection_footprint)
--   per_connection_footprint ~= sort_buffer + join_buffer + read_buffer + read_rnd_buffer
--                             + net_buffer + thread_stack + performance_schema_overhead
--
-- SQL Server 2025: connections are threads, 1 MB of stack each by default, and the
-- -- relevant limit is the memory grant budget rather than a count. The number that
-- -- bites is MAX_CONNECTIONS in the availability group listener, which defaults to
-- -- 0 = unlimited and produces a listener that accepts connections it cannot serve.
--
-- ALTER AVAILABILITY GROUP saas_ag
--   SET LISTENER_URL = (..., PORT = 1433),
--   SET LISTENER_OPTIONS = (
--        DYNAMIC_IP_LISTENER_PORT = OFF,
--        MAX_CONNECTIONS = 2000);       -- and read only routing, so a
--                                         -- -- connect listener
--                                         -- -- routes to a replica
--                                         -- -- when no primary is
--                                         -- -- available
--                                         -- -- -- and a read-only
--                                         -- -- -- error otherwise
--

--
-- The pool metrics that predict failure 1, collected every ten seconds, and
-- -- -- their shapes. A pool is healthy when pool_size is constant and the queue
-- -- -- is zero; the failure sequence is always queue-then-growth, which means the
-- -- -- queue is the leading indicator and the count is the lagging one.
--
-- -- Pool queue depth rising, pool_size stable   -> the application is slow, not the DB
-- -- Pool queue depth rising, pool_size falling   -> connections are being closed, often
-- --                                           -- by a server-side timeout
-- -- Pool size at max, queue zero                -> the limit is the limiter; raise it or
-- --                                           -- find the leak
-- -- Acquire time p99 spiking, everything else flat -> a network or DNS problem between
-- --                                           -- the pool and the database
--
-- Postgres-specific, and the difference between a pool exhaustion problem and a
-- -- server problem. In pg_stat_activity the states are distinguishable and the
-- -- distinction is diagnostic:
--
--   state = 'idle'                  the connection is pooled and healthy
--   state = 'idle in transaction'   the application is holding a transaction and not
-- --                              working in it. THIS IS THE LEAK, and it is also
-- --                              the vacuum blocker from 14.2 failure 4.
--   state = 'active', wait = 'Client'  the application has the connection and is
-- --                              thinking. High counts here mean the pool is
-- --                              sized for latency, not for concurrency.
--   state = 'active', wait = 'Lock'   real work, blocked.
--
-- SELECT state, wait_event_type, count(*), max(now() - state_change) AS oldest
--   FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE datname = current_database()
--  GROUP BY 1,2 ORDER BY 3 DESC;
--
-- The two alerts that come out of this and that are worth having:
--
--   count(idle in transaction) > 0 sustained for 60s   -> page. Always a bug.
--   count(*) / max_connections > 0.8 sustained for 2min -> warn. This one is the
-- -- -- -- -- -- -- leading indicator of exhaustion by hours.
--

Every engine’s memory model makes a connection expensive, and the price is paid whether or not the connection is used. A PostgreSQL backend is a process with a stack, a memory context tree and entries in the shared lock manager’s arrays, and 10 000 of them is 10 000 processes competing for memory and for the lock table. A MySQL connection has thread buffers that are allocated on connect, not on use, so the configured max_connections is a memory budget decision before it is a concurrency one. A SQL Server connection is a thread with a 1 MB stack. The consequence for the pooling configuration is that the server-side limit must be derived from the hardware and the per-connection footprint, and that a large max_connections is not generosity — it is a decision to make the failure mode a memory-exhaustion kill rather than a clean connection rejection.

14.6 Backup is not a restore, and the 3am runbook

A backup that has never been restored is a hypothesis. The gap between a backup job that reports success and a database that can be recovered is where the entire reliability posture lives, and closing it requires a scheduled drill rather than a policy.

The measurable properties of a backup system are: the recovery point objective, which is how much data is lost and which is determined by the archive interval rather than by the backup frequency; the recovery time objective, which is how long recovery takes and which is determined by the volume of data to restore and by whether the restoration is a full restore or a replica promotion; the restore test rate, which is the only one of the three that is under the team’s control; and the coverage, which is whether the backup contains everything — and a backup system that does not include the log, the object storage, the KMS keys and the search index is not a backup system, it is a table dump.

-- The restore drill, as a script. It is the deliverable, and it runs on a
-- -- -- schedule whether or not anyone is watching. The steps are numbered
-- -- -- because the ones that are missed are the ones that are not numbered.
-- --
-- --   D1  Take a logical backup of a production-shaped clone with RPO between
-- --       the backup and the drill, and with the schema at the current version.
-- --   D2  Record the RPO actually achieved: the timestamp of the last committed
-- --       transaction in the restored database, minus the backup's start time.
-- --       Report it. It is a number, not a claim.
-- --   D3  Apply every migration issued since the backup, in order. A restore to a
--       -- schema that is behind production is a restore to a state you cannot
--       -- -- serve, and skipping this step is the most common reason a drill
--       -- -- "passes".
-- --   D4  Re-apply every erasure request and every retention policy that the
--       -- backup predates (12.6). MANDATORY, and the step most often missing.
-- --   D5  Verify integrity: constraint validation, a row-count comparison against
--       -- the source, and a checksum over a sampled table.
-- --   D6  Run the test suite against the restored instance, with the database
--       -- pointing at it. This is what makes it a restore and not a file.
-- --   D7  Time the whole thing, and record the time to a serving state -- not to
--       -- -- "restore complete" but to "traffic served". Those are different numbers
--       -- -- and the difference is migrations, DNS, cache warm and connections.
-- --   D8  Destroy the instance and the volume. A forgotten drill instance is a
--       -- -- forgotten copy of production, with production's personal data.
-- --   D9  Report: RPO achieved, RTO achieved, row-count deltas, pass or fail, and
--       -- a link to the log. Alert on a failure. Nobody reads a report that
--       -- does not page.

--
-- The three restore strategies and their RTOs, because the choice determines
-- -- what the drill must actually test.
--
-- 1. Restore a backup. RTO = the size of the data. Minutes for a recent replica,
-- -- hours for a 2 TB archive. This is the disaster scenario, and a drill on a
-- -- 4-hour RTO is a drill nobody will perform on a schedule.
--
-- 2. Promote a replica. RTO = seconds to a minute, plus the gap between the
-- -- replica's data and the failure. This is the availability scenario and it is the
-- -- one that must be drilled, because every step in 11.6 is manual and an
-- -- undrilled manual procedure does not work at 3am.
--
-- 3. Logical restore into a new cluster, for a major version upgrade. RTO = the
-- -- dump and restore time, and it is the slowest and the one most often needed,
-- -- because a major version upgrade is where logical replication earns its place
-- -- (11.1).
--

--
-- THE 3AM RUNBOOK. Unhealthy database, request latency elevated, no change
-- -- deployed. Everything below is in the order it should be tried, and the
-- -- order is the runbook: cheap and reversible first, and the first three steps
-- -- are read-only and take under sixty seconds between them.
--
--   R1  Is it the database?   Compare the application p99 with the database's own
--       -- p99 over the same window. If the database is fine, stop: the problem is
--       -- elsewhere and continuing here wastes the window.
--
--   R2  Is it connections?     count(*) vs max_connections, and the idle-in-
--       -- transaction count. If idle-in-transaction is non-zero, that is the answer
--       -- and the fix is to terminate those backends (14.2 failure 1).
--
--   R3  Is it locks?          pg_blocking_pids / blocking_session_id. If there is a
--       -- chain, terminate the head of it. The head is usually a report or an
--       -- idle-in-transaction session from a batch job.
--
--   R4  Is it one statement?  pg_stat_statements, sorted by mean_exec_time, over
--       -- the last five minutes if the tooling supports it, or the current deltas.
--       -- A single statement at 100x its usual mean is the answer.
--
--   R5  Did a plan change?     Query Store, or a plan capture. If the shape changed,
--       -- the fix is to restore the previous plan (SQL Server: force_plan) or to
--       -- ANALYZE the affected table and let it re-plan (14.2 failure 6).
--
--   R6  Is the storage slow?   wait = IO, or a rising storage latency metric from R2 in
--       -- 14.2. If yes, this is a storage incident, not a database one, and the
--       -- database actions that help are: reduce the working set, stop the
--       -- checkpoint storm, and shed read traffic to a replica.
--
--   R7  Is the disk full?      ALWAYS check. It is one query, and the failure it
--       -- explains is total and immediate. Extend the volume if you can; it is the
--       -- only action at this stage that reliably works.
--
--   R8  Is the replica behind? If reads are stale, the primary may be fine and the
--       -- incident is a routing problem. Check the byte lag and the applier state
--       -- separately (11.4); they have different causes.
--
--   R9  Shed load.            Move reads to the replica. If it is behind, at least it
--       -- is serving something. Rate-limit the heaviest tenants (13.5). Disable the
--       -- -- non-essential background jobs, which are usually a third of the load
--       -- -- in a system nobody profiled.
--
--   R10 Is it a recent change? Only now look at deployments, config changes,
--       -- parameter changes and cert or key rotations. Rolling back is a big
--       -- hammer and should be the last resort, not the first.
--
-- The three actions that are safe at 3am without approval, and the three that
-- -- are not. Writing this down is what stops the incident from becoming a
-- -- judgement call made by a tired person.
--
--   Safe:     terminate a stuck backend; kill a runaway query;
--             route reads to a replica; enable a rate limit; disable a job.
--             All are reversible, none destroys data, all are observable.
--
--   NOT safe: DROP anything; kill -9 the database; disable a constraint;
--             restore from a backup -- which is a failover, not a fix, and
--             costs the data since the last backup; promote a replica when
--             the primary is merely unreachable, because that is a split brain
--             (11.6).
--

--
-- CAPACITY PLANNING. Four resources, four projections, and the one that is
-- -- -- always wrong: connections.
--
--   Storage.  Data volume is not linear in time; it is linear in time times the
--       -- write rate times the retention period, and the write rate itself grows
--       -- as customers grow. Project: current_bytes + daily_growth * retention_days,
--       -- plus indexes at 0.6-1.2x the data depending on access pattern, plus WAL
--       -- at max_wal_size or 2x the largest transaction burst, plus 30 % for the
--       --         autovacuum's inability to return space immediately (14.2 failure 5).
--       -- For this schema at 12 M orders: orders 2.1 GB, lines 6.8 GB, payments 1.4 GB,
--       -- indexes 4.9 GB, WAL 16 GB, headroom 9.3 GB = 40.5 GB for 7 years.
--
--   Memory.  The working set, not the data. Measure it: for PostgreSQL,
--       -- pg_buffercache in 18 gives per-page residency, and the number to
--       -- size shared_buffers on is the distinct pages touched in a day, not the
--       -- database size. 25 % of RAM, up to 32 GB, is the sane default.
--       -- Everything else -- OS cache, MySQL's buffer pool, the storage layer's
--       -- cache -- is the same memory counted once, not three times.
--
--   CPU.  Derive it from the measured per-request CPU cost, not from a
--       -- ratio: cores = peak_requests_per_second * cpu_ms_per_request / 1000 /
--       -- target_utilisation, and target_utilisation is 0.6, not 0.8, because the
--       -- remaining 40 % is what a failover, a vacuum and a report need.
--
--   IOPS.  From bytes_per_second / avg_io_size for reads and, for writes, from the
--       -- flush behaviour: PostgreSQL's full_page_writes after a checkpoint
--       -- mean a first write to a page is a full 8 KB write, so an update-heavy
--       -- workload's write IOPS are several times its row count per second. This
--       -- -- is the number people get wrong by a factor of 5.
--
--   Connections.  The projection that is wrong because it is a function of load
--       -- and not of data, and load grows with customers while the connection budget
--       -- is fixed by the backend's memory. Little's law is the whole calculation:
--       --
--       --   concurrency = throughput * latency
--       --   2000 requests/s * 8 ms average = 16 concurrent
--       --   2000 requests/s * 400 ms at p99      = 800 concurrent if the tail is
--       --       what the pool is sized for, which is why pools sized on the mean
--       --       are 50x too small exactly when they are needed most.
--       --
--       -- Size the pool on p99 concurrency, and set the server-side limit to a
--       -- fraction of what the memory allows (14.5).
--
-- The projection to keep, and the one question it answers: "on what date, at the
--       -- current growth rate, does the first resource run out?" Four dates, four
--       -- resources, and the earliest one is the one to plan against. The common
--       -- failure is that the earliest date is 19 months away and nothing is
--       -- triggered until 60 days out, because the alert was on a percentage.
--
--   resource        current      growth/day     capacity    runs out
--   -----------    ----------   -----------   ---------   ----------
--   storage         40.5 GB     0.31 GB        500 GB      2027-11-02
--   memory (RSS)    22 GB       0.9 GB         64 GB       2027-03-14
--   CPU (cores)     6.1         0.07           16          2027-01-20
--   connections     41 / 200    0.06           200         2027-09-08
--   WAL             16 GB       --             32 GB       n/a (bounded)
--
