--       -- The three things that make a projection real rather than arithmetic:
--       -- (1) Re-run it monthly and store the history, so the growth rate is measured
--       --         over months rather than over the last seven days, which a bulk load
--       --         distorts.
--       -- (2) Separate the growth that is inside the retention window from the growth
--       -- that is not. Only the first one consumes capacity indefinitely; the second
--       -- one is bounded by the retention policy, and a projection that does not
--       --         make that distinction will overestimate by the ratio of
--       --         retention to window.
--       -- (3) Include the failure modes, not just the growth. Disk full is a
--       --         retention-job failure, a runaway query, or a failed erasure; each
--       --         is a step change rather than a slope, and a linear projection
--       --         cannot express it.
--

Drill the promotion, not the restore. A restore drill tests a procedure that is run perhaps twice a year and that everyone remembers. A promotion drill tests a procedure that must work in the next outage, that is entirely manual on MySQL, and that involves an irreversible decision. The promotion drill is also cheaper — promote a replica in a staging topology, measure, fail back — and it exercises the parts that actually break: the fencing check that stops the old primary accepting writes, the routing change, the plan-cache invalidation, and the connection pool that held a socket to a node that no longer exists. Do both, but if only one is done, do this one, because the restore will take as long as the data is large and the promotion takes as long as the sequence is.

Exercises 14

  1. Write the SLO for a system you can reach as a table of the four layers from section 14.1, with a real number against each and a query or metric that produces it. Then compose them — an end-to-end objective and a database objective — and record the composed number. Most teams discover that their two objectives were inconsistent, and that is the exercise’s value.
  2. Reproduce each of the eight failures in the catalogue on a scratch instance, at a size where it takes under ten minutes: exhaust the connections, create a lock chain, stall a replica, starve autovacuum, inflate a table with updates, drift the statistics, pollute the plan cache, and fill a disk. For each, record the detection query, the time from cause to detection, and the time from detection to recovery. The two time columns are what a runbook needs and what nobody has.
  3. Enable the three slow-logging tiers from section 14.4 and run them under a synthetic load for a day. Record the log volume produced by each tier, the overhead attributable to each, and the number of distinct plans captured. Then set the thresholds so that the total is under 500 MB a day and confirm that the statement you most wanted to catch is still captured.
  4. Demonstrate both pooling defects from section 14.5. For the state leak, use a SET without LOCAL and read the value back from a different request on a returned connection. For the prepared-statement problem, deploy a PgBouncer with max_prepared_statements = 0 in transaction mode and drive a workload with extended-protocol prepared statements until the error appears. Then fix each and confirm.
  5. Run a full restore drill on a production-shaped backup, executing steps D1 to D9 in order, and record the achieved RPO and RTO. Then deliberately omit step D4 — the erasure reapplication — on a backup that predates an erasure request, and confirm the erased data is present in the restored instance. That demonstration is the argument for the step.
  6. Take the 3am runbook and test it: have someone who did not write it follow the first six steps against a deliberately degraded instance, with a stopwatch. Record where they hesitate, which steps they cannot interpret, and which figures are missing. Then add the promotion drill to the schedule and run it once end to end, including the failback, and time it.
  7. Build the capacity projection for a system you can reach, with the four resources and the runs-out dates, and separate the growth inside the retention window from the growth outside it. Identify the earliest date and the resource. Then find the three step changes that could invalidate the projection — a failed retention job, a runaway query, a bulk import — and write the alert for each, because a linear projection cannot express a step.

15 · Sharding and Distributed Data

15.1 When to shard, and the four questions that decide it15.2 The shard key is the whole design15.3 Mechanisms: coordinator, range, hash, directory15.4 Distributed transactions and their real cost15.5 Resharding, dual writes and cutover15.6 Cross-shard queries and the scatter-gather tax15.7 Why most systems never need to

Sharding is the point at which a data problem becomes a distributed systems problem, and every property the relational model gave you for free — a single transaction across related data, a foreign key, a single serialisable point of truth — is surrendered one at a time. The engineering discipline is to be certain you have exhausted the cheaper options, and then to accept the losses deliberately rather than discovering them.

15.1 When to shard, and the four questions that decide it

Sharding is irreversible in practice and expensive to reverse. The decision should be made against a measured ceiling, not an extrapolated one, and four questions distinguish a genuine ceiling from a slow query and a missing index.

Question 1 — is the ceiling a single machine’s ceiling? If the working set exceeds RAM no matter how much is added because the total dataset exceeds any plausible machine, that is a volume problem and sharding addresses it. If the working set is 40 GB on a 256 GB machine, the problem is a query or an index, and sharding makes it worse.

Question 2 — is it write throughput, or is it lock contention? A single engine will usually exceed any application the team can build. Contention on a hot row or a hot index page is a design bug, and sharding by an unrelated key does not fix it.

Question 3 — does the partition key appear in the overwhelming majority of queries? This is the question teams skip and it is the one that determines whether sharding succeeds. If 90% of queries can name the key, a hash-sharded design is invisible to the application. If 30% cannot, every one of them is a scatter-gather and the design is not viable.

Question 4 — what is the operational cost? Cross-shard queries, resharding, replica topology per shard, backup and restore per shard, and a class of incident that no single-node runbook covers. This cost is permanent and it is usually underestimated by a factor of three.

The dominant real-world reason to shard is regulatory or geographic data residency, not capacity. A residency requirement — personal data must remain within a named jurisdiction, or must be physically separable for a named customer — cannot be satisfied by partitioning, because the requirement is about where the bytes physically are. It is a sharding requirement with no capacity component at all, and it is the one case where the operational cost is justified by construction.

15.2 The shard key is the whole design

The shard key is the column that determines placement. It is the most consequential decision in the system, and it is best evaluated by asking what it does to the three properties the relational model provided: locality, uniqueness and joinability.

PropertyGood shard keyBad shard key key
LocalityNearly every query and every foreign key carries the key, so a request touches one shardQueries filter on a different column, so they fan out to all shards and then discard 99% of the result
UniquenessThe key is unique within the shard, so a global unique constraint is enforceable by making it a composite of the shard keyThe key is not unique even globally, so global uniqueness requires a separate coordination service or an allocation range
EvennessCardinality is high and distribution is roughly uniform: tenant_id across thousands of tenantsSkewed: one tenant is 40% of the rows, so it dominates capacity and any single-shard maintenance is a large event
JoinabilityRelated entities share the key, so their join is a local joinRelated entities live in different shards, so every join is a network call and a failure mode
StabilityImmutable for the life of the row, so placement never needs to changeMutable, so every change is a row move, which is a distributed operation
-- The composite-unique trick, and why it is the strongest argument for
-- choosing the key well. If tenant_id is the shard key, then:

CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    tenant_id   bigint      NOT NULL,
    order_id    bigint      NOT NULL,
    placed_at   timestamptz NOT NULL,
    total_minor bigint      NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT pk_sales_order PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, order_id)
);

-- order_id alone is NOT globally unique. (tenant_id, order_id) is unique
-- globally, and because it is enforced on a single shard, no coordination
-- service, no sequence allocation, no timestamp trick is required. This is
-- the property that makes a sharded schema tractable.

-- And the foreign key stays local for the same reason:
CREATE TABLE sales_order_line (
    tenant_id     bigint NOT NULL,
    order_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    line_no       smallint NOT NULL,
    sku           text   NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT pk_sales_order_line PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, order_id, line_no),
    CONSTRAINT fk_line_order
        FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, order_id)
        REFERENCES sales_order (tenant_id, order_id)
);

-- Contrast with a key that does NOT appear in the child table. This FK
-- cannot exist in a sharded schema, and forcing it to is the single most
-- common reason sharded designs have no referential integrity at all.

When choosing a shard key, write down the six or seven queries that will be most frequent, then check each against the key. If any of them cannot name it, either the design changes or that query is accepted as a scatter-gather with a measured cost. Decide it now, in writing, rather than discovering it from a latency graph in production.

15.3 Mechanisms: coordinator, range, hash, directory

Four mechanisms place a key on a node, and they differ on how a client finds the node and how movement is handled. The choice of mechanism is a large part of why a sharded system’s operational characteristics are what they are.

MechanismLookupRebalancingFailure mode
Coordinator / directoryOne lookup against a small, highly available directory. Adds a network hop and a dependencyDirectory is rewritten; can be moved independently of the dataDirectory unavailable means total unavailability, unless it is itself replicated and cached
RangeComputed locally from the key and a range map. No hopSplit a range, migrate it. Cheap and onlineHot range at the high end; requires splitting key ranges carefully
Consistent hash with virtual nodesComputed locally, O(1) hops. The standard choice for large clustersEach vnode is small, so movement is bounded; large movements need a balancer running alongsideHash distribution is uniform but opaque: you cannot range-scan, and diagnosing placement requires a tool
Native partitioningHandled by the engine inside a single nodeAttach and detach partitions; metadata onlyPartition-key queries are fast, non-key queries scan every partition. The right first step, and often the last one needed
-- Native partitioning first: it is available on all three engines, it costs
-- nothing operationally, and it defers the sharding decision indefinitely.
CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    tenant_id  bigint      NOT NULL,
    order_id   bigint      NOT NULL,
    placed_at  timestamptz NOT NULL,
    total_minor bigint     NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id)
) PARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id);

-- Sixteen partitions. A partition is a physical table, so a query with
-- tenant_id = 42 in the predicate reads one of them, not all sixteen.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_p00 PARTITION OF sales_order FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 16, REMAINDER 0);
-- ... p01 through p15

-- Then subpartition by month, so that retention is a partition drop and
-- so that a single hot tenant is still spread across sixteen partitions.
-- HASH outer, RANGE inner: the two mechanisms compose, and the order matters.
CREATE TABLE sales_order_p00_2026_09 PARTITION OF sales_order_p00
    FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-09-01') TO ('2026-10-01');

-- The two-axis lesson: HASH distributes load, RANGE makes deletion cheap.
-- Range alone gives you deletion but keeps a hot tenant hot. Hash alone gives
-- you distribution but makes retention a mass DELETE. Compose them.

PostgreSQL: the partition key must be in every unique key, which is why the primary key above is (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id) rather than a bare order_id. MySQL: the same rule applies, and it additionally requires the partitioning expression to produce a value for every row, so a NULL tenant is a partitioning error rather than a silent skew. SQL Server: partitioning is available on all editions in 2025, and a partitioned index can be aligned to a partition scheme for a fast SWITCH.

15.4 Distributed transactions and their real cost

A transaction that spans shards is no longer a local transaction. Every engine’s answer involves a two-phase commit, and two-phase commit has a property that is not a performance characteristic but a correctness one: the coordinator can fail after a participant has prepared, and the participant is then permanently blocked until the coordinator recovers.

-- The correct design is to remove the need for a distributed transaction,
-- not to make one faster. Three patterns, in order of preference.

-- 1. LOCAL TRANSACTION + OUTBOX. The state change and the intent to publish
--    are one local transaction. Correctness comes from the atomicity of that
--    single commit, and at-least-once delivery from the relay.
BEGIN;
UPDATE sales_order
   SET order_state = 'shipped'
 WHERE tenant_id = 42 AND order_id = 8817;
INSERT INTO outbox (topic, aggregate_id, payload, at)
VALUES ('order.shipped', '42:8817', '{"order_id":8817}', now());
COMMIT;

-- 2. SAGA. Each step is a local transaction; a later step compensates an
--    earlier one. Compensation is a business action, not a rollback: you cannot
--    un-ship a parcel, you can issue a return.
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO reservation (tenant_id, order_id, sku, qty, at)
VALUES (42, 8817, 'SKU-9', 2, now())
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, order_id, sku)
DO UPDATE SET qty = reservation.qty + EXCLUDED.qty;
COMMIT;
-- ... later, if payment fails:
-- DELETE FROM reservation WHERE tenant_id = 42 AND order_id = 8817;  -- compensate

-- 3. TWO-PHASE COMMIT. Use only when the resource is a real database and
--    only when holding a lock during coordinator failure is genuinely preferable
--    to an application-level saga. It is the strongest consistency and the
--    weakest availability, and it should be the last choice, not the first.
MechanismConsistencyAvailability during a partial failureOperational cost
2PC across shardsAtomic, as if one databasePoor. A failed coordinator leaves participants in-doubt and blockedCoordinator state, in-doubt recovery tooling, latency on every commit
Outbox + relayEventually consistent; no intermediate state is observableGood. The transaction is local, so it commits or fails on its own nodeA relay to operate, ordering and deduplication to reason about, at-least-once delivery
Saga with compensationEventually consistent; intermediate states are observable and must be legalGood, provided every step is independently retryableEach compensation is a business rule that must be written, tested and owned

The question to ask before choosing: is there an intermediate state in which the user’s data is observably wrong, and would a user be harmed by seeing it? A payment that briefly shows as pending is acceptable. An inventory count that briefly shows stock which was already sold is not, and it requires either a real reservation with a hold, or an ordering discipline that makes the intermediate state unreachable.

15.5 Resharding, dual writes and cutover

Moving data between shards while serving traffic is the hardest operational problem in a sharded system, and it is the one a team discovers during its first rebalance rather than during design. The technique that works is the same expand-and-contract sequence from section 8.2, applied to topology rather than schema.

-- Phase 1: the topology change is visible but not yet authoritative.
-- Bump a routing epoch, teach the application the new placement, and let
-- it dual-write. Reads still come from the old location for keys not yet moved.

-- Phase 2: backfill the moved rows, in batches, with a cursor, comparing
-- source and destination row by row. Do not trust a copy: verify it.
SELECT count(*)
  FROM sales_order s
  JOIN sales_order_moved m
    ON m.tenant_id = s.tenant_id AND m.order_id = s.order_id
 WHERE s.total_minor <> m.total_minor
    -- must be zero before the cutover

-- Phase 3: flip reads for the moved range. Keep dual-write on for a
-- stated period, not until someone remembers to remove it.
-- Old code tolerating the old topology is not a plan; it is a delay.

-- Phase 4: remove the old location. Only now may a rollback to the previous
-- topology be impossible, and the schedule should say so explicitly.

-- The dual-write failure nobody plans for: a write that succeeds on the new
-- location and fails on the old one, or vice versa. Two systems, no shared
-- transaction, no way to know which is right. Mitigations, in order:
--   1. Make the operation idempotent, and verify by read-back after write.
--   2. Record every dual write in a reconciliation table, and reconcile on a
--      schedule. A discrepancy found in an hour is a repair; found in a month
--      it is an incident.
--   3. Never dual-write across shards for a money movement. Balance is not
--      reconstructible from a log if the log disagrees with itself.

Where a managed service helps. Every major cloud relational service offers a native split — Aurora DSQL, Cloud Spanner, SQL Server Hyperscale, Cosmos DB — where the sharding is implemented by the service and the application sees a single logical database. This removes the resharding problem almost entirely. The trade is that the partitioning model is fixed by the service, and the access patterns you can serve are constrained by it. For a system whose expected growth is known and whose workload is ordinary, a managed split is almost always the better decision than operating your own.

15.6 Cross-shard queries and the scatter-gather tax

A query that does not name the shard key must visit every shard. This is not merely slow: it changes the failure model, because a query that is a single node-local operation becomes an operation whose success depends on the health of every node in the system.

-- The global report, done honestly. Every shard is queried, every partial
-- result is merged, and the cost is N times the per-shard cost with a
-- network fan-out in between. This is a batch job, not a request path.
SELECT tenant_id, sum(total_minor) AS revenue
  FROM sales_order
 WHERE placed_at >= '2026-09-01' AND placed_at < '2026-10-01'
 GROUP BY tenant_id;
-- dispatched to all N shards, results merged in the coordinator

-- The rule that keeps the request path clean: a user-facing request
-- that must be answered in under 200 ms must be single-shard. If a
-- requirement needs a global number, the number must already be stored
-- as an aggregate, not computed across shards at request time.

-- Precomputed global counter, maintained by the outbox consumer, is a
-- denormalisation across shards rather than within one. Same trade, wider blast
-- radius, so the invalidation path must be explicit.
CREATE TABLE tenant_revenue_rollup (
    tenant_id    bigint PRIMARY KEY,
    period_start date   NOT NULL,
    revenue_minor bigint NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    UNIQUE (tenant_id, period_start)
);

-- The dangerous query shape is a paginated global list. It is a
-- scatter-gather whose result is only correct if every shard has the same
-- view of the ordering, and ordering across shards is not defined without
-- a global sequence. Reject the requirement or give it a key.

A paginated list ordered by a globally non-unique value is not merely an optimisation problem. It is a correctness problem that produces duplicate and missing rows, and the bug appears only at page boundaries, which is where users are least likely to be and most likely to be harmed. A cross-shard ordered pagination requirement needs a global, monotonic, unique sort key — a snowflake identifier, or a global sequence allocated in ranges — and no smaller answer is correct.

15.7 Why most systems never need to

The honest summary of this chapter is that the majority of systems that could be sharded profitably from doing everything in the previous fourteen chapters first. This is not conservatism; it is arithmetic about where the ceiling actually is.

Single-node ceiling, 2026 hardwareOrder of magnitudeReached by
Storage per instance~100 TB usable, with a filesystem and a backup strategy that survives itAlmost no application. The operational cost arrives long before the storage limit
Read throughputHundreds of thousands of simple indexed reads per secondLarge read-heavy applications
Sequential write throughputTens of thousands of small commits per second with synchronous durabilityMost multi-tenant SaaS products
Concurrent transactionsHundreds to low thousands, bounded by max_connections and lock behaviourWhere contention usually appears first
Working set in RAMThe real limit. A 64 GB instance needs a working set under ~40 GBFrequently the wall, and the one that is a query problem rather than a capacity one

The pattern across every well-run system is the same: index and query problems present as a capacity problem, are diagnosed as a capacity problem, and are solved by sharding. The result is a distributed system that is harder to operate, slower for the queries that were already slow, and no larger than it needed to be — while the original single-node bottleneck remains, now behind sixteen shards.

When you do decide to shard, shard for the reason you can state in one sentence, and let that sentence be the one written at the top of the design document. "We must keep EU customer personal data physically within the EU" is a sentence that justifies the operational cost. "We anticipate growth" is not, because the growth is a projection and the cost is certain.

Exercises 15

  1. Write the one-sentence justification for sharding your own system, and identify which of the four questions in section 15.1 it actually answers. If it answers none, measure the working set against RAM and fix the query instead.
  2. Take your candidate shard key and test it against the seven most frequent queries in your system. For each, state whether it can name the key. Produce the percentage, and design for the queries that cannot.
  3. Model the composite primary key (tenant_id, order_id) in a sharded schema. Show that global uniqueness needs no coordination service, and contrast it with a globally unique order_id and the sequence-allocation problem it creates.
  4. Convert a single-key range-partitioned table to hash-then-range, and measure whether a single hot tenant still saturates one node. Record the partition pruning behaviour for a key query and a non-key query before and after.
  5. Replace a two-phase-commit flow between two resources with an outbox plus a compensating saga. Document every intermediate state a user can observe, and justify each one as either acceptable or prevented.
  6. Design the resharding cutover for a key range, including the reconciliation query that proves no row differs between source and destination. Then describe what a rollback looks like at each of the four phases, and identify the phase at which rollback stops being possible.

16 · Security and Data Protection in the Database

16.1 The privilege model and why app users must not own tables16.2 Granting the minimum, per engine16.3 Views, row filters and information hiding16.4 Encryption at rest, in transit and per column16.5 Key management, rotation and the envelope pattern16.6 Secrets in the connection string16.7 Auditing who read what

Database security is the layer where a vulnerability becomes a breach rather than a bug, and it is the layer most often left to defaults. The defaults are permissive in the specific way that matters: an application account that owns its tables can read every column, alter the schema, and in most configurations read the entire server.

16.1 The privilege model and why app users must not own tables

A relational engine has a specific, well-designed privilege hierarchy that most applications never use. The single most important decision is that the account the application connects as is not the account that owns the schema.

RoleOwnsCan do in productionNever does
Schema owner / migration accountTables, indexes, types, functionsRun migrations. Present in production only while a migration is runningServe application traffic
Application runtime accountNothingSELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, EXECUTE on named objectsDROP, ALTER, TRUNCATE, create objects, read other schemas
Read-only accountNothingSELECT on named views onlySee a table, including one with columns it should not
Reporting / analytics accountNothingSELECT on a reporting schema, with a statement timeout and a concurrency capRun an unindexed scan against the primary during peak hours
Break-glass accountEverythingEmergency access only, with every session logged and reviewedExist as a shared password in a wiki
-- PostgreSQL: the model stated in full. Every engine has an equivalent,
-- and every one of them has a default that is too permissive.

-- 1. Objects belong to a non-login role. Nothing connects AS it.
CREATE ROLE shop_owner NOLOGIN;
CREATE SCHEMA shop AUTHORIZATION shop_owner;

-- 2. The application role inherits privileges but has no ownership and no
--    CREATEROLE, and crucially not superuser.
CREATE ROLE shop_app LOGIN PASSWORD 'from-the-secret-store'
      NOSUPERUSER NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE NOINHERIT;
GRANT shop_owner TO shop_app;   -- NOINHERIT: privileges are granted per object
--                               explicitly, so a new table is not automatically
--                               readable until someone grants it

-- 3. The migration role, separate again, used only during deployment.
CREATE ROLE shop_migrator LOGIN PASSWORD 'from-the-secret-store';
GRANT shop_owner TO shop_migrator;

-- 4. Column-level grants, so one column is not readable because the table is.
REVOKE ALL ON shop.app_user FROM shop_app;
GRANT SELECT (id, email, status, created_at) ON shop.app_user TO shop_app;
-- password_hash and last_login_ip are now unreachable by the application
-- role, which means a SQL injection in a login query cannot read them.

-- 5. Schemas: the public schema is a legacy default, not a recommendation.
REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC;
REVOKE ALL ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC;
ALTER ROLE shop_app SET search_path = shop, pg_temp;
-- pg_temp last is essential: it stops a caller from shadowing a table
-- with a temp table of the same name and capturing the application's writes.

The shadowing attack is worth understanding. If search_path puts public before the application schema, and the application account has CREATE on public, then anyone who can run SQL can create a table named sales_order and every subsequent unqualified query in the application writes to their table instead. Setting search_path = shop, pg_temp and revoking CREATE on public closes it. This is why ALTER ROLE ... SET default_transaction_read_only and a tightened search_path are both worth doing on the runtime account.

16.2 Granting the minimum, per engine

The vocabulary differs by engine, and the defaults differ more. A table of what each engine restricts on its own, and what must be restricted by hand.

ControlPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Application account may own tablesPrevented by NOLOGIN owner role plus NOINHERIT on the login roleNot prevented. GRANT ALL ON shop.* TO 'app'@'%' includes CREATE and DROP. Grant per tableNot prevented. db_owner implies everything; grant per object or per schema
Read other databases / schemasPrivileges per database, per schema. Public schema must be revoked explicitlyPer database, by grant. No GRANT on mysql.* is needed and should not be givenPer database, per schema, or via roles. CONTROL SERVER is the server-wide equivalent of root
Filesystem accessMust be granted per function: pg_read_server_files, pg_write_server_files, pg_execute_server_program. Not granted to PUBLIC by default since 9.1FILE privilege is global and enables LOAD DATA LOCAL INFILE and SELECT ... INTO OUTFILE on the server, so it is a server-compromise primitive. Do not grant itNot applicable. Use OPENROWSET or bcp via xp_cmdshell, both of which require explicit sysadmin and should be disabled
Row-level restrictionNative POLICY. Section 13.2No native RLS. Views with a mandatory predicate, or per-tenant schemas, or an application-enforced filterNative ROW LEVEL SECURITY security policy, and VIEW DEFINITION control for metadata
Column-level restrictionNative GRANT and REVOKE per columnNative per columnNative per column, and GRANT VIEW DEFINITION must be withheld or the metadata is still visible
Statement timeoutPer role: ALTER ROLE app SET statement_timeout = '5s'Per session; set in the connection init command, because max_execution_time applies only to SELECTPer role via a resource pool, or a login trigger
-- MySQL: the default is too permissive and the fix is one line per grant.
CREATE USER 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%' IDENTIFIED BY '...';
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON shop.sales_order     TO 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%';
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON shop.sales_order_line TO 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%';
-- Not GRANT ALL ON shop.* : that includes DROP, ALTER, CREATE, TRIGGER
-- and GRANT OPTION, which is a schema-compromise primitive.
-- Also note the host: 'shop_app'@'%' is reachable from anywhere.
-- Restrict to a subnet or a specific host, and require TLS.
ALTER USER 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%' REQUIRE SSL;
-- And cap what a compromised application account can cost you:
SET GLOBAL max_execution_time = 5000;

-- SQL Server: the equivalent, with VIEW DEFINITION withheld.
CREATE ROLE shop_app;
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON SCHEMA::shop TO shop_app;
GRANT EXECUTE ON SCHEMA::shop TO shop_app;
-- Without this, the role can read the metadata of every object on the
-- instance, including other databases it has no data privilege on.
DENY VIEW DEFINITION TO shop_app;
-- And restrict what a login can do at the server level:
DENY VIEW ANY DATABASE TO shop_app;

16.3 Views, row filters and information hiding

A view is a privilege boundary that survives the departure of the person who created it. It is also a performance liability, and the two facts are in tension often enough to be worth stating precisely.

-- A view that both hides columns and filters rows. The application connects
-- to the view; the base table is unreachable.
CREATE VIEW shop.customer_summary WITH (security_barrier = true) AS
SELECT o.tenant_id,
       o.order_id,
       o.placed_at,
       o.total_minor,
       c.email,
       c.display_name
  FROM shop.sales_order o
  JOIN shop.app_user c
    ON c.tenant_id = o.tenant_id AND c.user_id = o.customer_id
 WHERE o.order_state <> 'draft';

REVOKE ALL ON shop.sales_order FROM shop_app;
GRANT SELECT ON shop.customer_summary TO shop_app;

-- security_barrier matters more than it looks. Without it, a predicate on
-- the view can be leaked out of a user-supplied function call:
--   WHERE email LIKE '%' || current_user || '@%'
-- evaluates to TRUE for the creator, and the optimiser is permitted to
-- evaluate it BEFORE the view's own WHERE clause, so rows the view was
-- meant to hide become visible. security_barrier makes the view's
-- quals run first, which is the whole point of the view.

-- The performance tension, stated honestly. On PostgreSQL a simple view
-- is folded into the calling query and costs nothing, and the optimiser can
-- use the base table's indexes. Three things break that:
--   1. More than one base table: the planner can no longer push a predicate
--      into a single table's index.
--   2. An aggregate, DISTINCT, LIMIT, or set operation: the view becomes a
--      subquery that must be materialised.
--   3. A recursive view, or one referenced recursively: always materialised.
-- For those three cases, use a MATERIALIZED view and accept the
-- invalidation problem from section 10.2, or write the query in the app.
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW shop.monthly_revenue AS
SELECT tenant_id, date_trunc('month', placed_at) AS period,
       sum(total_minor) AS revenue
  FROM shop.sales_order
 GROUP BY 1, 2;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ix_monthly_revenue ON shop.monthly_revenue (tenant_id, period);
-- The unique index is not optional: REFRESH CONCURRENTLY requires it,
-- and it is what makes concurrent refreshes safe.

MySQL has a different and dangerous default. A VIEW in MySQL is executed with the privileges of its DEFINER, not the invoker, unless it is created SQL SECURITY INVOKER. Every view is therefore a privilege-escalation path unless explicitly created the other way. PostgreSQL and SQL Server both use invoker semantics by default, so the same mistake is not available there.

16.4 Encryption at rest, in transit and per column

Three different mechanisms with three different threat models, and confusing them is how organisations end up with a compliance claim that does not describe their system.

LayerMechanismProtects againstDoes not protect against
In transitTLS, with certificate verification enforced, not merely presentEavesdropping on the network; a compromised switch or routerAn endpoint that logs plaintext; a client configured to skip verification
Storage volumeFull-disk or full-volume encryption, usually managed by the platformA stolen disk; a decommissioned drive that was not wiped; a cloud storage dumpAn attacker with a running instance, which sees decrypted data. This is the layer most often overstated
Backup and replicaEncrypted independently of the primary, with a separate keyA leaked backup file; a replica stream captured in transitAn attacker with a running replica, which sees decrypted data
ColumnApplication-layer encryption before insert, with the key outside the databaseA full database dump; a stolen backup; a read-only SQL accountAnything requiring the plaintext in the database: sorting, indexing, LIKE, range predicates, and most aggregates
-- Column-level encryption with the key held by the application, so a database
-- dump reveals nothing. What this costs is the point of the section.

-- The searchable case, solved honestly: encrypt the value, store a keyed
-- hash of its lower-cased form for equality lookups, and accept that the
-- hash is enumerable if the input space is small.
--   A keyed HMAC over a full email address resists a dictionary attack.
--   A keyed HMAC over a 4-digit PIN does not: 10,000 candidates, any of which
--   an attacker with read access can try directly.

CREATE TABLE shop.contact (
    tenant_id      bigint NOT NULL,
    contact_id     bigint NOT NULL,
    email_cipher   bytea  NOT NULL,   -- AES-256-GCM, nonce prepended
    email_lookup   bytea  NOT NULL,   -- HMAC-SHA-256(pepper, lower(email))
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, contact_id)
);
CREATE INDEX ix_contact_email_lookup ON shop.contact (tenant_id, email_lookup);

-- What you have now given up, in full:
--   * ORDER BY email            -- ciphertext is not ordered by plaintext
--   * WHERE email LIKE 'a%'    -- a prefix search over ciphertext is
--                               meaningless
--   * GROUP BY / COUNT DISTINCT on the value itself
--   * Indexes on the column other than the lookup hash
--   * Any aggregate the reporting layer wants
-- This is a real, permanent product constraint, not a performance note.

-- A key stored in the database is not encryption against a database
-- compromise; it is obfuscation, and a SELECT * finds it. The key belongs
-- in a secret manager, and the application must hold it to write a row.
-- SELECT pgcrypto;   -- then encrypt(key, passphrase) is a footgun, not a design

The correct use of column encryption is a small, well-understood set of fields: government identifiers, payment card data where PCI scope cannot be avoided, medical reference numbers, and anything whose exposure is a reportable breach. Applied to those, the loss of queryability is an acceptable, bounded cost. Applied to an email address that the product needs to sort, it is a functional regression disguised as a security improvement.

16.5 Key management, rotation and the envelope pattern

Encryption without key management is a liability with an extra step: it produces data that cannot be read after the key is lost, and keys that are never rotated. The envelope pattern is the standard answer to both problems.

-- Envelope encryption: one root key encrypts many data keys, and only the
-- data keys travel. Rotation is then a re-wrap, not a re-encrypt.
--
--   root key (HSM, never leaves it)  --> wraps -->  data key  --> encrypts -->  ciphertext
--                                     ^                 |
--                                     |                 + stored alongside each row,
--                                     |                   wrapped, not plaintext
--                                     + re-wrapping on rotation is fast and safe

-- The row format. key_id is not secret and exists so that rotation can
-- tell which data key a row was encrypted with, and so that a partial
-- rotation is detectable rather than silent.
CREATE TABLE shop.secret (
    tenant_id     bigint      NOT NULL,
    secret_id     bigint      NOT NULL,
    key_id        uuid        NOT NULL,   -- which data key, in the clear
    wrapped_key   bytea       NOT NULL,   -- data key, wrapped by the root key
    nonce         bytea       NOT NULL,
    ciphertext    bytea       NOT NULL,
    created_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, secret_id)
);

-- Rotation, in the order the steps must happen:
--   1. Generate a new data key. The old key remains valid for reads.
--   2. New writes use the new key. Mixed-key rows are now expected.
--   3. Re-wrap: decrypt each old data key with the root key, re-wrap with
--      the new root key. No ciphertext changes. Minutes, not days.
--   4. Only then retire the old root key.
-- Step 3 is the one teams skip, and it is the reason "we rotate keys" is
-- usually untrue: a compromise of the old root key still decrypts every
-- row written before the rotation, indefinitely.

-- Rotation is also the only way to satisfy an erasure request for data
-- protected by a keyed hash: destroy the key, and every derived value
-- becomes unrecoverable. See section 12.5.

Do not use a nonce twice with the same key. Reusing a nonce with AES-GCM or ChaCha20-Poly1305 leaks the authentication key and, for GCM, the XOR of the two plaintexts. A random 96-bit nonce is safe until roughly 2³² encryptions under one key, which is why the pattern is: random nonce stored with the ciphertext, and a key rotated well before the birthday bound is approached. A counter that resets on restart is the classic way this goes wrong.

16.6 Secrets in the connection string

The database password is the one secret that is required to be present in every process that talks to the database, which makes it the one most likely to leak. Four things reduce that risk, and all four are cheap.

-- 1. Never a password. A certificate or an identity assertion means there is
--    no shared secret to steal from a connection string, an environment
--    variable, a log line, or a process listing.
--    MySQL 8.0+: caching_sha2_password with REQUIRE SSL, or a client cert.
--    PostgreSQL: cert auth in pg_hba.conf, SCRAM for the cases that need it.
--    SQL Server: integrated Windows authentication, or a managed identity.

-- 2. Scoping the credential to the network it is used from. PostgreSQL's
--    pg_hba.conf is a connection allow-list and should be exactly one rule.
--    local   all   all                        peer
--    hostssl  shop  shop_app  10.0.4.0/24    scram-sha-256
--    hostssl  shop  shop_app  0.0.0.0/0      reject   <-- explicit, not implicit
--    host    all   all   0.0.0.0/0          reject   <-- nothing plaintext, ever

-- 3. Keeping it out of logs. This is the leak that actually happens, and it
--    is usually a framework debug line, not a deliberate log statement.
--    PDO: the DSN is echoed in some error paths. Build it from parts and
--    keep the password in a variable that is never interpolated into a string
--    that might be logged.
--    A DSN of 'pgsql:host=db;dbname=shop' is safe to log. A DSN built as
--    "pgsql:host=$host;dbname=shop;password=$pw" is not, and it will end up
--    in a stack trace that ends up in a ticket.

-- 4. Rotating without downtime. The sequence that works on PostgreSQL:
--    a. ALTER ROLE shop_app PASSWORD 'new';   -- inside a transaction is
--       not possible; two sessions, so there is a window
--    b. Deploy: new instances connect with the new password. Existing pooled
--       connections keep working, because the password was checked at
--       connect time, not per query.
--    c. Drain and restart the old instances.
--    d. Only now remove the old password's validity. If the old credential is
--       still in a secret store, it is still a live credential.
--    The fact that step (b) works is not obvious and is the reason this is
--    safe: password authentication is a connection-time event, so a
--    connection pool does not need to be recycled to make a rotation work.

16.7 Auditing who read what

Write auditing is standard and mostly solved. Read auditing is harder, less often attempted, and is what an investigation after a data-exposure incident actually requires. All three engines can log statements; the question is what survives the log volume.

-- PostgreSQL: log every statement, then filter. pgaudit gives structured
-- output including the parameter values, which plain log_statement does not.
--   shared_preload_libraries = 'pgaudit'
--   ALTER SYSTEM SET pgaudit.log = 'ddl,role,read,write';
--   ALTER SYSTEM SET pgaudit.log_relation = 'app_user,sales_order';
--   ALTER SYSTEM SET log_statement = 'all';
--   ALTER SYSTEM SET log_line_prefix = '%m [%p] %u@%d app=%a ';
--   That prefix is the point: the application_name is what ties a statement to
--   a request, a user and a service. Set it per connection, at connect time.

-- MySQL: the audit plugin, or general_log to a separate file. general_log
-- under load is a performance problem; never enable it in production.
--   SET GLOBAL log_output = 'TABLE';
--   -- audit_log_filter rules, then:
--   SET GLOBAL audit_log_policy = 'LOGINS,QUERIES,OBJECTS';
--   SELECT FROM_UNIXTIME(event_time), user, host, argument
--     FROM mysql.general_log WHERE command_type = 'Query';

-- SQL Server: the native audit, which writes to a server audit file and
-- survives a compromised login because it is not writable by that login.
--   CREATE SERVER AUDIT SPECIFICATION audit_read
--   TO SERVER AUDIT FILE
--   WHERE (object_name = 'dbo.app_user' OR object_name = 'dbo.sales_order')
--     AND statement_type <> 'SELECT';
--   ALTER SERVER AUDIT SPECIFICATION audit_read WITH (STATE = ON);
--   ALTER SERVER AUDIT SET (FILE_PATH = 'C:\audit\shop_audit.sqlaudit');

-- The two design points that make audit logs usable rather than merely
-- present, and both are routinely missed:
--   1. Ship them off-host on the append, continuously. A log on the same
--      host as the database is deleted by the same person who broke in.
--   2. Have a retention policy with a legal floor AND a deletion policy with a
--      legal ceiling. Audit logs contain the same personal data as the
--      database, and they are the least-governed data most systems hold.
--      Section 12.1's three questions apply to them directly.

What to log, in priority order: every DDL and privilege change, permanently and cheaply. Then authentication successes and failures, permanently. Then administrative statements, which are rare. Then data reads on the sensitive tables, which are the most expensive and the most operationally intrusive — start with a sampled or thresholded policy and widen it only where a real question requires it. Logging every read of every table is how audit logging gets switched off.

Exercises 16

  1. Enumerate every role that can connect to your production database. For each, list its grants. The expected result is that more than one role can do more than its name suggests, and that at least one application role can alter the schema.
  2. Split your application credential from its ownership credential, deploy it, and prove the runtime account cannot DROP TABLE, read a column it was not granted, or create an object. Then check search_path and confirm pg_temp is last.
  3. Revoke CREATE on public and set search_path on the runtime role. Then attempt the temp-table shadowing attack described in section 16.1 and confirm it no longer works.
  4. Encrypt one column at the application layer with an envelope key, and list every query in the codebase that can no longer be written. Quantify the loss before deciding, not after.
  5. Write the zero-downtime password rotation for your engine as a runbook, and execute it against a staging environment while monitoring for authentication failures. Find the step where a failure would lock out the application.
  6. Turn on statement auditing with an application_name prefix, confirm it can be attributed to a request, and write the off-host shipping and retention policy including the personal-data deletion rule.

17 · Naming, Anti-Patterns and the Design Review

17.1 Naming rules that survive growth17.2 Constraints as executable documentation17.3 The anti-pattern catalogue17.4 Entity, attribute, value, knowledge: the smell test17.5 Refactoring a schema you cannot redeploy17.6 The design review, as a gate

A schema is the longest-lived artefact in a system. Application code is rewritten every few years; a table definition written once is still being queried a decade later by a team that has never read the discussion. This chapter is about the conventions, constraints and smells that make that survivable, and about the review that catches them before the first row is written rather than after ten million are.

17.1 Naming rules that survive growth

The purpose of a naming convention is not beauty. It is that a reader who has never seen the schema can predict which of three tables to open, without asking. Conventions fail when they encode incidental detail that later changes, and succeed when they encode the thing’s identity.

ConcernRuleReason it survives growth
Case and separatorsnake_case, all lowercase, everywhere, in every engine. Never quote an identifier to preserve its caseUnquoted identifiers are folded to upper or lower case by the engine, and PostgreSQL folds to lower while SQL Server folds to upper. A mixed-case schema requires quoting on one of them, permanently
Table namingSingular or plural, but consistent, and never mixed. Prefer singular for an entity table, plural for an associationThe value is the consistency, not the choice. Mixed conventions make a table list unreadable and force a lookup for every name
Primary keyThe table name plus _pkey or _pk: sales_order_pkey. Constraint names must be unique per schema, so name them from the table rather than genericallyGeneric names like pk_id or fk_parent collide on the first ALTER TABLE that needs a second one, and the error message names neither
Foreign key<table>_<column>_fkey by convention, which is what PostgreSQL and MySQL generate. Do not fight itFollowing the engine’s default means every FK in a dumped schema is identifiable by pattern, which is how you find all references to a table
Indexix_<table>_<columns> for a plain index, uq_ for unique, ck_ for check, fk_ for foreign key, pk_ for primary keyThe prefix tells you the object class without a catalog query. Once you have a naming convention, a text search for ix_sales_order answers "what indexes exist on this table" instantly
BooleanPrefix is_, has_ or can_: is_active, has_shipped, can_refundA bare active is ambiguous between the state and the action, and a bare flag says nothing at all. The prefix makes the column read as a sentence
TimestampAlways suffix _at for a point in time, _on for a calendar date, _date for a date used as a value. created_at, start_dateDistinguishes timestamptz from date at the point of use, which is the difference between a timezone bug and a correct query
MoneySuffix the unit: total_minor, total_cents, amount_pence. Never amount, never priceSection 6.2 is a worked failure caused entirely by an unstated unit. The column name should make the arithmetic impossible to get wrong
Foreign key columnMatch the referenced primary key’s name exactly: customer_id referencing app_user(user_id) is confusing; referencing app_user(id) is notMatching names make a join readable without a schema lookup, and make a missing join condition far more likely to be caught by a reviewer
Reserved wordsNever as an identifier. order, group, user, session, check all require quoting in at least one engine and all are load-bearing words in a queryQuoted identifiers are case-sensitive and case-folded, so they break every future tool that assumes folding. The cost appears years later, in a migration written by someone else
-- A named-constraint schema. Every identifier is greppable, and the only
-- quoting required is the DDL's own, which is not an identifier quote.
CREATE TABLE shop.sales_order (
    tenant_id      bigint       NOT NULL,
    order_id       bigint       NOT NULL,
    customer_id    bigint       NOT NULL,
    order_state    text         NOT NULL,
    placed_at      timestamptz  NOT NULL,
    total_minor    bigint       NOT NULL,
    is_paid        boolean      NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_pkey
        PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, order_id),
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_state_ck
        CHECK (order_state IN ('draft','placed','paid',
                                'shipped','cancelled','refunded')),
    -- A domain-level rule stated once, rather than in every service
    -- that inserts a row. This is the constraint from section 17.2.
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_total_ck
        CHECK (total_minor >= 0)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);

-- Index names carry their own class prefix and their columns, so a
-- question about an index is answered by grep rather than by a catalog
-- query against three different engines' catalogs.
CREATE INDEX ix_sales_order_placed_at
    ON shop.sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_sales_order_external_ref
    ON shop.sales_order (tenant_id, external_ref)
    WHERE external_ref IS NOT NULL;
-- The partial predicate is what makes this work: a NULL external_ref is
-- legitimate for internal orders, and a full unique index would reject
-- the second one. See section 5.4.

17.2 Constraints as executable documentation

A constraint is the only form of documentation that cannot become stale, because it is executed on every write. Application validation is documentation that has already stopped being true, in the specific and common case where a second code path — a script, a migration, an admin tool, an integration partner — writes without going through it.

-- Every rule that is true of the data belongs in the schema, not in the
-- service. Four kinds, and the first is the one most often omitted.

-- 1. DOMAIN. A rule about a value, stated once and reused. Section 6 covers
--    why money and identifiers are the two that matter most.
CREATE DOMAIN order_id_t AS bigint
    CHECK (VALUE > 0);
CREATE DOMAIN email_t AS text
    CHECK (VALUE ~ '^[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+$');
CREATE DOMAIN money_minor_t AS bigint
    CHECK (VALUE >= 0);

-- 2. COLUMN: the type and its nullability. The most fundamental rule
--    and the one most often left nullable for convenience.
    placed_at  timestamptz NOT NULL

-- 3. TABLE: a rule about the row. CHECK, and the exclusion constraints
--    that nothing else can express.
    -- A booking must not overlap itself. No amount of application code
    -- makes this true under concurrency; only the constraint does.
    ALTER TABLE room_booking
        ADD CONSTRAINT room_booking_no_overlap
        EXCLUDE USING GIST (
            room_id  WITH =,
            tstzrange(check_in, check_out) WITH &&
        );

-- 4. REFERENCE: a rule about a relationship. ON DELETE behaviour is part of
--    the rule, and choosing RESTRICT over CASCADE is a real decision -- see
--    section 12.4, where the whole erasure problem is a cascade question.
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_customer_fkey
        FOREIGN KEY (customer_id)
        REFERENCES shop.app_user (tenant_id, user_id)
        ON DELETE RESTRICT   -- refuse, do not silently delete
        ON UPDATE CASCADE    -- an id change is rare; propagate it

-- A constraint with a name, so the violation message identifies itself.
-- 'new row for relation "sales_order" violates check constraint
-- "sales_order_total_ck"' is actionable in a log at 3am; 'violates check
-- constraint "sales_order_check"' is not.

The exclusion constraint is the most under-used feature in this chapter. Anything of the form "these two rows must not both exist in this relationship" — a booking, a shift, a licence, an ad slot, a device session — is a correctness rule that application-level checking cannot enforce, because the check and the insert are not atomic. It is a race condition that a constraint makes impossible rather than unlikely.

17.3 The anti-pattern catalogue

Each entry names the pattern, states why it appears, describes the specific failure it produces, and gives the replacement. They are ordered roughly by how often they are found in a schema written by an experienced developer.

Anti-patternThe specific failureReplacement
SELECT * in application SQLPrevents index-only scans; a new column added to the table silently changes every result shape, every serialised payload and every test fixture that asserted on itName the columns. Generate the projection from the model, so it is updated deliberately
Nullable everythingNULL is a distinct third value that every comparison must handle, and it defeats NOT NULL as documentation of what is required. A nullable NOT NULL-in-practice column is a required field with no enforcementNullable only where absence is a real, meaningful state, and name it to say so: shipped_at rather than date2
The flag1, flag2, type columnA row with flag1 = true and flag2 = true violates a rule no constraint states, and the invalid combination is reachable by any writerTwo tables, or a check constraint enumerating the valid combinations, or an enum. Usually a check constraint is enough
Storing a computed value with no source of truthTwo copies of one fact, differing. The denormalised copy has no invalidation path, so the divergence is a matter of timeSection 3.6. If you denormalise, the invalidation path is part of the same change, and it is tested
Foreign key as a bare bigint with no constraintOrphan rows accumulate silently and are discovered during a report, or neverAdd the constraint. If validation is too slow, add it NOT VALID now and validate in batches — section 8.4
Enormous TEXT column for structured dataUnqueryable, unindexable, and unvalidatable. JSON in a text column has no schema, so every consumer parses and checks it itselfSection 6.5. jsonb with a check constraint on the required keys, and a generated column for anything queried
Timestamp as text or intLexicographic ordering, no timezone, no EXTRACT, no index-friendly comparisontimestamptz, always. Section 6.4
Surrogate key plus a natural key that is not uniqueTwo rows for one real-world thing, which is then discovered when totals do not reconcile. A natural key that should be unique is not declared uniqueUnique index on the natural key. Section 2.3
One wide table for an entire aggregateNullable columns for every subtype, an entity_type discriminator, and a table that no query can index well because the hot columns are not co-located with the discriminatorClass table inheritance or single-table inheritance with the discriminator first in every index. Section 2.5
No ON DELETE clauseEngine default, which is NO ACTION on PostgreSQL and RESTRICT-like on MySQL — an accidental, undocumented policy. Deleting a parent then fails, and the fix is a cascade somebody adds under pressureChoose it deliberately per relationship. RESTRICT for anything you would want to notice
Index on every column queried "just in case"Every index is a write cost and a storage cost, and an index on a low-cardinality column is often never chosen by the optimiser. Section 5.5 is the ledgerAdd an index when a measured query needs it, and remove the ones no query uses. pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan = 0 since the last stats reset is the list
Table used as a queue, with a status column and no lockTwo workers take the same row. SELECT then UPDATE ... WHERE status = 'new' is not atomic, and section 7.4 is the whole discussion of how to make it soSELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, or the engine’s native queue table, or move it off the OLTP database entirely

One more that hides in plain sight: a deleted_at soft-delete column with no constraint and no discipline. Section 12.3 covers the design; the failure mode is that six of the seven tables referencing the entity filter it and the seventh does not, and the data leak is discovered by a customer rather than by a test. A VIEW that owns the filter, and a CI check that fails if a query references the base table, is the mechanism.

17.4 Entity, attribute, value, knowledge: the smell test

Eric Kent Kay’s four-way question is still the fastest way to find the modelling error that costs the most to fix later, because all four mistakes produce a schema that works and is wrong. The question is: is this a thing, a value, a relationship, or a piece of knowledge?

It is aIt hasIt is modelled asThe smell
Entity — a thing with identity that persists through changes to its attributesAn identifier, and attributes that can change without making it a different thingIts own table with a surrogate keyIf the thing disappears when an attribute changes, it was an attribute, not an entity. A row with no natural key is the usual symptom
Attribute — a value belonging to a thing, with no identity of its ownNo identifier. Two identical attributes are still the same attributeA column on the entity’s tableA separate table with a surrogate key for what is a single value. This is the single most common modelling error, and it produces a pointless join on every query
Value — an attribute with a limited domain, stored in a reference table because it has meaning and is reusedAn identifier, plus a description that is edited rarely and read oftenA lookup table with a foreign key, often with cachingStoring the string 'shipped' in 400,000 rows when 'shipped' has a name, a colour and a sort order. The cost is not storage; it is that a spelling change becomes an update to 400,000 rows
Knowledge — knowledge about things: the fact that a product belongs to a category, that a user is over 18, that a region uses a currencyVaries over time and by context, so it needs effective datingAn association entity, often bi-temporal. Section 2.6Placed on the wrong side of the relationship. "This product is in category X" is knowledge, so it is not a column on product — it is a row in product_category with effective dates, because it was not true last year
-- The attribute-that-is-really-a-entity, and why the join is not free.
-- One address per customer is a column. Many addresses per customer is
-- a table. The deciding question is not 'is it reused' but 'does it have
-- identity independent of its owner'.

-- WRONG: an address modelled as attributes of the customer.
CREATE TABLE app_user (
    user_id         bigint PRIMARY KEY,
    billing_line1   text,
    billing_line2   text,
    billing_city    text,
    billing_postcode text,
    shipping_line1  text,      -- and now a second set, because
    shipping_line2  text,      -- the customer may ship to a
    shipping_city   text,      -- different address
    shipping_postcode text
);

-- RIGHT: an entity with its own identity and its own validity period.
CREATE TABLE address (
    tenant_id   bigint      NOT NULL,
    address_id  bigint      NOT NULL,
    line1       text        NOT NULL,
    line2       text,
    city        text        NOT NULL,
    postcode    text        NOT NULL,
    country     char(2)     NOT NULL,
    valid_from  timestamptz NOT NULL,
    valid_to    timestamptz,                  -- NULL = current
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, address_id),
    -- Overlap exclusion: a customer cannot have two current addresses
    -- of the same kind. Enforced by the database, not by the service.
    CONSTRAINT address_no_overlap
        EXCLUDE USING GIST (
            tenant_id   WITH =,
            customer_id WITH =,
            address_kind WITH =,
            tstzrange(valid_from, COALESCE(valid_to, 'infinity')) WITH &&
        )
);

-- Where the address applies is a relationship with knowledge in it,
-- not a column pair:
CREATE TABLE customer_address (
    tenant_id     bigint      NOT NULL,
    customer_id   bigint      NOT NULL,
    address_id    bigint      NOT NULL,
    address_kind  text        NOT NULL,   -- 'billing' | 'shipping'
    valid_from    timestamptz NOT NULL,
    valid_to      timestamptz,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, customer_id, address_kind, valid_from)
);

17.5 Refactoring a schema you cannot redeploy

Every technique in section 8 assumes you can deploy. Sometimes you cannot — a third-party integration reads the table directly, a report is built in a tool you do not control, or a partner’s extract job has a hard-coded column list. In that situation the schema is a published contract and the only available move is additive.

-- Constraint: you cannot change or drop anything a consumer may reference,
-- and you cannot change a column's type or narrow a nullability.
-- Permitted: ADD COLUMN, ADD INDEX, ADD TABLE, ADD CONSTRAINT NOT VALID.
-- Forbidden: DROP, RENAME, ALTER TYPE, ADD NOT NULL, anything that changes
-- the result shape of a SELECT * the consumer runs.

-- The pattern for a type change under this constraint: add, dual-write,
-- read-new, and never drop. The old column becomes vestigial permanently,
-- which is the real cost and should be stated to the business.
ALTER TABLE shop.sales_order
    ADD COLUMN total_minor bigint;      -- phase 1: nullable, instant

-- A synchronising trigger keeps the two columns in agreement without
-- depending on every writer being updated in the same deploy. On
-- PostgreSQL a generated column is preferable, because it cannot be
-- written to and so cannot diverge.
ALTER TABLE shop.sales_order
    ADD COLUMN total_minor bigint
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (total_cents) STORED;
-- A generated column is exactly as trustworthy as its expression. It
-- cannot be written to, so it cannot diverge, and it needs no trigger,
-- no backfill, and no application change. Use one wherever the new column
-- is a pure function of columns that already exist.
-- Where it is NOT a pure function, use a trigger and accept the maintenance.

-- The keep-both discipline that makes this safe, in three lines:
--   1. Add. Nullable, no default, no rewrite. Section 8.4.
--   2. Backfill in batches, then verify: zero rows where old is not null and
--      new is null.
--   3. Deploy readers on the new column. Leave writers dual-writing for a
--      stated period with a reconciliation query on a schedule.
--   4. Document that the old column is now vestigial, and that removing it
--      requires the consumers to be gone. Put that in the schema comment,
--      because the person who needs it will not be you.

Schema comments are the mechanism for this. COMMENT ON TABLE and COMMENT ON COLUMN are supported by all three engines, they survive a dump and restore, they are visible in every client, and they cost nothing. A comment on a vestigial column naming the deploy that introduced it, the column that replaced it, and the condition under which it can be removed is the difference between a schema that gets cleaned up and one that accumulates for a decade.

17.6 The design review, as a gate

A design review is worth having only if it has a defined pass condition. "Any concerns?" is not a gate. This is a checklist with the questions in the order they should be asked, and the pass condition is that every row has an answer that somebody wrote down.

GateQuestionFails when
1 · RequirementWhat are the six queries this data must serve, written as SQL, and what is the expected row count and latency for each?The access patterns are described in prose. Prose cannot be tested against a plan, and section 1.2's whole argument is that the queries determine the schema
2 · IdentityWhat is the primary key, is it stable, and what is the natural key it stands in for?There is no natural key, or the natural key is not uniquely constrained. Both mean duplicate rows are possible
3 · IntegrityWhich rules live in the schema as constraints, and which live in application code that a second writer could bypass?A rule that is true of the data lives only in the service. Every such rule is a constraint that should be a constraint
4 · CardinalityFor every relationship, what is the fan-out and what happens to a query that crosses it?No fan-out is stated. Section 1.3: the multiplication factor determines whether the query is viable, and it is invisible until production
5 · PhysicalWhat is the clustered key, what is the partition key, and is it in every unique key?Partitioning is planned after the unique constraints exist. Then the primary key has to be rewritten, which is a rewrite
6 · IndexFor each of the six queries, which index serves it, and what does each index cost on write?An index has no query attached to it. The ledger in section 5.5 is the answer to whether it earns its place
7 · GrowthWhat is the row count and byte size in 12 months, and what does each index add?Growth is described as "fast". Index growth outpaces row growth and is the number that usually causes the incident
8 · LifecycleWhat is retained, for how long, by what authority, and how is it deleted?Retention is not answered. Section 12.1: it is a schema decision, and a retroactive one is a project
9 · IsolationFor a multi-tenant table, what happens if one query omits the tenant predicate?The answer is "we are careful". Row-level security from section 13.2 is the answer that survives a new engineer
10 · EvolutionWhat is the first change this schema will need, and is it possible without a rewrite?Nobody has thought about it. The first change is always needed within a year, and it is always a column

Exercises 17

  1. Apply the naming rules in section 17.1 to a table you own. Produce the diff of renamed constraints and apply it, then confirm that no application query changed behaviour — a rename should be a zero-risk change, and if it is not, something in the schema depended on the name.
  2. Move every rule that currently lives in application validation into a constraint, one at a time, with a backfill where existing data violates it. Record which migration was the hardest and why.
  3. Run the anti-pattern catalogue in section 17.3 over a table you own and count the hits by category. For each hit, state whether the fix is worth doing now or documented as accepted debt.
  4. Take a column you believe is an attribute and prove it. Find two real instances that differ, and if none exist, convert the separate table back to a column. If two exist, the table stays and the join is justified.
  5. Add a PostgreSQL generated column for a value currently stored redundantly, delete the stored copy after a verification query proves they agree, and measure the write-cost difference against the ledger in section 5.5.
  6. Run the ten-gate review against a schema in your system. Score each gate pass or fail, and identify which single failing gate would have prevented the most expensive problem you have actually experienced.

18 · Appendices: Reference Matrices and Checklists

18.1 Engine version matrix and what each supports18.2 Feature-by-feature support, side by side18.3 The reference DDL, all three engines18.4 Diagnostic query quick reference18.5 The incident runbook, one page18.6 Glossary

Fourteen chapters of prose resolve into a small number of decisions and a small number of queries. This appendix holds the reference material: the version matrix the guide is written against, the feature comparisons that were deferred, a working schema on all three engines, the diagnostic statements used throughout, and a one-page runbook intended to be readable at three in the morning.

18.1 Engine version matrix and what each supports

Every version claim in this guide is pinned to a specific release. The matrix below records what was current and supported at the time of writing, and the features that changed behaviour within the two most recent major versions — because those are the ones that invalidate older advice.

EnginePrimary versionLanguageSupport windowNotes
PostgreSQL18—Five years per major, 18 supported to November 203018 adds asynchronous logical replication into pg_dump/pg_restore, further asynchronous I/O, and continued work on the sequential scan interface. The metadata-only constant-default optimisation dates from 11; the NOT VALID validation path from 9.2
MySQL9.x (InnoDB)—Innovation releases quarterly, LTS for eight releases9.0 deprecated the old query cache entirely; 8.0 remains in wide production use and is the version most tutorials describe. Instant DDL for ADD COLUMN at the end requires 8.0.29+
SQL Server2025 (17.x)T-SQLMainstream to 2031Online index operations remain Enterprise-only. MERGE is present and should be avoided as a statement — the concurrency bugs are documented by Microsoft itself and have no clean fix at the statement level
Redis8.x—LTS policy with named releasesUsed here as a cache only. Section 10.3: the version prefix in a key is what makes invalidation tractable
PgBouncer1.24+—Rollingmax_prepared_statements exists from 1.21 and is what makes transaction pooling compatible with server-side prepared statements. See section 11.5

Where behaviour changed within a major version. MySQL 8.0.29 changed instant DDL semantics; PostgreSQL 11 changed default-value handling; PostgreSQL 16 changed how VACUUM handles the visibility map. An operational runbook written against 8.0.20 and never revisited will contain instructions that are no longer optimal, and a migration written before 11 may include workarounds that are now unnecessary. Re-verify the operational claims in your runbook against the version you actually run, on a schedule.

18.2 Feature-by-feature support, side by side

The consolidated comparison. Each row is a decision from the preceding chapters, with the engine-specific mechanism, so that a design can be checked against a target engine without re-reading the chapter.

CapabilityPostgreSQL 18MySQL 9.xSQL Server 2025
Row-level securityNative POLICY; needs FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY to apply to the table ownerNone. Per-tenant schema, or a view with SQL SECURITY INVOKER and a mandatory predicateNative security policy, with VIEW DEFINITION withheld
Statement-level timeoutPer role, via ALTER ROLE; honoured by all statement typesmax_execution_time applies to SELECT only. No general timeout — use MAX_EXECUTION_TIME hint or a proxyPer resource pool, or SET LOCK_TIMEOUT for lock waits specifically
Keyset paginationRow-value comparison (a,b) < (x,y), index-friendlyRow constructors supported from 8.0, usable but the optimiser is less consistent with themFully supported and index-friendly
Exclusion constraintsNative EXCLUDE USING GIST. The strongest single answer to a non-overlap ruleNone. Enforce with a unique index on a generated hash key, or in the applicationNative EXCLUDE constraint in the same syntax family as PostgreSQL’s
Generated columnsGENERATED ALWAYS AS ... STORED, cannot be written to. Also VIRTUAL since 18GENERATED ALWAYS AS ... STORED or VIRTUAL. VIRTUAL columns are not indexable in 9.xComputed column, persisted or non-persisted. Not indexable unless declared PERSISTED
Partial / filtered indexNative WHERE clause on CREATE INDEXNone. No filtered index syntax. Emulate with a generated column that is NULL for excluded rows, in a unique indexNative CREATE INDEX ... WHERE, available on all editions

Locking read that skips locked rowsSELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKEDSELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, InnoDB onlySELECT ... WITH (UPDLOCK, READPAST). Different syntax, same semantics
UpsertON CONFLICT (...) DO UPDATE, with EXCLUDED and a WHERE on the updateINSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE, with VALUES(col) or the row alias form from 8.0.19MERGE — avoid it. Or UPDATE then INSERT on a unique-violation, which is the safest pattern

Multi-masterLogical replication with conflict handling; no automatic merge.
