The only one of the three with a workable multi-writer story, and it requires Enterprise

18.3 The reference DDL, all three engines

The schema used throughout this guide, written out in full on all three engines. It is deliberately small — four tables — and deliberately complete: every constraint from section 17.2, a partition scheme, both composite and partial indexes, and the composite key that makes tenancy work.

-- ══ PostgreSQL 18 ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════
CREATE SCHEMA shop AUTHORIZATION shop_owner;

CREATE DOMAIN money_minor_t AS bigint CHECK (VALUE >= 0);

CREATE TABLE shop.app_user (
    tenant_id     bigint       NOT NULL,
    user_id       bigint       NOT NULL,
    email         text         NOT NULL,
    display_name  text         NOT NULL,
    password_hash bytea        NOT NULL,
    is_active     boolean      NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    created_at    timestamptz  NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    CONSTRAINT app_user_pkey PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, user_id),
    CONSTRAINT app_user_email_ck
        CHECK (email ~ '^[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+$')
) PARTITION BY HASH (tenant_id);

CREATE TABLE shop.sales_order (
    tenant_id     bigint        NOT NULL,
    order_id      bigint        NOT NULL,
    customer_id   bigint        NOT NULL,
    order_state   text          NOT NULL,
    placed_at     timestamptz   NOT NULL,
    total_minor   money_minor_t NOT NULL,
    external_ref  text,
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_pkey PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id),
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_state_ck CHECK (order_state IN (
        'draft','placed','paid','shipped','cancelled','refunded')),
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_customer_fkey
        FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, customer_id)
        REFERENCES shop.app_user (tenant_id, user_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT
) PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at);

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_app_user_email
    ON shop.app_user (tenant_id, email);
CREATE INDEX ix_sales_order_placed_at
    ON shop.sales_order (tenant_id, placed_at DESC);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_sales_order_external_ref
    ON shop.sales_order (tenant_id, external_ref) WHERE external_ref IS NOT NULL;

-- Row-level security, the second line of defence from section 13.2.
ALTER TABLE shop.sales_order ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE shop.sales_order FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
CREATE POLICY sales_order_tenant_isolation ON shop.sales_order
    USING (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::bigint);
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON shop.sales_order TO shop_app;
ALTER ROLE shop_app SET search_path = shop, pg_temp;
-- ══ MySQL 9.x (InnoDB) ══════════════════════════════════════════════════
-- Differences that matter, each of which is a design decision rather than
-- a syntax preference:
--   * No CHECK-violating domains: CHECK is a real constraint since 8.0.16.
--   * No native RLS: the tenant predicate is application-enforced here, and
--     section 13.2 is the mitigation that does not exist on this engine.
--   * HASH partitioning needs the partition key in every unique key.
--   * No partial index: a generated column set to NULL is the substitute.

CREATE TABLE app_user (
    tenant_id     bigint       NOT NULL,
    user_id       bigint       NOT NULL,
    email         varchar(320)  NOT NULL,
    display_name  varchar(255)  NOT NULL,
    password_hash binary(32)   NOT NULL,
    is_active     boolean      NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    created_at    timestamp(6)   NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, user_id),
    UNIQUE KEY uq_app_user_email (tenant_id, email),
    CONSTRAINT app_user_email_ck
        CHECK (email REGEXP '^[^@[:space:]]+@[^@[:space:]]+\\.[^@[:space:]]+$')
) ENGINE=InnoDB PARTITION BY KEY (tenant_id) PARTITIONS 16;
-- PARTITION BY KEY, not HASH: KEY uses the primary key's prefix, so it is
-- guaranteed unique per partition and needs no extra unique key. HASH
-- on a nullable or low-cardinality column silently skews.

CREATE TABLE sales_order (
    tenant_id     bigint       NOT NULL,
    order_id      bigint       NOT NULL,
    customer_id   bigint       NOT NULL,
    order_state   varchar(16)   NOT NULL,
    placed_at     timestamp(6)   NOT NULL,
    total_minor   bigint       NOT NULL,
    external_ref  varchar(64),
    -- The partial-index substitute: NULL for rows that should not be
    -- unique. MySQL unique indexes permit multiple NULLs.
    external_ref_key varchar(64)
        GENERATED ALWAYS AS (external_ref) STORED,
    PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id),
    UNIQUE KEY uq_sales_order_external_ref (tenant_id, external_ref_key),
    KEY ix_sales_order_customer (tenant_id, customer_id),
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_state_ck
        CHECK (order_state IN ('draft','placed','paid',
                                  'shipped','cancelled','refunded')),
    CONSTRAINT sales_order_total_ck CHECK (total_minor >= 0),
    CONSTRAINT fk_sales_order_customer
        FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, customer_id)
        REFERENCES app_user (tenant_id, user_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT
) ENGINE=InnoDB
  PARTITION BY RANGE (placed_at) (
    PARTITION p2026_09 VALUES LESS THAN ('2026-10-01'),
    PARTITION pmax     VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE)
  );

-- Application-side tenant scoping, which on this engine is not optional
-- belt-and-braces but is in fact the only defence.
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON shop.sales_order TO 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%';
REVOKE CREATE ON shop.* FROM 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%';
ALTER USER 'shop_app'@'10.0.0.%' REQUIRE SSL;
-- ══ SQL Server 2025 ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════
-- Differences that matter:
--   * datetime2(7), never datetime. datetime has 3.33 ms resolution and a
--     range ceiling of 9999.
--   * money is a fixed 4-decimal currency type. Prefer bigint minor units,
--     exactly as on the other two engines.
--   * Partitioning is on all editions in 2025; online index operations are not.
--   * IDENTITY for the surrogate; SEQUENCE where you need gaps not to matter.

CREATE SCHEMA shop AUTHORIZATION dbo;

CREATE TABLE shop.app_user (
    tenant_id     bigint         NOT NULL,
    user_id       bigint         IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    email         nvarchar(320)   NOT NULL,
    display_name  nvarchar(255)   NOT NULL,
    password_hash varbinary(32)    NOT NULL,
    is_active     bit             NOT NULL CONSTRAINT df_app_user_active DEFAULT 1,
    created_at    datetime2(7)    NOT NULL CONSTRAINT df_app_user_created DEFAULT SYSUTCDATETIME(),
    CONSTRAINT pk_app_user PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, user_id),
    CONSTRAINT uq_app_user_email UNIQUE (tenant_id, email),
    -- Check constraints on nvarchar need a length guard, because
    -- LIKE '%' is not a valid pattern and the constraint would reject
    -- every row rather than raising a useful error.
    CONSTRAINT ck_app_user_email CHECK (
        email LIKE '%@%.%' AND email NOT LIKE '% %'
        AND email LIKE '%@%' AND email NOT LIKE '%@@%')
) ON ps_shop_hash (tenant_id);

CREATE TABLE shop.sales_order (
    tenant_id     bigint         NOT NULL,
    order_id      bigint         IDENTITY(1,1) NOT NULL,
    customer_id   bigint         NOT NULL,
    order_state   varchar(16)    NOT NULL,
    placed_at     datetime2(7)    NOT NULL,
    total_minor   bigint         NOT NULL,
    external_ref  varchar(64)    NULL,
    CONSTRAINT pk_sales_order PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, placed_at, order_id),
    CONSTRAINT ck_sales_order_state CHECK (order_state IN (
        'draft','placed','paid','shipped','cancelled','refunded')),
    CONSTRAINT ck_sales_order_total CHECK (total_minor >= 0),
    CONSTRAINT fk_sales_order_customer FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, customer_id)
        REFERENCES shop.app_user (tenant_id, user_id) ON DELETE NO ACTION
) ON ps_shop_range (placed_at);

-- Filtered index: the native partial index, and note that it is the only
-- way to express "unique where not null" without a filtered unique index
-- failing on the NULL rows.
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_sales_order_external_ref
    ON shop.sales_order (tenant_id, external_ref)
    WHERE external_ref IS NOT NULL;

-- Row-level security, the one form that is genuinely native here.
CREATE SECURITY POLICY sp_sales_order_tenant
    ADD FILTER PREDICATE
        tenant_id = CONVERT(bigint, SUSER_SNAME())
    ON shop.sales_order
    WITH (STATE = ON);

One detail in the SQL Server version is worth singling out: the policy predicate uses SUSER_SNAME() to derive the tenant, which means an application login per tenant. That is defensible for a small number of large customers and wrong for thousands of tenants, where a login per tenant is an operational impossibility. The common compromise is a CONTEXT_INFO or session-context value set by the application, which is functionally identical to PostgreSQL’s current_setting and carries the same risk: it must be set inside the transaction, or a pooled connection leaks it.

18.4 Diagnostic query quick reference

The statements used throughout this guide, gathered in one place. Each is the query to reach for first when a specific symptom appears, and each is read as a delta between two samples rather than as an absolute value.

-- ── Running right now, and how long ─────────────────────────────────────────
SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS duration, state, wait_event_type, wait_event,
       left(query, 120) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state <> 'idle' ORDER BY duration DESC;

-- ── Long transactions: the ones that block vacuum and hold locks ─────────
--   This single query prevents more incidents than any index in this guide.
SELECT pid, xact_start, now() - xact_start AS age, state, left(query, 100)
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL
   AND now() - xact_start > interval '5 minutes'
   AND backend_type = 'client backend'
 ORDER BY xact_start;

-- ── Blocking tree: who is waiting on whom ────────────────────────────────
SELECT blocked.pid AS blocked_pid,
       blocked.query AS blocked_query,
       unnest(pg_blocking_pids(blocked.pid)) AS blocking_pid
  FROM pg_stat_activity blocked
 WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(blocked.pid)) > 0;

-- ── Unused, duplicate and invalid indexes ────────────────────────────────
SELECT s.relname AS table_name, s.indexrelname AS index_name,
       s.idx_scan, pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid)) AS size
  FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s JOIN pg_index i ON i.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
 WHERE s.idx_scan = 0 AND NOT i.indisunique
 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid) DESC;

-- ── Bloat: dead tuples against live tuples ───────────────────────────────
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       round(100.0 * n_dead_tup / NULLIF(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 1) AS dead_pct
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 20;

-- ── Cumulative statement cost: the delta between two samples ────────────
SELECT calls, round(mean_exec_time, 2) AS mean_ms,
       round(total_exec_time) AS total_ms, rows, shared_blks_read, query
  FROM pg_stat_statements ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 20;

-- ── Replication lag: bytes and seconds, separately ──────────────────────
SELECT application_name,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn)) AS byte_lag,
       EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM now() - reply_time) AS time_lag_s
  FROM pg_stat_replication;

-- ── Connection counts, grouped by who ───────────────────────────────────
SELECT usename, application_name, client_addr, state, count(*)
  FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY 1,2,3,4 ORDER BY 5 DESC;

-- ── Size: table, indexes, total ──────────────────────────────────────────
SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size('shop.sales_order')) AS total,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('shop.sales_order'))          AS heap,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_indexes_size('shop.sales_order'))             AS indexes;

-- ── MySQL equivalents of the three that matter most ─────────────────────
SHOW FULL PROCESSLIST;                              -- running and waiting
SELECT * FROM information_schema.innodb_trx ORDER BY trx_started;
SHOW REPLICA STATUS\G                              -- Seconds_Behind_Source

-- ── SQL Server: the three that matter most ──────────────────────────────
SELECT session_id, blocking_session_id, wait_type, wait_time,
       percent_complete, left(t.text, 120) AS query
  FROM sys.dm_exec_requests r CROSS APPLY sys.dm_exec_sql_text(r.sql_handle) t
 WHERE r.session_id <> @@SPID AND r.blocking_session_id <> 0;
-- The Query Store is the tool for plan regression. It records every plan
-- with its cost and its actual execution statistics, and it will tell
-- you the date the plan for a statement last changed, which is the
-- single most useful fact in diagnosing a query that worked yesterday.

18.5 The incident runbook, one page

Everything in this guide, reduced to what is needed at three in the morning. The order is deliberate: cheapest and safest first, and nothing irreversible until the diagnosis is in hand.

0 · Before anything else. What is the user-visible symptom, when did it start, and did anything change at that time — a deploy, a migration, a traffic shift, a certificate expiry? The answer is usually in the last hour of a deploy log and it saves the next thirty minutes.

1 · Is it the database, or is the database fine? Connect directly and run SELECT 1. If that is fast, the problem is the application, the network, or a pooler — not the engine. Do not begin tuning an engine that is answering in a millisecond.

2 · What is running, and what is waiting? The pg_stat_activity query above, ordered by duration. One query holding the CPU is a different incident from fifty queries all waiting, and the fix is different.

3 · If everything is waiting: find the blocker. The blocking-tree query. Then, before killing anything, establish what transaction it is and whether it is safe to lose. A payment capture in progress is not safe. A report query is.

4 · Cancel before you terminate. pg_cancel_backend() releases locks and returns an error to the client. pg_terminate_backend() kills the session. Cancel first in every case; terminate only when cancel has not worked and you have decided the transaction is expendable.

5 · Check for the two silent killers. A long-running transaction blocks VACUUM, and a lagging replica means the next failover loses data. Both are invisible in the request path and both turn a today incident into a tomorrow one.

6 · If latency is up but CPU is not. Look at I/O wait, at lock waits, and at connection count. On PostgreSQL, enable log_lock_waits — short blocking waits are not logged by default, so the logs can look empty during a lock storm.

7 · If a migration is running. Check which statement and which lock. If it holds ACCESS EXCLUSIVE and the row count is large, you have an outage caused by a planned change, and the decision is whether to wait or to kill and roll back. Know the rollback statement before you need it.

8 · After the incident. Write down the timeline, the query that diagnosed it, and the one change that would have shortened it. Schedule the change. An incident with no scheduled follow-up change is a repeat with a delay.

Never do these during an incident without a second person confirming. Killing the oldest transaction without reading it. Restarting the engine to "clear" a lock — it converts a degraded state into a full outage and loses every uncommitted transaction. Promoting a replica without checking its lag, which trades an outage for silent data loss. And increasing max_connections, which makes the next exhaustion slower rather than preventing it.

18.6 Glossary

TermDefinition as used in this guide
Access patternA query written as SQL, with its expected frequency, row count and latency. Section 1.2 argues these are the input to schema design, not an output of it
CardinalityNumber of distinct values in a column. Determines index usefulness: a boolean has two, and an index on it is nearly always wrong. Also used for relationship degree, which is section 1.3's fan-out
Casting a writeAny write whose purpose is to change how existing rows are stored or interpreted — a type change, a backfill, a reindex. Distinguishes itself from a data write because of its lock profile
Clustered keyThe column defining physical row order. On PostgreSQL the CLUSTER command and the ctid; on SQL Server the clustered index; on MySQL the InnoDB primary key, which is the clustered index by definition
Covering indexAn index containing every column a query needs, permitting the read to be answered without touching the table. On PostgreSQL also called an index-only scan, and dependent on the visibility map for its performance
DML, DDL, DCLData Manipulation, Data Definition, Data Control. The third is the one most often forgotten, and it is where privileges live
Enforced predicateA tenant or partition filter that the database itself applies, so omitting it yields an empty result rather than another tenant’s rows. Section 13.2
Expand and contractThe four-phase sequence that makes a schema change safe under live traffic, by ensuring each phase is individually compatible with both the old and the new code. Section 8.2
Logical replicationReplication of row-level changes as a stream. Feeds CDC and cross-engine copies; does not replicate DDL, which must be applied separately. Section 11.1
MVCCMulti-version concurrency control. Readers never block writers and writers never block readers, at the cost of dead row versions that must eventually be reclaimed. The reason autovacuum exists, and the reason a long transaction is dangerous
Physical replicationReplication of storage-level changes, producing an exact copy including DDL. Restrictive: same engine, same major version, no filtering. Section 11.1
Plan cachingThe engine reusing a prepared plan across executions. A source of intermittent slowness when parameter values shift and the cached plan is no longer appropriate. Section 9.4, and the reason the Query Store exists
Predicate lockA lock on rows matching a condition rather than on identified rows. The mechanism by which serialisable isolation prevents phantom reads, and the source of the aborts a serialisable transaction must retry
Primary key, surrogate, naturalThe identifying constraint; a key with no domain meaning, usually an auto-increment or UUID; and a key drawn from the domain, such as a national insurance number. Section 2.3 argues the surrogate is usually right and the natural key must still be constrained
Read-your-writeThe guarantee that a user sees their own writes, broken by replication lag and repaired by routing, versioning or a read-your-own-write session flag. Section 7.6, 11.3
ReshardingMoving data between nodes to change the partition count or key distribution, under live traffic. Section 15.5
RollupA precomputed aggregate, either a MATERIALIZED VIEW or a maintained summary table. Trades freshness for read cost, and needs an invalidation path to be trustworthy. Section 3.5
Scatter-gatherA query that must be dispatched to every node and merged. Acceptable in a batch report, disqualifying on a request path. Section 15.6
Shard keyThe column determining which node holds a row. Section 15.2
Snapshot isolationEach statement sees a consistent snapshot taken at its start. Permits write skew, which is the anomaly section 7.3 works through in full
Statement timeoutA configured limit on a single statement’s duration, after which the engine aborts it. Distinct from a lock timeout, and the only self-limiting mechanism that prevents a runaway query from becoming an outage
Write skewTwo transactions each valid in isolation, whose combination violates a constraint neither checks. Needs serialisable isolation or an explicit predicate lock to prevent. Section 7.3
Zero-downtime migrationA schema change applied to a running system with no period in which any version of the application fails. Achievable for additive changes via section 8.2; not achievable for a type change on a large table without a documented maintenance window

Exercises 18

  1. Write the reference schema from section 18.3 on all three engines and diff them. For every syntactic difference, state whether it is a portability problem or a design decision — and confirm that each is one or the other rather than an accident.
  2. Take the ten diagnostic queries from section 18.4 and run them against a database under load. Read each as a delta between two samples sixty seconds apart, and record which of the ten tells you something the other nine do not.
  3. Reduce your own incident runbook to one page, in the order of section 18.5. Then time a colleague reading it during a simulated incident. Every question they had to ask is a line that was missing.
  4. Verify the operational claims in section 18.1 against the exact engine version you run. Record every claim that was true when written and is no longer true for your version.
  5. Take one term from the glossary that you use routinely and re-read the definition. If it differs from your usage, find the chapter that says so and decide which of you is wrong.

Advanced Database System Design — Enterprise Data Architecture. One-page interactive textbook. Progress is saved in your browser (theme, last chapter). Open in any modern browser. No internet needed.
